# Claims Compilation — All Claims (claim-0001 through claim-0194)

This file contains all 183 claims from the knowledge base, compiled into self-contained entries with no links. Each entry includes statement, plain-English explanation, mechanism, evidence, policy implications, and inlined dependency summaries.

---


---
## claim-0001: Doubling city population raises wages or productivity by 2.7–6.4%, median 4.6%

**Statement**
Doubling city population size is associated with a 2.7–6.4% increase in wages or productivity, with a median estimate of 4.6%, based on a meta-analysis of 6,684 estimates from 295 studies covering 54 countries over six decades.

**Meaning (plain English)**
Every time a city doubles in size, workers and firms in that city become roughly 4–5% more productive on average. This relationship holds across a wide range of countries and time periods, though the exact gain varies considerably depending on how it is measured.

**Why it matters**
For New Zealand, this means that growing Auckland and other urban centres is not just a demographic trend but an economic opportunity — larger cities generate real productivity gains. Policies that constrain urban growth (restrictive zoning, infrastructure underinvestment) suppress these gains, while policies that enable agglomeration can lift national productivity.

**Mechanism**
Larger cities bring more workers, firms, and ideas into proximity, enabling better job-worker matching, knowledge spillovers between firms, and sharing of specialised inputs and infrastructure. These three channels — matching, learning, and sharing — reinforce each other as city size grows.

**Evidence**
Donovan et al. (2021) conducted a Bayesian meta-analysis of 6,684 estimates from 295 studies across 54 countries and six decades. This is the most comprehensive synthesis of the agglomeration literature to date, and its evidence strength is rated as meta-analysis — the highest available.

**Dependencies**
None listed in the relationships block.

**Policy implications**
- Enabling Auckland to grow denser and more connected should be a core national productivity strategy, not merely a housing or planning issue.
- Infrastructure investment that expands the effective labour market catchment of major cities can unlock agglomeration gains.
- Policies restricting mobility or urban growth impose productivity costs that compound over time.
- The 2.7–6.4% range means NZ policymakers should treat agglomeration benefits as real but uncertain — a range rather than a point estimate should inform benefit-cost analysis.
- Publication bias in the underlying literature suggests the true elasticity may be somewhat below 4.6%; policy appraisals should use conservative assumptions.

**Relevant institutions**
MBIE, Treasury, Auckland Council, Te Waihanga (NZ Infrastructure Commission), HUD.

---
## claim-0011: Metropolitan-scope agglomeration elasticity is 3.3%; national-scope is 6.4%

**Statement**
Agglomeration elasticity estimates are lower when measured at metropolitan spatial scope (populations 0.2m–1m) than at national scope: the median metropolitan estimate is 3.3% (90% CI: 1.5–5.0%) compared to 6.4% (90% CI: 4.6–8.2%) at national scope.

**Meaning (plain English)**
The productivity gain from city size looks larger when you measure it using big national geographic units than when you measure it at the city level. For city-level policy analysis, the more relevant and more conservative 3.3% metropolitan estimate should be used.

**Why it matters**
NZ urban policy assessments that cite headline agglomeration figures from national-scope studies risk overstating the productivity gains available from making Auckland larger. The correct benchmark for Auckland-specific policy analysis is closer to 3.3% per doubling of population.

**Mechanism**
Metropolitan-scale measures capture a smaller spatial footprint of spillovers than national-level potential measures, which integrate connectivity across the whole country. The city-level estimate is thus a lower bound on the total agglomeration effect but more directly applicable to local planning decisions.

**Evidence**
Donovan et al. (2021) meta-analysis of 6,684 estimates; the spatial scope finding comes from their preferred Bayesian mixed-effects model. Evidence strength is meta-analysis.

**Dependencies**
Qualifies: claim-0001 — the global median of 4.6% per doubling (from the full sample across all spatial scopes) is the headline finding that claim-0011 refines downward when the analysis is restricted to metropolitan spatial scope.
Depends on: claim-0001 — the metropolitan-scope estimate is derived from the same meta-analysis dataset and modelling framework as the overall 4.6% estimate.

**Policy implications**
- Benefits-cost analyses for Auckland transport or zoning reform should use 3.3% as a conservative agglomeration elasticity, not the global headline of 4.6%.
- The credible interval (1.5–5.0%) is wide — sensitivity analysis across this range is essential for robust appraisal.
- Smaller NZ cities (Wellington, Christchurch) fall at the lower end of the metropolitan population range and may yield elasticities toward the lower bound.
- International-scope estimates (11.6%) reflect trade and cross-border effects and are not relevant for domestic city-level policy.
- Treasury and MBIE guidance on benefit-cost assessment of urban investment should specify which elasticity estimate is appropriate for which spatial scale.

**Relevant institutions**
Treasury, MBIE, Auckland Council, Te Waihanga.

---
## claim-0012: Estimated agglomeration elasticities peaked around 2000 and have since declined ~1.5%

**Statement**
Estimated agglomeration elasticities in production rose from 1980 to approximately 2000 and have since declined; by approximately 2020 they were roughly 1.5% below their peak, suggesting the productive advantages of cities are not stable over time.

**Meaning (plain English)**
The measurable benefit of being in a big city has been shrinking since around 2000. By 2020, the estimated productivity gain from city size was about 1.5 percentage points lower than it was two decades earlier. The causes are uncertain but may include growing congestion costs, changing technology, and cheaper freight.

**Why it matters**
If agglomeration benefits are declining over time, NZ's case for investing heavily in urban densification purely on productivity grounds becomes weaker at the margin. Policy analysis should not assume agglomeration benefits are stable at historical levels.

**Mechanism**
Proposed but speculative explanations include: congestion costs offsetting scale benefits as cities grew; ICT initially concentrating then dispersing economic activity; declining freight costs reducing the premium on proximity; and stricter environmental regulations. The year-of-data trend is a statistical observation in the meta-data, not a proven causal mechanism.

**Evidence**
Donovan et al. (2021) meta-analysis; the time trend is estimated from the year-of-data variable in their preferred Bayesian mixed-effects model. Evidence strength is meta-analysis, but the trend interpretation is acknowledged by the authors as speculative.

**Dependencies**
Qualifies: claim-0001 — the 4.6% median from claim-0001 is a pooled estimate across the full time period; claim-0012 shows this figure has been declining and that the current level is lower than the historical average.

**Policy implications**
- NZ agglomeration benefit estimates used in policy appraisal should be drawn from recent data rather than historical averages.
- The declining trend warrants monitoring whether Auckland's productivity premium is growing or shrinking over time.
- ICT and remote work trends post-2020 may accelerate or reverse this trend — evidence should be updated regularly.
- Policy should not assume the link between city size and productivity is permanent or unconditional.
- MBIE and Treasury should flag the time-trend caveat in guidance documents on agglomeration benefits.

**Relevant institutions**
MBIE, Treasury, Auckland Council, Stats NZ.

---
## claim-0013: Manufacturing sector agglomeration elasticities are ~0.6% lower and have declined for six decades

**Statement**
Manufacturing sector agglomeration elasticities are approximately 0.6% lower than economy-wide estimates and have declined throughout the entire six-decade period 1960–2020, with estimates by 2020 approximately 10% smaller than in 1960.

**Meaning (plain English)**
Factories and manufacturers benefit less from being in big cities than the economy as a whole, and this gap has been widening for sixty years. The productivity advantage of locating manufacturing in dense cities has shrunk considerably since 1960, which may partly explain why manufacturing has increasingly left central urban areas.

**Why it matters**
NZ economic development strategies that rely on manufacturing agglomeration — for example, industrial land in Auckland — face a headwind: the empirical evidence suggests manufacturing benefits less and less from urban concentration. Services and knowledge industries are the sectors where density pays off most.

**Mechanism**
Declining freight costs reduce the premium on proximity to suppliers and customers for goods producers. Stricter environmental regulations in cities raise costs for manufacturers specifically. ICT has enabled coordination without co-location for many manufacturing activities. These are speculative explanations acknowledged as such by the authors.

**Evidence**
Donovan et al. (2021) meta-analysis; the −0.6% manufacturing differential is from Model 5 (preferred specification). The six-decade trend is from Figure 8 of the paper. Evidence strength is meta-analysis.

**Dependencies**
Qualifies: claim-0001 — the economy-wide 4.6% median overstates the benefit for manufacturing specifically; claim-0013 shows manufacturing is below average and declining.
Qualifies: claim-0012 — the overall decline in agglomeration elasticities since 2000 documented in claim-0012 is partly driven by the manufacturing sector's sustained downward trend, which has been occurring since 1960.

**Policy implications**
- Industrial land policy in Auckland and other NZ cities should account for declining manufacturing agglomeration; protecting industrial land from residential conversion may have lower opportunity cost than previously assumed.
- Economic development strategies for NZ cities should prioritise service sector agglomeration, particularly knowledge-intensive and financial services.
- NZ's manufacturing sector is already spatially dispersed; the evidence does not support clustering it into central urban areas as a productivity lever.
- Regional development strategies should recognise that secondary cities may be increasingly competitive with Auckland specifically for manufacturing activities.
- Trade and logistics infrastructure (ports, freight corridors) remains important for manufacturing even as agglomeration benefits decline.

**Relevant institutions**
MBIE, Auckland Council, regional councils, Te Waihanga.

## claim-0014: Rigorous controls substantially reduce agglomeration elasticity estimates

**Statement**
More rigorous empirical controls consistently and substantially reduce measured agglomeration elasticities: individual worker effects reduce estimates by approximately 1.1%, housing supply or price controls by approximately 3.8%, and capital intensity controls by approximately 2.4%.

**Meaning (plain English)**
Studies that fail to control for the types of workers, the cost of housing, and the amount of capital used will overestimate how much of a city's productivity comes from mere size. Once these factors are accounted for, the measured productivity benefit of being in a big city falls substantially — sometimes by more than half.

**Why it matters**
NZ policy analysis that relies on uncorrected agglomeration estimates from the academic literature risks overstating productivity benefits. Benefit-cost assessments for Auckland housing, transport, or zoning reform should use estimates from well-controlled studies, not raw correlations between city size and wages.

**Mechanism**
Cities attract more skilled workers (sorting bias), have higher housing costs that compensate for some wage gains, and tend to have more capital-intensive firms. Studies that ignore these factors misattribute sorting advantages and cost-of-living effects to agglomeration. More rigorous instrumental variable and fixed-effects approaches strip out these confounds.

**Evidence**
Donovan et al. (2021) Bayesian meta-analysis of 6,684 estimates; the control variable effects are from their preferred Model 5. Evidence strength is meta-analysis. The housing control effect (−3.8%) is estimated from a small share of observations (2.4%) and should be treated with some caution.

**Dependencies**
Qualifies: claim-0001 — the median 4.6% from claim-0001 is the preferred meta-analytic estimate that already accounts for study quality; claim-0014 explains why less rigorous studies produce higher estimates and why controlling for observable confounds matters.
Qualifies: claim-0011 — the same methodology insight applies to metropolitan-scope estimates; studies without rigorous controls produce upward-biased estimates at every spatial scale.
Depends on: claim-0001 — the parameter estimates for each control variable are derived from the same meta-analysis dataset that produces the 4.6% headline figure.

**Policy implications**
- Treasury and MBIE should only use agglomeration benefit estimates from studies that control for worker skills, housing costs, and capital intensity.
- NZ-specific estimates from Maré & Graham (2009) that use within-industry fixed effects are preferable to international estimates without these controls.
- The large housing price control effect (−3.8%) suggests that failing to build enough housing actively suppresses the measurable agglomeration benefit by raising costs.
- Benefit-cost guidance for major urban infrastructure should specify minimum methodological standards for the agglomeration estimates used.
- Publication bias in the agglomeration literature means the true elasticity is probably below even the preferred meta-analytic estimates; a downward adjustment is prudent.

**Relevant institutions**
Treasury, MBIE, Te Waihanga, Auckland Council.

## claim-0015: Auckland workers are 11.1% more productive than those outside Auckland

**Statement**
Workers based in Auckland are 11.1% more productive than workers outside Auckland, per 2020 data; this differential reflects both genuine agglomeration effects and compositional sorting of higher-skilled workers into the city.

**Meaning (plain English)**
The average Auckland worker produces about 11% more output per hour than the average worker elsewhere in New Zealand. This is partly because Auckland's density and connectivity create genuine agglomeration benefits, and partly because more skilled and educated workers tend to move to and stay in Auckland.

**Why it matters**
Auckland's productivity premium is the single most important fact in NZ urban economic policy. It implies that anything reducing Auckland's economic effectiveness — housing constraints, congestion, poor governance — imposes costs on the whole New Zealand economy, not just Aucklanders.

**Mechanism**
Agglomeration economies (matching, learning spillovers, shared inputs) raise productivity for all workers in dense areas. Sorting (skilled workers choosing Auckland) raises average productivity without creating spillovers. Both mechanisms are at work, and the 11.1% figure does not separate them — the true agglomeration component is lower.

**Evidence**
Source is Infometrics (2021) Auckland economic profile, reported in Gluckman et al. / Koi Tū (2022). Evidence strength is administrative data. The figure is a gross differential without controls for worker skills or sector mix.

**Dependencies**
Supports: claim-0001 — the Auckland-specific 11.1% productivity differential is consistent with global meta-analysis evidence that doubling city size raises productivity by 2.7–6.4%; Auckland as New Zealand's only large city captures these gains relative to smaller NZ centres.

**Policy implications**
- Auckland's productivity premium should anchor NZ's national economic strategy — policies that improve Auckland's functioning have outsized national returns.
- The 11.1% is a gross figure; controlled estimates of the genuine agglomeration component are lower and should be used for policy appraisal.
- Sorting into Auckland reflects skill concentration; policies that help skilled workers in other regions thrive could reduce regional inequality without sacrificing national productivity.
- Auckland Council and central government should jointly track this differential over time to assess whether policy interventions are working.
- The gap between Auckland's GDP share (37.9%) and population share (33%) implies a per-worker premium broadly consistent with the 11.1% figure.

**Relevant institutions**
MBIE, Treasury, Auckland Council, Stats NZ, HUD.

## claim-0016: Auckland accounts for 37.9% of NZ GDP with 35.5% of the workforce

**Statement**
Auckland (population 1.72 million in 2020) accounts for 37.9% of New Zealand's GDP and 35.5% of its workforce — a level of urban economic primacy that significantly exceeds Auckland's 33% population share.

**Meaning (plain English)**
Auckland punches above its weight economically: it has roughly a third of New Zealand's people but produces nearly 38% of national output. The gap between its GDP share and its population share reflects higher productivity per worker in Auckland than in the rest of the country.

**Why it matters**
Auckland's economic primacy makes it the principal lever for national productivity policy. Investments that make Auckland more productive — better transport, more housing, clearer governance — have effects that ripple through the entire NZ economy.

**Mechanism**
Agglomeration economies and the concentration of higher-skilled workers in Auckland together produce higher output per worker than in smaller NZ centres. Auckland also hosts disproportionate shares of traded-sector firms and knowledge-intensive industries that benefit most from density.

**Evidence**
Source is Infometrics (2021) Auckland economic profile, cited in Koi Tū / Gluckman et al. (2022). Evidence strength is administrative data.

**Dependencies**
Supports: claim-0001 — Auckland's disproportionate GDP share is consistent with global evidence that larger cities are more productive per worker; the GDP/population gap quantifies the city-level productivity premium.
Supports: claim-0015 — the GDP share gap (37.9% vs 33% population) is the macro-level counterpart to the micro-level finding that Auckland workers are 11.1% more productive.

**Policy implications**
- Any policy that constrains Auckland's growth or productivity directly damages national economic performance at scale.
- The Māori economy's contribution (~9% of Auckland GDP, ~$9.6bn in 2018) should be explicitly tracked and supported within Auckland's economic strategy.
- Auckland's projected growth to 2 million residents in the early 2030s will increase its economic weight further, raising the stakes for getting governance and planning right.
- Wellington and Christchurch are New Zealand's next-largest cities; their contribution to national GDP should be tracked similarly to identify secondary agglomeration potential.
- Central government and Auckland Council should have a shared economic strategy given Auckland's national fiscal and productivity significance.

**Relevant institutions**
MBIE, Treasury, Auckland Council, Stats NZ, HUD.

## claim-0017: Auckland road congestion costs approximately NZ$1.3 billion per year

**Statement**
Auckland road congestion imposes an estimated economic cost of approximately NZ$1.3 billion per year, making it a significant drag on regional productivity and a contributing factor to Auckland's relatively low quality-of-life rankings.

**Meaning (plain English)**
Traffic congestion in Auckland wastes roughly $1.3 billion of economic value annually — through lost time, delayed freight, higher fuel and vehicle costs, and reduced business efficiency. This is a direct tax on Auckland's productivity and on the wellbeing of its residents.

**Why it matters**
A $1.3 billion annual cost is roughly 1% of Auckland's GDP (which is approximately $130 billion). Investing in rapid transit, road pricing, or land-use reform that reduces congestion could therefore generate substantial national economic returns.

**Mechanism**
Congestion adds travel time and unpredictability to commutes and freight trips, reducing the effective size of Auckland's labour market and raising business operating costs. Longer commutes also reduce workforce participation and quality of life, affecting both productivity and residential attractiveness.

**Evidence**
Source is Leung et al. (2017), an NZIER study for the Employers and Manufacturers Association, reported in Koi Tū (2022). Evidence strength is cost model. The estimate reflects conditions circa 2017 and congestion has likely worsened since.

**Dependencies**
None listed in the relationships block.

**Policy implications**
- Road pricing (congestion charging) in Auckland could directly reduce the $1.3bn annual cost and fund alternative transport investment.
- Rapid transit investment that reduces car dependency — particularly rail and BRT on congested corridors — can recover part of this congestion cost.
- Land-use reform enabling higher density near employment centres reduces trip distances and congestion.
- NZTA and Auckland Transport should update the congestion cost estimate regularly to inform investment prioritisation.
- The congestion cost provides a floor for the benefit side of rapid transit business cases — travel time savings alone can justify significant investment.

**Relevant institutions**
NZTA, Auckland Transport, Auckland Council, Treasury, MBIE.

---
## claim-0018: Auckland PT fares are globally third-highest; transport poverty falls on Māori and Pasifika

**Statement**
Auckland's public transport fares are among the highest in the world (third highest globally as of 2018), and transport poverty — spending more on transport than one can afford — falls disproportionately on Māori and Pasifika residents who have lower average incomes and live in areas with poor PT coverage.

**Meaning (plain English)**
Getting around Auckland by public transport is unusually expensive by world standards, and those least able to afford it face the worst coverage. Māori and Pasifika residents in South and West Auckland bear the biggest burden, spending a higher share of their income on transport while having the fewest alternatives to the car.

**Why it matters**
Transport poverty is both an equity issue and a productivity issue: workers who cannot afford or access quality transport are cut off from job markets, reducing employment rates, incomes, and wellbeing disproportionately for already-disadvantaged communities.

**Mechanism**
High fares combined with sparse PT coverage in lower-income suburbs mean low-income residents face a choice between unaffordable public transport and expensive car ownership. Spatial mismatch between where Māori and Pasifika communities live and where jobs are located compounds the problem. Psychological distress from transport difficulties further reduces workforce participation.

**Evidence**
Fare ranking from Greater Auckland (2018) advocacy organisation; transport poverty data from Ministry of Transport (2020); distributional data from Spoonley et al. and related sources, all cited in Koi Tū / Gluckman et al. (2022). Evidence strength is policy report.

**Dependencies**
None listed in the relationships block.

**Policy implications**
- Fare subsidies or fare-free public transport for low-income households in Auckland should be evaluated as a targeted equity intervention.
- PT investment should be prioritised toward South and West Auckland corridors where Māori and Pasifika communities face the greatest transport poverty.
- Auckland's fare structure should be benchmarked against comparable OECD cities and reformed to reduce relative costs.
- HUD and Auckland Council housing strategies should locate social and affordable housing near existing or planned PT corridors.
- Te Puni Kōkiri and Auckland Council should jointly track transport poverty rates for Māori and Pasifika households as a wellbeing indicator.

**Relevant institutions**
Auckland Transport, Auckland Council, NZTA, Ministry of Transport, HUD, Te Puni Kōkiri.

## claim-0019: Auckland ranked lowest of all NZ cities on quality of life; failed global liveability top 10

**Statement**
Auckland ranked lowest of all New Zealand cities on quality of life in the 2018 Nielsen Quality of Life Survey, and did not penetrate the global top 10 most liveable cities in the two years before the Covid-19 pandemic; the primary drivers cited were housing unaffordability, inadequate infrastructure, and traffic congestion.

**Meaning (plain English)**
Despite being New Zealand's largest and most economically productive city, Auckland's residents rate their quality of life more poorly than residents of smaller NZ cities. Housing costs, poor infrastructure, and traffic are the most commonly cited reasons, and these failings also prevent Auckland from competing with peer cities internationally.

**Why it matters**
If Auckland cannot attract and retain talent because of poor liveability, its agglomeration potential is diminished. Cities compete globally for skilled workers; liveability deficits act as a tax on Auckland's human capital stock and constrain the productivity gains that come with urban growth.

**Mechanism**
Housing unaffordability raises the cost of living in Auckland relative to the wage premium it offers, reducing the net attractiveness of working there. Congestion reduces effective access to jobs and amenities. These combined effects make Auckland less competitive as a destination for mobile skilled workers relative to peer cities in Australia and Asia.

**Evidence**
Nielsen Quality of Life Survey (2018) — a resident perception survey. EIU Global Liveability Index — objective multi-criteria ranking. Both cited in Koi Tū / Gluckman et al. (2022). Evidence strength is survey.

**Dependencies**
None listed in the relationships block.

**Policy implications**
- Auckland's liveability gap relative to peer cities should be treated as a direct constraint on national economic performance, not merely a residential satisfaction issue.
- Housing supply reform and transport investment are the two highest-priority liveability levers available to Auckland policymakers.
- Liveability deficits are spatially uneven within Auckland — South and West Auckland should receive disproportionate infrastructure investment to address within-city inequality.
- Auckland Council and central government should jointly track Auckland's liveability performance against Australian and Asian peer cities.
- Immigration policy should account for liveability factors: attracting skilled migrants to Auckland requires making the city more competitive on housing and transport.

**Relevant institutions**
Auckland Council, HUD, NZTA, Auckland Transport, MBIE, Immigration NZ.

---
## claim-0020: Auckland governance is structurally fragmented between central and local government

**Statement**
Auckland's governance is characterised by structural fragmentation between central government (which controls most fiscal and strategic levers) and local government (Auckland Council plus multiple CCOs), producing disjointed planning, unresolved long-term decisions, and a dominant electoral-cycle focus that crowds out intergenerational thinking.

**Meaning (plain English)**
Central government and Auckland Council each control parts of what Auckland needs but neither has full authority or accountability. The result is that major decisions — about the port, a second harbour crossing, light rail — go unresolved for years, while short electoral cycles mean long-term investment planning gets deprioritised.

**Why it matters**
Governance fragmentation is one of the most significant barriers to Auckland realising its economic potential. The evidence from OECD cities (claims 0023–0024) shows that fragmentation reduces productivity by 5–6%, and that effective metropolitan governance bodies can halve that penalty. Auckland's current arrangements fall short of this standard.

**Mechanism**
When responsibility is split across central government agencies and Auckland Council, each actor can block or delay decisions without being fully accountable for the consequences. Information asymmetries and differing incentives between central and local government lead to chronic underinvestment in infrastructure with long time horizons.

**Evidence**
Qualitative assessment based on 120+ stakeholder interviews conducted for Koi Tū / Gluckman et al. (2022). Evidence strength is policy report. Not a quantitative governance effectiveness study.

**Dependencies**
Supports: claim-0004 — claim-0004 does not exist as a file in this collection, so no inline summary is available.

**Policy implications**
- A formal central-local partnership mechanism for Auckland — with shared decision-making authority and joint accountability — should replace the current fragmented structure.
- The Auckland Policy Office within central government is too small to provide effective coordination; its mandate and resourcing should be expanded.
- Long-term infrastructure decisions (port, second harbour crossing, light rail) should be made through a framework that removes them from short electoral-cycle politics.
- Governance reform should address the multiplicity of CCOs and clarify accountability lines between Auckland Council and its entities.
- A statutory joint planning committee with central government representation could provide the metropolitan governance body function identified in OECD evidence as halving the productivity penalty from fragmentation.

**Relevant institutions**
Auckland Council, DPMC, MBIE, Treasury, Te Waihanga, Ministry for the Environment.

---
## claim-0021: OECD metro areas cover 4% of land but house 50% of population and generate 55% of GDP

**Statement**
OECD metropolitan areas (population ≥500,000) cover approximately 4% of OECD land area, house roughly 50% of the OECD population, and generate approximately 55% of OECD GDP.

**Meaning (plain English)**
A tiny fraction of land in wealthy countries — the areas around major cities — is home to half the population and produces the majority of economic output. Cities are extraordinarily efficient concentrators of both people and economic activity.

**Why it matters**
This basic fact justifies the strategic priority placed on cities in NZ economic policy. Auckland, Wellington, and Christchurch together cover a small fraction of NZ's land but account for the large majority of NZ's GDP. Getting urban policy right matters more than getting rural policy right for aggregate national welfare.

**Mechanism**
Agglomeration economies concentrate productive activity in cities through matching, learning, and sharing mechanisms. Land scarcity in cities drives vertical development and higher-density use, compounding output per unit of land area.

**Evidence**
OECD (2015) analysis based on Functional Urban Areas (FUAs) for metropolitan areas with population ≥500,000. Evidence strength is cross-sectional. Figures are approximations that vary by year and by delimitation method.

**Dependencies**
Supports: claim-0030 — the finding that metropolitan areas contributed ~50% of OECD GDP growth over 2000–2010 is consistent with their generating ~55% of GDP levels; claim-0021 provides the stock figure that grounds the flow finding in claim-0030.

**Policy implications**
- NZ urban policy should be understood as NZ economic policy — the two cannot be separated.
- Population projections for Auckland, Wellington, and Christchurch should be integrated into long-run national economic forecasting.
- Land use in and around major NZ cities is disproportionately economically important — restrictions on urban expansion impose national costs.
- Regional development in NZ should account for the productivity differential between metropolitan and non-metropolitan areas before committing to spatial redistribution.
- MBIE and Treasury should update their urban economic modelling to reflect the GDP concentration in NZ's major functional urban areas.

**Relevant institutions**
MBIE, Treasury, Stats NZ, Auckland Council, Te Waihanga.

---
## claim-0022: Doubling city population raises GDP per worker by 2–5% across OECD cities

**Statement**
Doubling city population is associated with a 2–5% increase in GDP per worker, based on OECD analysis of functional urban areas.

**Meaning (plain English)**
Across rich-country cities, each time a city's population doubles, its workers become 2–5% more productive on average. This is a robust relationship that holds across different countries and city sizes, though the exact figure varies.

**Why it matters**
The 2–5% range provides the empirical foundation for quantifying the economic returns to urban growth in NZ policy analysis. It implies that policies enabling Auckland to grow and function better yield measurable productivity gains that can be monetised in standard cost-benefit assessment.

**Mechanism**
Larger cities enable better matching of workers to jobs, denser knowledge networks that accelerate innovation and skill diffusion, and more efficient sharing of specialised inputs and infrastructure. These three agglomeration mechanisms compound as city size grows.

**Evidence**
OECD (2015) analysis citing Ahrend et al. (2014) for OECD functional urban areas. Evidence strength is observational. The 2–5% range summarises multiple studies; individual estimates vary.

**Dependencies**
Supports: claim-0001 — the OECD 2–5% estimate is consistent with and falls within the lower range of the global meta-analysis 2.7–6.4% credible interval from Donovan et al. (2021); claim-0022 confirms the OECD-specific finding supports the global result.

**Policy implications**
- Investment in Auckland's growth capacity (housing, transport, governance) can be justified on standard cost-benefit grounds using the 2–5% productivity elasticity.
- Wellington and Christchurch are substantially smaller than Auckland; doubling their effective size would generate comparable percentage gains — pointing to a case for investment in NZ's second-tier cities too.
- Land-use liberalisation that allows denser development directly supports the agglomeration mechanism underlying this estimate.
- The lower end of the range (2%) should be used as the floor in conservative policy appraisal, with 5% as the ceiling for optimistic scenarios.
- Fragmented metropolitan governance (as documented for Auckland) reduces the realisation of this potential — see claims on governance fragmentation.

**Relevant institutions**
MBIE, Treasury, Auckland Council, Te Waihanga.

---
## claim-0023: Doubling municipalities per 100k within a metro area cuts labour productivity 5–6%

**Statement**
Doubling the number of municipalities per 100,000 inhabitants within a metropolitan area is associated with a 5–6% reduction in labour productivity.

**Meaning (plain English)**
Cities that are divided into many small local governments are measurably less productive than cities of the same size with fewer, larger councils. The fragmentation of local government across a metropolitan area imposes a real economic penalty roughly equal in size to the gain from doubling the city's population.

**Why it matters**
This finding directly challenges the assumption that smaller local governments are necessarily more responsive or efficient. For NZ, it raises questions about whether Auckland's multiple CCOs and the boundary between Auckland Council and central government functions constitute a form of metropolitan fragmentation with real productivity costs.

**Mechanism**
Municipal fragmentation creates coordination failures: transport networks, land-use plans, and infrastructure are planned by separate entities with different incentives, producing mismatched boundaries, duplication, and gaps. Agglomeration economies cannot be fully realised when the labour market area spans multiple jurisdictions that do not cooperate.

**Evidence**
Based on Ahrend et al. (2014) cross-sectional analysis of OECD metropolitan areas, cited in OECD (2015). Evidence strength is observational. Causal interpretation requires caution — fragmentation may be endogenous to governance history rather than a pure policy choice.

**Dependencies**
Supports: claim-0056 — Ahrend et al. (2015) independently finds a 3.2% productivity penalty from doubling municipalities within a metro area, and a 6% penalty where no governance body exists; claim-0056 replicates and refines the finding from claim-0023 using a different dataset and methodology.

**Policy implications**
- Auckland's governance structure should be evaluated against the 5–6% fragmentation penalty to estimate the cost of current coordination failures.
- Any proposals to sub-divide Auckland Council or create new territorial authorities within the Auckland metro area should be assessed against this evidence.
- A metropolitan governance body for Auckland (or strengthened joint decision-making between Auckland Council and central government) is justified by this evidence.
- Central government coordination mechanisms for Auckland — currently the small Auckland Policy Office — should be strengthened as a direct productivity intervention.
- Wellington's multi-council structure (Wellington City Council, Hutt City, Upper Hutt, Porirua, Kāpiti) may impose similar fragmentation penalties and should be evaluated.

**Relevant institutions**
DPMC, Auckland Council, MBIE, Local Government Commission, Te Waihanga.

---
## claim-0024: A metropolitan governance body reduces the fragmentation-productivity penalty by ~50%

**Statement**
A metropolitan governance body reduces the fragmentation-productivity penalty by approximately 50%.

**Meaning (plain English)**
Having a single body responsible for planning and coordinating across a metropolitan area — even when multiple municipal councils still exist — roughly halves the productivity cost of government fragmentation. A metro governance body does not eliminate the penalty but substantially reduces it.

**Why it matters**
This finding identifies metropolitan governance reform as a high-return policy lever. For NZ, creating or strengthening a metropolitan governance body for Auckland could recover roughly half the productivity loss associated with current governance fragmentation.

**Mechanism**
A metropolitan governance body can coordinate land-use planning, transport networks, and fiscal decisions across the whole city-region, reducing the coordination failures that drive the fragmentation penalty. It does not eliminate the underlying fragmentation of local councils but provides a coordination overlay that captures at least some of the agglomeration potential.

**Evidence**
Based on Ahrend et al. (2014) cross-sectional analysis of OECD metropolitan areas, cited in OECD (2015). Evidence strength is observational. "Metropolitan governance body" is broadly defined and the analysis does not distinguish governance body types by fiscal or planning powers.

**Dependencies**
Qualifies: claim-0023 — the fragmentation-productivity penalty of 5–6% from claim-0023 is partially offset when a metropolitan governance body exists; claim-0024 quantifies this offset at approximately 50%, so a fragmented metro area with a governance body still underperforms an unfragmented one.
Depends on: claim-0023 — the 50% reduction finding is derived from the same analytical framework as the base fragmentation penalty in claim-0023 and cannot be interpreted without it.

**Policy implications**
- NZ should establish or strengthen a metropolitan governance body for Auckland with meaningful planning and coordination powers, not merely advisory functions.
- The specific functions that drive the governance body benefit (land use coordination, transport planning, fiscal pooling) should be identified and prioritised in any governance reform.
- Wellington and Christchurch should be evaluated for similar governance body arrangements given their multi-council metropolitan structures.
- Any Auckland governance reform should be assessed against both the 5–6% fragmentation penalty (claim-0023) and the 50% recovery potential from this claim.
- The governance body should have cross-party political support to insulate long-term planning from electoral cycles.

**Relevant institutions**
DPMC, Local Government Commission, Auckland Council, MBIE, Treasury.

---
## claim-0025: Metro areas with a governance body had higher population growth and less sprawl 2000–2010

**Statement**
Metropolitan areas with a governance body experienced 0.28 percentage points higher annual population growth over 2000–2010 and declining urban sprawl over 2000–2006; those without a governance body experienced increasing sprawl over the same period.

**Meaning (plain English)**
Cities with metropolitan governance bodies attracted more population growth and became more compact over the 2000s, while cities without such bodies sprawled outward. This suggests governance matters for shaping urban form, not just for economic coordination.

**Why it matters**
Compact urban form has multiple benefits: lower per-capita transport emissions, lower infrastructure costs per resident, and better access to agglomeration economies. Metropolitan governance bodies appear to support compactness — a finding directly relevant to Auckland's planning challenges.

**Mechanism**
Metropolitan governance bodies can coordinate land-use policy across municipal boundaries, directing growth to infill and transit-served areas rather than allowing piecemeal peripheral expansion. Without this coordination, individual municipalities may approve fringe development that maximises their own rates base while imposing network costs on the wider metro area.

**Evidence**
OECD (2015) analysis using cross-sectional and longitudinal comparison. Evidence strength is observational. Causality is uncertain: governance bodies may form in faster-growing areas rather than causing growth.

**Dependencies**
Supports: claim-0024 — the anti-sprawl outcome associated with metropolitan governance bodies reinforces the broader finding that such bodies reduce the productivity penalty of fragmentation; more compact urban form supports better agglomeration outcomes.

**Policy implications**
- Auckland Council's Unitary Plan and its compact city strategy align with what metropolitan governance evidence suggests produces better urban outcomes.
- Central government should support Auckland's compact city direction rather than creating pressure for peripheral greenfield expansion.
- Wellington's multi-council structure may be producing unnecessary sprawl into Kāpiti and the Hutt Valley; a regional governance body could produce a more compact outcome.
- Infrastructure funding mechanisms should be designed to favour infill and brownfield development over greenfield expansion.
- The 0.28pp annual population growth premium associated with governance bodies compounds meaningfully over decades — it is not a trivial effect.

**Relevant institutions**
MfE, Auckland Council, MBIE, HUD, Te Waihanga, regional councils.

---
## claim-0026: Metro areas with a dedicated transport authority have PT satisfaction 14 percentage points higher

**Statement**
Metropolitan areas with a dedicated transport authority have public transport satisfaction rates approximately 14 percentage points higher than those without.

**Meaning (plain English)**
When a city has a single authority responsible for planning and running its public transport network, residents are substantially more satisfied with the service — about 14 percentage points more satisfied than in cities where transport is fragmented across multiple bodies.

**Why it matters**
Higher satisfaction with public transport reflects better service quality, which in turn drives higher usage, reduced car dependency, lower congestion, and lower emissions. For Auckland, which has historically poor PT satisfaction, dedicated transport authority arrangements are directly relevant.

**Mechanism**
A dedicated transport authority can plan integrated routes, unified fares, coordinated timetables, and consistent service standards across the whole metropolitan network. Without this, fragmented operators produce poorly connected routes, inconsistent fare systems, and service gaps that reduce both the quality and perceived reliability of PT.

**Evidence**
Based on OECD Metro Governance Survey, cited in OECD (2015). Evidence strength is survey. Satisfaction is self-reported and may reflect expectations as well as actual service quality; usage and mode share outcomes are not directly measured.

**Dependencies**
None listed in the relationships block.

**Policy implications**
- Auckland Transport's mandate and integration with regional planning should be evaluated against the metropolitan transport authority model associated with higher satisfaction.
- The relationship between Auckland Transport, NZTA, and Auckland Council should be rationalised to ensure unified planning authority for PT.
- PT satisfaction should be tracked as a regular indicator in Auckland's performance monitoring framework.
- Fare integration, timetable coordination, and network connectivity should be priority reform areas given their influence on satisfaction.
- Wellington's transport governance, split across Greater Wellington Regional Council and multiple territorial authorities, may benefit from a more unified authority model.

**Relevant institutions**
Auckland Transport, NZTA, Auckland Council, Greater Wellington Regional Council, Ministry of Transport.

---
## claim-0027: Building regulations in central London and Manhattan raise floor-space prices 2–8x above construction cost

**Statement**
Building regulations in central London and Manhattan raise floor-space prices to 2–8 times above construction costs.

**Meaning (plain English)**
In the most restrictive parts of London and New York City, planning rules and building regulations drive the price of floor space far above what it actually costs to build. The gap between price and cost represents a regulatory wedge that suppresses supply and inflates prices.

**Why it matters**
This finding illustrates the potential scale of regulatory distortion in housing markets. While London and Manhattan are extreme cases, they demonstrate the mechanism that operates in Auckland and other NZ cities where restrictive zoning creates a wedge between construction costs and market prices.

**Mechanism**
Floor-space price limits imposed by height restrictions, FAR controls, heritage rules, and planning process costs prevent supply from responding to demand. When developers cannot build more even when prices far exceed costs, the regulatory wedge is capitalised into land prices, benefiting existing owners at the expense of renters and new entrants.

**Evidence**
Original findings from Glaeser, Gyourko, and Saks (2005) for Manhattan and Cheshire and Hilber (2008) for central London, both cited in OECD (2015). Evidence strength is observational. The 2–8x range is wide; the upper end reflects the most constrained submarkets.

**Dependencies**
None listed in the relationships block.

**Policy implications**
- Auckland's planning rules should be evaluated for whether they create a similar regulatory wedge between construction costs and floor-space prices.
- Height limits and FAR restrictions in Auckland city centre and suburban centres should be assessed for their supply-restricting effect.
- Auckland's National Policy Statement on Urban Development (NPS-UD) and its upzoning provisions directly target this mechanism.
- Cost-benefit analysis of planning reform in NZ should use the floor-space price–construction cost wedge as a measure of regulatory restrictiveness.
- HUD and Auckland Council should commission regular analysis of whether construction cost trends are tracking market prices or whether the wedge is widening.

**Relevant institutions**
HUD, Auckland Council, MfE, MBIE, Treasury.

---
## claim-0028: Per-capita ground-transport CO₂ emissions fall consistently as urban density rises

**Statement**
Per capita ground-transport CO₂ emissions fall consistently as urban density rises; compact cities emit substantially less transport CO₂ per capita than sprawling cities of equivalent population.

**Meaning (plain English)**
Denser cities have much lower transport carbon emissions per person than sprawling ones. People in compact cities walk, cycle, and use transit more, and drive less and shorter distances, producing far less transport-related carbon.

**Why it matters**
For NZ, which has high per-capita transport emissions due to car-dependent urban form, this finding implies that densification and transit investment are simultaneously productivity and climate policies. Auckland's sprawl is partly responsible for NZ's transport emissions profile.

**Mechanism**
Higher density reduces travel distances between homes, workplaces, and services; supports viable public transport networks; and enables walking and cycling for more trips. Lower vehicle kilometres travelled directly reduces fuel use and emissions. The density-transit-emissions relationship is reinforcing: denser areas make transit viable, which further reduces car use.

**Evidence**
OECD (2015) drawing on Kennedy et al. (2009) cross-city analysis. Evidence strength is cross-sectional. The association does not fully control for income level, fuel mix, or vehicle fleet composition.

**Dependencies**
Supports: claim-0197 — the density-emissions relationship documented here for transport CO₂ is a related but distinct finding from claim-0197, which covers general (non-transport) energy consumption; claim-0028 provides the transport-specific mechanism underpinning the broader density-environment relationship.

**Policy implications**
- NZ's emissions reduction plan should treat urban densification and PT investment as core transport decarbonisation tools, not just housing and productivity policies.
- Auckland's compact city strategy directly supports NZ's climate commitments in the transport sector.
- Sprawl-enabling policies (motorway expansion without complementary transit) lock in high-emission transport patterns for decades given the path dependence of urban form.
- Ministry for the Environment and Auckland Council should jointly model the emissions implications of different urban form scenarios for Auckland.
- Greenfield development on Auckland's periphery should require transport emissions assessments as part of plan change evaluation.

**Relevant institutions**
MfE, NZTA, Auckland Council, Ministry of Transport, Climate Change Commission.

---
## claim-0029: Large cities generate productivity spillovers to regions up to 300km away

**Statement**
Large cities (population >2 million) generate positive productivity spillovers to surrounding regions up to 200–300km distant; halving travel time between a large city and its hinterland region is associated with 0.2–0.4 percentage points higher annual GDP per capita growth in the hinterland.

**Meaning (plain English)**
The economic benefits of a large city extend well beyond its own boundaries. Regions within a few hundred kilometres of a major metropolitan area grow faster, partly because better transport links spread the productivity gains outward. Reducing travel times between cities and their hinterlands produces measurable regional growth dividends.

**Why it matters**
For NZ, this finding supports investment in transport corridors linking Auckland to Northland, Waikato, and Bay of Plenty, and linking Wellington to the wider lower North Island. Better connectivity doesn't just help commuters — it raises economic growth across whole regions.

**Mechanism**
Spillovers operate through supply chain linkages, labour market integration (longer-range commuting), knowledge diffusion, and firms in hinterland regions accessing the agglomeration economies of the large city without physically relocating. Halving travel time effectively brings more of the hinterland population within the labour market catchment of the large city.

**Evidence**
OECD (2015) cross-regional regression analysis. Evidence strength is observational. The spillover distance (200–300km) is specific to large cities (>2m population); smaller metro areas have shorter spillover reach.

**Dependencies**
Supports: claim-0021 — the finding that metro areas contribute disproportionate GDP growth is reinforced by claim-0029, which shows this growth diffuses into surrounding regions through connectivity; claim-0021 documents the metro GDP concentration and claim-0029 shows it is not a zero-sum gain for hinterlands.

**Policy implications**
- Transport investment connecting Auckland to the Waikato, Northland, and Bay of Plenty should be evaluated for regional growth benefits, not just Auckland-specific benefits.
- Wellington-Palmerston North and Wellington-Lower Hutt/Kāpiti transport investment generates spillovers to wider lower North Island regions.
- NZ's transport investment prioritisation framework (Te Waihanga, NZTA) should incorporate inter-regional spillover effects in benefit calculation.
- Rail freight and intercity passenger rail investment between Auckland and Hamilton/Tauranga could deliver 0.2–0.4pp additional annual GDP growth in those hinterland regions.
- Regional economic development policy should be designed around connectivity to major centres rather than trying to create self-contained economic clusters in isolation.

**Relevant institutions**
Te Waihanga, NZTA, MBIE, Treasury, regional councils.

---
## claim-0030: OECD metro areas contributed ~50% of total OECD GDP growth over 2000–2010

**Statement**
OECD metropolitan areas (population ≥500,000) contributed approximately 50% of total OECD GDP growth over 2000–2010, despite covering only approximately 4% of OECD land area.

**Meaning (plain English)**
Over a decade, major cities in wealthy countries generated half of all national economic growth, while occupying a tiny fraction of national land. Cities are not just concentrators of existing output — they are the engine rooms of new economic growth.

**Why it matters**
For NZ, this means that Auckland's trajectory determines New Zealand's growth trajectory to a large degree. Policies that help Auckland grow faster and smarter have proportionate effects on national GDP growth rates.

**Mechanism**
Metro areas drive growth through both population expansion (bringing more workers into productive agglomeration) and productivity growth (within-city improvements in technology, firm quality, and specialisation). Both mechanisms are at work in the 50% figure.

**Evidence**
OECD (2015) analysis of Functional Urban Areas over 2000–2010. Evidence strength is cross-sectional. The period includes the 2008–09 financial crisis, which differentially affected metro-concentrated industries like finance, so the share may differ across decades.

**Dependencies**
Supports: claim-0021 — claim-0021 establishes that metro areas hold ~55% of OECD GDP stock; claim-0030 shows this stock position translates into ~50% of GDP growth flow; the two together confirm metro areas' central role in both the level and growth of OECD economies.
Depends on: (none listed).

**Policy implications**
- National economic growth targets in NZ should be anchored to Auckland and other major city performance, not averaged across all regions.
- MBIE's economic growth framework should track the Auckland contribution to NZ GDP growth as a primary indicator.
- Underinvestment in Auckland's productive capacity (infrastructure, housing) has compounding national costs as growth is foregone.
- NZ's regional development policies should not crowd out Auckland investment with the aim of redistribution — the net national growth effect of redirecting investment away from productive metros is negative.
- Treasury's long-run fiscal projections should incorporate Auckland's metro growth contribution as a key driver of the tax base.

**Relevant institutions**
MBIE, Treasury, Auckland Council, Stats NZ.

---
## claim-0031: Urban productivity in developing cities rises 5–6% per doubling of population; IV estimate ~8%

**Statement**
Urban productivity rises approximately 5–6% with each doubling of city population in developing economies (output elasticity 0.067–0.080 using LandScan population data); instrumental variable estimates raise this to approximately 8%.

**Meaning (plain English)**
In developing countries, doubling a city's size raises firm-level productivity by about 5–8%. This is broadly consistent with evidence from richer countries, suggesting that agglomeration benefits operate across the development spectrum — though with important Africa-specific exceptions.

**Why it matters**
Developing country urbanisation is the largest source of new city growth globally. Understanding that agglomeration benefits of 5–8% per doubling are achievable in middle-income developing cities provides justification for policies that facilitate urbanisation rather than resist it.

**Mechanism**
The same matching, learning, and sharing mechanisms that drive agglomeration in rich cities operate in developing cities, provided that infrastructure quality and connectivity are adequate. The IV estimate (8%) suggests some of the OLS estimate reflects sorting of productive firms into cities; the true causal effect is nonetheless substantial.

**Evidence**
Collier, Jones, and Spijkerman (2018) using World Bank Enterprise Survey data for formal-sector firms across developing cities. Evidence strength is observational-IV. Covers formal firms (5+ permanent workers) only; informal sector not captured.

**Dependencies**
Supports: claim-0001 — the 5–6% developing country estimate (and IV estimate of ~8%) is broadly consistent with and overlaps the global meta-analysis 2.7–6.4% credible interval from Donovan et al. (2021), supporting the generality of agglomeration benefits across development contexts.
Supports: claim-0022 — the developing country elasticity of 5–6% is consistent with OECD estimate of 2–5% per doubling; claim-0031 provides the developing-economy complement to claim-0022.

**Policy implications**
- Developing country urbanisation should be facilitated rather than resisted; the productivity gains from city size are real and comparable to those in advanced economies.
- Infrastructure quality is the key condition for realising agglomeration benefits in developing cities (see claim-0032 on the Africa exception).
- NZ development assistance should support urbanisation management, urban infrastructure, and planning capacity in Pacific Island and other developing-country partners.
- The 5–8% elasticity applies to formal-sector firms; policies that support formalisation of the informal economy also support realisation of agglomeration benefits.
- Informal sector exclusion from these estimates means the full welfare impact of urbanisation in developing countries is understated.

**Relevant institutions**
MFAT, MBIE (for international development-facing NZ firms), World Bank partners.

---
## claim-0032: African cities do not generate productivity or wage benefits from size once infrastructure is controlled

**Statement**
African cities do not generate the same productivity, wage, or employment benefits from city size as cities in Asia and Latin America; once infrastructure quality is controlled for, the city-size effect on African firm productivity becomes statistically insignificant.

**Meaning (plain English)**
In African cities, being located in a big city does not make firms measurably more productive, once you account for the quality of infrastructure. African firms lose about four times more sales to power outages than firms in other developing regions. The agglomeration mechanism is broken by infrastructure failure, not absent by nature.

**Why it matters**
This finding shows that agglomeration benefits are not automatic — they require a minimum level of infrastructure quality to activate. For NZ development policy, it implies that supporting urban infrastructure in Pacific and African development partners is a precondition for realising urbanisation's growth potential.

**Mechanism**
Reliable power, functional roads, and basic regulatory certainty are prerequisites for firms to benefit from proximity to other firms and workers. When firms spend management time on power backup, regulation compliance, and logistics workarounds, the productivity gains from density are absorbed by these costs. Leapfrog spatial development (see claim-0035) further reduces effective connectivity.

**Evidence**
Collier, Jones, and Spijkerman (2018) using World Bank Enterprise Survey data. Evidence strength is observational. Results apply to formal-sector firms; the large informal sector in African cities is not captured.

**Dependencies**
Qualifies: claim-0031 — the 5–6% agglomeration elasticity for developing cities documented in claim-0031 does not hold for Africa; claim-0032 is an important exception that limits the generality of claim-0031 to Asia and Latin America.

**Policy implications**
- NZ and international development policy in African urban contexts should prioritise power reliability and basic infrastructure before trying to build industrial clusters or special economic zones.
- Urban planning assistance to African cities should focus on spatial connectivity — ensuring new development is connected to existing networks rather than leapfrogging outward.
- Agglomeration-focused policies in African cities will fail without prior investment in basic enabling infrastructure.
- The lesson for NZ Pacific engagement is that islands and small developing economies face similar infrastructure constraints; connectivity investment should precede cluster development.
- NZ foreign aid should incorporate urban infrastructure as a priority, recognising it as an enabling condition for economic development.

**Relevant institutions**
MFAT, MBIE, NZ Aid Programme.

---
## claim-0033: Urban wage premiums are 45% in China, 122% in India, 176% in Brazil; only 6–11% in Africa

**Statement**
Urban wage premiums in large emerging economies are far larger than in either advanced economies or Africa: approximately 45% in China, 122% in India, and 176% in Brazil; by contrast, estimated urban wage premiums in Africa (after full worker controls) are only 6–11%.

**Meaning (plain English)**
In Asia and Latin America, moving to a city dramatically raises workers' wages — more than doubling them in Brazil and India. But in Africa, the wage advantage of living in a city is much smaller once education and experience are accounted for. This reflects both the strength of city economies in Asia/Latin America and the weakness of urban productivity in Africa.

**Why it matters**
The variation in urban wage premiums across regions reveals that the city-growth payoff depends heavily on the economic and institutional context. For NZ and Pacific development, this suggests the mechanisms driving agglomeration are not universal — context matters enormously.

**Mechanism**
In fast-growing Asian and Latin American cities, rapid industrialisation and rising traded-sector activity generate large productivity gains concentrated in urban areas. In Africa, weaker institutions, infrastructure failures, and spatial fragmentation (leapfrog development) suppress the urban wage premium despite rapid population growth in cities.

**Evidence**
Cross-country wage premium estimates from Chauvin et al. (2017) for China, India, and Brazil; Jones et al. (2017) for African countries; cited in Collier, Jones, and Spijkerman (2018). Evidence strength is cross-sectional. Different studies use different control sets, so comparisons are indicative.

**Dependencies**
Supports: claim-0001 — the variation in urban wage premiums across regions is consistent with the global meta-analysis finding in claim-0001 that agglomeration elasticities vary substantially; claim-0033 provides context showing that the distribution of benefits is not uniform across development contexts.
Qualifies: claim-0031 — the 5–6% average agglomeration elasticity for developing cities in claim-0031 conflates very different underlying wage premium patterns; claim-0033 shows the Africa exception dramatically reduces the average.

**Policy implications**
- NZ policy engagement with developing economies should be calibrated to regional context; agglomeration advice appropriate for Asian cities may not transfer to African contexts.
- Pacific Island nations face different structural conditions than large Asian or Latin American economies; urban development support should be tailored accordingly.
- For NZ domestically, the evidence that even modest urban wage premiums have large welfare effects supports policies that maintain Auckland's attractiveness relative to smaller NZ centres.
- Monitoring urban-rural wage differentials within NZ (Auckland vs rest of NZ) is important context for evaluating whether agglomeration benefits are being shared broadly.
- NZ immigration policy targeting skilled migrants should account for the fact that urban wage premiums attract migrants primarily to Auckland.

**Relevant institutions**
MFAT, MBIE, Stats NZ, Immigration NZ.

---
## claim-0034: City connectivity — not population density — drives formal employment growth in developing cities

**Statement**
City connectivity — measured by road density and market potential within 10km (the De la Roca–Puga index) — is the key driver of formal sector employment growth in developing cities; formal employment more than doubles with each doubling of market potential within 10km of the average resident.

**Meaning (plain English)**
What matters for jobs in developing cities is not how many people are packed in per square kilometre, but how well-connected those people are to each other through roads and walkable proximity. A city of a million people sprawled across a disconnected landscape creates far fewer formal jobs than a well-connected city of the same size.

**Why it matters**
This finding reframes urbanisation policy: the goal should not be density for its own sake but connectivity — ensuring people and firms can interact efficiently within the urban area. For NZ, it reinforces the case for transport investment as a productivity tool distinct from, and complementary to, densification.

**Mechanism**
Market potential within 10km captures how many economic interactions a resident can access without long travel. Higher connectivity enables more supplier-customer, employer-employee, and firm-firm interactions per unit of time. Population density per se is insignificant in the employment regressions — it is the quality and accessibility of density that matters.

**Evidence**
Collier, Jones, and Spijkerman (2018) using World Bank Enterprise Survey panel data across developing cities, 2006–2012. Evidence strength is panel fixed effects. Covers formal sector firms (5+ workers) only.

**Dependencies**
Qualifies: claim-0031 — the 5–6% agglomeration elasticity from population size in claim-0031 partly operates through the connectivity channel identified in claim-0034; population growth without connectivity improvements does not realise the full agglomeration benefit.

**Policy implications**
- NZ transport investment appraisal should use market potential (accessibility) metrics rather than simple density measures to evaluate the agglomeration benefits of transport projects.
- Auckland's rapid transit network investment should be evaluated partly on its contribution to expanding effective market potential within the city.
- Urban planning in NZ should prioritise connected infill development over disconnected greenfield expansion even at similar gross densities.
- Te Waihanga's investment guidance should incorporate connectivity metrics (travel time to employment, market potential indices) in economic appraisal.
- Spatial mismatch between workers and jobs in South and West Auckland (see claim-0018) is a connectivity failure that directly suppresses formal employment in those communities.

**Relevant institutions**
Te Waihanga, NZTA, Auckland Transport, Auckland Council, MBIE.

---
## claim-0035: Leapfrog development accounts for >40% of built-up area growth in several African cities

**Statement**
Leapfrog development — new urban land parcels that are spatially disconnected from existing built-up areas — accounted for more than 40% of built-up area growth in several major African cities (Accra, Kigali, Lagos, Nairobi, Maputo) over 2000–2010, creating fragmented cities with ~40% fewer potential interactions than Asian cities of similar size.

**Meaning (plain English)**
When new urban growth happens in disconnected patches far from the existing city, it destroys the connectivity that makes cities productive. African cities that grew mostly through leapfrog development ended up with 40% fewer economic interactions per resident than comparable Asian cities — a direct productivity tax.

**Why it matters**
The leapfrog development pattern is not unique to Africa — elements of it appear wherever land markets are weak, planning enforcement is poor, and infrastructure doesn't lead development. For NZ, the lesson is that allowing disconnected peripheral expansion risks replicating the African pattern in attenuated form.

**Mechanism**
Leapfrog development creates spatial fragmentation: residents in disconnected patches face long travel times to employment centres and other residents, reducing the market potential that drives agglomeration. Without road networks connecting these patches to the core, the density gains from urbanisation are not converted into economic interactions.

**Evidence**
Leapfrog statistics from Lall et al. (2017) based on satellite imagery, cited in Collier, Jones, and Spijkerman (2018). Interaction deficit from Henderson and Nigmatulina (2016). Evidence strength is observational.

**Dependencies**
Supports: claim-0032 — leapfrog development is the spatial mechanism that explains why African cities fail to generate agglomeration benefits even as they grow; claim-0032 establishes that African cities don't generate productivity gains from size, and claim-0035 identifies the spatial fragmentation as a key structural cause.

**Policy implications**
- NZ urban planning should explicitly prohibit or strongly disincentivise disconnected leapfrog development on Auckland's periphery.
- Infrastructure investment (roads, water, wastewater) should lead development rather than following after disconnected expansion.
- The NPS-UD's focus on infill and mixed-use development in existing urban areas directly addresses the leapfrog risk.
- Auckland's special housing areas and rapid transit network expansions should be coordinated to ensure new development is connected to the existing fabric.
- Auckland Council's greenfields plans should require minimum connectivity standards (road connections, PT access) before land can be developed.

**Relevant institutions**
Auckland Council, MfE, NZTA, HUD, Te Waihanga.

---
## claim-0036: Atlanta and Barcelona had similar populations but Barcelona had 10x lower transport CO₂ per capita

**Statement**
Atlanta (density: 6 people/hectare) and Barcelona (density: 176 people/hectare) had similar populations (~2.5–2.8 million) in 1990 but vastly different transport outcomes: per capita CO₂ from transport was 400 metric tons in Atlanta and 38 tons in Barcelona; 30% of Barcelona trips were by mass transit versus 4.5% in Atlanta.

**Meaning (plain English)**
Two cities of similar size can have wildly different carbon footprints from transport depending purely on their density and transit infrastructure. A compact, transit-served city like Barcelona emits about one-tenth the transport carbon per person as a sprawling, car-dependent city like Atlanta. This is not a small difference — it is transformational.

**Why it matters**
Auckland has density and transit mode shares closer to Atlanta than Barcelona. This comparison illustrates the scale of emission reductions possible through urban form and transport system change — and the scale of the challenge NZ faces in decarbonising its transport sector without addressing urban form.

**Mechanism**
Higher density reduces trip distances and makes public transit commercially viable. Transit availability reduces car ownership and use. The density-transit-car ownership system is mutually reinforcing: once locked into a low-density, high-car-dependency pattern, it is very difficult and expensive to reverse.

**Evidence**
World Bank (2009) World Development Report, citing Bertaud (2004) and Kenworthy (2005). Evidence strength is cross-sectional. The CO₂ figures are for 1990; comparability across cities depends on consistent measurement boundaries.

**Dependencies**
Supports: claim-0028 — the Atlanta-Barcelona comparison provides vivid quantitative illustration of the cross-city finding in claim-0028 that compact cities have substantially lower per-capita transport emissions; claim-0036 is a specific case study confirming the general pattern.

**Policy implications**
- Auckland's long-run urban form trajectory should be explicitly planned toward Barcelona-type compactness and transit use, not a continuation of Atlanta-type sprawl.
- NZ's transport emissions reduction targets should be grounded in urban form scenarios, not just vehicle technology assumptions.
- The scale of the Atlanta-Barcelona difference (10x emissions, 7x transit mode share) illustrates why vehicle electrification alone is insufficient — urban form must also change.
- NZTA and Auckland Council should jointly develop a decades-long compact city and rapid transit strategy anchored to emission reduction trajectories.
- NZ's National Land Transport Programme should weight urban transit investment heavily given this evidence on transport emissions and urban form.

**Relevant institutions**
NZTA, Auckland Council, MfE, Ministry of Transport, Climate Change Commission.

---
## claim-0037: Mumbai's FSI restriction produced 4 sq m per person average space and 54% slum population

**Statement**
Mumbai's Floor Space Index restriction (1.33 since 1991, down from 4.5) produced average residential space of only 4 square metres per person and left 54% of its 16 million residents living in slums.

**Meaning (plain English)**
When Mumbai's government cut the limit on how much floor space could be built on a given plot of land, it created catastrophic housing shortages. With only 4 square metres per person on average — less than a third of Shanghai's — and more than half the city's residents forced into slums, Mumbai shows what happens when building regulations prevent supply from meeting demand.

**Why it matters**
Mumbai is an extreme case of the regulatory housing supply problem, but the mechanism — restrictive floor-space rules pushing people into informal settlements — operates in attenuated form in Auckland. NZ's relatively restrictive historic zoning (since partly reformed) has produced its own version of the supply-demand gap.

**Mechanism**
Low FSI limits cap the amount of housing that can be built per block of land. When population grows and demand rises but supply is capped, prices rise until some residents are priced out of the formal market entirely. Those priced out either leave the city or resort to informal settlement, producing the slum outcome observed in Mumbai.

**Evidence**
World Bank (2009) World Development Report, drawing on Bertaud (2003). Evidence strength is observational. Slum share is an estimate; rent control regulations also contributed independently of FSI restrictions.

**Dependencies**
Supports: claim-0027 — the Mumbai FSI case is a more extreme version of the regulatory floor-space premium documented for London and Manhattan in claim-0027; both show how building regulations drive prices above construction costs and suppress supply.

**Policy implications**
- NZ's historic height limits and low-density zoning imposed a version of the FSI problem that Auckland's upzoning reforms (NPS-UD, Auckland Unitary Plan) are correcting.
- HUD and Auckland Council should track the ratio of market housing prices to construction costs as a measure of regulatory restrictiveness.
- Minimum height and density standards in Auckland's mixed-use and residential zones should be maintained and extended to prevent regulatory constraints re-emerging.
- NZ's building consent system should be assessed for whether it imposes compliance costs that function similarly to restrictive FSI limits.
- Pacific Island capital cities with similar supply constraints should be assisted to reform their planning rules through NZ development engagement.

**Relevant institutions**
HUD, Auckland Council, MfE, MBIE.

---
## claim-0038: Urbanisation policy must sequence institutions, connectivity, and targeting to urbanisation stage

**Statement**
Urbanisation policy must be sequenced to the stage of urbanisation: incipient areas need spatially blind institutions (land rights, basic services); intermediate areas also need connective transport infrastructure; advanced areas also need spatially targeted interventions. Targeted slum interventions consistently fail when applied before functioning land markets and connective infrastructure are in place.

**Meaning (plain English)**
There is a right order for urban policy: first establish secure property rights and basic services for everyone; then build transport links that connect people to economic opportunity; only then target specific places or groups for additional support. Skipping steps — particularly attempting targeted place-based interventions before the basics are in place — consistently fails.

**Why it matters**
NZ, as an advanced urbanised economy, is in the third stage: it can and should use spatially targeted policies to address concentrated disadvantage in South and West Auckland and other deprived urban areas. But the framework also warns that targeted policies must rest on functioning housing markets and good transport connectivity to succeed.

**Mechanism**
Without secure land rights, targeted interventions create perverse incentives (people move into targeted areas to get benefits) or are undermined by insecure tenure. Without connectivity, placing disadvantaged communities in assisted housing far from jobs replicates the South Africa failure (claim-0039). Sequencing ensures each policy stage builds on the foundation laid by the previous one.

**Evidence**
World Bank (2009) World Development Report — a normative design framework synthesised from historical case studies including UK, Korea, Singapore, South Africa, and Bogotá. Evidence strength is historical case study. The framework is illustrative, not causally tested.

**Dependencies**
None listed in the relationships block.

**Policy implications**
- NZ's place-based social investment programmes (e.g., Whānau Ora, social housing) operate in stage-three conditions and must be supported by functioning housing markets and transport access to succeed.
- Social housing in Auckland should be located near public transport corridors, not on peripheral sites with poor connectivity.
