- Māori land governance reforms should be evaluated as stage-one institution-building that enables stage-three targeted policies to work better.
- NZ development assistance to Pacific Island nations should identify which urbanisation stage each country is in before prescribing policy tools.
- Auckland's targeted area plans (e.g., Māngere, Manukau) should explicitly assess whether transport connectivity is sufficient to enable effective place-based investment.

**Relevant institutions**
HUD, MSD, MBIE, NZTA, Te Puni Kōkiri, MFAT (for Pacific engagement).

---
## claim-0039: South Africa's peripheral housing programme without transport links failed to reduce spatial division

**Statement**
South Africa's post-1994 housing programme built low-cost developments on city peripheries without transport links to employment centres; these developments failed to reduce intra-urban spatial division, and the program was redesigned only when connectivity was incorporated.

**Meaning (plain English)**
After apartheid, South Africa rapidly built large numbers of houses for low-income families — but put them on city outskirts with no buses or trains to jobs. The result was that families had houses but remained economically isolated, unable to reach employment. The programme only improved outcomes when transport access was built in.

**Why it matters**
This is a direct cautionary case for NZ social housing policy. Housing Kāinga Ora residents in peripheral locations with poor transport access replicates the South African failure. Transport connectivity must be an explicit design criterion for social housing programmes, not an afterthought.

**Mechanism**
Peripheral housing without transport severs the link between residence and employment. Workers face prohibitive commuting costs (time, money, or both) and are effectively trapped in residential enclaves distant from economic opportunity. This perpetuates poverty rather than reducing it, and spatial division is reproduced rather than reversed.

**Evidence**
World Bank (2009) World Development Report — cited as a cautionary case study. Evidence strength is historical case study. The WDR acknowledges South Africa's exceptional severity of starting conditions (1.5m unit deficit, 7.4m slum residents, 35-piece legislative reform).

**Dependencies**
Supports: claim-0038 — South Africa's housing failure directly illustrates the sequencing principle in claim-0038: targeted housing interventions (stage-three policy) were deployed without prior investment in connective transport infrastructure (stage-two), producing predictable failure.

**Policy implications**
- Kāinga Ora and HUD site selection for new social housing must require minimum public transport access standards — not just housing completions as the metric of success.
- Existing social housing in peripheral Auckland locations with poor PT access should be prioritised for transport investment or for relocation closer to employment centres.
- The NZ Housing Acceleration Fund should incorporate transport connectivity as an eligibility criterion for funding approval.
- Housing and transport planning in NZ should be jointly assessed, not treated as separate policy streams.
- Auckland's future social housing strategy should be co-designed with Auckland Transport to ensure transport access is built in from the start.

**Relevant institutions**
HUD, Kāinga Ora, Auckland Transport, NZTA, MSD.

---
## claim-0040: Bogotá's TransMilenio BRT cut travel times 15 min, reduced poverty, raised house values 11%

**Statement**
Bogotá's TransMilenio bus rapid transit system (launched 2000) reduced average travel times by 15 minutes, with larger reductions for households in poorer parts of the city; combined with neighbourhood upgrading, the program reached 930,000 people and is estimated to have raised house values by up to 11%.

**Meaning (plain English)**
Bogotá built a high-quality bus rapid transit system that cut commute times for all residents and gave the biggest travel time savings to the poorest. Combined with neighbourhood improvements, the BRT lifted property values and connected lower-income residents to jobs across the city.

**Why it matters**
Bogotá shows that affordable, well-designed BRT can simultaneously improve productivity (better job access), equity (larger gains for poor residents), and asset values. For NZ, where BRT is being evaluated as an alternative to light rail in some corridors, Bogotá provides evidence of its potential at a fraction of rail costs.

**Mechanism**
BRT provides rapid, frequent transit on dedicated lanes at far lower cost than metro rail (approximately $10m/km vs $100m+/km). Travel time reductions improve job access for workers who previously faced long commutes, enabling better worker-job matching. Property value increases near stations reflect improved accessibility being capitalised into land prices.

**Evidence**
World Bank (2009) World Development Report. Evidence strength is observational. The 11% property value increase reflects the combined upgrading programme, not TransMilenio alone; attribution is partial.

**Dependencies**
Supports: claim-0038 — TransMilenio is the positive case for claim-0038's principle that connective transport infrastructure is a prerequisite for effective targeted place-based interventions; claim-0040 shows that when connectivity is provided first, neighbourhood upgrading succeeds.

**Policy implications**
- Auckland's rapid transit investment should evaluate BRT as a cost-effective option for corridors where light rail is not yet viable.
- New Zealand's public transport investment framework should incorporate property value uplift near new rapid transit as a partial offset to construction costs.
- Equity outcomes — travel time reductions for low-income residents — should be a primary criterion in Auckland rapid transit route prioritisation.
- The cost comparison with light rail (~$10m/km for BRT vs $30m for LRT) should inform NZ's rapid transit programme, particularly in corridors where demand is developing.
- Bogotá's political leadership model (sustained commitment across multiple mayors) is relevant for Auckland's long-term transport planning.

**Relevant institutions**
Auckland Transport, NZTA, Auckland Council, Ministry of Transport, HUD.

---
## claim-0041: Doubling per-capita income raises floor space 29%; doubling population reduces it 40%

**Statement**
Doubling a city's per-capita income (population held constant) raises floor space per person by approximately 29%; doubling population (income held constant) reduces floor space per person by approximately 40%. Income growth — not population growth — is the primary driver of vertical layering and floor space expansion; pyramid-form cities are an economic outcome, not a planning target.

**Meaning (plain English)**
Richer cities build upward because rising incomes create demand for more and better floor space. Growing cities that don't get richer simply crowd existing space. The tall, dense skylines of successful cities are not the cause of their success but a consequence of economic growth — you can't mandate a Manhattan by building height without the income growth that creates demand for it.

**Why it matters**
For NZ, this means upzoning alone will not produce tall, dense Auckland unless it is accompanied by economic growth and rising incomes. Supply-side reforms enable the market to respond when demand exists; they don't create demand by themselves. The evidence cautions against expecting planning reform to transform Auckland's built form rapidly.

**Mechanism**
Rising incomes generate demand for more floor space per person (both residential and commercial). Developers respond by building taller when land prices are high relative to construction costs. Population growth without income growth merely distributes existing floor space more thinly, producing crowding rather than vertical expansion.

**Evidence**
Lall et al. (2021) "Pancakes to Pyramids" World Bank report, using GHS-UCDB and WSF3D satellite data covering 9,500 cities globally 1990–2015. Evidence strength is observational.

**Dependencies**
Supports: claim-0042 — the income-floor space elasticity finding in claim-0041 explains the mechanism behind the observed pattern in claim-0042 that high-income cities develop vertically while low-income cities expand horizontally; claim-0041 provides the demand-side micro-foundation for claim-0042's macro pattern.

**Policy implications**
- Auckland's upzoning reforms (NPS-UD) create the supply-side conditions for vertical development, but actual uptake depends on continued income growth and market confidence.
- NZ productivity policy (raising incomes) and planning policy (enabling density) are complements, not substitutes.
- HUD's housing supply metrics should track actual floor space per person, not just dwelling count, to capture whether supply reforms are improving residential conditions.
- FAR and height limit reforms should be accompanied by infrastructure investment to make dense development commercially viable.
- Policy should not conflate supply enablement with guaranteed development outcomes — the market still needs income-driven demand to respond.

**Relevant institutions**
HUD, Auckland Council, MfE, MBIE, Treasury.

---
## claim-0042: Every income group's cities expanded built-up area faster than population 1990–2015, but modes differed

**Statement**
Between 1990 and 2015 every income group's cities expanded built-up area faster than population grew. But the mode of expansion differed sharply: low- and lower-middle-income cities grew almost entirely through horizontal spread; high-income cities added substantial infill and vertical layering alongside horizontal expansion, producing denser, taller central districts.

**Meaning (plain English)**
All cities worldwide sprawled outward faster than their populations grew over 25 years, but rich cities also built upward and filled in gaps in their existing fabric. Poor cities just spread outward — no infill, no upward growth. The result is that poor cities are actually quite dense in places but lack the economic productivity of high-income city density because the sprawl is disconnected.

**Why it matters**
NZ cities — especially Auckland — are high-income but have historically behaved more like lower-middle-income cities in their expansion pattern (horizontal growth on the fringe). Auckland's planning reforms aim to shift it toward the high-income pattern of infill and vertical growth as well as horizontal expansion.

**Mechanism**
Rising income creates demand for floor space that can be met vertically within the existing urban footprint. Without income growth (or with regulatory restrictions on building up), expansion can only go outward. High-income cities' infill pattern reflects both market demand and better-functioning land markets that price central-city land appropriately.

**Evidence**
Lall et al. (2021) using GHS-UCDB and WSF3D satellite data for 9,500 cities globally, 1990–2015. Evidence strength is observational.

**Dependencies**
Qualifies: claim-0041 — the observed expansion pattern (horizontal dominant in low-income, vertical in high-income) is the macro empirical confirmation of claim-0041's floor space demand elasticity findings; claim-0042 shows what the income-floor space relationship produces at the aggregate city level.
Depends on: claim-0041 — claim-0042's income-group pattern is explained by the demand elasticities established in claim-0041.

**Policy implications**
- Auckland's historic horizontal expansion pattern is consistent with a city under-performing its income level — the NPS-UD upzoning reforms are correctly targeted.
- Monitoring Auckland's infill-to-greenfield ratio over time will show whether planning reforms are shifting the expansion pattern toward the high-income mode.
- NZ's infrastructure investment should prioritise servicing infill development (stormwater, wastewater capacity in inner suburbs) rather than only funding greenfield network extensions.
- Wellington and Christchurch similarly need infill-enabling infrastructure to shift their expansion patterns.
- Stats NZ and Auckland Council should track built-up area expansion by type (horizontal vs infill vs vertical) as a planning performance metric.

**Relevant institutions**
Auckland Council, HUD, MfE, Te Waihanga, Stats NZ.

---
## claim-0043: RETIRED — see claim-0195 through claim-0199

**This claim has been retired (2026-06-30).** It bundled five distinct outcomes (wages, patents, energy, pollution, public service costs) into a single claim, sourced second-hand via Box 1.4 of Lall et al. (2021) rather than the primary paper. The primary source (Ahlfeldt and Pietrostefani 2019) has since been ingested directly as source-0032, and the second-hand citation turns out to have inverted the sign of the pollution effect: doubling density is associated with **higher**, not lower, local pollution concentration (one of the few categories the primary source treats as causally identified), and the public-service-cost figure is more precisely −17%, not −14.4%.

Replaced by:
- claim-0195: Doubling density → +4% wages (causal)
- claim-0196: Doubling density → +21% patent rate (associative)
- claim-0197: Doubling density → −7% energy consumption (associative)
- claim-0198: Doubling density → **+13% local pollution concentration** (causal — corrects the sign error in the retired claim)
- claim-0199: Doubling density → −17% unit cost of local public services (associative)

See claim-0195.md through claim-0199.md for full statements, evidence, and policy implications.

---
## claim-0044: Density-GHG emissions relationship holds for high-income cities but not for low-income cities

**Statement**
The inverse relationship between urban density and per-capita greenhouse gas emissions holds in high- and upper-middle-income cities but does not hold in low- and lower-middle-income cities. The latter are already far denser than high-income cities, and high density in poor cities reflects overcrowding and poverty rather than low-carbon lifestyles.

**Meaning (plain English)**
In rich countries, compact cities genuinely do emit less carbon. But in poor countries, people live in extremely dense conditions because they are poor — not because they have chosen low-carbon lifestyles. The density-emissions relationship there is driven by income, not urban form.

**Why it matters**
This finding prevents a naive extension of compact city climate policy to developing countries. For NZ development policy, it means advising Pacific Island cities to densify for climate reasons misses the point — their density is already high and driven by poverty, not choice.

**Mechanism**
In high-income cities, density reduces car ownership and travel distances, which are the main drivers of transport emissions. In low-income cities, car ownership is already minimal because of poverty, so additional density doesn't further reduce emissions from this channel. As incomes rise in low-income cities, the key policy question is whether rising car ownership is avoided through transit investment before lock-in occurs.

**Evidence**
Lall et al. (2021) using Global Gridded Model of Carbon Footprints (Moran et al. 2018) and GHS-UCDB city data. Evidence strength is observational.

**Dependencies**
Qualifies: claim-0197 — the −7% energy benefit of doubling density in claim-0197 applies to the high-income OECD city context; claim-0044 shows these benefits do not transfer to low- and lower-middle-income cities, limiting the generality of claim-0197's sustainability findings.
Qualifies: claim-0028 — the compact cities-lower emissions relationship from claim-0028 similarly applies to high-income contexts; claim-0044 provides the important qualification that this relationship breaks down at lower income levels.

**Policy implications**
- NZ's climate and urban policy advice to Pacific Island nations should focus on preventing car-dependent infrastructure lock-in as incomes rise, not on increasing density per se.
- Auckland and other NZ cities are correctly in the high-income category where compact city policies reduce emissions.
- NZ's emissions reduction plan should distinguish between transport decarbonisation pathways for high-income compact cities and those for developing cities.
- International climate finance from NZ should support developing cities to invest in transit infrastructure before car ownership rises with income.
- NZ development assistance in urban planning should prioritise transit-oriented development frameworks for rapidly urbanising Pacific neighbours.

**Relevant institutions**
MfE, Ministry of Transport, Climate Change Commission, MFAT, NZTA.

---
## claim-0045: Urban built structures last 150+ years; early infrastructure locks in city form for generations

**Statement**
Urban built structures have a "putty clay" quality — once constructed, they can remain in place for 150+ years. Early infrastructure determines a city's spatial form for generations: Dar es Salaam's 1970s sites-and-services road plans still match its road network today, and those neighbourhoods command the highest land values in the city, exceeding even wealthy residential districts.

**Meaning (plain English)**
Cities are extraordinarily slow to change. Roads, drainage, and building layouts laid down fifty years ago still shape how a city works today, and will continue to do so for another century. Getting infrastructure right early is therefore far more valuable than retrofitting it later — and the window for shaping a city's long-run form is narrow.

**Why it matters**
For NZ, this means decisions about Auckland's rapid transit corridors, water infrastructure extensions, and greenfields development sequencing being made now will shape Auckland's form until 2100 or beyond. The cost of getting these decisions wrong compounds over generations.

**Mechanism**
Infrastructure investments fix the spatial template of a city because buildings, land uses, and economic activity concentrate along infrastructure lines. Retrofitting — rebuilding roads, re-routing rail, upgrading stormwater under existing buildings — is far more expensive and politically difficult than building correctly the first time. Land values capitalise infrastructure quality, creating incentives to maintain and extend the initial infrastructure template.

**Evidence**
Lall et al. (2021) citing Michaels et al. (forthcoming, Journal of Political Economy) for the Dar es Salaam road network persistence finding. Evidence strength is case study. The 150+ year structure lifespan reflects general construction durability evidence.

**Dependencies**
Supports: claim-0038 — the path dependence of early infrastructure investment directly reinforces claim-0038's sequencing principle that infrastructure must come before targeted interventions; claim-0045 explains why getting the sequencing wrong creates lock-in that is very costly to reverse.

**Policy implications**
- Auckland's current decisions about rapid transit corridors (light rail routes, heavy rail extensions) are generational decisions and should be made with long time horizons, not electoral-cycle cost-benefit analysis.
- Te Waihanga should develop a 50-year spatial infrastructure plan for Auckland and other major NZ cities that sets the template for future development.
- Greenfields development in Auckland should only be approved where infrastructure is planned and funded to be built first, not as a retrospective afterthought.
- NZ's three-water infrastructure reforms should account for long-run spatial planning, not just immediate service delivery.
- Central government's infrastructure decisions for Auckland should be made jointly with Auckland Council, given the multi-generational consequences.

**Relevant institutions**
Te Waihanga, Auckland Council, NZTA, HUD, Three Waters entities, DPMC.

---
## claim-0046: Mexico City metro expansion reduced informality 4pp; $1 invested yielded 20% real income gain

**Statement**
Mexico City metro expansion reduced informality by 4 percentage points near new stations. For every dollar invested in metro construction, average real incomes rose by 20% relative to a counterfactual of a perfectly efficient labour market — capturing large welfare gains from connecting workers to better job matches in a city with spatial misallocation.

**Meaning (plain English)**
When Mexico City expanded its metro network, workers near new stations shifted out of informal jobs into formal employment, and real wages rose substantially. The returns to the investment were large because Mexico City's labour market was badly spatially misaligned — many workers couldn't reach the best-matched jobs without the metro.

**Why it matters**
The 20% real income gain from metro investment reflects how much productivity is being wasted by spatial misallocation in large cities. NZ's own labour markets have some spatial misallocation, particularly in Auckland where congestion and poor PT limit effective job access for residents of South and West Auckland.

**Mechanism**
Metro expansion reduces commuting costs and extends the geographic reach of workers' job searches and firms' talent pools. In cities where spatial mismatch is large (workers far from best-matched firms), transit investment resolves a market failure and generates large productivity gains. The informality reduction reflects formal firms being newly accessible to previously disconnected workers.

**Evidence**
Zarate (2020) structural model, cited in Lall et al. (2021). Evidence strength is model-based. The 20% figure is relative to a perfectly efficient labour market counterfactual, not relative to no intervention.

**Dependencies**
Supports: claim-0034 — the Mexico City metro results directly support claim-0034's finding that connectivity (market potential within 10km) drives formal employment; the informality reduction and income gain confirm that transit investment increases effective connectivity with large productivity effects.

**Policy implications**
- Auckland's rapid transit investment (City Rail Link, light rail) should be evaluated using labour market accessibility metrics, not just travel time savings.
- The informality-reduction effect of transit suggests NZ should track formal employment rates in areas served by new rapid transit infrastructure.
- The 20% real income gain estimate (from a structural model) should be triangulated against reduced-form evidence before use in NZ cost-benefit analysis.
- South and West Auckland rapid transit should be prioritised partly on equity grounds: spatial mismatch between residents and formal employment is largest in these areas.
- NZ's transport business case methodology (NZTA) should incorporate agglomeration benefits from improved labour market accessibility, not just travel time savings.

**Relevant institutions**
NZTA, Auckland Transport, Auckland Council, Treasury, MBIE.

---
## claim-0047: Urban primacy reduces GDP growth by ~1.5 percentage points per standard deviation increase

**Statement**
Urbanisation per se does not affect economic growth. But urban primacy does: a one standard deviation increase in urban primacy (~15 percentage points, from a mean of 31% of the urban population living in the largest city) is associated with approximately 1.5 percentage points lower annual GDP growth. The relationship is non-linear — moderate primacy can be growth-enhancing, excessive primacy is harmful.

**Meaning (plain English)**
It's not urbanisation itself that hurts or helps growth, but how concentrated that urbanisation is in a single city. Countries where one dominant city absorbs most urban population growth tend to grow more slowly. Moderate urban concentration can be good, but excessive primacy — one city sucking the economic life out of the rest of the country — is costly.

**Why it matters**
Auckland accounts for 33% of NZ's population and a disproportionate share of economic activity. This claim raises the question of whether Auckland's primacy has become excessive. If so, NZ may be incurring a 1.5pp annual GDP growth penalty from concentration that has gone beyond the productivity-enhancing level.

**Mechanism**
Excessive primacy implies that agglomeration benefits in the primate city are offset by congestion and diseconomies at the top, while the rest of the system is starved of the critical mass needed to function effectively. The political economy behind primacy (see claim-0048) independently suppresses growth elsewhere through resource misallocation.

**Evidence**
Henderson (2003) cross-country growth regression using GMM with lagged primacy instruments, reviewed in Duranton (2008). Evidence strength is econometric. This is the only cross-country study meeting rigorous methodological standards per Duranton's review.

**Dependencies**
Supports: claim-0056 — the primacy-growth relationship in claim-0047 provides a national-level counterpart to claim-0056's finding that metropolitan fragmentation reduces productivity; both suggest that the structure of urban systems, not just city size, determines growth outcomes.
Qualifies: claim-0001 — the agglomeration benefit from city size documented in claim-0001 is qualified by claim-0047, which shows that excessive primacy (concentration of too much growth in one city) can reverse the national growth benefit.

**Policy implications**
- NZ should assess whether Auckland's primacy level is growth-enhancing or has moved into the zone where it suppresses national growth.
- Regional development investment in Wellington, Christchurch, Hamilton, and Tauranga may improve national growth by building productive secondary cities rather than allowing excessive Auckland primacy.
- Auckland's congestion, housing costs, and infrastructure constraints may reflect diseconomies of primacy — evidence for investing in Auckland's internal productivity rather than just its size.
- NZ's spatial planning strategy should maintain productive secondary cities as genuine alternatives to Auckland, not just as overflow for those priced out.
- Treasury should model the national growth effects of different urban system configurations, including alternative primacy scenarios.

**Relevant institutions**
MBIE, Treasury, regional economic development agencies, Auckland Council.

---
## claim-0048: Urban primacy is driven by political and institutional factors, not trade; deregulation is the key remedy

**Statement**
Urban primacy in developing countries is driven by political and institutional factors — particularly undemocratic and unstable regimes — not by trade policy. Trade plays no systematic role in determining primacy. Primate city favouritism operates through many small advantages: easier permit access, subsidised public goods, underpriced utilities, and better infrastructure in the capital. Administrative deregulation is the most tractable tool to reduce it.

**Meaning (plain English)**
Countries with politically dominant capital cities didn't get that way because of trade patterns — they got that way because of politics. Authoritarian and unstable governments concentrate resources and advantages in the capital. The way to reduce excessive primacy is not trade reform but administrative deregulation that removes the many small privileges the capital city enjoys over other places.

**Why it matters**
For NZ, this suggests the appropriate response to Auckland's primacy is not restricting Auckland's growth but ensuring that Wellington, Christchurch, and other centres have equivalent regulatory environments and infrastructure quality. Removing bureaucratic advantages that favour one location is more tractable than trying to redistribute economic activity through direct intervention.

**Mechanism**
Capital city advantage operates through accumulated small privileges — faster permits, better utilities, preferential infrastructure placement — that collectively make the capital more productive and attractive than it would be on pure market terms. These privileges are politically rational (governments benefit from a concentrated, accessible population) but economically distorting.

**Evidence**
Reviewed in Duranton (2008) drawing on Ades and Glaeser (1995), Davis and Henderson (2003), Nitsch (2006), and Henderson et al. (2001). Evidence strength is observational. The Korean evidence on administrative deregulation is from a single country case.

**Dependencies**
Supports: claim-0047 — the political economy explanation for primacy in claim-0048 provides the causal mechanism behind the primacy-growth relationship observed in claim-0047; if primacy is caused by political distortions, then the growth penalty reflects resource misallocation driven by those distortions.

**Policy implications**
- NZ should assess whether government location decisions, regulatory processes, and infrastructure spending systematically favour Auckland or Wellington over other regions.
- Administrative deregulation — simplifying permits, consents, and business regulation equally across all NZ regions — is a tractable tool to reduce distortions.
- Central government co-location of functions across NZ regions (not just Wellington and Auckland) can reduce administrative concentration.
- If Wellington's role as the capital creates bureaucratic primacy effects, addressing this through deliberate decentralisation of agency functions may support regional growth.
- NZ's regulatory reform programme should include a spatial analysis of where regulatory burdens fall disproportionately on non-metro regions.

**Relevant institutions**
DPMC, MBIE, Treasury, regional economic development agencies, Local Government Commission.

---
## claim-0049: China's hukou restrictions kept cities below optimal size; costs of undersizing exceed costs of oversizing

**Statement**
China's hukou migration restrictions kept cities significantly below their productivity-maximising size, causing large income losses. The net wage curve is quasi-flat after its maximum — cities slightly too large incur small welfare losses — but the cost of being constrained below the optimum is substantially larger. This asymmetry implies that anti-mobility policies impose greater welfare harm than overcrowding would.

**Meaning (plain English)**
When China prevented workers from moving freely to cities, it made everyone poorer than they needed to be. Cities that are a bit too big barely suffer — the congestion and crowding costs are small. But cities kept too small miss out on large productivity gains. This asymmetry means it is far better to err on the side of allowing too much urban growth than to restrict it.

**Why it matters**
NZ's historic restrictive zoning effectively constrained Auckland below its productivity-maximising size, much as China's hukou restricted Chinese cities. The asymmetry finding — undersizing is much costlier than mild oversizing — suggests NZ should liberalise generously rather than cautiously.

**Mechanism**
As cities grow, agglomeration benefits accumulate rapidly at first (strong matching, spillover, and sharing effects) and then taper off. Beyond the optimum, congestion and cost-of-living effects cause the net wage curve to flatten. The flat right-hand side means overshoot is cheap; the steep left-hand side means undershooting is expensive.

**Evidence**
Au and Henderson (2006a, 2006b) exploiting variation from China's hukou system to identify productivity-maximising city sizes, reviewed in Duranton (2008). Evidence strength is econometric.

**Dependencies**
Supports: claim-0022 — the finding that Chinese cities were kept below their productivity-maximising size provides a natural experiment for the agglomeration-city size relationship at the heart of claim-0022; the large income losses from hukou constraints confirm the magnitude of agglomeration benefits from city size.

**Policy implications**
- NZ's upzoning reforms (NPS-UD) and housing supply liberalisation should be bold rather than incremental, given the asymmetry finding — oversupply is much less costly than undersupply.
- Arguments that Auckland is already large enough and needs no further growth should be evaluated against evidence that city size costs rise slowly above the optimum.
- Internal migration within NZ — movement from smaller centres to Auckland — should be facilitated rather than discouraged through housing policy.
- NZ immigration policy enabling population growth in Auckland is consistent with the asymmetry finding: the cost of allowing too many people in is low relative to the cost of keeping too few.
- Any policy proposal to redirect Auckland growth to secondary cities should be tested against whether those cities are truly above their productivity-maximising size.

**Relevant institutions**
MBIE, HUD, Treasury, Auckland Council, Immigration NZ.

---
## claim-0050: Minimum lot-size zoning in developing cities forces workers into squatter settlements; land titling raises female labour supply

**Statement**
Exclusionary zoning (minimum lot-size requirements and similar regulations) in developing cities raises formal housing costs until squatter settlements become the cheaper alternative for low-income workers. Removing these unnecessary regulations lowers living costs, raises net wages, and eliminates the market incentive for squatter settlement formation. Land titling has independent benefits: formal title is associated with greater female labour supply and investment in housing improvement.

**Meaning (plain English)**
When governments require minimum plot sizes that are too large for poor families to afford, they effectively ban low-income workers from the formal housing market, pushing them into illegal settlements. Removing these rules makes legal housing affordable again. Separately, giving residents formal title to their homes (even in existing informal settlements) increases women's economic participation and investment in home improvement.

**Why it matters**
NZ's historic minimum section sizes in suburban zoning rules (e.g., 400–600m² in many Auckland suburbs) imposed exactly this mechanism, making formal housing unaffordable for lower-income households. The NPS-UD's removal of minimum lot sizes is therefore evidence-based policy, not just ideology.

**Mechanism**
Minimum lot-size rules raise the cost of legal housing by requiring more land per household than the market would freely provide. When formal housing is priced above what low-income workers can pay, squatter or informal housing on public land becomes the rational alternative. Land titling improves tenure security, enabling residents to invest in property improvement and freeing women from constant defence of informal tenure to participate in the labour market.

**Evidence**
Henderson (2007) for minimum lot-size and squatter settlements in Brazil; Field (2007) and Di Tella et al. (2007) for land titling effects; reviewed in Duranton (2008). Evidence strength is case study.

**Dependencies**
Supports: claim-0037 — Mumbai's FSI restriction case in claim-0037 is an extreme version of the same mechanism documented in claim-0050 for Brazilian minimum lot-size rules; both show how supply-restricting regulations drive low-income households into informal settlements.

**Policy implications**
- NZ's removal of minimum lot-size requirements through the NPS-UD is directly consistent with this evidence and should be maintained.
- Auckland's legacy minimum section size rules in areas not yet covered by upzoning should be identified and reformed.
- Māori land governance reforms (Papakāinga housing, Māori land court reforms) should incorporate clear title arrangements that enable investment and labour market participation.
- NZ housing policy should track whether regulatory reform has reduced the effective barrier to formal housing for low-income households.
- Any future minimum lot-size requirements proposed by councils should be assessed against this evidence on their welfare costs.

**Relevant institutions**
HUD, MfE, Auckland Council, Te Puni Kōkiri, MBIE.

---
## claim-0051: 10% increase in effective employment density raises NZ firm productivity by 0.69%

**Statement**
A 10% increase in effective employment density is associated with a 0.69% increase in firm productivity in New Zealand, using a within-local-industry specification that controls for the sorting of productive firms into dense areas.

**Meaning (plain English)**
New Zealand firms located in denser employment areas are measurably more productive, even after accounting for the fact that more productive firms tend to choose denser locations. A 10% increase in surrounding employment density raises a firm's productivity by about 0.7%.

**Why it matters**
This is the best available NZ-specific estimate of agglomeration benefits for firm productivity. It can be directly applied to NZ policy analysis without relying on international elasticities that may not transfer to NZ's institutional and geographic context.

**Mechanism**
Effective density is a distance-weighted measure of employment accessible from a location. Higher effective density enables better firm-worker matching, more supplier-customer interactions, and faster knowledge spillovers within industries. The within-local-industry specification ensures these effects are not driven by industry composition differences between dense and sparse areas.

**Evidence**
Maré and Graham (2009) using the NZ Prototype Longitudinal Business Database, 1999–2006. Evidence strength is quasi-experimental (within-industry fixed effects). Applies to the measured sector only; does not cover government or non-market activities.

**Dependencies**
None listed in the relationships block (note: relationships field is absent from this claim's YAML; related claims mentioned in the text are 0052, 0053, and 0001).

**Policy implications**
- Auckland's effective employment density should be tracked over time as a productivity indicator for MBIE and Treasury.
- Transport and zoning policies that increase effective density — by bringing more workers and firms into close proximity — have directly measurable productivity benefits calibrated to the 0.69% per 10% density increase.
- The City Rail Link and other rapid transit investments increase effective employment density by expanding the accessible employment pool for workers across the city.
- MBIE should use the 0.69% NZ-specific elasticity (not international estimates) as the default for NZ urban productivity appraisal.
- Wellington and Christchurch agglomeration policies should be evaluated using the same framework, with city-specific density measurements.

**Relevant institutions**
MBIE, Treasury, Auckland Council, Stats NZ, Te Waihanga.

---
## claim-0052: 91% of apparent density-productivity link in NZ reflects sorting, not true agglomeration

**Statement**
In New Zealand, 91% of the apparent density–productivity relationship reflects the sorting of productive firms into dense locations rather than true agglomeration: the pooled OLS elasticity of 0.171 falls to 0.048 with composition controls and to 0.015 with firm fixed effects.

**Meaning (plain English)**
Raw data in NZ suggests that firms in dense areas are dramatically more productive. But most of this is because productive firms choose to locate in dense areas, not because density made them productive. Once you hold firm identity constant over time, only about 9% of the raw relationship reflects genuine agglomeration economies.

**Why it matters**
This finding is crucial for avoiding policy errors. If policymakers misinterpret the raw correlation between density and productivity as causal, they will dramatically overestimate the productivity gains from densification policies. The preferred NZ-specific estimate (0.69% per 10% density increase, from claim-0051) is the correctly-controlled figure to use.

**Mechanism**
Sorting occurs because productive firms (which have higher productivity for reasons unrelated to location) find it optimal to pay the higher rents of dense areas for access to customers, suppliers, and skilled workers. When these firm-specific characteristics are controlled for with fixed effects, the density premium falls sharply, leaving only the genuine agglomeration externality.

**Evidence**
Maré and Graham (2009) using NZ Prototype Longitudinal Business Database, 1999–2006. Evidence strength is quasi-experimental. The 91% figure captures both sorting and potential attenuation from measurement error.

**Dependencies**
None listed in the relationships block (related claims per text: 0051 and 0014).

**Policy implications**
- NZ policy analysis should never use pooled OLS estimates of density-productivity relationships; only controls-adjusted estimates from Maré and Graham (2009) or similar should be used.
- MBIE and Treasury should include guidance on avoiding sorting bias in all agglomeration benefit assessments for NZ investment appraisal.
- The 91% sorting finding does not invalidate densification policy — the 9% genuine agglomeration effect is still real and valuable — but it does mean the returns are lower than naive analysis suggests.
- Auckland's zoning reforms attract productive firms through agglomeration, but policymakers should not conflate firm attraction (which might just relocate firms from elsewhere) with new productivity creation.
- Research on worker-level sorting versus firm-level sorting in NZ would further sharpen this estimate.

**Relevant institutions**
MBIE, Treasury, Stats NZ, Auckland Council.

---
## claim-0053: NZ service sectors benefit most from agglomeration; property/business services elasticity is 0.16

**Statement**
In New Zealand, service sectors benefit most from agglomeration: property and business services have an elasticity of 0.16 and finance and insurance 0.087 (within local industry estimates at mean density), while manufacturing and mining elasticities are lower or statistically insignificant.

**Meaning (plain English)**
In New Zealand, the firms that benefit most from being in dense, well-connected areas are service businesses — particularly those dealing with property, business advice, and finance. Manufacturers and miners gain little or nothing from urban density. This means Auckland's knowledge economy sectors are the primary beneficiaries of agglomeration policy.

**Why it matters**
NZ's economic growth strategy should focus agglomeration-enabling policies (upzoning, transport investment, CBD amenity) on the service sectors that benefit most. Industrial land policy should recognise that manufacturing gains little from density, while zoning that enables professional and financial service clustering delivers the highest productivity returns.

**Mechanism**
Knowledge-intensive services rely heavily on face-to-face interaction, information exchange, and professional networks — all of which are facilitated by co-location in dense areas. Manufacturing and mining depend more on physical supply chains and access to resources, which are less density-dependent.

**Evidence**
Maré and Graham (2009) industry-level elasticity estimates from the NZ Prototype Longitudinal Business Database, 1999–2006. Evidence strength is observational with controls. Finance and insurance figure of 0.087 is the within-local-industry estimate; the 0.13 figure uses a different specification.

**Dependencies**
None listed in the relationships block (related claims per text: 0051 and 0013).

**Policy implications**
- Auckland CBD and mixed-use centre zoning should prioritise professional, financial, and business services as the highest-return agglomeration sectors.
- Industrial land protection policies that prevent conversion to mixed-use or service activities impose opportunity costs given manufacturing's low agglomeration returns.
- Auckland's innovation precinct strategies (Wynyard Quarter, Tāmaki Regeneration) are well-targeted at the high-agglomeration service sectors.
- MBIE's regional economic development investment should be directed to activities with high agglomeration returns in each region, not just general economic stimulus.
- Wellington's CBD and Christchurch's innovation precinct should be developed with the service-sector agglomeration profile in mind.

**Relevant institutions**
MBIE, Auckland Council, Auckland Unlimited, Wellington City Council, Ōtautahi Christchurch economic development agencies.

---
## claim-0054: NZ firms in denser areas use 9–15% less capital and labour but 12% more intermediate inputs

**Statement**
In New Zealand, firms in denser areas use 9–15% less capital and labour per unit of output but consume 12% more intermediate inputs — agglomeration raises productive efficiency but also raises input costs, partially offsetting the output gains.

**Meaning (plain English)**
Firms in dense Auckland locations are more efficient at using workers and equipment to produce output, but they pay more for the business services and intermediate goods they buy from other firms. Agglomeration makes firms leaner but more expensive to supply — a genuine but partial trade-off.

**Why it matters**
The higher intermediate input costs in dense areas (reflecting higher rents and service costs) partially offset the efficiency gains from agglomeration. This means the net productivity benefit (claim-0051) is the result of competing forces, and policies that raise intermediate input costs in dense areas (e.g., commercial rent inflation) reduce the net gain.

**Mechanism**
Dense locations provide better access to specialised inputs and services (hence less capital and labour needed to produce the same output), but those inputs are priced higher in competitive urban markets. Firms effectively buy efficiency at a higher unit cost of intermediate inputs. This is an equilibrium outcome of the market for agglomeration.

**Evidence**
Maré and Graham (2009) factor demand model using NZ Prototype Longitudinal Business Database, 1999–2006. Evidence strength is observational with controls.

**Dependencies**
None listed in the relationships block (related claims per text: 0051 and 0027).

**Policy implications**
- Policies that inflate commercial property costs in Auckland CBD (e.g., restrictive office zoning) raise intermediate input costs for dense-area firms, partially neutralising agglomeration efficiency gains.
- Auckland's commercial property supply (office, retail, industrial in mixed-use zones) should be enabled to expand to prevent rent inflation from eroding agglomeration benefits.
- The net productivity gain from agglomeration (0.69% per 10% density increase) already accounts for higher intermediate input costs; policy appraisal should use the net figure.
- Supply-side reforms enabling more commercial floor space in Auckland will reduce intermediate input cost pressure and increase the net agglomeration benefit.
- Monitoring commercial rental trends in Auckland is important for assessing whether agglomeration benefits are being maintained or eroded.

**Relevant institutions**
Auckland Council, MBIE, Treasury, Stats NZ.

---
## claim-0055: Doubling city population raises productivity by 3.8% (pooled OECD); range 1.6% (UK) to 6.3% (US)

**Statement**
Across five OECD countries, doubling city population is associated with 3.8% higher productivity (pooled estimate), with country-level elasticities ranging from 1.6% in the UK to 6.3% in the US.

**Meaning (plain English)**
The productivity benefit of a bigger city is about 3.8% per doubling of population on average across wealthy countries, but this varies considerably. Cities in the US gain much more from size than cities in the UK. NZ's elasticity likely sits somewhere in this range, influenced by its own institutional and geographic features.

**Why it matters**
The wide country variation (1.6% to 6.3%) shows that agglomeration benefits are not fixed by city size alone — institutions, land-use regulation, transport infrastructure, and labour market flexibility all affect how much productivity gain a city actually realises. NZ-specific estimates (claim-0051) are therefore more reliable than applying international averages.

**Mechanism**
The same matching, learning, and sharing mechanisms drive agglomeration across all five countries, but the magnitude varies with how well land and labour markets function, how well-connected the city is, and how many barriers to agglomeration (regulatory, transport, skills) exist. Countries with more flexible markets realise larger agglomeration gains.

**Evidence**
Ahrend et al. (2015) using harmonised OECD Functional Urban Area definitions across Germany, Mexico, Spain, UK, and USA. Evidence strength is quasi-experimental (two-step estimation controlling for worker sorting). Published as SERC Discussion Paper 178.

**Dependencies**
None listed in the relationships block (related claims per text: 0001, 0022, 0011).

**Policy implications**
- NZ's agglomeration elasticity likely lies within the 1.6–6.3% range; the NZ-specific estimate of 0.69% per 10% density increase from Maré and Graham (2009) should be used for domestic appraisal.
- The variation across countries shows that institutional reform (better land markets, governance, transport) can raise NZ's position within the international range.
- Policy comparison with the UK (1.6%) — another small, open OECD economy — is more appropriate than comparison with the US (6.3%), which has different city size distributions and land markets.
- Auckland should be benchmarked against UK cities of similar size (Birmingham, Manchester, Leeds) to assess whether it is over- or under-performing on agglomeration.
- MBIE should commission regular updates to NZ-specific agglomeration elasticity estimates as the Longitudinal Business Database is updated.

**Relevant institutions**
MBIE, Treasury, Stats NZ, Auckland Council.

---
## claim-0056: Doubling municipalities within a metro area cuts productivity 3.2–6%; governance body halves the penalty

**Statement**
Doubling the number of municipalities within a metropolitan area is associated with 3.2% lower productivity; where no metropolitan governance body exists the fragmentation penalty reaches 6%; a metropolitan governance body roughly halves the penalty to ~3%.

**Meaning (plain English)**
Fragmenting city government across many councils reduces productivity, and the penalty is worst where there is no coordinating body spanning the whole metro area. A metropolitan governance body doesn't eliminate the problem but cuts the damage roughly in half.

**Why it matters**
This five-country OECD study provides independent confirmation of the OECD (2015) finding (claim-0023), using different data and methods. The consistency across studies strengthens the evidence that metropolitan governance reform is a genuine productivity lever for Auckland.

**Mechanism**
Municipal fragmentation creates mismatched planning boundaries, duplicate infrastructure, and transport network gaps. A metropolitan governance body coordinates across these boundaries, partially internalising the externalities that fragmented councils would otherwise ignore. The residual penalty reflects the limits of coordination without full integration.

**Evidence**
Ahrend et al. (2015) across Germany, Mexico, Spain, UK, and USA using harmonised OECD FUA definitions. Evidence strength is quasi-experimental. Results vary by country; Mexico shows the largest fragmentation penalty.

**Dependencies**
None listed in the relationships block (related claims per text: 0023 and 0024).

**Policy implications**
- Wellington's multi-council metropolitan structure (five councils plus Greater Wellington Regional Council) likely imposes a fragmentation penalty comparable to the 3.2–6% documented here and should be reformed.
- Auckland's single council structure is better placed than Wellington's, but the fragmentation between Auckland Council and central government creates a different kind of coordination failure.
- Any local government structure review in NZ (e.g., Wellington amalgamation proposals) should reference this evidence as primary justification.
- The 3% residual penalty even with a governance body shows that even well-governed fragmented metros underperform — full amalgamation where feasible is superior to coordination bodies.
- Treasury should estimate the NZ fiscal and productivity cost of current metropolitan fragmentation using this elasticity as a benchmark.

**Relevant institutions**
Local Government Commission, DPMC, Wellington City Council, Greater Wellington Regional Council, Auckland Council, MBIE.

---
## claim-0057: 10 percentage point rise in city graduate share yields 3% productivity externality

**Statement**
A 10 percentage point increase in the share of university graduates in a city is associated with a 3% productivity premium — an externality from knowledge spillovers rather than a direct effect of individual human capital.

**Meaning (plain English)**
When a city has more university graduates, all firms — not just those employing the graduates — become more productive. A 10-point increase in the graduate share raises city-wide productivity by 3% as a spillover effect. This is above and beyond the individual productivity of educated workers themselves.

**Why it matters**
Knowledge spillover externalities justify public investment in graduate retention and attraction policies for Auckland and other NZ cities. If the 3% productivity gain were fully internalised by the graduate, there would be no market failure. The externality means the market underprovides graduate concentration relative to the social optimum.

**Mechanism**
Educated workers generate ideas, processes, and practices that diffuse to other workers and firms through professional networks, labour market interactions, and informal exchange. These spillovers are not compensated in individual wages, creating a positive externality that justifies place-based policy to attract and retain graduates.

**Evidence**
Ahrend et al. (2015) using two-step estimation across five OECD countries. Evidence strength is quasi-experimental. The two-step approach separates individual-level returns (first stage) from city-level externalities (second stage); the 3% is the residual city-level spillover.

**Dependencies**
None listed in the relationships block (related claims per text: 0031).

**Policy implications**
- Auckland and Wellington should invest in tertiary education quality, graduate amenity, and housing affordability specifically as tools for retaining graduates and capturing knowledge spillover benefits.
- NZ's Talented Workforce visa and skills-focused immigration should prioritise graduate concentration in Auckland as a productivity strategy.
- Auckland Council economic development should track graduate retention rates as a performance indicator alongside productivity metrics.
- MBIE should evaluate whether NZ loses knowledge externality benefits through graduate emigration to Australia, and design retention policies accordingly.
- Regional development investment in universities and research institutes outside Auckland should account for knowledge spillover benefits that may justify location in secondary cities.

**Relevant institutions**
MBIE, MoE (Tertiary Education Commission), Auckland Council, Immigration NZ, Universities NZ.

---
## claim-0058: Doubling distance-weighted population of nearby cities within 300km raises productivity 1–2%; port cities 2–4% higher

**Statement**
Doubling the distance-weighted population of other cities within 300km is associated with 1–2% higher city productivity; port cities show an additional 2–4% productivity premium.

**Meaning (plain English)**
Cities surrounded by other cities within a few hundred kilometres are more productive — a kind of inter-city agglomeration benefit. Cities with ports are additionally more productive, reflecting their trade access and specialisation advantages.

**Why it matters**
For NZ, this finding matters because Auckland, Wellington, Christchurch, and Hamilton form a partial urban system with inter-city spillovers. Auckland's port status also represents a productivity advantage. But NZ's geographic isolation from other major city systems means it captures less of the 300km inter-city spillover benefit than equivalent-sized European or US cities.

**Mechanism**
Cities within commuting and supply-chain range of each other share labour markets, knowledge networks, and business relationships that magnify agglomeration beyond what any single city could generate alone. Port cities access international supply chains and trade-exposed productivity norms that raise the performance bar for all firms.

**Evidence**
Ahrend et al. (2015) across five OECD countries. Evidence strength is quasi-experimental. Capital city status showed no independent premium after controlling for size and industry mix.

**Dependencies**
None listed in the relationships block (related claims per text: 0029 and 0035).

**Policy implications**
- Transport investment linking Auckland to Hamilton and Tauranga (within 200km) should be evaluated for inter-city agglomeration benefits, not just within-city travel time savings.
- Auckland's port is a genuine productivity asset and its long-run location and capacity decisions should account for the port productivity premium.
- Wellington's proximity to Palmerston North and Lower Hutt creates inter-city spillover opportunities that regional transport investment can strengthen.
- NZ's geographic isolation from Australia and the rest of the world reduces its inter-city spillover benefit relative to European cities — this structural disadvantage should be acknowledged in NZ productivity policy.
- Digital connectivity infrastructure (ultra-fast broadband, international data cables) may partially substitute for geographic inter-city proximity in generating knowledge spillovers.

**Relevant institutions**
Te Waihanga, NZTA, Ministry of Transport, Auckland Council, Ports of Auckland, MBIE.

---
## claim-0059: NZ GDP per capita fell from 110% of OECD average in 1970 to ~79% by 2010, driven by lagging labour productivity

**Statement**
New Zealand's GDP per capita fell from approximately 110% of the OECD average in 1970 to ~79% by 2010 — a decline driven entirely by lagging labour productivity, not by lower labour utilisation.

**Meaning (plain English)**
New Zealand used to be one of the richest countries in the OECD — wealthier than average. Over forty years it fell to about 80% of the OECD average, making it a middle-of-the-pack wealthy country rather than a leader. This happened because NZ workers produce less per hour than workers in peer countries — not because they work fewer hours, but because each hour produces less.

**Why it matters**
This long-run productivity decline is the central challenge of NZ economic policy. Urban policy — making cities more productive through better planning, transport, and governance — is one of the most direct levers available to address the labour productivity gap that drove NZ's relative decline.

**Mechanism**
Labour productivity (output per hour worked) reflects the quality of capital, technology, management practices, and the economic environment in which workers operate. NZ's productivity gap reflects underinvestment in physical capital, limited technology adoption, weak competition in some sectors, and geographic isolation — all of which interact with urban policy through agglomeration effects.

**Evidence**
Conway and Meehan (2013) NZ Productivity Commission Research Paper, using OECD comparative data. Evidence strength is descriptive. Labour utilisation has been relatively high in NZ — the gap is entirely in output per hour.

**Dependencies**
None listed in the relationships block (related claims per text: 0062, 0063, and 0016).

**Policy implications**
- Auckland's productivity premium (claim-0015, claim-0016) means that policies improving Auckland's economic functioning are the most direct domestic lever on NZ's OECD productivity standing.
- NZ's productivity gap is a four-decade trend requiring sustained, long-horizon policy commitment — not short-term electoral-cycle interventions.
- Urban agglomeration policies (densification, transport, governance) should be explicitly framed as components of NZ's productivity improvement programme.
- MBIE and Treasury should maintain regular monitoring of NZ's OECD productivity position as a primary national performance indicator.
- The gap between NZ and top-quartile OECD countries in labour productivity provides an estimate of the welfare gains available from sustained productivity improvement.

**Relevant institutions**
MBIE, Treasury, NZ Productivity Commission, Stats NZ, Auckland Council.

---
## claim-0060: NZ labour productivity growth slowed from 2.6% p.a. in the 1990s to 0.5% in 2008–2011; driven by MFP decline

**Statement**
New Zealand's labour productivity growth decelerated from 2.6% p.a. in the 1990s to 1.3% in the 2000s to 0.5% in 2008–2011; the slowdown was driven by declining MFP growth, not by reduced capital deepening.

**Meaning (plain English)**
New Zealand's productivity growth has been slowing sharply over three decades. In the 1990s, post-reform productivity surged; by the late 2000s it had nearly stalled. The problem is not a lack of investment in equipment or buildings (capital deepening has been relatively stable) but a failure to generate innovation, technology diffusion, and organisational improvement (multi-factor productivity).

**Why it matters**
The MFP collapse — not capital shortfall — is the primary mechanism behind NZ's productivity slowdown. This points to systemic failures in innovation, technology adoption, management practices, and competitive pressure rather than a simple investment gap. Urban agglomeration policy matters because cities are the primary environments where MFP growth occurs through knowledge spillovers and competitive learning.

**Mechanism**
Multi-factor productivity (MFP) captures gains from better technology, management, and organisation — the residual after accounting for labour and capital inputs. NZ's 1990s surge likely reflected one-off gains from market reforms. The subsequent decline reflects exhaustion of those gains combined with weak technology diffusion and a global productivity slowdown post-GFC.

**Evidence**
Conway and Meehan (2013) NZ Productivity Commission Research Paper, using Statistics NZ national accounts. Evidence strength is descriptive. MFP was 1.9% p.a. in the 1990s, falling to approximately 0.2% in the 2000s.

**Dependencies**
None listed in the relationships block (related claims per text: 0059 and 0064).

**Policy implications**
- NZ's productivity strategy should focus on MFP drivers — innovation, technology adoption, management quality, competitive intensity — rather than primarily on capital investment.
- Urban agglomeration policies that enable knowledge spillovers (densification, university-firm linkages, innovation precincts) directly address the MFP gap.
- Auckland's role as NZ's primary innovation hub makes its productivity performance directly relevant to the national MFP trajectory.
- MBIE's R&D tax credit, Callaghan Innovation, and university-industry linkage programmes should be evaluated against their MFP contribution.
- NZ Productivity Commission should prioritise MFP-focused research and policy advice, as the data confirms this is where the gap lies.

**Relevant institutions**
NZ Productivity Commission, MBIE, Treasury, Stats NZ, Callaghan Innovation, Auckland Council.

---

## claim-0061

**Statement:** There is no industry-level convergence between New Zealand and Australia post-1997: only 3 of 10 industries where NZ lagged Australia had narrowed the gap by 2010; the overall NZ–Australia labour productivity gap of 38% in 2009 is 58% attributable to lower MFP and 39% to lower capital intensity.

**Meaning:** New Zealand has not been catching up to Australia's productivity levels in most industries. The gap between the two countries comes mainly from New Zealand firms producing less output per unit of combined inputs (MFP), and from New Zealand workers having less capital equipment to work with.

**Why it matters:** The NZ–Australia gap is a key benchmark for NZ economic policy because Australia is a direct comparator with similar institutions and geography. Failure to converge indicates structural barriers rather than a temporary shortfall, requiring targeted policy rather than a wait-and-see approach. For New Zealand Treasury and MBIE, understanding whether the gap is primarily about capital or efficiency guides the choice of policy levers.

**Dependencies:**
- claim-0059: NZ GDP per capita declined from above to below the OECD average over a multi-decade period (file not in this compilation range — see Part 1).
- claim-0064: New Zealand's capital stock per hour worked is roughly half that of comparator countries including Australia, consistent with the 39% capital-intensity attribution.

**Mechanism:** Lower multi-factor productivity means New Zealand firms get less output from the same bundle of labour, capital, and technology — likely reflecting weaker management practices, slower technology adoption, and less efficient resource allocation. Lower capital intensity compounds this because workers without adequate equipment are less productive regardless of their effort.

**Evidence:** Conway and Meehan (2013) using industry-level data across 10 sectors comparing NZ and Australia, 1997–2010. The MFP/capital decomposition is from Mason (2013). This is a descriptive analysis, not a causal identification study.

**Policy implications:**
- Target capital investment incentives at sectors with the largest capital-intensity gaps relative to Australian peers.
- Investigate firm-level management quality and technology adoption rates to address the MFP gap component.
- Monitor industry-level convergence as a scorecard for structural reform progress.
- Avoid assuming the gap will close automatically through market forces given 13 years of non-convergence evidence.

**Relevant institutions:** MBIE, Treasury, NZ Productivity Commission, Statistics NZ

---

## claim-0062

**Statement:** New Zealand's GDP per hour worked has been below the OECD average for over 50 years; the relative decline began with the 1973 oil shock and continued to the mid-2000s, when NZ's position stabilised at well below OECD peers.

**Meaning:** New Zealand workers have been producing less output per hour than workers in comparable rich countries for more than half a century. The decline was not a recent phenomenon but a persistent structural condition that began in the 1970s and has not been reversed.

**Why it matters:** The longevity and depth of NZ's productivity underperformance rules out cyclical explanations and points to deep structural factors in the economy. For policymakers, this history establishes that incremental adjustments are unlikely to close the gap without significant structural reform.

**Dependencies:**
- claim-0059: NZ GDP per capita declined relative to OECD average from 1970 onward (not in this compilation range — see Part 1).
- claim-0063: NZ's economic growth has come from working more hours rather than higher hourly productivity, compounding this deficit.
- claim-0064: New Zealand's capital stock per hour worked is roughly half that of comparator countries, a structural constraint on productivity.

**Mechanism:** The 1973 oil shock disrupted NZ's commodity-export-dependent economy at a time of high import protection and limited market competition. The subsequent trade liberalisation of the 1980s–1990s improved efficiency but not enough to close the gap, while capital accumulation remained weak relative to peers.

**Evidence:** NZ Productivity Commission (2023) using OECD national accounts data for early OECD members (those joining before 1975), tracking GDP per hour worked from 1970 to 2022. This is a descriptive statistical analysis.

**Policy implications:**
- Treat productivity as a long-term structural priority rather than a short-term cyclical concern.
- Design reforms with 10–20 year horizons given the persistence of the underperformance.
- Benchmark NZ policy settings against countries that have successfully closed comparable productivity gaps.
- Resist complacency when the gap appears to stabilise — stabilisation at a low level is not the same as convergence.

**Relevant institutions:** Treasury, MBIE, NZ Productivity Commission, Statistics NZ

---

## claim-0063

**Statement:** New Zealand's output and income growth since the 1980s has come primarily from working more hours (labour utilisation), not from higher hourly productivity — New Zealanders work longer hours than most OECD peers while producing less output per hour.

**Meaning:** NZ has grown its economy largely by employing more people and working longer hours, rather than by becoming more efficient. This is the less sustainable growth path: it has a natural ceiling as there are limits to how many hours people can work, whereas productivity growth is theoretically unlimited.

**Why it matters:** Growth driven by labour utilisation rather than productivity is unsustainable as the population ages and labour force growth slows. For NZ policy, this means the demographic slowdown (see claim-0091–0095) will expose the structural productivity deficit more acutely, making productivity reform more urgent, not less.

**Dependencies:**
- claim-0059: NZ GDP per capita declined relative to OECD average, driven by productivity rather than utilisation (not in this compilation range — see Part 1).
- claim-0064: New Zealand's capital stock per hour worked is roughly half that of comparator countries, a structural constraint preventing the shift to intensive growth.

**Mechanism:** High employment rates and stable or rising average hours worked have masked the underlying productivity shortfall by adding labour volume rather than value per unit of labour. This creates an illusion of growth without the efficiency gains that would make it sustainable.

**Evidence:** NZ Productivity Commission (2023) drawing on OECD national accounts data, comparing NZ with early OECD member countries since the 1980s. This is descriptive analysis using growth accounting decomposition.

**Policy implications:**
- Prioritise productivity-enhancing investment (capital, technology, skills) over labour force expansion alone.
- Monitor the distinction between GDP growth from utilisation versus productivity in macroeconomic reporting.
- Ensure immigration policy is accompanied by capital and infrastructure investment to avoid perpetuating the utilisation-led growth trap.
- Frame demographic change as an opportunity to shift the growth model, not just a threat to workforce numbers.

**Relevant institutions:** Treasury, MBIE, NZ Productivity Commission, Statistics NZ, MSD

---

## claim-0064

**Statement:** New Zealand's capital stock per hour worked is roughly half that of Australia, Sweden, and the US, and the gap has been widening — Denmark deployed 81% more capital per hour than NZ in 1970, rising to 164% more by 2019; South Korea went from 15% of NZ's level to 50% above it over the same period.

**Meaning:** New Zealand workers have significantly less machinery, equipment, and infrastructure to work with compared with workers in peer countries. This capital deficit has been getting worse over time relative to both rich countries and fast-growing economies, suggesting NZ is not investing enough in productive assets.

**Why it matters:** Capital per worker is a direct input to labour productivity. If NZ workers have half the capital of their Australian or American counterparts, they will naturally produce less per hour regardless of their skill or effort. This makes capital investment a necessary (though not sufficient) condition for closing the productivity gap.

**Dependencies:**
- claim-0061: 39% of the NZ–Australia labour productivity gap is attributable to lower capital intensity.
- claim-0063: NZ's extensive growth pattern (more hours) rather than intensive growth (more capital per hour) is confirmed by this capital stock deficit.

**Mechanism:** Low investment rates direct savings into non-productive assets (particularly housing) rather than plant, equipment, and software. High capital costs, low savings rates, and a small domestic market all reduce the incentive to invest. The result compounds over time as each year of underinvestment widens the stock gap.

**Evidence:** NZ Productivity Commission (2023) using Penn World Tables data, tracking real capital stock per hour worked from 1970 to 2019 across OECD comparators. This is descriptive analysis.

**Policy implications:**
- Review tax settings affecting the return to productive capital investment relative to housing investment.
- Promote FDI as a channel to import capital alongside technology (see claim-0084).
- Set explicit targets for capital-to-labour ratio growth as part of a productivity strategy.
- Consider whether KiwiSaver allocation rules channel retirement savings toward productive investment.

**Relevant institutions:** Treasury, MBIE, NZ Productivity Commission, Statistics NZ

---

## claim-0065

**Statement:** Auckland's GDP per capita is only 13% above the national average, compared with the 25–35% typical for comparable primate cities in small-to-medium economies — suggesting significant unrealised agglomeration potential.

**Meaning:** Auckland's advantage over the rest of New Zealand is much smaller than would be expected for a city of its dominance and size. Cities of Auckland's relative scale in similar countries typically produce much larger economic premiums, suggesting Auckland is not fully capturing the productivity benefits that come from urban density and agglomeration.

**Why it matters:** The gap between Auckland's observed premium (13%) and what comparable primate cities achieve (25–35%) represents potentially forgone GDP at the national level. Policies that reduce urban frictions — such as housing supply reform, transport investment, and densification — could unlock this unrealised agglomeration potential.

**Dependencies:**
- claim-0015: Auckland workers are 11.1% more productive than the rest of NZ (not in this compilation range — see Part 1).
- claim-0016: Auckland's share of NZ GDP versus workforce share (not in this compilation range — see Part 1).
- claim-0051: NZ firm-level agglomeration elasticities confirm a density-productivity link (not in this compilation range — see Part 1).

**Mechanism:** Agglomeration economies — thicker labour markets, knowledge spillovers, and specialised supplier networks — are supposed to raise productivity in larger, denser cities. If Auckland's premium is smaller than expected, urban frictions (congestion, land use constraints, misallocation) are likely suppressing these gains.

**Evidence:** Auckland Council Chief Economist (Blick and Stewart, 2025, Auckland Economic Quarterly Q1–Q2), drawing on GDP per capita comparisons for primate cities. The 25–35% comparator figure is based on 2021–22 peer data. This is descriptive benchmarking.

**Policy implications:**
- Prioritise land use reforms and transport investment to reduce Auckland's urban frictions.
- Use the gap between 13% and 25–35% as a long-term target for agglomeration policy.
- Commission further analysis of which specific frictions are binding — congestion, housing costs, or land use restrictions.
- Monitor Auckland's GDP per capita premium as a performance indicator for urban policy.

**Relevant institutions:** Auckland Council, MBIE, Treasury, HUD/MHuD, NZTA, NZ Productivity Commission

---

## claim-0066

**Statement:** Restrictive land use in Auckland has contributed to rising house prices and is estimated to have reduced New Zealand's national GDP by 0.9–1.8% by preventing workers from living where their productivity would be highest.

**Meaning:** Auckland's planning rules that restricted housing supply have not only made houses more expensive; they have also made the whole New Zealand economy smaller by stopping workers from moving to where they would be most productive. The loss is estimated at roughly 1–2% of all of NZ's economic output.

**Why it matters:** A GDP loss of 0.9–1.8% from a single city's planning rules is a substantial policy failure. For NZ, where aggregate productivity is already below the OECD average, this represents a tractable source of economic gain — one that can be addressed through planning reform rather than requiring long-term changes to skills or technology.

**Dependencies:**
- claim-0050: Exclusionary zoning causes squatter settlements and wage suppression in developing cities (not in this compilation range — see Part 1).
- claim-0067: Auckland congestion adds a further productivity drag of approximately $0.7 billion per year on top of land use constraints.

**Mechanism:** When planning rules prevent housing supply growth, workers who could be more productive in Auckland cannot afford to move there. This is spatial misallocation — the economy fails to put workers in their highest-productivity locations. The resulting output loss accumulates across all the workers who are displaced by high housing costs.

**Evidence:** Nunns (2019) economic modelling study, using a spatial general equilibrium framework. This is a model-based estimate; the 0.9–1.8% range reflects different modelling assumptions. The estimate predates the Auckland Unitary Plan reforms.

**Policy implications:**
- Continue and extend intensification reforms that increase housing supply near employment centres.
- Evaluate post-Unitary Plan housing supply outcomes against the potential GDP recovery identified by Nunns.
- Ensure regional infrastructure investment accompanies upzoning to enable the productivity gains.
- Use GDP impact estimates to justify funding for planning reform implementation.

**Relevant institutions:** Auckland Council, HUD/MHuD, MBIE, Treasury, NZ Productivity Commission, NZTA

---

## claim-0067

**Statement:** Auckland congestion costs approximately $0.7 billion per year (~0.5% of Auckland's GDP); dispersed urban growth compounds this by lengthening commutes, raising infrastructure costs, and forgoing the productivity benefits of compact urban form.

**Meaning:** Traffic congestion in Auckland directly costs the city economy close to a billion dollars annually in wasted time and vehicle costs. On top of this, the city's sprawling growth pattern makes things worse by pushing homes and businesses further apart, increasing the cost of servicing them and reducing the economic benefits of proximity.

**Why it matters:** Transport and land use are deeply connected policy areas. Investing in roads alone without addressing land use will not solve congestion (see claim-0111), while upzoning without transport investment creates density without accessibility. Auckland Council and NZTA need to coordinate both levers.

**Dependencies:**
- claim-0017: Auckland road congestion costs approximately NZ$1.3 billion per year nationally (not in this compilation range — see Part 1).
- claim-0199: Doubling urban density reduces unit cost of local public services by approximately 17% (not in this compilation range — see Part 1).
- claim-0066: Restrictive land use in Auckland has already reduced NZ GDP by 0.9–1.8%, and congestion is an additional cost on top of this.

**Mechanism:** Congestion raises travel time and reduces journey reliability, making it harder for firms and workers to interact face-to-face. Dispersed growth extends average journey distances. Together, these reduce the density of economic interaction that agglomeration theory predicts raises productivity.
