**Evidence:** Auckland Council Chief Economist (Blick and Stewart, 2025) citing EY (2025) for the $0.7 billion congestion cost figure. This is a model-based estimate of direct time and vehicle costs; it excludes broader productivity losses from unreliable travel.

**Policy implications:**
- Prioritise integrated land use and transport planning to enable compact growth near existing infrastructure.
- Investigate congestion pricing to reduce the direct cost of congestion and fund public transport alternatives.
- Evaluate urban growth boundary decisions against the additional congestion and infrastructure costs of dispersal.
- Fund public transport improvements that enable densification without proportional road network expansion.

**Relevant institutions:** Auckland Council, NZTA, HUD/MHuD, NZ Infrastructure Commission (Te Waihanga), Treasury

---

## claim-0068

**Statement:** Auckland firms have labour productivity 17.9% higher than firms in other New Zealand urban areas; this falls to 13.5% once industry composition is adjusted for — meaning composition explains roughly 4.4 percentage points of the raw premium, but a substantial within-industry urban effect remains.

**Meaning:** Auckland firms are meaningfully more productive than firms in other NZ cities even when you account for the fact that Auckland has more high-productivity industries like finance. The remaining 13.5% gap reflects genuine urban advantages — thicker labour markets, knowledge spillovers, and agglomeration — not just industry mix.

**Why it matters:** This confirms that Auckland's productivity advantage is real and attributable to urban agglomeration, not just to the types of businesses that happen to locate there. Policies that help Auckland function better as a city (housing supply, transport, density) will therefore have measurable productivity returns.

**Dependencies:**
- claim-0071: The primary source record of these figures from Maré (2016) directly.
- claim-0072: Auckland MFP is 7.9% (unadjusted) or 2.2% (quality-adjusted) above other urban areas, isolating pure agglomeration from labour sorting.
- claim-0065: Auckland's overall GDP per capita premium of approximately 13% above the national average is consistent with these firm-level findings.
- claim-0051: NZ firm-level density elasticity confirms the agglomeration mechanism (not in this compilation range — see Part 1).

**Mechanism:** Industry composition accounts for about a quarter of Auckland's raw productivity premium (Auckland has more high-productivity sectors). The remaining three-quarters reflect genuine agglomeration effects — benefits of proximity, deeper labour markets, and faster knowledge diffusion that lift productivity within each industry.

**Evidence:** Maré (2016, Motu Working Paper 16-21) using New Zealand Longitudinal Business Database, comparing firm-level labour productivity across urban areas with controls for industry composition, firm heterogeneity, and labour quality. This is a rigorous empirical study.

**Policy implications:**
- Use the composition-adjusted premium (13.5%) as a conservative minimum estimate for the productivity value of expanding Auckland.
- Design housing and transport policy to reduce barriers to firms and workers accessing Auckland's agglomeration premium.
- Investigate why Auckland's premium falls short of international comparators, as identified in claim-0065 and claim-0086.
- Encourage second-tier cities to develop sectoral specialisations that could generate their own agglomeration benefits.

**Relevant institutions:** MBIE, Auckland Council, NZ Productivity Commission, Treasury, Statistics NZ

---

## claim-0070

**Statement:** High land prices and property values do not directly absorb or reduce GDP in the national accounts — land price changes are wealth transfers, not subtractions from output. The real mechanism by which housing and land-use constraints reduce productivity is misallocation: workers and firms cannot sort into high-productivity locations, reducing matching quality, agglomeration, and effective capital allocation.

**Meaning:** It is a misconception to think that high house prices directly eat into economic output. What actually reduces productivity is that expensive housing stops workers from living where they would be most productive. The harm is invisible in the GDP accounts but real in terms of forgone output from suboptimal worker placement.

**Why it matters:** Getting the mechanism right matters for policy design. Policies targeting house prices as a wealth issue may differ from policies targeting housing supply as a productivity and access issue. For NZ, the productivity framing suggests that housing supply reform should be evaluated partly on its contribution to worker mobility and firm access to talent.

**Dependencies:**
- claim-0066: Restrictive Auckland land use reduced NZ GDP by 0.9–1.8% through spatial misallocation — this clarifies the channel through which that loss occurs.
- claim-0067: Auckland congestion approximately $0.7 billion per year adds another dimension of spatial friction on top of land use constraints.
- claim-0049: China hukou restrictions demonstrate the costs of preventing workers from accessing urban opportunities — being undersized in productive cities costs more than being oversized (not in this compilation range — see Part 1).

**Mechanism:** Housing and land scarcity in high-productivity locations forces workers to live in lower-productivity areas or accept longer commutes, reducing their matching quality with employers and limiting the density of economic interaction. Firms also misallocate capital when they cannot co-locate with the workers and suppliers they need.

**Evidence:** Ministry of Housing and Urban Development (2024) synthesis of misallocation economics research. This is an analytical rather than empirical claim.

**Policy implications:**
- Evaluate housing reform primarily through a labour mobility and spatial allocation lens, not just a housing affordability lens.
- Use spatial wage differentials (wages in Auckland vs. other cities) as a proxy for the degree of misallocation.
- Design infrastructure and upzoning reforms jointly so that increased housing supply near productive employment is matched by transport access.
- Communicate to policymakers that house price stabilisation alone, without supply-side reform, does not address the underlying productivity mechanism.

**Relevant institutions:** HUD/MHuD, Treasury, MBIE, Auckland Council, NZ Productivity Commission, NZTA

---

## claim-0071

**Statement:** Auckland firms have labour productivity 17.9% higher than firms in other New Zealand urban areas. Industry composition explains approximately a quarter of this gap; within industries, the Auckland premium is 13.5%.

**Meaning:** Auckland businesses generate substantially more output per worker than businesses in other NZ cities. Even after accounting for the types of industries concentrated in Auckland, the city retains a meaningful productivity edge that reflects genuine agglomeration benefits.

**Why it matters:** This is the core empirical evidence that Auckland's economic concentration delivers real productivity benefits. For policy purposes, it justifies investing in Auckland's urban function while also explaining why the city's housing and transport constraints have national-level productivity costs.

**Dependencies:**
- claim-0068: A synthesis record incorporating these same figures from Maré (2016) in the context of other agglomeration evidence.
- claim-0072: Auckland MFP is 7.9% (unadjusted) or 2.2% (quality-adjusted) above other urban areas, which further decomposes the productivity premium by stripping out firm and worker quality sorting.

**Mechanism:** The within-industry premium reflects agglomeration economies — benefits of proximity, thicker matching markets, and knowledge spillovers — that operate independently of sector composition. The composition-adjusted gap is the cleanest measure of the urban environment's contribution to productivity.

**Evidence:** Maré (2016, Motu Working Paper 16-21) using the New Zealand Longitudinal Business Database (LBDB), comparing labour productivity (output per worker-hour) across Auckland firms and non-Auckland urban firms, with industry composition controls. This is a rigorous empirical study using administrative business data.

**Policy implications:**
- Use the 13.5% composition-adjusted premium as a lower bound for the economic value of Auckland's urban function.
- Target policies reducing Auckland's frictions (housing supply, transport) using this evidence of real agglomeration returns.
- Investigate why the premium remains well below international comparator cities.
- Consider whether policy reforms could help second-tier cities develop their own within-industry premiums.

**Relevant institutions:** MBIE, Auckland Council, NZ Productivity Commission, Statistics NZ, Treasury

---

## claim-0072

**Statement:** Auckland firms are 7.9% more productive on total factor productivity (TFP/MFP) than comparable firms in other New Zealand urban areas in the preferred specification; this falls to 2.2% once labour quality differences are controlled for.

**Meaning:** When you strip out both industry composition and the fact that more skilled workers tend to cluster in Auckland, the city still has a genuine productivity advantage of 2.2%. The larger unadjusted figure (7.9%) partly reflects Auckland attracting better workers rather than the city itself making workers more productive.

**Why it matters:** The distinction between 7.9% and 2.2% matters for policy because it separates two levers: (1) enabling more high-skilled workers to access Auckland (sorting channel) and (2) making the city environment itself more productivity-enhancing (agglomeration channel). Both matter, but they call for different interventions.

**Dependencies:**
- claim-0071: The raw labour productivity gap (17.9%) and composition-adjusted gap (13.5%) from which this MFP analysis proceeds.
- claim-0073: Agglomeration elasticities derived from this methodology — a 10% increase in effective city size raises MFP by 0.79% or 0.22% depending on specification.
- claim-0074: Wellington's MFP is counterintuitively 3.8% above Auckland, reflecting its concentration in high-value government and professional services.

**Mechanism:** The gap between 7.9% and 2.2% (approximately 5.7 percentage points) reflects worker quality sorting — highly skilled workers disproportionately cluster in Auckland, raising average productivity. The residual 2.2% is attributable to pure agglomeration: the urban environment itself generates productivity gains through proximity, knowledge spillovers, and thick markets.

**Evidence:** Maré (2016, Motu Working Paper 16-21) using the LBDB with multiple specifications including labour quality proxies. The preferred quality-adjusted specification is the most conservative and most methodologically defensible estimate.

**Policy implications:**
- Target housing reforms near major employment centres to help high-skilled workers access Auckland's agglomeration premium.
- Use the 2.2% pure agglomeration estimate as a conservative lower bound for urban policy evaluation.
- Investigate what specific features of the Auckland urban environment generate the residual premium.
- Design skills and immigration policy to improve labour quality distribution across NZ cities, not just to increase Auckland's skilled worker concentration.

**Relevant institutions:** MBIE, Auckland Council, NZ Productivity Commission, Statistics NZ, Treasury, Immigration NZ

---

## claim-0073

**Statement:** New Zealand's urban agglomeration elasticity is 0.079 (unadjusted for labour quality) or 0.022 (quality-adjusted), implying a 10% increase in effective city size raises firm MFP by 0.79% or 0.22% respectively.

**Meaning:** Doubling the effective size of a New Zealand city would raise firms' total factor productivity by roughly 1.5% to 5.5% depending on the method used. This is the quantitative link between city scale and economic efficiency in New Zealand.

**Why it matters:** Knowing the agglomeration elasticity allows policymakers to estimate the productivity gains from policies that increase effective city size — such as upzoning, transport investment, or infrastructure enabling densification. Both estimates being positive confirms the case for urban scale policies.

**Dependencies:**
- claim-0072: The Auckland MFP premiums from which these elasticities are derived.
- claim-0076: Auckland's agglomeration elasticity of approximately 0.04 per MHuD (2024), citing a different study (Donovan et al. 2022), sits between Maré's two bounds.
- claim-0001: Donovan et al. (2021) global meta-analytic range of approximately 0.04–0.05, which brackets Maré's quality-adjusted estimate (not in this compilation range — see Part 1).

**Mechanism:** Larger effective city size produces thicker labour markets (better worker-firm matching), more knowledge spillovers (ideas spread faster when more people are proximate), and deeper input-output networks (specialised suppliers become viable). These jointly raise the productivity of each firm within the city.

**Evidence:** Maré (2016, Motu Working Paper 16-21) using New Zealand Longitudinal Business Database with cross-city variation in effective employment density. This is a rigorous empirical study within a well-established methodological tradition.

**Policy implications:**
- Apply the elasticity range to evaluate specific urban policy investments: for example, a transport project that increases effective Auckland labour market size by 10% would yield 0.22–0.79% MFP gains.
- Use the mid-range estimate (consistent with international evidence) as the central case for cost-benefit analysis of urban intensification.
- Monitor whether agglomeration elasticities are changing over time as Auckland's spatial structure evolves.
- Ensure that second-tier city development policy uses city-specific elasticity estimates rather than assuming Auckland figures apply.

**Relevant institutions:** MBIE, NZ Productivity Commission, Auckland Council, Treasury, HUD/MHuD

---

## claim-0074

**Statement:** Wellington's labour productivity is 4.2% below Auckland on raw measures but Wellington's MFP is 3.8% above Auckland (unadjusted) and 2.7% above (quality-adjusted), reflecting its concentration in government and high-value professional services.

**Meaning:** Wellington appears less productive than Auckland by simple output-per-worker measures, but when you look more carefully at total factor productivity, Wellington's firms are actually more efficient. This is because Wellington is concentrated in high-value but labour-intensive sectors (government, law, finance) that generate a lot of value per unit of all inputs.

**Why it matters:** This finding shows that urban productivity comparisons depend heavily on measurement methodology and sectoral mix. For NZ policy, it suggests that Wellington's economic function is complementary to Auckland's rather than inferior, and that place-based policy should account for how cities specialise rather than simply ranking them by raw productivity.

**Dependencies:**
- claim-0072: The Auckland MFP premium and quality-adjustment methodology provide the comparison point.
- claim-0073: NZ agglomeration elasticities — Wellington's higher MFP reflects sectoral composition, not that Wellington generates higher agglomeration returns per unit of scale.

**Mechanism:** Wellington's concentration in public administration, professional services, and finance generates high output per combined unit of inputs (labour, capital) within those sectors. Labour productivity is lower than Auckland partly because Wellington has fewer workers in high-output-per-worker manufacturing and logistics sectors, and partly because finance and insurance sectors show the largest Auckland premium in labour productivity terms.

**Evidence:** Maré (2016, Motu Working Paper 16-21) using LBDB data with MFP specifications that control for industry, firm size, and labour quality. This is a rigorous empirical study.

**Policy implications:**
- Avoid using crude labour productivity rankings to dismiss Wellington's economic contribution.
- Develop Wellington's capacity as a specialised hub for government innovation, professional services, and public sector efficiency.
- Use city-specific productivity metrics when evaluating place-based investments rather than applying a single national benchmark.
- Consider whether Wellington's public sector concentration provides spillovers (contracting, procurement, policy expertise) that benefit broader NZ productivity.

**Relevant institutions:** MBIE, Treasury, NZ Productivity Commission, Statistics NZ

---

## claim-0075

**Statement:** Removing all housing supply constraints in New Zealand would raise output per worker by 1.1–1.6%, expand the labour force by 3.3–7.2%, and increase GDP by 5.0–8.4%, according to Nunns (2021).

**Meaning:** If New Zealand eliminated all planning and regulatory barriers to housing construction across all cities, the total economic gain could be 5–8% of GDP — a very large number for a single policy reform. The gains come from workers being able to live closer to productive jobs, from a larger labour force retained in NZ, and from more efficient capital allocation.

**Why it matters:** A 5–8% GDP gain from housing supply reform would be one of the largest single policy dividends available to NZ policymakers. Even achieving a fraction of this through partial reforms (as the Auckland Unitary Plan and Medium Density Residential Standards represent) justifies continued investment in planning reform.

**Dependencies:**
- claim-0066: Auckland-specific land use constraints already cost approximately 0.9–1.8% of NZ GDP; this national estimate is larger because it covers all NZ cities and all constraint types.
- claim-0070: Misallocation is the channel through which housing constraints reduce productivity — this provides the theoretical foundation for the estimate.
- claim-0076: Auckland's agglomeration elasticity and size premium underpin the spatial reallocation component of the GDP estimate.

**Mechanism:** Removing housing supply constraints allows workers to relocate to higher-productivity locations, raises the labour force by reducing emigration and increasing immigration, and redirects savings from land speculation into productive capital. Each channel contributes to the estimated GDP gain.

**Evidence:** Nunns (2021) using a national general equilibrium model applied to New Zealand, covering all cities and all constraint types. This is a full-reform scenario — a theoretical maximum rather than a realistic near-term target. Evidence strength is model-based.

**Policy implications:**
- Use the 5–8% range as a long-run target for housing supply reform, recognising that partial reforms will realise a fraction of this.
- Evaluate specific reforms (NPS-UD, MDRS, infrastructure investment) against their expected contribution to the overall gain.
- Reform housing supply in all major NZ cities, not just Auckland, to capture the full national gain.
- Monitor labour force size and emigration rates as leading indicators of whether housing reforms are retaining workers in NZ.

**Relevant institutions:** HUD/MHuD, MBIE, Treasury, Auckland Council, NZ Productivity Commission, NZ Infrastructure Commission (Te Waihanga)

---

## claim-0076

**Statement:** Auckland's agglomeration elasticity is approximately 0.04 — identical to other major New Zealand cities — and Auckland is estimated to be 5–8% more productive than Hamilton, Tauranga, Wellington, and Christchurch due to size alone.

**Meaning:** A 10% increase in Auckland's effective size raises firm productivity by about 0.4% — the same rate as in smaller NZ cities. Auckland's productivity advantage over other NZ cities is driven by its larger scale, not because Auckland operates by different agglomeration rules.

**Why it matters:** Finding the same agglomeration elasticity across all NZ cities has important policy implications: it means scale matters everywhere in NZ, not just in Auckland. Growing secondary cities could generate agglomeration benefits comparable to growing Auckland, with potentially lower infrastructure and congestion costs.

**Dependencies:**
- claim-0073: Maré (2016) estimated NZ agglomeration elasticity at 0.022 (quality-adjusted) to 0.079 (unadjusted); the 0.04 figure from Donovan et al. (2022) sits in the middle.
- claim-0001: Donovan et al. (2021) global meta-analytic range of approximately 0.04–0.05 (not in this compilation range — see Part 1).
- claim-0077: Sydney and Melbourne being three times Auckland's size could explain about one-third of the NZ–Australia productivity gap, based on this elasticity.

**Mechanism:** Agglomeration works through the same channels in all NZ cities — thicker labour markets, knowledge spillovers, and specialised suppliers. Auckland's larger productivity advantage is simply the arithmetic result of being larger, not a qualitatively different urban economy.

**Evidence:** MHuD (2024) citing Donovan et al. (2022) New Zealand-specific agglomeration study. The 5–8% Auckland advantage is calculated by applying the 0.04 elasticity to the population size ratio between Auckland and peer NZ cities.

**Policy implications:**
- Invest in secondary city development (Hamilton, Tauranga, Christchurch) as a complementary strategy to Auckland intensification.
- Apply agglomeration benefit calculations to infrastructure investment decisions in all major NZ cities.
- Consider whether targeted sector clustering in secondary cities could achieve agglomeration benefits that offset their smaller scale.
- Use the 0.04 elasticity as the central case for NZ-specific cost-benefit analysis of urban investments.

**Relevant institutions:** MBIE, HUD/MHuD, Treasury, NZ Infrastructure Commission (Te Waihanga), Auckland Council, NZ Productivity Commission

---

## claim-0077

**Statement:** Sydney and Melbourne are approximately three times the size of Auckland; applying New Zealand's agglomeration elasticity, this size gap alone could explain approximately one-third of the 20% productivity gap between New Zealand and Australia.

**Meaning:** A major reason Australia is more productive than New Zealand may simply be that its biggest cities are much larger than Auckland, generating stronger agglomeration benefits. If NZ cities were the size of Sydney or Melbourne, the productivity gap would narrow significantly, though two-thirds of the gap would still require other explanations.

**Why it matters:** This is an important framing for NZ policy: some of the NZ–Australia gap reflects structural geography rather than policy failure, but some is within NZ's control through urban growth enablement. It also indicates that if NZ prevents Auckland from growing, it is choosing to perpetuate a portion of its productivity gap with Australia.

**Dependencies:**
- claim-0076: Auckland's agglomeration elasticity of approximately 0.04 is the key parameter applied in this back-of-envelope calculation.
- claim-0068: Auckland's productivity premium over other NZ cities shows that agglomeration effects are real in NZ.
- claim-0075: Removing NZ housing constraints could raise GDP by 5.0–8.4%, a range broadly consistent with closing this agglomeration portion of the gap.

**Mechanism:** At an elasticity of 0.04, a city three times the size would generate approximately ln(3) × 0.04 ≈ 4.4% higher MFP. Applied across the urban economy, this generates the estimated one-third share of the productivity gap.

**Evidence:** MHuD (2024) back-of-envelope calculation applying Donovan et al. (2022) elasticity to the Auckland–Sydney/Melbourne population ratio. This is an illustrative calculation, not a causal identification study.

**Policy implications:**
- Frame Auckland growth enablement as a national competitiveness issue, not just a city planning matter.
- Communicate to national policymakers that housing and planning constraints in Auckland have explicit implications for NZ's gap with Australia.
- Investigate what share of the remaining two-thirds of the gap is addressable through capital investment, R&D, and skills policy.
- Use this analysis in bilateral economic cooperation discussions with Australia.

**Relevant institutions:** Treasury, MBIE, HUD/MHuD, NZ Productivity Commission, Auckland Council

---

## claim-0078

**Statement:** New Zealand's housing stock is valued at approximately $1.6 trillion — approximately four times GDP — compared with approximately two times GDP in the 1990s, reflecting a doubling of housing wealth relative to economic output over three decades.

**Meaning:** New Zealand has tied up an enormous and growing share of its national wealth in housing. Homes that were once worth two years of national output are now worth four years — meaning household savings and investment have flowed into land and property rather than into businesses and productive equipment.

**Why it matters:** This concentration of wealth in housing represents a capital misallocation problem at national scale. Every dollar invested in land appreciation is a dollar not invested in machinery, technology, or business development. This directly compounds NZ's capital intensity deficit (see claim-0064).

**Dependencies:**
- claim-0070: High land prices do not directly absorb GDP in the national accounts; misallocation is the channel through which this wealth concentration harms productivity.
- claim-0075: Removing housing supply constraints would recover GDP gains of 5.0–8.4% by reorienting the economy toward productive capital.
- claim-0066: Restrictive Auckland land use has contributed to the housing stock overvaluation by constraining supply and inflating land prices.

**Mechanism:** Restrictions on housing supply inflate land values, making housing a more attractive investment relative to productive assets. This redirects household savings and business investment toward land, reducing the capital available for productivity-enhancing investment in plant, equipment, and technology.

**Evidence:** MHuD (2024) using New Zealand national balance sheet data, comparing the housing stock to GDP ratio across time periods. The $1.6 trillion figure is a stock-level estimate from national accounts.

**Policy implications:**
- Address housing supply constraints to reduce the incentive to hold housing purely as an investment asset.
- Review tax settings that preference housing investment over productive capital (e.g., absence of capital gains tax on housing).
- Monitor the housing stock to GDP ratio as an indicator of capital misallocation.
- Design retirement savings policy (KiwiSaver) to direct household savings toward productive investment rather than solely into housing markets.

**Relevant institutions:** Treasury, HUD/MHuD, MBIE, NZ Productivity Commission, Statistics NZ

---

## claim-0079

**Statement:** New Zealand's capital stock per worker is approximately 50% of OECD comparator levels — a structural deficit that directly suppresses labour productivity relative to peers.

**Meaning:** New Zealand workers have about half the equipment and infrastructure backing them compared with workers in comparable rich countries. This is not a temporary gap — it is a structural feature of the NZ economy that persistently reduces how much each worker can produce.

**Why it matters:** Capital per worker is the most direct non-human input to labour productivity. A 50% deficit means NZ workers are working harder with less, rather than being enabled by technology and infrastructure to produce more. Closing this gap would require sustained higher investment rates over many years.

**Dependencies:**
- claim-0064: NZ capital stock per hour worked is roughly half that of Australia, Sweden, and the US — directly consistent with this 50% figure and providing the international detail.
- claim-0061: 39% of the NZ–Australia labour productivity gap is attributable to lower capital intensity, confirming capital as a binding constraint.

**Mechanism:** Low investment rates over many decades have compounded into a stock-level deficit. Each year of underinvestment relative to peers widens the gap further because comparator countries continue investing while NZ's base remains low. Low capital per worker reduces the output each worker can generate per hour.

**Evidence:** Winter et al. (2025, Treasury Analytical Note AN 25/12) drawing on Penn World Tables data, comparing NZ capital stock per worker to a peer group of small advanced OECD economies. This is observational analysis using established international data.

**Policy implications:**
- Set explicit investment-to-GDP ratio targets benchmarked against comparator OECD economies.
- Review all tax and regulatory settings that reduce the after-tax return to productive capital investment.
- Promote FDI specifically for capital-intensive industries to address the stock deficit more rapidly.
- Complement capital investment policy with technology adoption support to ensure new capital embeds modern technology.

**Relevant institutions:** Treasury, MBIE, NZ Productivity Commission, Statistics NZ

---

## claim-0080

**Statement:** New Zealand's share of firms using fully up-to-date technology declined from 51% (2007) to 44% (2022), indicating that the technology diffusion problem is worsening rather than improving.

**Meaning:** Fewer New Zealand businesses are operating with modern technology now than fifteen years ago. Rather than technology adoption improving over time, it has gone backwards — suggesting that whatever is causing NZ firms to lag in technology uptake is getting worse, not better.

**Why it matters:** Technology currency is a key determinant of productivity growth. If the majority of NZ firms are using outdated technology, the gap between NZ and technology-frontier economies will widen even if NZ's absolute performance improves. This makes technology diffusion a priority policy area, not just a long-run background condition.

**Dependencies:**
- claim-0079: NZ capital stock per worker approximately 50% of OECD comparators — capital and technology are linked because new capital investment embeds newer technology vintages.
- claim-0082: NZ R&D underspend (1.5% of GDP vs 2.7% OECD average) creates a headwind for technology frontier access.
- claim-0083: Capital investment and technology adoption are complementary — declining capital investment explains part of the declining technology currency rate.

**Mechanism:** Technology currency declines when firms fail to replace ageing capital or adopt new software and processes. This can result from low capital investment rates, small domestic markets that reduce competitive pressure to adopt, or weak management capability in evaluating and implementing technology.

**Evidence:** Winter et al. (2025, Treasury Analytical Note AN 25/12) citing Business Operations Survey data for 2007, 2012, 2017, and 2022. The measure is self-reported technology currency; methodology details are limited in the Analytical Note.

**Policy implications:**
- Design a technology adoption programme targeting the approximately 56% of firms not using fully up-to-date technology.
- Use industry extension services (analogous to Manufacturing Extension Partnership programmes in the US) to help SMEs adopt technology.
- Link capital investment incentives to technology currency requirements to ensure new capital embeds modern vintages.
- Monitor the technology currency share as a KPI for innovation and productivity policy.

**Relevant institutions:** MBIE, Treasury, NZ Productivity Commission

---

## claim-0081

**Statement:** Approximately 95% of New Zealand firms innovate by adopting or adapting technologies developed elsewhere; only approximately 5% create new-to-world products or processes — making diffusion, not invention, the dominant innovation pathway for the economy.

**Meaning:** Almost all innovation in NZ consists of copying or adapting ideas from elsewhere. Only one in twenty firms creates something genuinely new. This means that improving NZ's ability to find, adopt, and implement technologies developed overseas is far more important to aggregate productivity than increasing spending on original research.

**Why it matters:** This 95/5 split fundamentally shapes what NZ's innovation policy should focus on. Policies targeting R&D tax credits or frontier research are relevant for the 5%, but the overwhelming policy priority for aggregate productivity is improving the channels through which overseas technology reaches and gets adopted by NZ firms.

**Dependencies:**
- claim-0079: NZ capital stock deficit — new capital is the primary vehicle for embedding adopted technology.
- claim-0080: Technology currency is declining — adoption is not working well enough.
- claim-0082: NZ R&D underspend — even the frontier-facing 5% of firms are constrained by low R&D investment.

**Mechanism:** As a small, open economy far from global technology centres, NZ firms rationally specialise in adoption rather than invention. The problem is not the specialisation itself but the barriers to effective adoption — import costs, infrastructure quality, management capability, and FDI levels all affect how quickly and effectively overseas technology reaches NZ firms.

**Evidence:** Winter et al. (2025, Treasury Analytical Note AN 25/12) citing Business Operations Survey (2023). The 95/5 split is approximate and based on self-reported innovation activity classifications.

**Policy implications:**
- Rebalance innovation policy toward technology adoption support rather than frontier R&D subsidies.
- Invest in management capability programmes that help firms identify, evaluate, and implement new technologies.
- Reduce import barriers on technology-embodying capital goods and intermediate inputs.
- Use FDI attraction strategy to bring technology-frontier firms whose practices can diffuse into the domestic economy.

**Relevant institutions:** MBIE, Treasury, NZ Productivity Commission

---

## claim-0082

**Statement:** New Zealand's gross domestic expenditure on R&D is approximately 1.5% of GDP, against an OECD average of approximately 2.7%; business R&D is roughly 1% of GDP; and New Zealand conducts only 0.02% of total OECD R&D.

**Meaning:** New Zealand spends much less on research and development than comparable rich countries — roughly half the OECD average. NZ's contribution to global R&D is tiny (0.02%), meaning the country relies almost entirely on importing technology rather than contributing to its creation.

**Why it matters:** While technology adoption matters more than invention for NZ's aggregate productivity (see claim-0081), very low R&D spending means NZ has limited ability to develop absorptive capacity — the knowledge base needed to effectively identify and adopt overseas technology. It also limits NZ's ability to attract high-value R&D-performing FDI.

**Dependencies:**
- claim-0081: 95% of NZ firms are technology adopters rather than creators — R&D underspend is consistent with and partly explains this concentration.
- claim-0079: NZ capital stock deficit — low business R&D and low capital investment are correlated symptoms of the same investment climate problem.
- claim-0064: NZ productivity gap in international context — R&D underspend is one structural contributor.

**Mechanism:** Low business R&D spending reflects a combination of small firm size (which reduces R&D returns), limited access to external finance for R&D (which is high-risk), distance from global knowledge networks, and an industry composition weighted toward primary production and services that have lower R&D intensities.

**Evidence:** Winter et al. (2025, Treasury Analytical Note AN 25/12) using OECD R&D statistics for 2024. Business R&D (BERD) is approximately NZ$4.3 billion and total GERD approximately NZ$6.4 billion.

**Policy implications:**
- Strengthen R&D tax incentives (the current Callaghan Innovation scheme) to raise business R&D intensity toward OECD average.
- Focus public research investment on sectors where NZ can credibly build comparative advantage (agri-tech, biotech, marine science).
- Use university-industry collaboration programmes to raise the return on public R&D investment.
- Attract R&D-intensive multinationals through FDI policy to supplement domestic R&D spending.

**Relevant institutions:** MBIE, Treasury, NZ Productivity Commission

---

## claim-0083

**Statement:** Capital investment and technology adoption are complementary, not separable: when a firm invests in new plant, equipment, or software, it simultaneously raises its capital-to-labour ratio and embeds newer technology vintages — treating them as separate policy silos misses this joint mechanism.

**Meaning:** Buying new machinery or software is both a capital investment and a technology upgrade at the same time. Policy that treats capital investment and innovation as separate issues misses this — the best way to help NZ firms adopt new technology is often simply to help them invest in new capital.

**Why it matters:** This insight has direct implications for NZ policy design. If investment and technology adoption are treated as separate programmes, the combined effect will be less than if they are designed together. It also means that raising NZ's capital intensity (see claim-0079) would simultaneously improve its technology currency (see claim-0080).

**Dependencies:**
- claim-0079: NZ capital stock per worker approximately 50% of OECD comparators — this deficit suppresses both capital intensity and technology currency simultaneously.
- claim-0080: Technology currency declining from 51% to 44% of firms — addressed by raising capital investment rates.
- claim-0084: NZ below OECD median on technology diffusion channels including import intensity and infrastructure quality.

**Mechanism:** New capital purchases typically embody more recent technology vintages than old capital (vintage capital theory). A firm that invests in new equipment necessarily upgrades its technology; a firm that does not invest is left with older, less efficient technology regardless of any innovation programme.

**Evidence:** Winter et al. (2025, Treasury Analytical Note AN 25/12) presenting this as an analytical observation drawing on established economic theory (Haskel and Westlake 2022 is cited). This is a conceptual rather than directly empirical claim.

**Policy implications:**
- Design investment incentives and innovation support as an integrated programme, not separate schemes.
- Evaluate the technology-vintage improvement as a co-benefit when assessing capital investment incentives.
- Use capital allowances or accelerated depreciation that specifically reward replacement of old equipment with newer vintages.
- Ensure Callaghan Innovation and MBIE investment support programmes are coordinated rather than operating in separate silos.

**Relevant institutions:** MBIE, Treasury, NZ Productivity Commission

---

## claim-0084

**Statement:** New Zealand sits below the OECD median on three of four key technology diffusion channels: import intensity of manufactures, foreign direct investment stock, and infrastructure quality — limiting the pathways through which frontier technology reaches domestic firms.

**Meaning:** The main ways technology spreads across countries — through imports of goods, through foreign direct investment, and through good infrastructure — are all weaker in NZ than in most comparable countries. This means NZ firms have fewer opportunities to observe, copy, and implement global best practice.

**Why it matters:** Technology diffusion channels are the arteries through which modern economies absorb the benefits of global R&D without having to invent everything themselves. NZ's weakness across three of four channels indicates a systemic vulnerability, not just a minor shortfall in one area. Addressing this requires coordinated action on trade, investment, and infrastructure policy.

**Dependencies:**
- claim-0083: Capital investment and technology adoption are complementary — the import intensity deficit reduces both capital upgrading and technology access simultaneously.
- claim-0079: NZ capital stock deficit — low FDI and import intensity reduce the flow of new capital with embedded technology.
- claim-0065: Auckland infrastructure and connectivity — infrastructure quality deficits are both a diffusion barrier and an agglomeration constraint.

**Mechanism:** Manufactured imports expose domestic industries to higher-quality products that embody advanced technology, creating adoption pressure and learning opportunities. FDI brings management practices, processes, and technology directly into domestic firms. High-quality infrastructure reduces the cost of adoption by enabling rapid distribution and connectivity.

**Evidence:** Winter et al. (2025, Treasury Analytical Note AN 25/12) using OECD comparative data across member economies. The Australian Productivity Commission (2023) estimated technology diffusion revival could lift Australian GDP by 1.6–4.9%.

**Policy implications:**
- Negotiate and implement trade agreements that specifically improve NZ's access to technology-embodying imports.
- Develop an active FDI attraction strategy targeting technology-intensive sectors and R&D-performing multinationals.
- Prioritise infrastructure investment (broadband, logistics, transport) as a technology diffusion enabler, not just a productivity input.
- Monitor NZ's performance on all four diffusion channels annually as a composite indicator for technology policy.

**Relevant institutions:** MBIE, Treasury, NZTA, NZ Infrastructure Commission (Te Waihanga), NZ Productivity Commission

---

## claim-0085

**Statement:** Auckland ranks 99th globally and last among its ten international peer cities on productivity, as measured by the State of the City 2025 benchmarking report.

**Meaning:** Among a group of cities selected as Auckland's international peers — including Austin, Brisbane, Copenhagen, Dublin, Helsinki, Portland, Tel Aviv, and Vancouver — Auckland has the lowest productivity score. Globally, it ranks 99th in this metric.

**Why it matters:** Peer city benchmarking reveals that Auckland's productivity underperformance is not a NZ-only problem but a failure relative to comparable international cities. This framing is useful for Auckland policymakers because it identifies specific cities whose strategies Auckland can study and adapt.

**Dependencies:**
- claim-0086: Auckland's productivity premium over the rest of NZ (approximately 15%) is substantially lower than the 30–40% premium that peer capital cities enjoy over their national averages.
- claim-0016: Auckland's economic concentration as NZ's dominant city makes this ranking a national economic concern, not just a city one (not in this compilation range — see Part 1).
- claim-0065: Auckland Economic Quarterly findings on Auckland's 13% GDP per capita premium provide consistent evidence.

**Mechanism:** Auckland's low productivity rank reflects the compounding of multiple constraints: inefficient land use limiting agglomeration, low capital investment, low FDI, and an industry mix with lower value-added intensity than peer cities such as Dublin, Helsinki, and Tel Aviv.

**Evidence:** Committee for Auckland et al. (2025, State of the City 2025) using Resonance Consultancy World's Best Cities methodology, which uses GDP per capita and labour productivity proxies. Ten peer cities were selected for broadly similar size, economic profile, or strategic function. This is observational benchmarking, not a direct productivity measure.

**Policy implications:**
- Use peer city benchmarking as a governance accountability mechanism for Auckland Council and national government.
- Study the urbaneconomic strategies of Dublin, Helsinki, and Tel Aviv — cities of similar size that achieve substantially higher productivity premiums.
- Commission analysis of which specific factors (FDI, housing density, transport, R&D) explain the gap between Auckland and its highest-performing peers.
- Set a target for Auckland to move out of the bottom quartile among peer cities within a defined timeframe.

**Relevant institutions:** Auckland Council, MBIE, Treasury, HUD/MHuD, NZ Productivity Commission

---

## claim-0086

**Statement:** Auckland's productivity premium over the rest of New Zealand is only approximately 15%, substantially lower than the 30–40% premium that comparable peer capital cities such as Dublin, Helsinki, and Tel Aviv enjoy over their national averages.

**Meaning:** Auckland generates less of an economic boost over the rest of New Zealand than similar-sized dominant cities do elsewhere. Dublin, Helsinki, and Tel Aviv each contribute far more to their country's economic lift than Auckland does to NZ's, suggesting Auckland is significantly under-performing its potential.

**Why it matters:** The gap between Auckland's 15% premium and the 30–40% typical for peer capital cities represents a large amount of forgone national economic output. This is the clearest evidence that Auckland's urban policy settings are constraining economic performance, and provides a clear aspirational target for reform.

**Dependencies:**
- claim-0085: Auckland ranks last among its ten peer cities on productivity overall.
- claim-0090: Auckland's prosperity pillar score has declined sharply in recent years, consistent with worsening relative performance.

**Mechanism:** A smaller-than-expected productivity premium for a dominant primate city indicates that urban frictions are suppressing agglomeration returns. Housing unaffordability reduces worker mobility, congestion reduces the effective density of economic interaction, and low FDI limits technology transfer.

**Evidence:** Committee for Auckland et al. (2025, State of the City 2025) benchmarking analysis comparing Auckland's GDP-per-worker premium over NZ national average with peer capital city premiums. Specific methodology for the ~15% figure is not fully detailed.

**Policy implications:**
- Set a policy objective to close the gap between Auckland's current 15% premium and the 25–40% range achieved by peer capital cities.
- Prioritise housing supply, transport, and FDI attraction as the primary levers to raise Auckland's productivity premium.
- Report annually on Auckland's productivity premium as a headline metric for urban economic performance.
- Use Dublin, Helsinki, and Tel Aviv as case study models for what policies have driven their higher premiums.

**Relevant institutions:** Auckland Council, MBIE, Treasury, HUD/MHuD, NZ Productivity Commission, NZ Infrastructure Commission (Te Waihanga)

---

## claim-0087

**Statement:** Auckland's city centre generates NZ$33 billion in GDP — approximately 21% of Auckland's total output — and has been growing approximately 2 percentage points per year faster than the rest of New Zealand.

**Meaning:** The inner-city core of Auckland is the most productive part of NZ's most productive city, and it has been pulling ahead of the national average at a rapid pace. This cluster of high-value economic activity has grown from 16% to 21% of Auckland's total output since 2000.

**Why it matters:** The city centre's outperformance provides concrete evidence that density and agglomeration work in NZ. Policies that strengthen Auckland's city centre — including transport access, housing supply nearby, and high-density zoning — are backed by observable economic returns.

**Dependencies:**
- claim-0085: Auckland's overall productivity rank is low, but the city centre is a relative bright spot within this picture.
- claim-0016: Auckland's total share of NZ GDP (~38%) context within which the city centre's 21% of that total operates (not in this compilation range — see Part 1).
- claim-0065: Auckland Economic Quarterly findings on Auckland's aggregate productivity premium.

**Mechanism:** Higher density in the city centre enables thicker labour markets (more workers accessible within short travel times), faster knowledge spillovers (more face-to-face interaction), and deeper specialisation (enough demand to support niche service providers). These agglomeration returns increase productivity in proportion to the density of economic interaction.

**Evidence:** Committee for Auckland et al. (2025, State of the City 2025) citing GDP by area data. The shift from approximately 16% (2000) to approximately 21% of Auckland's output represents observable, measurable growth. This is observational analysis.

**Policy implications:**
- Protect and extend city centre intensification through zoning that allows continued high-density development.
- Invest in city centre transport access (rail, rapid transit, cycling) to increase the effective labour market catchment.
- Encourage mixed-use development in and around the city centre to sustain the density of economic interaction.
- Use city centre GDP performance as a model for what more intensive urban development could achieve in other parts of Auckland.

**Relevant institutions:** Auckland Council, NZTA, HUD/MHuD, NZ Infrastructure Commission (Te Waihanga)

---

## claim-0088

**Statement:** Auckland's housing delivery now ranks in the top third globally for new homes completed annually per capita, exceeding the peer-city average by approximately 60%, following the 2016 Unitary Plan and subsequent intensification reforms.

**Meaning:** Auckland has dramatically improved how many new homes it builds each year relative to its population, going from a poor performer to a global leader. This follows planning reforms that allowed more housing in more places, and represents a significant policy achievement.

**Why it matters:** Auckland's housing delivery improvement demonstrates that planning reform can work. However, high completion rates alone have not yet made Auckland affordable — the stock-level deficit from decades of underbuilding still weighs on prices. This finding motivates both celebrating the reform progress and maintaining supply-side pressure.

**Dependencies:**
- claim-0019: Auckland liveability benchmarking from earlier periods showing historical underperformance (not in this compilation range — see Part 1).
- claim-0070: Housing constraints and productivity misallocation — supply improvements begin to address this through better worker mobility.

**Mechanism:** The 2016 Auckland Unitary Plan removed many single-dwelling-only zoning restrictions and allowed medium density as of right across much of the city. This reduced the regulatory cost of housing supply, allowing market signals (high prices) to more effectively call forth new construction.

**Evidence:** Committee for Auckland et al. (2025, State of the City 2025) citing international housing delivery benchmarks. Among ten peer cities, only Austin delivers more new homes per capita. Demographia 2025 data shows Auckland improved from 11th to 16th least affordable among English-speaking cities. This is observational benchmarking.

**Policy implications:**
- Maintain and extend the Unitary Plan's intensification provisions and subsequent Medium Density Residential Standards.
- Ensure infrastructure investment (water, wastewater, transport) keeps pace with housing construction to avoid bottlenecks.
- Monitor housing affordability alongside construction rates to determine whether supply-side progress is translating into affordability gains.
- Share Auckland's post-Unitary Plan experience as a model for other NZ cities and internationally.

**Relevant institutions:** Auckland Council, HUD/MHuD, NZTA, NZ Infrastructure Commission (Te Waihanga)

---

## claim-0089

**Statement:** Auckland's annual share of new business attributable to foreign direct investment is more than 80% below the OECD peer average — a severe underperformance that limits technology transfer and management practice diffusion.

**Meaning:** Auckland attracts a tiny fraction of the foreign business investment that comparable cities receive. This matters because foreign firms typically bring advanced technology and management practices that then spread to local businesses, so Auckland's FDI deficit limits the city's ability to absorb global best practice.

**Why it matters:** FDI is one of the most effective channels for technology diffusion in medium-sized open economies. Auckland's 80% shortfall relative to peers means the city is missing one of its primary routes to productivity improvement. Closing this gap would not only bring direct investment but also the technology and management knowledge that FDI carries.

**Dependencies:**
- claim-0084: NZ is below OECD median on FDI stock nationally — Auckland's city-level data is consistent with and confirms the national picture.
- claim-0085: Auckland's overall productivity rank last among peer cities — FDI underperformance is one of the identified structural causes.
- claim-0090: Identified structural causes of Auckland's prosperity decline include low capital investment and below-average management capability, both of which FDI addresses.

**Mechanism:** FDI brings new firms with higher average productivity, technology, and management practices into the local economy. These diffuse to domestic firms through supply chains, labour mobility (workers who move from foreign to domestic firms), and competitive pressure to adopt better practices.

**Evidence:** Committee for Auckland et al. (2025, State of the City 2025) citing OECD FDI data. The "more than 80% below" is a relative comparison to the OECD peer average for the annual share of new business attributable to FDI. This is observational benchmarking.

**Policy implications:**
- Develop an active FDI attraction strategy for Auckland specifically targeting technology-intensive sectors.
- Reduce regulatory and compliance barriers for foreign businesses establishing in Auckland.
- Position Auckland as an Asia-Pacific hub to leverage its geographic position for FDI from growing Asian economies.
- Track Auckland's FDI share annually and set a target to close the 80% gap to the OECD peer average.

**Relevant institutions:** MBIE, Auckland Council, Immigration NZ, Treasury

---

## claim-0090

**Statement:** Auckland's prosperity pillar score dropped from the 6th decile (2023) to the 4th decile (2024 and 2025) — the largest single-year decline of any pillar across all three editions of the State of the City report.

**Meaning:** Auckland's economic performance relative to peer cities dropped sharply in 2024 and has not recovered in 2025. Of all the dimensions tracked in the benchmarking report, this was the biggest decline — signalling a deterioration in the city's economic competitiveness rather than a temporary fluctuation.

**Why it matters:** A drop of two deciles in a single year, without recovery, indicates structural rather than cyclical deterioration. The identified causes — inefficient land use, small domestic market, low capital investment, low export intensity, and management capability gaps — are all medium-to-long-term structural issues, not ones that will self-correct.

**Dependencies:**
- claim-0085: Auckland's overall productivity rank last among peer cities provides the broader context for this decline.
- claim-0086: Auckland's modest productivity premium over rest of NZ (approximately 15%) shows that the underperformance pre-dates the 2024 drop.
- claim-0079: NZ capital stock deficit at approximately 50% of OECD comparators is one of the identified structural causes.

**Mechanism:** The prosperity decline reflects the combined effect of: relative stagnation in Auckland's productivity growth while peer cities accelerated; the impact of post-COVID cost inflation on business profitability; low FDI; and the ongoing drag of housing unaffordability on labour force participation and worker mobility.

**Evidence:** Committee for Auckland et al. (2025, State of the City 2025) using Resonance Consultancy World's Best Cities methodology tracking Auckland's Prosperity decile score across 2023, 2024, and 2025 editions. This is observational comparative benchmarking.

**Policy implications:**
- Treat Auckland's prosperity decline as a national economic emergency requiring coordinated response across housing, transport, FDI, and investment settings.
- Identify which of the five identified structural causes (land use, market size, capital, exports, management) is most binding and prioritise that lever.
- Set a recovery target: returning Auckland to the 6th decile within five years as a minimum.
- Commission detailed analysis comparing Auckland's trajectory to peer cities that have improved their prosperity rankings.

**Relevant institutions:** Auckland Council, MBIE, Treasury, HUD/MHuD, NZ Productivity Commission, NZ Infrastructure Commission (Te Waihanga)

---

## claim-0091

**Statement:** New Zealand's total fertility rate reached 1.55 in 2025 — well below the replacement rate of 2.1 — having declined rapidly since approximately 2014, driven by increased female workforce participation, housing costs, and inadequate parental leave settings.

**Meaning:** New Zealand women are having far fewer children than the number needed to maintain the population without immigration. The decline has been rapid and is driven by real economic pressures — the cost of housing and the inadequacy of paid parental leave both make it harder to have and raise children.

**Why it matters:** A fertility rate of 1.55 means New Zealand's domestic-born population will eventually shrink without immigration. This creates growing dependency on migrants to maintain the workforce, with implications for housing, infrastructure, and social cohesion. It also signals policy failures in housing affordability and family support that have multi-generational consequences.

**Dependencies:**
- claim-0092: NZ population growth slowed from 1.7% to 0.7% in one year — fertility decline is a key driver.
- claim-0093: BusinessNZ projects a labour shortage of 250,000 by 2045, partly driven by low fertility reducing future workforce supply.
- claim-0095: The cost of NZ Superannuation is forecast to rise from 5.1% to 8% of GDP by 2065 — a declining fertility rate worsens this fiscal pressure.

**Mechanism:** Higher female education and workforce participation (60% in 1990 to 80% in 2024) raises the opportunity cost of having children and reduces time available for childcare. High housing costs (one-third of low-income households spending over 40% of income on housing) reduce household financial capacity to support children. Inadequate paid parental leave (maximum $780/week) makes the early years financially difficult.

**Evidence:** Lala, Spoonley, and Gluckman (2026, Koi Tū report) citing Statistics NZ fertility data through 2025. The TFR of 1.55 is the most recent available figure; the declining trend since 2014 is documented across multiple Statistics NZ publications.

**Policy implications:**
- Strengthen paid parental leave settings to reduce the economic penalty of having children, particularly for lower-income families.
- Use housing supply reform to reduce the housing cost burden that suppresses fertility.
- Evaluate targeted childcare subsidies as a lever for supporting both fertility and female workforce participation.
- Design immigration policy to compensate for fertility-driven workforce shortfalls without relying on this as the only lever.

**Relevant institutions:** MSD, Treasury, HUD/MHuD, Statistics NZ, Immigration NZ

---

## claim-0092

**Statement:** New Zealand's annual population growth slowed from 1.7% (June 2023–June 2024) to 0.7% (June 2024–June 2025); the population is forecast to grow from approximately 5.34 million (2025) to 6.53 million by 2048 on the Stats NZ 50th-percentile projection.

**Meaning:** New Zealand's population grew much more slowly in the year to June 2025 than in the previous year. Long-term projections suggest moderate growth continuing, but this depends heavily on immigration — the domestic natural increase from births over deaths is declining.

**Why it matters:** A sudden slowdown in population growth from 1.7% to 0.7% represents a major shift in the economic environment. For businesses, infrastructure planners, and housing developers, knowing the trajectory of population growth is essential. The regional divergence — metro growth driven by immigration, rural decline — creates place-specific planning challenges.

**Dependencies:**
- claim-0091: NZ fertility decline to 1.55 is a structural driver of the slowdown.
- claim-0093: Labour force shortage projection of 250,000 by 2045 is directly related to this population trajectory.
- claim-0094: Growing reliance on immigration to compensate for fertility-driven shortfalls in workforce supply.

**Mechanism:** Population growth slowed because net New Zealand citizen emigration increased sharply (approximately 44,000 net departures in 2024 — the largest in 25 years), and domestic natural increase declined as fertility fell. International immigration partially offsets these trends but is volatile and policy-dependent.

**Evidence:** Lala, Spoonley, and Gluckman (2026, Koi Tū report) citing Statistics NZ population data and Stats NZ (2025) 50th-percentile population projections. This is observational data with modelled projections.

**Policy implications:**
- Factor the population slowdown into infrastructure planning — building for 6.5 million by 2048 is very different from planning for faster growth.
- Develop regionally differentiated population strategies given the divergence between metro growth and rural decline.
- Use the population slowdown as an opportunity to improve per-capita infrastructure quality rather than just expanding capacity.
- Monitor the 50th-percentile projection assumptions regularly and adjust planning accordingly.

**Relevant institutions:** Statistics NZ, MBIE, Treasury, HUD/MHuD, NZ Infrastructure Commission (Te Waihanga), Auckland Council

---

## claim-0093

**Statement:** BusinessNZ projects a New Zealand labour force shortage of 250,000 people by 2045; the dependency ratio is forecast to rise from 54% (2025) to 73% by 2073 as the workforce ages.

**Meaning:** In roughly twenty years, NZ could be short of a quarter million workers relative to what the economy needs. Meanwhile, the proportion of people not in the workforce relative to those who are working will increase significantly, meaning fewer working-age people will need to support more dependents.

**Why it matters:** A labour shortage of this scale would constrain economic growth, public service delivery, and the tax base needed to fund superannuation and healthcare for an ageing population. Addressing it requires a combination of immigration strategy, workforce participation improvements, and productivity growth to do more with fewer workers.

**Dependencies:**
- claim-0091: NZ fertility decline to 1.55 reduces future workforce supply from domestic sources.
- claim-0094: Immigration is growing in importance but NZ's talent competitiveness ranking is declining.
- claim-0095: Superannuation fiscal pressure will intensify as the dependency ratio rises.

**Mechanism:** Ageing of the baby boom cohorts reduces the working-age share of the population while increasing the retiree share. Low fertility means smaller cohorts entering the workforce each year, not compensating for larger cohorts retiring. Without productivity growth or immigration, total workforce hours decline.

**Evidence:** Lala, Spoonley, and Gluckman (2026, Koi Tū report) citing BusinessNZ projection and Statistics NZ labour force participation forecasts (70.5% in 2021 projected to 63.22% in 2073). This is a modelled projection with significant uncertainty over long time horizons.

**Policy implications:**
- Develop a comprehensive population and workforce strategy covering fertility support, skilled immigration, and workforce participation for older workers and underutilised groups.
- Invest in productivity-enhancing capital and technology to reduce the labour requirement per unit of output.
- Raise workforce participation rates among currently underutilised groups (Māori, Pacific peoples, migrants, disabled people).
- Plan fiscal policy for the rising dependency ratio by building long-term savings buffers and reviewing superannuation settings.

**Relevant institutions:** Treasury, MSD, Immigration NZ, Statistics NZ, MBIE

---

## claim-0094

**Statement:** New Zealand fell from 11th to 18th in the Global Talent Competitiveness Index since 2015; in 2024, net emigration of New Zealand citizens reached approximately 44,000 — the largest net loss in 25 years — compounding the fertility-driven workforce contraction.

**Meaning:** New Zealand is both becoming less attractive to global talent and losing more of its own people overseas than at any point in a generation. These two trends together mean the strategy of using immigration to offset fertility decline is becoming harder to execute.

**Why it matters:** If NZ's ability to attract and retain talent is declining at the same time as domestic fertility falls, the projected labour shortage will be worse than headline projections suggest. For policy, this means addressing the conditions that cause New Zealanders to leave (housing costs, wages, career opportunities) is as important as attracting new migrants.

**Dependencies:**
- claim-0092: Population growth slowdown from 1.7% to 0.7% is driven partly by this net emigration surge.
- claim-0093: Labour shortage projection depends on immigration compensating for fertility decline — this claim shows immigration is not guaranteed.
- claim-0084: NZ below OECD median on FDI stock — low FDI reduces the career opportunities that would retain high-skill workers.

**Mechanism:** NZ citizens emigrate primarily to Australia and the UK where wages are higher and housing more affordable relative to income. The GTCI ranking decline across all six pillars suggests NZ's institutional environment, education quality, and talent retention policies have all deteriorated relative to peers. International graduates and medical workers (43% of NZ specialists are international medical graduates) fill critical gaps but are also mobile.

**Evidence:** Lala, Spoonley, and Gluckman (2026, Koi Tū report) citing Global Talent Competitiveness Index rankings and Statistics NZ migration data. GTCI decline is from 11th to 18th since 2015. Net citizen emigration of approximately 44,000 in 2024.

**Policy implications:**
- Address the root causes of citizen emigration: housing affordability, wage levels, and career opportunities.
- Develop a talent retention strategy alongside a talent attraction strategy.
- Streamline credential recognition for skilled immigrants to reduce the time lag before they contribute productively.
- Use the GTCI as a benchmark and set a target to return to the top 12 within ten years.

**Relevant institutions:** Immigration NZ, MBIE, Treasury, MSD, Statistics NZ

---

## claim-0095

**Statement:** The cost of New Zealand Superannuation is forecast to rise from 5.1% to 8% of GDP by 2065; the worker-to-retiree ratio is falling from 7 (1960s) to 4 (2025) to an anticipated 2 by 2065.

**Meaning:** The cost of supporting NZ's retirees will nearly double as a share of the economy by 2065. The number of working-age people available to fund each retiree has fallen dramatically and will continue falling, making the current superannuation model increasingly expensive to maintain.

**Why it matters:** Rising superannuation costs constrain fiscal space for productive investment in infrastructure, education, and health. The combination of an ageing population, low fertility, and workforce shrinkage makes this the defining long-run fiscal challenge for New Zealand. Addressing it requires either changing superannuation settings or generating enough productivity growth to expand the tax base.

**Dependencies:**
- claim-0093: Labour force shortage of 250,000 by 2045 directly worsens the worker-to-retiree ratio.
- claim-0091: NZ fertility decline to 1.55 is the structural driver of future workforce shortfalls that underlie this fiscal projection.

**Mechanism:** As the baby boom cohort retires and smaller cohorts follow, each retiree is supported by fewer workers. The government spends approximately five times as much per person over 65 as per person under 65. As the ratio of over-65 to working-age people rises, aggregate superannuation spending rises faster than GDP unless productivity growth compensates.

**Evidence:** Lala, Spoonley, and Gluckman (2026, Koi Tū report) citing Treasury (2025) Long-Term Fiscal Statement. The 5.1% to 8% of GDP projection assumes current policy settings are maintained. By 2048, 22% of NZ's population will be over 65. Several regions will exceed 30% aged 65+ by 2048.

**Policy implications:**
- Begin cross-party dialogue on superannuation settings (eligibility age, means testing, indexation) with a long lead time.
- Invest in productivity growth as the primary route to expanding the tax base without raising tax rates.
- Develop regional plans for hyper-ageing communities (Tasman, Marlborough, Nelson, West Coast) where over-65 shares will exceed 30%.
- Raise labour force participation among 65–74-year-olds as a partial offset to the declining worker-to-retiree ratio.

**Relevant institutions:** Treasury, MSD, Statistics NZ, MBIE

---

## claim-0096

**Statement:** Auckland will become a majority non-Pākehā city before the early 2040s: by 2043, the city is projected to be 42% Asian, 45% NZ European, 18% Pacific, and 13% Māori — a transformation that is faster and more pronounced than national trends.

**Meaning:** Auckland's population is shifting rapidly toward becoming a majority Asian and Pacific city, with NZ European Pākehā moving from the majority to a plurality. This is one of the fastest demographic transformations of a major city in the developed world.

**Why it matters:** Planning systems, housing typologies, service delivery models, and governance structures that were designed for one demographic profile will need to adapt to serve a fundamentally different population. Failure to adapt will generate equity gaps and reduce the economic contribution of Auckland's growing ethnic minority communities.

**Dependencies:**
- claim-0097: Auckland's ethnic minority communities contributed approximately NZ$50 billion to Auckland's GDP in 2023, confirming the economic importance of the incoming demographic majority.
- claim-0018: Transport poverty affects Māori and Pacific communities disproportionately — planning systems that do not adapt will generate worsening equity outcomes (not in this compilation range — see Part 1).
- claim-0092: Population growth and immigration — international immigration is the primary driver of Auckland's demographic transformation.

**Mechanism:** Auckland's growth is driven predominantly by net international immigration, and migrants are predominantly from Asia and the Pacific. Meanwhile, NZ European Pākehā have higher emigration rates. These two flows together produce the projected demographic shift.

**Evidence:** Lala, Spoonley, and Gluckman (2026, Koi Tū report) citing Stats NZ demographic projections. National trend: NZ European Pākehā 65% (2025) to 55% (2043); Asian 21% to 31%. These are 50th-percentile projections with uncertainty ranges.

**Policy implications:**
- Review Auckland's urban planning and housing typologies to ensure they accommodate diverse household structures and preferences.
- Invest in multilingual service delivery and culturally appropriate public services to maintain social cohesion through the transition.
- Ensure Māori and Pacific communities have voice in urban planning decisions, given their growing share of Auckland's population.
- Use Auckland's demographic transformation as a strategic opportunity to build an Asia-Pacific economic hub.

**Relevant institutions:** Auckland Council, Statistics NZ, MSD, HUD/MHuD, Immigration NZ

---

## claim-0097

**Statement:** Auckland's ethnic minority communities contributed approximately NZ$50 billion to Auckland's GDP in 2023; cultural diversity is positively associated with innovation at the regional level, including through cross-fertilisation of ideas and increased aggregate demand.

**Meaning:** The growing share of Auckland's population from Asian, Pacific, and Māori backgrounds already generates half the city's economic output. Cultural diversity also appears to directly stimulate innovation by bringing different perspectives and ideas into contact with each other.

**Why it matters:** This evidence challenges any narrative that demographic change is primarily a challenge for Auckland. The incoming demographic majority is already a massive economic contributor, and diversity itself generates innovation spillovers. Policy that enables ethnic minority communities to participate fully in the economy will raise Auckland's aggregate performance.

**Dependencies:**
- claim-0096: Auckland's projected demographic transformation — by 2043, the city will be majority non-Pākehā, amplifying this economic contribution further.
- claim-0018: Transport poverty affects Māori and Pacific communities disproportionately — removing these barriers would increase the economic contribution identified here (not in this compilation range — see Part 1).
- claim-0087: Auckland city centre economic performance — ethnic minority businesses contribute to this concentration of economic activity.

**Mechanism:** Diverse communities generate economic value through multiple channels: starting businesses, providing labour in sectors with shortages, increasing aggregate consumer demand, and creating cross-cultural knowledge spillovers. Ozgen, Nijkamp, and Poot (2011) find that innovation levels across 12 European countries are positively associated with the cultural diversity of migrant communities.

**Evidence:** Lala, Spoonley, and Gluckman (2026, Koi Tū report) citing Eaqub and Sohanpal (2026) for the NZ$50 billion GDP figure and Ozgen, Nijkamp, and Poot (2011) for the innovation relationship. The GDP figure is an estimate; the innovation relationship is a cross-country correlation, not a causal identification.

**Policy implications:**
- Remove barriers to ethnic minority labour market participation (credential recognition, discrimination, transport access) to increase their economic contribution.
- Leverage Auckland's growing diversity as a competitive advantage in attracting global businesses seeking culturally capable workforces.
- Invest in social cohesion programmes to ensure diversity benefits are realised rather than fragmented into parallel communities.
- Measure and report on ethnic minority economic contribution as a standard part of Auckland's economic monitoring.

**Relevant institutions:** Auckland Council, MBIE, MSD, Immigration NZ, Statistics NZ

---

## claim-0099

**Statement:** Across OECD countries, approximately 22% of workers are overqualified for their jobs, approximately 13% are underqualified, and approximately 40% work in occupations unrelated to their field of study — three distinct but overlapping forms of mismatch that together represent a pervasive feature of developed-economy labour markets.

**Meaning:** In rich countries, about one in five workers is doing a job that requires lower qualifications than they have, and four in ten are working in a field unrelated to what they studied. This is not a fringe problem — it is a normal feature of how modern labour markets allocate human capital, though one with real productivity costs.

**Why it matters:** Skills mismatch at this scale means economies are not getting full value from their investment in education and training. For NZ, where mismatch rates are among the highest in the OECD (see claim-0105) and where the economy needs to maximise productivity from a shrinking workforce, reducing mismatch is a significant policy lever.

**Dependencies:**
- claim-0100: Overqualified workers earn approximately 14% less than well-matched workers with the same qualifications — the individual wage consequence of mismatch.
- claim-0103: Macro-economic consequences of mismatch include increased unemployment and reduced GDP growth.
- claim-0105: NZ's above-average mismatch rates, consistent with its high foreign-born share.

**Mechanism:** Labour markets clear through wage and occupation adjustments rather than perfect matching. Workers accept jobs below their qualification level when alternatives are unavailable, mobility is costly, or credential recognition is imperfect. Field-of-study mismatch reflects the limited alignment between educational choices and labour demand.

**Evidence:** OECD (2016, Skills Matter) using Programme for the International Assessment of Adult Competencies (PIAAC) data across 28 countries. Qualification mismatch is measured by self-reported job requirements versus actual attainment; field-of-study mismatch uses ISCO-08 occupation-to-field mappings. This is large-scale cross-national observational data.

**Policy implications:**
- Improve real-time labour market information to help students and job-seekers make better-informed qualification and field choices.
- Strengthen employer engagement in curriculum design to reduce the mismatch between educational supply and labour demand.
- Develop credential recognition pathways that reduce the time overqualified immigrants spend in jobs below their attainment level.
- Monitor NZ's qualification mismatch rates as an indicator of labour market efficiency.

**Relevant institutions:** MBIE, MSD, Statistics NZ, Immigration NZ

---

## claim-0100

**Statement:** Overqualified workers earn approximately 14% less than well-matched workers with the same qualifications and proficiency; however, when compared with colleagues in the same job, overqualified workers earn approximately 4% more — reflecting their higher productivity — so the aggregate wage loss falls on the worker, not the firm, and the economy as a whole underperforms its human capital potential.

**Meaning:** Being overqualified costs workers about 14% in wages compared with what they would earn in a job matched to their qualifications. At the same time, firms actually benefit slightly from hiring overqualified workers (they get more productivity for their wage). The loss is borne by the worker and by society through underused human capital.

**Why it matters:** This finding shows that the cost of skills mismatch is not shared evenly — workers bear a significant wage penalty while firms benefit. For NZ policy, this means reducing mismatch is primarily a fairness and social investment issue, not something the market will fix on its own because firms have little incentive to improve matching.

**Dependencies:**
- claim-0099: Prevalence rates — approximately 22% of workers are overqualified, so this wage penalty affects a large share of the workforce.
- claim-0101: Skills proficiency has an independent wage return of 6% per standard deviation — mismatch reduces the return workers receive on their skills.
- claim-0103: Macro-economic consequences of mismatch — aggregate productivity is reduced when workers produce less than their human capital potential.
- claim-0105: NZ's above-average mismatch rates mean this 14% penalty falls on more NZ workers than the OECD average.

**Mechanism:** Overqualified workers are constrained by the job's wage band — they cannot fully capture the value of their qualifications in a role that does not require them. The 4% within-job premium shows they are more productive, but this is captured by the firm as a higher margin rather than converted into wages.
