**Evidence:** OECD (2016, Skills Matter) using PIAAC data, conditioning on qualifications, skills proficiency, age, gender, work experience, occupation, and field of study. The 14% penalty and 4% premium are OECD averages; country variation exists. The penalty exceeds 20% in Turkey and the US.

**Policy implications:**
- Prioritise credential recognition reforms to reduce the share of qualified migrants who accept jobs below their attainment.
- Design active labour market programmes that help overqualified workers access jobs better matched to their qualifications.
- Use the 14% wage penalty to estimate the aggregate wage loss from NZ's above-average mismatch rates as a policy target.
- Ensure immigration settlement support includes employment matching services that reduce overqualification duration.

**Relevant institutions:** Immigration NZ, MSD, MBIE, Statistics NZ

---

## claim-0101

**Statement:** A one standard deviation increase in literacy proficiency (approximately 48 score points on the PIAAC scale) is associated with a 6% increase in hourly wages on average across OECD countries, independently of educational attainment — confirming that skills have a direct economic return beyond the credential signal, and that proficiency and qualifications are separate components of human capital.

**Meaning:** Workers who are more literate and numerate earn more, even after accounting for how many years of school they completed. This means that improving actual skills — not just getting more qualifications — has real economic value. Skills development that raises proficiency levels without changing formal credentials still improves earnings and productivity.

**Why it matters:** For NZ, where a large share of the workforce has skills below what their qualifications might suggest (partly due to mismatch), this finding means that both credentials and actual skills proficiency are policy levers. Improving adult literacy and numeracy through workplace and community programmes generates measurable wage and productivity returns.

**Dependencies:**
- claim-0100: Wage effects of mismatch — proficiency returns are independent of the mismatch penalty; both operate simultaneously.
- claim-0102: Skills use at work correlates with national productivity — using skills in production, not just possessing them, drives returns.
- claim-0099: Mismatch prevalence rates — approximately 22% of workers are overqualified but their proficiency levels still generate a wage return within their jobs.

**Mechanism:** Higher literacy proficiency enables workers to process information faster, communicate more clearly, and learn new tasks more quickly. These capabilities raise individual productivity directly and raise wages through both labour market competition and efficiency wages. The mechanism operates independently of formal qualifications.

**Evidence:** OECD (2016, Skills Matter) using PIAAC proficiency scores across 28 countries, conditioning on educational attainment, age, gender, work experience, occupation, and field of study. The 6% wage return per standard deviation is an OECD average; returns are higher in England, Singapore, and the US.

**Policy implications:**
- Invest in adult literacy and numeracy programmes as a direct productivity and wage enhancement, not just a welfare intervention.
- Incorporate skills proficiency measurement (not just qualification attainment) into workforce monitoring.
- Design workplace learning programmes that raise proficiency levels in existing workers, complementing formal credentialing.
- Prioritise skills development for underutilised groups (Māori, Pacific peoples, migrants) where proficiency gains have the largest aggregate impact.

**Relevant institutions:** MSD, MBIE, Statistics NZ

---

## claim-0102

**Statement:** At country level, the intensity of reading skills use at work correlates strongly with GDP per hour worked, and this relationship persists after adjusting for literacy proficiency — evidence that the deployment of skills in production, not merely their possession, drives national labour productivity.

**Meaning:** Countries where workers use their reading and writing skills more intensively at work are more productive. This relationship holds even after accounting for how skilled workers actually are, suggesting that how skills are used in the workplace — not just whether workers have them — is what drives national productivity.

**Why it matters:** For NZ, this finding implies that possessing a qualified workforce is not enough if workplace conditions, job design, and technology do not demand and deploy those skills. Productivity policy must address both skill formation and the intensity of skills use in the economy, including through automation, job redesign, and management practices.

**Dependencies:**
- claim-0101: Skills proficiency has a direct wage return — skills use and proficiency are both important but distinct.
- claim-0103: Macro-economic consequences of mismatch — this claim provides the productivity mechanism linking skills use to aggregate output.
- claim-0105: NZ mismatch ranking — high mismatch means skills are not being deployed at the intensity required, reducing NZ's position on this cross-country skills-use curve.
- claim-0079: NZ capital stock deficit — low capital intensity reduces the demand for skilled work, suppressing skills use intensity.

**Mechanism:** Jobs that deploy high reading and writing intensity tend to be higher value-added, involve more complex problem-solving, and are associated with higher-productivity firms. Countries with a larger share of such jobs generate more output per hour. Skills deployment is endogenous to job design, technology, and management quality — not just a function of worker characteristics.

**Evidence:** OECD (2016, Skills Matter) using PIAAC reading skills use at work data and OECD national accounts GDP per hour worked data, across 28 countries. This is a cross-country correlation; reverse causality cannot be ruled out.

**Policy implications:**
- Invest in workplace innovation and job redesign programmes that raise skills use intensity across the economy.
- Use technology adoption policy to create more skill-intensive jobs that fully deploy NZ's human capital.
- Monitor skills use intensity at work as a leading indicator of productivity improvement.
- Include skills utilisation measures alongside skills attainment measures in workforce reporting.

**Relevant institutions:** MBIE, MSD, NZ Productivity Commission, Statistics NZ

---

## claim-0103

**Statement:** Skills mismatch at the macro-economic level increases unemployment and reduces GDP growth by wasting human capital and suppressing productivity — because workers deployed below their capacity contribute less to firm performance, are less likely to upgrade their skills, and represent a net loss on the economy's investment in education and training.

**Meaning:** When people work in jobs below their skill and qualification level, the whole economy loses out — not just the individual. These workers contribute less than they could to their employers' performance, their own skills tend to stagnate, and the public investment made in their education goes partially to waste.

**Why it matters:** This macro-level argument provides the economic case for treating skills mismatch as a national productivity issue, not just an individual employment problem. For NZ, where mismatch rates are among the highest in the OECD and the economy needs to maximise output from a shrinking workforce, reducing mismatch is equivalent to raising effective labour supply.

**Dependencies:**
- claim-0099: Prevalence rates — approximately 22% overqualified and 40% in unrelated fields across OECD.
- claim-0100: Wage effects of mismatch — the 14% wage penalty signals the individual loss; this claim frames the macro-level aggregate.
- claim-0102: Skills use and productivity — the mechanism linking mismatch to productivity is suppressed skills deployment.
- claim-0105: NZ's above-average mismatch rates mean the macro costs identified here are larger in NZ than in the typical OECD country.

**Mechanism:** Overqualified workers produce less than their human capital potential. They also invest less in further skill development because the returns in their current role are low. Employers who can cheaply access overqualified workers have less incentive to invest in job redesign or training. These reinforcing dynamics reduce aggregate productivity growth.

**Evidence:** OECD (2016, Skills Matter) structural argument drawing on PIAAC data and firm-level literature on turnover and productivity. The macro-level GDP quantification is not directly provided; this is an analytical claim rather than a direct empirical estimate.

**Policy implications:**
- Include mismatch reduction as an explicit objective of skills and immigration policy, not just an incidental outcome.
- Use credential recognition reform and active labour market programmes to reduce the duration and depth of overqualification.
- Develop employer-side interventions (tax incentives for skills-matched hiring, job redesign support) to raise the demand for higher-skilled work.
- Monitor and report NZ-specific mismatch costs using Treasury and Statistics NZ analysis.

**Relevant institutions:** MSD, MBIE, Immigration NZ, Statistics NZ, Treasury, NZ Productivity Commission

---

## claim-0104

**Statement:** More than 25% of New Zealand adults are foreign-born — one of the three highest shares in the OECD survey (alongside Australia and Canada) — creating structural pressure toward qualification mismatch because immigrant credentials are frequently not fully recognised in the domestic labour market, leading qualified migrants to accept roles below their attainment level.

**Meaning:** Over a quarter of NZ adults were born overseas — far above the OECD average of about 12%. This high immigrant share, combined with imperfect systems for recognising overseas qualifications in NZ, means a large proportion of highly qualified migrants end up in jobs below their skill level.

**Why it matters:** NZ's high foreign-born share is both an economic asset (workforce growth, diversity, skills import) and a source of structural mismatch if credentials are not recognised. Improving credential recognition systems would reduce this form of mismatch and raise the economic return on immigration.

**Dependencies:**
- claim-0105: NZ's combined qualification and field-of-study mismatch ranking among the highest in the OECD — this claim identifies the structural driver.
- claim-0099: OECD baseline mismatch rates of approximately 22% overqualified — NZ's rate is higher, partly because of its immigrant share.
- claim-0093: NZ labour shortage and workforce underutilisation — underutilisation of migrants through credential non-recognition worsens the effective shortage.
- claim-0097: Auckland demographic transformation — this is where most migrants live and work, making Auckland's labour market particularly affected.

**Mechanism:** Immigrants arrive with qualifications earned in different educational systems that may not be straightforwardly equivalent to NZ credentials. Regulatory barriers to professional recognition (medical, engineering, legal), combined with employer risk aversion toward unfamiliar credentials, produce structural overqualification among migrant workers.

**Evidence:** OECD (2016, Skills Matter) using PIAAC data across 28 countries. NZ's foreign-born share exceeds 25%, approximately double the OECD average. Foreign-born adults scored on average 24 points lower in literacy than native-born adults across the OECD (approximately half a standard deviation).

**Policy implications:**
- Develop faster, more generous mutual recognition agreements for qualifications from high-source-country migrants' home systems.
- Require licensing bodies to streamline overseas credential assessment with transparent, time-limited processes.
- Fund bridge programmes that allow overqualified migrants to demonstrate competency and access jobs matched to their qualifications.
- Monitor the occupational distribution of new migrants by qualification level to track overqualification rates over time.

**Relevant institutions:** Immigration NZ, MBIE, MSD, Statistics NZ

---

## claim-0105

**Statement:** New Zealand ranks among the OECD countries with the highest combined qualification and field-of-study mismatch rates, consistent with its high foreign-born share (>25% of adults) and the structural tendency for immigrants to accept jobs outside their qualifications or field of study when credential recognition is imperfect — making skills mismatch one of NZ's more distinctive labour market features relative to OECD peers.

**Meaning:** New Zealand has an unusually high proportion of workers doing jobs unrelated to their qualifications or field of study compared with most rich countries. This is driven substantially by the challenges migrants face in getting their overseas qualifications recognised, and it represents a drag on the economy's use of its human capital.

**Why it matters:** High mismatch rates mean NZ is systematically underusing the human capital of a large share of its workforce. For an economy with a shrinking workforce and low capital intensity, reducing mismatch is one of the few ways to raise effective labour productivity without requiring new investment or migration.

**Dependencies:**
- claim-0104: NZ's foreign-born share exceeding 25% is the primary structural driver of high mismatch.
- claim-0099: OECD baseline mismatch rates — NZ's rates are high relative to these averages.
- claim-0100: Wage effects of overqualification — the 14% wage penalty applies at above-average rates in NZ given its higher mismatch prevalence.
- claim-0103: Macro consequences of mismatch — NZ's above-average mismatch implies above-average productivity and GDP losses.
- claim-0093: NZ labour shortage — underutilisation of existing workers through mismatch worsens the effective shortage.

**Mechanism:** High immigration combined with imperfect credential recognition produces structural overqualification. The NZ labour market also has a large proportion of occupations accessible through multiple qualification routes, which makes qualification-job matching noisier and increases apparent field-of-study mismatch.

**Evidence:** OECD (2016, Skills Matter) using PIAAC figures and ranking charts (Figures 5.2–5.8) across 28 countries. NZ appears in the high end of the mismatch distribution. The specific percentage-point rate for NZ is from document figures rather than extractable text.

**Policy implications:**
- Implement a comprehensive credential recognition reform programme as a priority for workforce policy.
- Develop active labour market services specifically for highly-qualified immigrants who are underemployed.
- Invest in employer education about the equivalence of overseas credentials to reduce employer-side risk aversion.
- Set a measurable target for reducing NZ's mismatch rate toward the OECD average within a defined timeframe.

**Relevant institutions:** Immigration NZ, MBIE, MSD, Statistics NZ, NZ Productivity Commission

---

## claim-0106

**Statement:** New Zealand's housing supply elasticity declined by roughly one-quarter to one-third between the mid-century period (1938–1977) and recent decades (1979–2018), meaning a given increase in housing demand now produces one-quarter to one-third fewer new homes than it did in the mid-20th century.

**Meaning:** New Zealand's ability to translate housing demand into new homes has significantly weakened since the 1970s. When populations and incomes grow today, far fewer homes get built in response compared with what happened in the mid-20th century under similar demand pressures.

**Why it matters:** A less elastic housing supply means house prices must rise more to equilibrate supply and demand. This price mechanism rations housing by income rather than by need, contributes to the housing wealth-to-GDP ratio doubling, and restricts worker mobility into high-productivity locations.

**Dependencies:**
- claim-0107: Price sensitivity to population growth increased fourfold between periods — the direct consequence of declining supply elasticity.
- claim-0108: Auckland 1970 downzoning is identified as the primary cause of the elasticity decline.
- claim-0109: Declining travel speeds are identified as the secondary cause, reducing the effective land area that can supply housing accessible to employment.

**Mechanism:** Declining housing supply elasticity results from two compounding factors: regulatory constraints (downzoning) that reduced the number of sites where building was permitted, and congestion that reduced the effective land supply accessible within reasonable commute times. Both factors emerged from the 1970s onward.

**Evidence:** Nunns and Dodge (2022, Te Waihanga New Zealand Infrastructure Commission) using a reduced-form econometric model applied to New Zealand national housing and population data from 1938 to 2018. Results are directionally robust across multiple demand elasticity assumptions, though standard errors are large.

**Policy implications:**
- Treat planning reform (upzoning, process simplification) as a restoration of mid-century supply responsiveness rather than a novel intervention.
- Combine upzoning with congestion management to maximise the effective land supply accessible to employment.
- Monitor housing supply elasticity as a policy performance indicator — a recovering elasticity is evidence that reforms are working.
- Ensure the Unitary Plan and Medium Density Residential Standards are not watered down by local opposition, as this would reverse supply elasticity gains.

**Relevant institutions:** HUD/MHuD, Auckland Council, NZ Infrastructure Commission (Te Waihanga), NZTA, NZ Productivity Commission

---

## claim-0107

**Statement:** A 1% rise in New Zealand's population caused house prices to increase by ~0.5% in the mid-century period (1938–1977) but by ~2.0% in recent decades (1979–2018) — a fourfold increase in the price impact of population growth, reflecting reduced housing supply responsiveness rather than stronger demand.

**Meaning:** The same amount of population growth now pushes house prices up four times as much as it did two or three generations ago. This is not because people want more housing than before — it is because the planning and regulatory system is far less willing to let housing be built in response to demand.

**Why it matters:** The fourfold increase in price sensitivity to population growth directly explains the dramatic increase in housing costs relative to incomes over this period. It also confirms that the root cause is supply-side — specifically regulatory constraints — rather than demand acceleration or demographics.

**Dependencies:**
- claim-0106: NZ housing supply elasticity declined by one-quarter to one-third between periods — this claim quantifies the demand-price consequence of that elasticity decline.
- claim-0108: Auckland downzoning in the 1970s as the structural cause — the price sensitivity increase began simultaneously with the downzoning.

**Mechanism:** Lower housing supply elasticity means less new housing is built in response to demand increases. When supply does not expand to meet demand, the price mechanism does all the work of rationing — prices must rise further to equilibrate the market. The price sensitivity coefficient directly measures this supply constraint.

**Evidence:** Nunns and Dodge (2022, Te Waihanga) from their reduced-form econometric model of NZ national housing data, 1938–2018. The 0.5% and 2.0% coefficients carry substantial uncertainty (wide confidence intervals), but the directional finding of a fourfold increase is robust across specifications.

**Policy implications:**
- Use the historical mid-century price sensitivity (0.5% per 1% population growth) as a benchmark for what restored supply responsiveness would achieve.
- Frame housing affordability policy as primarily a supply elasticity problem, not a speculative demand or mortgage credit problem.
- Evaluate planning reform success partly by monitoring whether the price-population relationship is reverting toward mid-century norms.
- Communicate to the public that population growth in a supply-constrained environment inevitably raises prices — framing immigration as a housing affordability problem misidentifies the cause.

**Relevant institutions:** HUD/MHuD, Auckland Council, NZ Infrastructure Commission (Te Waihanga), Treasury, NZ Productivity Commission

---

## claim-0108

**Statement:** Auckland's 1970 District Scheme halved housing capacity from ~475,000 (under the 1961 scheme) to ~250,000 people against a 1970 population of 152,200, reducing the average floor area ratio limit from ~1.28 to ~0.80 — a 'great downzoning' not reversed until the 2016 Auckland Unitary Plan.

**Meaning:** In the early 1970s, Auckland's planning rules were dramatically tightened, cutting the city's theoretical capacity to house people by approximately half. This decision — never reversed for over four decades — fundamentally constrained how much housing could be built as Auckland's population grew.

**Why it matters:** The 1970 downzoning is the most important single planning decision in Auckland's modern history. Counterfactual analysis (see claim-0110) shows that avoiding it alone would have reduced house price growth by approximately 41% over 1978–2018. For current policymakers, this history justifies the 2016 Unitary Plan intensification reforms and argues for going further.

**Dependencies:**
- claim-0106: NZ housing supply elasticity declined by one-quarter to one-third between periods — the downzoning is the primary explanation.
- claim-0110: AMM model counterfactual shows avoiding the downzoning would have reduced price growth by approximately 41%.
- claim-0112: The RMA 1991 removed infrastructure planning requirements, compounding the downzoning's legacy rather than correcting it.

**Mechanism:** By reducing floor area ratio limits from 1.28 to 0.80 across central Auckland, the 1970 scheme made it illegal to build at the densities previously permitted. This directly reduced the supply of housing that could be constructed on existing urban land and shifted growth toward lower-density greenfield expansion, raising infrastructure costs and lengthening commutes.

**Evidence:** Nunns and Dodge (2022, Te Waihanga) using historical Auckland District Scheme analysis, comparing population capacity estimates under the 1961 and 1970 schemes. Capacity estimates assume full redevelopment to maximum permitted density, so actual realised capacity was lower. International parallels: New York City (79% capacity reduction in 1961) and Los Angeles (60% reduction 1960–1980).

**Policy implications:**
- Recognise the 2016 Unitary Plan as a partial reversal of a 46-year downzoning legacy, and continue extending intensification provisions.
- Apply lessons from the 1970 error: never allow planning reductions to reduce housing capacity below existing or projected population needs.
- Use historical capacity analysis to set minimum zoning requirements rather than letting local opposition drive downzoning.
- Implement legally binding minimum density requirements near transport corridors to prevent future downzoning.

**Relevant institutions:** Auckland Council, HUD/MHuD, NZ Infrastructure Commission (Te Waihanga), MBIE

---

## claim-0109

**Statement:** Auckland average urban travel speeds increased ~50% between the 1930s and early 1990s as cars were adopted and roads were improved (sealed urban roads rose from ~20% in 1930 to ~90% in 1970), but then declined by ~one-third by the 2010s due to rising congestion — eroding a substantial share of the accessibility gains that had facilitated mid-century housing supply.

**Meaning:** For sixty years, Auckland residents got progressively faster at getting around, which meant homes could be further from employment while remaining accessible. Since the 1990s, congestion has reversed about a third of those speed gains despite continued road building, shrinking the accessible housing market around every employment node.

**Why it matters:** Travel speed is a key determinant of how large a city's effective housing market is. Faster travel means workers can access more potential homes within a given time budget. The reversal of speed gains since the 1990s has effectively reduced Auckland's accessible housing supply and is one of the two main causes of the supply elasticity decline identified in claim-0106.

**Dependencies:**
- claim-0106: NZ housing supply elasticity declined by one-quarter to one-third — travel speed decline is identified as the secondary cause alongside downzoning.
- claim-0110: AMM model counterfactual shows avoiding the travel speed decline alone would have reduced price growth by approximately 42%.
- claim-0111: New road capacity induces additional demand; speed gains from road investment alone are not sustained — the speed decline cannot be reversed through road-building.

**Mechanism:** Faster roads expanded the accessible catchment area around employment centres, effectively adding land supply to the housing market. As congestion eroded speeds, this catchment area shrank. More road capacity could not reverse this because additional capacity induced additional driving (see claim-0111), dissipating the speed gains.

**Evidence:** Nunns and Dodge (2022, Te Waihanga) reconstructing Auckland travel speeds from historical road quality data, mode share estimates, and Vallyon (2013) arterial road speed data (showing 30% decline across nine routes, 1986–2012). This is observational reconstruction with measurement error, particularly in pre-1950 estimates.

**Policy implications:**
- Implement congestion pricing as the most effective tool for restoring travel speeds and expanding accessible housing supply.
- Invest in rapid transit that maintains travel speed reliability even as road congestion worsens.
- Design integrated land use and transport plans that locate new housing near rapid transit nodes to reduce car dependency.
- Factor travel speed trends into long-term housing supply projections rather than assuming stable accessibility.

**Relevant institutions:** Auckland Council, NZTA, NZ Infrastructure Commission (Te Waihanga), HUD/MHuD

---

## claim-0110

**Statement:** AMM model counterfactual analysis shows Auckland house prices would have risen only ~80% over 1978–2018 (vs 251% observed) had both the 1970s downzoning and the post-1990s travel speed decline been avoided — a ~70% reduction in price inflation. Either intervention alone would have reduced price growth by ~40–42%.

**Meaning:** If Auckland had not downzoned in the 1970s and had managed congestion effectively since the 1990s, house prices would have risen much less than they did — by about 80% rather than 251%. Either fix alone would have cut price growth roughly in half.

**Why it matters:** This counterfactual provides the strongest available quantification of the cost of Auckland's planning and transport policy failures over the past half century. It also confirms that fixing both problems — through upzoning and congestion management — is more powerful than fixing either alone.

**Dependencies:**
- claim-0108: The 1970s downzoning that the first counterfactual avoids.
- claim-0109: The travel speed decline that the second counterfactual avoids.
- claim-0111: Why road investment alone cannot restore travel speeds and therefore why congestion pricing is necessary for the second counterfactual.

**Mechanism:** Avoiding downzoning allows more homes per square kilometre of urban land, raising supply responsiveness to demand. Avoiding speed decline maintains the effective catchment area of employment centres, ensuring more land is accessible for housing within reasonable commute times. Together, these effects compound to reduce price pressure dramatically.

**Evidence:** Nunns and Dodge (2022, Te Waihanga) using a calibrated simulation model (Auckland Monocentric Model, AMM) with Cobb-Douglas utility and housing production functions. Results depend on modelling assumptions; the model omits employment decentralisation and natural amenities. The observed price growth figure (251%) is consistent with REINZ data.

**Policy implications:**
- Maintain and deepen the 2016 Unitary Plan intensification reforms to capture the approximately 41% price growth reduction from avoiding equivalent future downzoning.
- Implement congestion pricing to achieve the approximately 42% price growth reduction from maintaining travel speeds.
- Combine both interventions to capture the approximately 70% combined price growth reduction.
- Use the 80% alternative price trajectory as a long-run affordability benchmark for Auckland housing policy.

**Relevant institutions:** Auckland Council, NZTA, HUD/MHuD, NZ Infrastructure Commission (Te Waihanga), Treasury

---

## claim-0111

**Statement:** New road capacity tends to induce additional driving, resulting in few sustained benefits for average urban travel speed; reversing the decline in travel speeds through road investment alone is not feasible, and congestion pricing combined with deployment of new transport options is identified as the more effective approach.

**Meaning:** Building more roads in congested cities fills up quickly with more cars and does not improve average travel speeds for long. To actually maintain or improve urban travel speeds, cities need to manage demand through pricing or mode shift, not just add supply.

**Why it matters:** Auckland and NZ's transport investment plans are heavily road-focused. The induced demand mechanism means these investments will not restore Auckland's travel speeds to earlier levels and will not reduce congestion costs (see claim-0067). Redirecting investment toward congestion pricing and rapid transit is the more evidence-based approach.

**Dependencies:**
- claim-0109: Auckland travel speed history — speeds rose then declined by approximately one-third by the 2010s, even with significant motorway expansion.
- claim-0110: Avoiding speed decline alone would reduce house price growth by approximately 42% — but this can only be achieved through demand management, not road building.

**Mechanism:** Induced demand occurs because new road capacity reduces travel time, which attracts additional trips and drivers who previously did not use the road (latent demand). This process continues until congestion returns to approximately pre-investment levels, consuming the capacity improvement. The effect is well-documented by Duranton and Turner (2012) and consistent with Auckland's own experience.

**Evidence:** Nunns and Dodge (2022, Te Waihanga) citing Duranton and Turner (2012) and Auckland's own ATAP transport model, which projects that a $31 billion investment programme will increase congestion delays by 10%. This is observational evidence supported by established economic theory.

**Policy implications:**
- Implement congestion pricing in Auckland as the most cost-effective tool for managing demand and funding alternative modes.
- Redirect a greater share of transport investment from road capacity to rapid transit and active modes.
- Evaluate all major road investment proposals against induced demand evidence before committing funding.
- Communicate to the public that road-building does not solve congestion, and that pricing is the evidence-based alternative.

**Relevant institutions:** Auckland Council, NZTA, Treasury, NZ Infrastructure Commission (Te Waihanga)

---

## claim-0112

**Statement:** The Resource Management Act 1991 removed specific requirements for infrastructure planning from New Zealand planning legislation, decoupling urban development planning from infrastructure provision; prior legislation (Town and Country Planning Acts 1953, 1977) had required councils to plan coordinated infrastructure investment alongside development.

**Meaning:** Before the RMA, NZ councils were legally required to plan roads, sewers, water, and transport as part of their development plans. The RMA replaced this with a general framework for managing environmental effects, removing the obligation to plan infrastructure proactively — contributing to the mismatch between urban development and infrastructure provision that has constrained housing supply.

**Why it matters:** The infrastructure planning gap created by the RMA has meant that development approval and infrastructure funding often proceed on separate tracks, creating bottlenecks where housing is approved in areas without adequate water, wastewater, or transport capacity. Restoring the link between development and infrastructure planning is a precondition for achieving the housing supply gains identified in claims 0106–0110.

**Dependencies:**
- claim-0108: The 1970s downzoning as the primary supply restriction — the RMA worsened the situation by removing planning coordination but did not originate the constraint.
- claim-0106: Overall decline in housing supply elasticity — the RMA's infrastructure planning gap is a contributing factor to the supply constraint that this claim documents.

**Mechanism:** When planning legislation requires infrastructure planning alongside development, councils proactively fund and stage infrastructure to enable development. Without this requirement, development can proceed faster than infrastructure, creating bottlenecks, or infrastructure providers can decline to fund works in areas approved for development, stalling supply.

**Evidence:** Nunns and Dodge (2022, Te Waihanga) using historical analysis of NZ planning legislation, comparing specific provisions of the Town and Country Planning Acts (1953, 1977) and the Resource Management Act (1991). This is documentary analysis of legislative change.

**Policy implications:**
- Restore statutory infrastructure planning requirements through replacement planning legislation or specific NPS obligations.
- Ensure the proposed Fast-track Approvals regime includes mandatory infrastructure funding and staging plans.
- Use the National Policy Statement on Urban Development to require local authorities to demonstrate infrastructure capacity before permitting major upzoning.
- Develop a NZ Infrastructure Strategy that specifically links development staging to infrastructure provision to reverse the RMA's decoupling effect.

**Relevant institutions:** HUD/MHuD, NZ Infrastructure Commission (Te Waihanga), Auckland Council, NZTA, MBIE, MfE

---
## claim-0113: New Zealand's population grew 27.3% between 2006 and 2025

**Statement**
New Zealand's population grew by 27.3% between 2006 and 2025, from 4,184,600 to 5,324,700 — an increase of approximately 1.14 million people over two decades.

**Meaning (plain English)**
Over the last twenty years, New Zealand's population grew by just over a quarter, adding roughly 1.14 million people — equivalent to adding more than two Wellingtons' worth of residents to the country.

**Why it matters**
This headline growth figure is the national benchmark against which all regional and city-level population trends are measured; understanding which places grew faster or slower than 27.3% over this period reveals where housing, infrastructure, and labour market pressure has actually concentrated.

**Mechanism**
This is a descriptive statistic rather than a causal claim: it reflects the combined effect of natural increase (births minus deaths) and net migration (both international and the redistribution of international arrivals across regions) compounding over nearly two decades.

**Evidence**
Kanoa RD (2026), an internal MBIE presentation citing Statistics NZ Aotearoa Data Explorer population estimates (1996–2025) and Subnational Population Estimates. Evidence strength is rated descriptive.

**Dependencies**
None listed in the relationships block. (Note: the claim file's "Qualifiers" section flags that this national aggregate masks substantial divergence across regions and cities, elaborated in claim-0115 and claim-0116, but no formal supports/qualifies/depends_on relationship is recorded.)

**Policy implications**
- National population growth figures should not be used as a proxy for regional or local growth — sub-national divergence is large enough to mislead infrastructure and housing planning if applied uniformly.
- The ~1.14 million increase implies substantial cumulative demand for housing, transport, schools, and health infrastructure concentrated mostly in specific high-growth areas rather than spread evenly.
- Long-run (two-decade) population baselines are useful for capital planning horizons (e.g., Te Waihanga's infrastructure pipeline) but can obscure recent inflection points such as the 2024–2025 growth slowdown.
- Central government population strategy documents should pair this national figure with the regional breakdown to avoid implying uniform growth pressure across all 88 TLAs.
- Statistics NZ's subnational estimates should be the standard reference dataset for any agency benchmarking regional growth against the national rate.

**Relevant institutions**
Kanoa – Regional Economic Development & Investment Unit (MBIE), Stats NZ, Treasury, HUD, Te Waihanga.

---
## claim-0114: Auckland's net internal migration is negative; population growth depends entirely on international migration

**Statement**
In the year to June 2025, Auckland's net internal migration was negative — without international migration, Auckland's population would have declined; internal out-migrants moved primarily to Waikato, Northland, Bay of Plenty, and Canterbury.

**Meaning (plain English)**
More people are leaving Auckland for other parts of New Zealand than are arriving from elsewhere in the country. The only reason Auckland's population isn't shrinking is that international migrants are arriving in large enough numbers to offset this domestic outflow.

**Why it matters**
This challenges any assumption that Auckland's growth is organically self-sustaining; its population trajectory is now hostage to international migration settings (visa policy, global labour markets) rather than being reinforced by domestic drawing power, with implications for the city's long-run labour supply and housing demand forecasts.

**Mechanism**
The presentation attributes the internal outflow primarily to relative job opportunities and housing affordability elsewhere — Aucklanders are moving to Waikato, Northland, Bay of Plenty, and Canterbury where housing costs are lower and which have benefited from spillover growth (Waikato and Bay of Plenty are themselves part of the "golden triangle" of above-average growth, see claim-0115).

**Evidence**
Kanoa RD (2026), citing Auckland Council's internal analysis of migration flow data for the year to June 2025. Evidence strength is rated descriptive; this is a single-year snapshot, not a multi-year average.

**Dependencies**
Related to (no formal relationship recorded): claim-0115 — Auckland is one of only three NZ cities (the "golden triangle," with Hamilton and Tauranga) to grow faster than the national rate over 2006–2025, a long-run pattern that this claim shows is being driven entirely by international migration rather than internal migration. Related to claim-0016 — Auckland generates 37.9% of NZ GDP on a 33% population share; that economic primacy depends on a workforce that this claim shows is no longer sustained by net domestic in-migration.

**Policy implications**
- Auckland and central government housing/labour supply forecasts should not assume internal migration will reinforce Auckland's population growth — it is currently a drag, not a contributor.
- Immigration policy settings are now the single largest lever determining whether Auckland's population (and thus its tax base, labour force, and GDP contribution) grows or shrinks.
- The regions receiving Auckland's outflow (Waikato, Northland, Bay of Plenty, Canterbury) should plan for sustained in-migration pressure on housing and infrastructure as a structural trend, not a one-off.
- Auckland Council and HUD should investigate whether housing affordability and job-opportunity gaps relative to neighbouring regions are narrowing or widening, since these are the cited drivers of outflow.
- A single year's data (to June 2025) should be treated cautiously; multi-year monitoring is needed to confirm this is a durable shift rather than a short-term fluctuation.

**Relevant institutions**
Auckland Council, Kanoa – Regional Economic Development & Investment Unit (MBIE), HUD, MBIE (Immigration NZ), Stats NZ.

---
## claim-0115: Only Auckland, Hamilton, and Tauranga grew faster than the national rate, 2006–2025

**Statement**
Over 2006–2025, only Auckland, Hamilton, and Tauranga — the "golden triangle" — grew faster than New Zealand's national rate of 27.3%; Auckland added approximately 443,000 people, and Tauranga grew by approximately 50% (from 107,300 to 161,000).

**Meaning (plain English)**
Of New Zealand's main cities, only three — Auckland, Hamilton, and Tauranga, which form a geographic triangle in the upper North Island — grew faster than the national average over the past two decades. Every other city grew more slowly than the country as a whole, even if their populations still increased in absolute terms.

**Why it matters**
This confirms that New Zealand's urban growth is geographically concentrated in a single connected sub-region (the upper North Island), rather than spread across the country's main centres — a pattern with direct implications for where national infrastructure investment, housing supply, and labour market policy should be targeted.

**Mechanism**
This is a descriptive ranking rather than a causal claim, but it implies that whatever combination of factors drives city growth (job opportunities, amenity, international migration, proximity to existing agglomeration) is currently concentrated on the upper North Island corridor rather than being a generic feature of "being a city" in New Zealand; Auckland's growth in particular is shown elsewhere (claim-0114) to depend on international migration since internal migration flows out of, not into, Auckland.

**Evidence**
Kanoa RD (2026), citing Statistics NZ population estimates for main urban areas, 2006–2025. Evidence strength is rated descriptive.

**Dependencies**
Depends on: claim-0113 — the comparison benchmark is the 27.3% national growth rate established in claim-0113; this claim identifies which cities exceeded that benchmark. Related to claim-0114 — Auckland's inclusion in the "golden triangle" of above-average growth coexists with negative net internal migration, meaning Auckland's growth is driven entirely by international arrivals rather than domestic drawing power. Related to claim-0116 — the concentration of fast growth in a few TLAs (Selwyn, Queenstown Lakes, and others) is consistent with this claim's finding that growth is geographically concentrated rather than evenly spread.

**Policy implications**
- National infrastructure and housing investment (transport corridors, water, schools) should prioritise the Auckland–Hamilton–Tauranga corridor, where growth pressure is demonstrably concentrated.
- Other major centres (Wellington, Christchurch, Dunedin) growing below the national average may have spare infrastructure capacity relative to demand, which should inform relative investment priorities.
- The "golden triangle" framing should prompt explicit central government consideration of upper-North-Island connectivity (e.g., inter-regional transport between Auckland, Hamilton, and Tauranga) as a single functional growth corridor.
- Tauranga's 50% growth rate — the fastest of the three — warrants particular attention to infrastructure and housing supply constraints in the Western Bay of Plenty.
- Regional development agencies outside the golden triangle should treat below-average population growth as a planning input, potentially shifting focus from growth accommodation toward retention and productivity within a more slowly growing population base.

**Relevant institutions**
Kanoa – Regional Economic Development & Investment Unit (MBIE), Auckland Council, Hamilton City Council, Tauranga City Council, Te Waihanga, HUD.

---
## claim-0116: Selwyn District grew 151%, New Zealand's fastest-growing territorial authority, 2006–2025

**Statement**
Selwyn District (south of Christchurch) grew by 151% between 2006 and 2025 — New Zealand's fastest-growing territorial authority; Queenstown Lakes grew 123%; of the ten fastest-growing TLAs, four were in Auckland, two in Canterbury, two in Otago, and one each in Waikato and Bay of Plenty.

**Meaning (plain English)**
At the most local level of government (territorial authorities, of which there are 88 in New Zealand), growth is even more concentrated than the city-level picture suggests. Selwyn, a district just outside Christchurch, more than doubled its population in under twenty years — faster than any other part of the country — followed by the Queenstown Lakes district.

**Why it matters**
This shows that rapid population growth in New Zealand is not just an "Auckland vs the rest" story — it includes earthquake-driven redistribution around Christchurch (Selwyn) and tourism/amenity-driven growth (Queenstown Lakes), each with very different underlying economic drivers and different infrastructure and housing pressures than agglomeration-driven city growth.

**Mechanism**
Selwyn's growth reflects population redistribution from Christchurch following the 2010–11 Canterbury earthquakes combined with greenfield residential development on Christchurch's urban fringe; Queenstown Lakes' growth reflects tourism- and amenity-driven migration, an economically distinct process from labour-market-driven urban agglomeration. The concentration of the ten fastest-growing TLAs within just four regions (Auckland, Canterbury, Otago, Waikato/Bay of Plenty) implies the majority of New Zealand's 88 TLAs grew slowly or declined over the period.

**Evidence**
Kanoa RD (2026), citing Statistics NZ population estimates by territorial authority, 2006–2025. Evidence strength is rated descriptive.

**Dependencies**
Related to claim-0113 — the 151% and 123% TLA-level growth rates are extreme outliers relative to the 27.3% national growth rate established in claim-0113, illustrating how concentrated growth is once disaggregated below city level. Related to claim-0115 — this claim extends the "golden triangle" city-level finding down to the TLA level, showing growth is concentrated in specific districts (including ones outside the golden triangle, like Selwyn and Queenstown Lakes) rather than spread evenly even within fast-growing regions.

**Policy implications**
- Selwyn District Council and Christchurch City Council should coordinate cross-boundary infrastructure planning, since Selwyn's growth is substantially a spillover effect of Christchurch's post-earthquake rebuild and housing market.
- Queenstown Lakes' tourism/amenity-driven growth model creates distinct pressures (seasonal housing demand, low-wage tourism labour market, visitor infrastructure) that differ from job-driven urban growth and warrant tailored policy responses rather than generic urban growth tools.
- With growth concentrated in roughly ten of 88 TLAs, central government funding formulas (e.g., for water, roading, and housing infrastructure) should be sensitive to this skew rather than allocating per-TLA funding evenly.
- Regions with TLAs absent from the fastest-growing list should be assessed for population decline risk and the associated fiscal sustainability challenges (shrinking rate base, ageing population).
- The redistribution dynamic after the Canterbury earthquakes is a useful case study for planning resilience and post-disaster population redistribution in other parts of New Zealand.

**Relevant institutions**
Selwyn District Council, Christchurch City Council, Queenstown Lakes District Council, Kanoa – Regional Economic Development & Investment Unit (MBIE), Te Waihanga, HUD.

---
## claim-0117: NZ population grew by 34,700 in the year to June 2025; 21 of 88 TLAs experienced natural population decrease

**Statement**
In the year to June 2025, New Zealand's population grew by 34,700, comprising natural increase of 21,000 (births minus deaths) and net international migration of 13,700; 21 of New Zealand's 88 territorial local authorities experienced natural population decrease.

**Meaning (plain English)**
In the most recent year of data, New Zealand's population grew by under 35,000 people — a relatively small increase split between more births than deaths (21,000) and net arrivals from overseas (13,700). But in 21 of the country's 88 local council areas, more people died than were born, a sign of ageing, often rural, populations.

**Why it matters**
This single-year snapshot shows both a national growth slowdown and a growing structural problem: a fifth of New Zealand's territorial authorities now have more deaths than births locally, which has direct implications for the long-run fiscal sustainability, school rolls, and workforce base of those areas, independent of migration policy.

**Mechanism**
This is a descriptive breakdown of population change rather than a causal claim, but the 21 TLAs with natural decrease reflect a structural feature of demographic ageing — older population age structures (more deaths) combined with declining fertility (fewer births), consistent with the national total fertility rate decline documented elsewhere. Natural increase nationally (21,000) is itself muted by the same falling-fertility dynamic.

**Evidence**
Kanoa RD (2026), citing Statistics NZ population estimates for the year to June 2025, broken down by natural increase, international migration, and internal migration. Evidence strength is rated descriptive; this is a single-year figure relatively low compared to the post-COVID migration peak in 2023.

**Dependencies**
Related to claim-0091 — national total fertility rate reached 1.55 in 2025, below the 2.1 replacement rate; this claim's modest natural increase of 21,000 and the 21 TLAs in natural decline are a direct consequence of that fertility decline. Related to claim-0092 — annual NZ population growth slowed sharply from 1.7% to 0.7% in one year; this claim provides the underlying components-of-change breakdown for that slowdown (34,700 total growth in the year to June 2025). Related to claim-0093 — BusinessNZ's projected labour force shortage of 250,000 by 2045 is the forward-looking consequence of the same ageing and low-natural-increase dynamics visible in this claim's TLA-level breakdown.

**Policy implications**
- The 21 TLAs experiencing natural population decrease should be flagged for targeted economic development and aged-care/health workforce planning, since their demographic trajectory is structural rather than cyclical.
- National population growth of 34,700 in a single year is low relative to historical post-COVID peaks; immigration policy settings should be reviewed if sustained labour force growth is a policy objective.
- Components-of-change data (natural increase vs. international vs. internal migration) should be published and monitored at the TLA level to allow targeted rather than one-size-fits-all population policy.
- Falling natural increase nationally reinforces the case for the labour force and superannuation fiscal pressure projections already identified in related demographic claims (claim-0093).
- Central government should consider whether regional development funding (e.g., via Kanoa) should be weighted toward TLAs with natural population decrease, which face compounding fiscal and labour supply pressures.

**Relevant institutions**
Kanoa – Regional Economic Development & Investment Unit (MBIE), Stats NZ, Treasury, Ministry of Health, MBIE (Immigration NZ).
---
## claim-0118: NZ spends 5.8% of GDP/year on infrastructure but ranks near the bottom of the OECD for efficiency

**Statement**
New Zealand invested around 5.8% of GDP per year on infrastructure over the past 20 years, making it one of the top-spending countries in the OECD, yet it ranks towards the bottom for efficiency or "bang for buck."

**Meaning (plain English)**
New Zealand spends a larger share of its national income on infrastructure than almost any other rich country, but it gets comparatively little value for that spending — the money does not translate into proportionately good infrastructure outcomes.

**Why it matters**
This is the headline framing for the entire 2026 National Infrastructure Plan: the problem is not under-investment but poor value capture from investment. For NZ policymakers, it shifts the debate from "spend more" to "spend better," with implications for procurement, consenting, and planning reform.

**Mechanism**
The Plan attributes the efficiency gap partly to New Zealand's small, dispersed population across challenging terrain, but explicitly states this does not fully explain the shortfall — self-imposed regulatory and procurement "hurdles" (such as fragmented zoning and high consenting costs) are presented as compounding causes.

**Evidence**
Te Waihanga (NZ Infrastructure Commission), National Infrastructure Plan 2026, Executive Summary and Figure 4 (Section 3.2), comparing NZ's public capital investment share of GDP (5.4%, 2010–2019) against OECD comparators. Evidence strength is descriptive — based on the Commission's own cross-country comparison rather than a standardised international efficiency index.

**Dependencies**
None listed in the relationships block (claim-0118 itself is the source claim referenced by others, e.g. claim-0120 and claim-0124, rather than depending on prior claims).

**Policy implications**
- Treasury and Te Waihanga should prioritise reforms that improve "bang for buck" (procurement, project selection, sequencing) rather than simply increasing infrastructure budgets further.
- Benefit-cost frameworks for infrastructure investment should explicitly account for NZ's historically poor efficiency record when appraising new spending commitments.
- The finding supports the case for resource management and consenting reform as infrastructure-cost levers, not just housing-supply levers.
- Cross-agency coordination (central government, local government, and the commercial/private sector, given their respective shares of investment) is needed to address efficiency, since no single actor controls the full pipeline.
- Any claims of "underinvestment" in NZ infrastructure debates should be weighed against this evidence that the spending level itself is already comparatively high.

**Relevant institutions**
Te Waihanga, Treasury, MBIE, Auckland Council, local government generally.

---
## claim-0119: Forward Guidance projects NZ infrastructure spending rising to over $40 billion/year by the 2050s, ~60% for renewals

**Statement**
The New Zealand Infrastructure Commission's Forward Guidance projects infrastructure spending rising from just over $20 billion per year currently to more than $40 billion per year by the 2050s, with around 60% of that spending needing to go towards renewing and replacing existing assets rather than new builds.

**Meaning (plain English)**
New Zealand's infrastructure bill is expected to roughly double over the next 25-30 years, and most of that extra money will not go toward shiny new projects — it will go toward patching up and replacing things that already exist, like ageing pipes, roads, and buildings.

**Why it matters**
This reframes the national infrastructure conversation away from "what new things should we build" and toward "how do we fund the renewal of what we already have." It has direct implications for long-term rates, taxes, and infrastructure financing settings in NZ.

**Mechanism**
A large stock of post-war infrastructure assets is simultaneously approaching the end of its useful life, while population growth and other demand drivers continue to add new pressure; renewals and new-build demand compound rather than substitute for each other, pushing total spending upward over time.

**Evidence**
Te Waihanga (NZ Infrastructure Commission), National Infrastructure Plan 2026, Section 4.2 ("Prioritise adequate maintenance and renewals"), drawing on the Commission's Forward Guidance demand-forecasting method. Evidence strength is model-based — a forecast/projection rather than an observed outcome.

**Dependencies**
Related to claim-0122 — the $330 billion existing asset base (most built after 1950, now reaching end of life) is the underlying stock that generates the renewals demand projected here; the two claims describe the same renewals problem from a stock (claim-0122) versus flow/forecast (claim-0119) perspective. Related to claim-0092 — population growth, even though it has slowed (from 1.7% to 0.7% annual growth per Stats NZ), remains one demand driver behind the Forward Guidance figures, alongside renewals.

**Policy implications**
- Long-term infrastructure funding mechanisms (rates, user charges, government bonds) need to be sized for a near-doubling of annual spending, not just incremental growth.
- Asset management and renewals planning should be treated as a first-order budget priority by central government agencies and local councils, not a residual after new-build commitments.
- The 60% renewals share — described as a floor that could be higher in low-growth areas — implies regions with declining or static populations face an even more renewals-dominated spending profile.
- This projection should be revisited periodically as it supersedes an earlier (2022) Forward Guidance estimate of roughly $30 billion/year, showing these forecasts are revised materially over time.
- Investment appraisal processes should distinguish renewals spending from growth-driven new-build spending, since the two have different risk and demand profiles.

**Relevant institutions**
Te Waihanga, Treasury, MBIE, local government, Stats NZ.

---
## claim-0120: Consenting costs NZ infrastructure providers $1.3 billion/year, rising to 16% of budget for small projects

**Statement**
Consenting costs New Zealand infrastructure providers around $1.3 billion per year, with a typical project spending 5.5% of its total budget seeking a resource consent — rising to around 16% for smaller projects worth less than $200,000.

**Meaning (plain English)**
Getting regulatory approval (a "resource consent") to build infrastructure in New Zealand is expensive, and disproportionately so for small projects — a $200,000 project effectively pays a much higher consenting "tax" than a large one.

**Why it matters**
Consenting cost and delay is a recurring theme in NZ infrastructure and housing policy debates; this figure quantifies the scale of the problem and shows that the regulatory burden falls hardest on smaller projects, which has implications for who can afford to build at all.

**Mechanism**
Resource consent processes impose largely fixed costs (legal, technical reporting, hearings) regardless of project size, so these costs make up a much larger proportion of the budget for small projects than large ones — a regressive effect on smaller infrastructure providers and developers.

**Evidence**
Te Waihanga (NZ Infrastructure Commission), National Infrastructure Plan 2026, Section 6.1, citing "The Cost of Consenting Infrastructure Projects in New Zealand" (Sapere, 2022, commissioned by Te Waihanga). This is a secondary citation within the Plan rather than original Commission analysis; evidence strength is observational, and the international cost-ranking claim is explicitly hedged ("may be near the upper end").

**Dependencies**
Related to claim-0121 — NZ's fragmented zoning landscape (1,175 land-use zones across 67 territorial authorities, versus 13 in Japan) is presented by the Plan as a structural driver of this consenting cost and complexity. Related to claim-0118 — the $1.3 billion consenting cost is cited as one concrete driver of NZ's poor infrastructure spending efficiency relative to OECD peers.

**Policy implications**
- Resource management reform should target fixed-cost components of the consenting process that disproportionately burden small projects.
- Streamlined or tiered consenting pathways for small-value infrastructure projects could materially reduce the 16% cost share faced by sub-$200,000 projects.
- The 1.3 billion annual figure provides a quantifiable baseline against which the benefits of RMA replacement legislation can be benchmarked.
- Time-to-consent trends (a reported 150% increase in consenting time between 2010-2014 and 2015-2019) suggest reform should address process duration as well as direct cost.
- Central government should monitor consenting cost-as-share-of-budget over time as a key performance indicator for resource management reform.

**Relevant institutions**
Te Waihanga, Ministry for the Environment, MBIE, local government, territorial authorities.

---
## claim-0121: NZ has 1,175 land-use zones across 67 councils, versus 13 in Japan

**Statement**
New Zealand has 1,175 land-use zones spread across 67 territorial authorities, compared to 13 land-use zones in Japan, a country with more than 20 times New Zealand's population.

**Meaning (plain English)**
New Zealand's planning system is extremely fragmented — there are vastly more distinct sets of zoning rules per person than in Japan, a much larger and more populous country that manages with far fewer, simpler zone categories.

**Why it matters**
This stylised comparison is used by Te Waihanga to argue that regulatory complexity, not just total regulation, is a major driver of NZ's high infrastructure and housing delivery costs — supporting calls for zoning simplification and standardisation as part of resource management reform.

**Mechanism**
A large number of distinct, locally varying zones increases the complexity, cost, and unpredictability of navigating planning rules for any given project, since proponents (and the firms serving them) face different rules in each jurisdiction rather than a consistent national or regional framework.

**Evidence**
Te Waihanga (NZ Infrastructure Commission), National Infrastructure Plan 2026, Executive Summary / Section 6.1. This is presented as a stylised fact without an attached citation/footnote (unlike most other quantitative claims in the Plan), and is descriptive rather than a controlled comparison accounting for other institutional differences between the two countries' planning systems.

**Dependencies**
Related to claim-0120 — this zoning fragmentation is presented as a structural contributor to the high consenting costs ($1.3 billion/year, 5.5-16% of project budgets) documented separately. Related to claim-0112 — the proliferation of zones and plan complexity reflects the post-1991 Resource Management Act framework, which removed specific infrastructure-planning requirements and decoupled urban development planning from coordinated infrastructure provision, allowing local plan complexity to grow largely unchecked.

**Policy implications**
- Resource management reform should consider standardising or consolidating zone categories nationally, rather than leaving each of the 67 territorial authorities to develop bespoke zoning schedules.
- A national planning framework (e.g., a small number of standard zone types with limited local variation) could reduce both consenting costs and uncertainty for infrastructure and housing proponents.
- This comparison, while illustrative, should be treated cautiously in policy debate since it does not control for differences in geography, hazard exposure, or local government structure between NZ and Japan.
- Any successor legislation to the RMA should be assessed against whether it reduces or further entrenches zone-count fragmentation.
- MBIE and the Ministry for the Environment should consider zone-count or plan-complexity metrics as part of resource management reform monitoring.

**Relevant institutions**
Te Waihanga, Ministry for the Environment, MBIE, territorial authorities, local government generally.

---
## claim-0122: NZ has over $330 billion in infrastructure assets, mostly post-1950, now reaching end of life

**Statement**
New Zealand has more than $330 billion worth of existing infrastructure assets, most of which was built after 1950, and the first big wave of post-war infrastructure is now reaching the end of its usable life.

**Meaning (plain English)**
New Zealand's roads, water networks, and other infrastructure are mostly products of the post-WWII building boom, and a large share of that infrastructure is now old enough that it needs major repair or replacement, all at roughly the same time.

**Why it matters**
This stock figure is the foundation for the Plan's central argument that renewals — not new construction — should be the dominant infrastructure priority for the next several decades, with direct implications for how central and local government budget for asset management.

**Mechanism**
Because a large share of the post-war infrastructure stock was built within a relatively compressed historical period, the assets are now ageing and reaching end-of-life in a similarly compressed window, creating a concentrated "wave" of renewal need rather than a smoothly distributed one.

**Evidence**
Te Waihanga (NZ Infrastructure Commission), National Infrastructure Plan 2026, Section 4.2, with the asset value series (Figure 30) presented in inflation-adjusted 2025 NZD back to 1875. Evidence strength is descriptive; the Plan itself flags that asset condition data quality is poor ("many agencies have inaccurate or incomplete data"), making it hard to verify exactly how much of current spending addresses this renewals need.

**Dependencies**
Related to claim-0119 — this $330 billion asset stock, concentrated in the post-1950 period, is the underlying base driving the projected rise in renewals-dominated spending (to over $40 billion/year by the 2050s, ~60% renewals) described in claim-0119. Related to claim-0123 — the ownership breakdown of infrastructure investment (commercial/private, local government, central government) describes who is financing the maintenance and renewal of this asset base.

**Policy implications**
- Asset management planning across central and local government should explicitly model the "wave" timing of post-war infrastructure reaching end-of-life, rather than assuming steady, linear renewal needs.
- Investment in better asset condition data collection is a prerequisite for credibly prioritising renewals spending, given the Plan's own admission of poor data quality.
- Long-term funding mechanisms should be designed around the concentrated nature of this renewals wave rather than smoothed historical spending patterns.
- This figure provides a quantifiable basis for intergenerational equity arguments — ensuring renewals are funded now rather than deferred as a burden onto future ratepayers and taxpayers.
- Central government should consider whether current depreciation and asset-revaluation practices in local government accounts adequately capture this renewals liability.

**Relevant institutions**
Te Waihanga, Treasury, local government, Department of Internal Affairs.

---
## claim-0123: Infrastructure investment is split 44% commercial/private, 31% local government, 25% central government

**Statement**
An estimated breakdown of New Zealand's infrastructure investment by ownership shows the commercial/private sector providing 44% ($10bn), local government 31% ($7.1bn), and central government 25% ($5.8bn).

**Meaning (plain English)**
Almost half of all the money spent on infrastructure in New Zealand comes from private and commercial sources, not from central or local government — government funding is actually the minority share of total investment.

**Why it matters**
This breakdown corrects a common assumption that infrastructure is primarily a government funding responsibility; it implies that infrastructure policy reform needs to engage commercial/private investors and local government funding tools, not just central government budgets.

**Mechanism**
This is a descriptive accounting breakdown rather than a causal claim — it implies that any reform aimed at improving NZ's infrastructure efficiency or funding settings must address incentives and constraints facing all three ownership groups (private firms, local councils, and central government), since each controls a substantial and roughly comparable share of total investment.

**Evidence**
Te Waihanga (NZ Infrastructure Commission), National Infrastructure Plan 2026, Section 3.2, Figure 3, adapted from the Commission's own 2024 research report "Build or maintain? New Zealand's infrastructure asset value, investment, and depreciation, 1990–2022." Evidence strength is descriptive; the Plan itself labels this an "estimated breakdown," not an audited figure, and the underlying time period for the dollar figures is not stated in the immediate figure caption.

**Dependencies**
Related to claim-0119 — this ownership split describes the funding composition behind the aggregate infrastructure spending levels (current ~$20bn/year, projected to exceed $40bn/year by the 2050s) discussed in claim-0119. Related to claim-0124 — the per-person infrastructure spending figure (almost $5,000 per person in 2022, 2025 NZD) is an alternative way of presenting the same broad infrastructure investment total that this ownership breakdown disaggregates by funder.

**Policy implications**
- Infrastructure funding reform should address constraints facing local government (rates caps, debt limits) given its 31% share, not only central government fiscal settings.
- Private/commercial sector investment, as the largest single share, suggests regulatory settings affecting private infrastructure investment (e.g., in energy, telecommunications) warrant equal policy attention to public infrastructure debates.
- Any future revision of this Figure 3-style breakdown should be tracked over time to assess whether the funding mix is shifting (e.g., toward more private investment via PPPs).
- The estimate's "not audited" status suggests Treasury and Te Waihanga should invest in more precise, regularly updated infrastructure investment accounting.
- Policymakers should be cautious about infrastructure funding proposals that assume central government bears the majority of investment responsibility, given its actual 25% share.

**Relevant institutions**
Te Waihanga, Treasury, local government, Department of Internal Affairs.

---
## claim-0124: NZ spent almost $5,000 per person on infrastructure in 2022 (2025 NZD)

**Statement**
In 2022, New Zealand spent almost $5,000 per person on infrastructure, expressed in 2025 NZD.

**Meaning (plain English)**
If you divide all the money New Zealand spent on infrastructure in 2022 by the number of people in the country, it comes to almost $5,000 per person — a high figure by international standards — yet the Plan notes that infrastructure quality still lags relative to this level of spending.

**Why it matters**
This per-person framing makes NZ's high aggregate infrastructure spending more tangible and reinforces the Plan's core message: the issue is not how much is spent but how effectively it is spent, since high per-person spending coexists with comparatively poor infrastructure quality outcomes.

**Mechanism**
This is a descriptive per-capita statistic rather than a causal claim; dividing total national infrastructure spend by population provides a normalised basis for international comparison, controlling for New Zealand's small population size relative to larger OECD economies.

**Evidence**
Te Waihanga (NZ Infrastructure Commission), National Infrastructure Plan 2026, Section 3.2. Evidence strength is descriptive; the figure is for the single year 2022, inflation-adjusted to 2025 NZD, and is not necessarily representative of the 20-year average (5.8% of GDP/year) referenced in the same passage.

**Dependencies**
Related to claim-0118 — this per-person figure and the 5.8%-of-GDP-per-year figure both describe NZ's comparatively high overall infrastructure spending, which the Plan elsewhere pairs with NZ's comparatively low ranking on spending efficiency ("bang for buck"). Related to claim-0123 — the per-person spending figure aggregates across the same total infrastructure investment that the ownership breakdown (44% commercial/private, 31% local government, 25% central government) disaggregates by funder.

**Policy implications**
- Public communication about infrastructure investment should use this per-person framing carefully, since it can be read as evidence of either generosity or inefficiency depending on what quality benchmark is paired with it.
- International per-capita comparisons of infrastructure spending should specify the reference year and inflation-adjustment basis, given that this figure is a single-year (2022) snapshot in 2025 NZD rather than an average.
- The juxtaposition with "infrastructure quality lags relative to what we spend" should be used by Treasury and Te Waihanga to argue for procurement and delivery reform rather than spending increases.
- This metric could be tracked over time as a headline indicator of whether infrastructure efficiency improves following resource management and procurement reforms.
- Policymakers comparing NZ's infrastructure spending to international peers should pair per-capita spending figures with quality or efficiency metrics to avoid misleading conclusions.

**Relevant institutions**
Te Waihanga, Treasury, Stats NZ.

---
## claim-0125: Time-of-use charging modelling suggests 35% less Auckland congestion delay, 20% less capital spend needed

**Statement**
Modelling for Auckland suggests time-of-use road charging could cut excess congestion delay by around 35% and deliver equivalent road network performance with roughly 20% less new capital investment than otherwise required.

**Meaning (plain English)**
Charging drivers more to use congested roads at peak times, instead of building more roads, could cut traffic delays by about a third in Auckland while letting the city spend a fifth less on new road construction to get the same level of network performance.

**Why it matters**
This is the key evidence base behind the Plan's recommendation to implement time-of-use charging in Auckland — it suggests pricing reform could be a substantially cheaper and more effective congestion solution than continued road-capacity expansion, which is highly relevant to Auckland Transport and central government transport investment decisions.

**Mechanism**
Time-of-use charging works by shifting some trips away from the most congested peak periods (to other times, modes, or routes), which reduces the peak traffic volumes that drive excess delay; because peak capacity is the most expensive capacity to provide, even modest demand-shifting can disproportionately reduce the need for costly new road capacity.

**Evidence**
Te Waihanga (NZ Infrastructure Commission), National Infrastructure Plan 2026, Section 3.3, citing "previous modelling for Auckland" — a secondary citation of earlier modelling work rather than new Commission analysis. Evidence strength is model-based, and the claim is explicitly hedged ("suggests," not a guaranteed outcome).

**Dependencies**
Related to claim-0126 — both claims sit within the Plan's broader push to get more value from existing and planned transport infrastructure (pricing reform for the existing road network here; upzoning to capture more value from transport infrastructure investment in claim-0126), reflecting a common "use what we have better" theme in the Plan's ten priorities.

**Policy implications**
- Central government and Auckland Transport should prioritise implementing time-of-use charging and fleetwide road user charges as a cost-effective alternative to further road-capacity expansion.
- Current national road pricing tools (fuel excise duty, road user charges) should be reformed to allow localised, time-varying pricing that reflects actual congestion patterns rather than a flat national rate.
- The modelled 20% capital-cost saving should be incorporated into Auckland transport investment business cases as an explicit alternative scenario to capacity expansion.
- Given Auckland's congestion costs are estimated to reach $2.6 billion by 2026, the relative cost-effectiveness of pricing reform versus capital investment should be revisited regularly as congestion costs evolve.
- Because the evidence is based on "previous modelling" rather than current real-world implementation data, government should commission updated modelling or pilot evaluation once time-of-use charging is introduced.

**Relevant institutions**
Te Waihanga, Waka Kotahi NZ Transport Agency, Auckland Transport, Auckland Council, Treasury.

---
## claim-0126: Plan recommends upzoning around transport corridors to capture infrastructure investment value

**Statement**
The Plan recommends upzoning around key transport corridors as one of ten priorities for the decade, arguing that enabling more housing in places with existing or planned infrastructure capacity (e.g. around Auckland's City Rail Link stations) increases the number of people who benefit from and pay for that infrastructure.

**Meaning (plain English)**
When New Zealand builds expensive infrastructure like a new rail line, it should also allow many more homes to be built near the stations, so that more people can actually use the new infrastructure and help pay for it through rates and fares, rather than restricting nearby areas to low-density housing.

**Why it matters**
This is one of Te Waihanga's ten national infrastructure priorities for the decade and connects directly to ongoing Auckland upzoning debates (e.g., near Kingsland and Mount Eden stations), making the case that land-use and infrastructure planning should be integrated rather than treated separately.

**Mechanism**
Zoning rules that restrict housing density near transport infrastructure limit the number of households able to access and fund that infrastructure; relaxing those restrictions increases ridership, rates revenue, and cost-recovery per dollar of infrastructure spent, improving the return on existing and planned investment such as the City Rail Link.

**Evidence**
Te Waihanga (NZ Infrastructure Commission), National Infrastructure Plan 2026, Section 6.1, "Commit to upzoning around key transport corridors," part of the Commission's Forward Guidance ten priorities. Evidence strength is observational, drawing on the Plan's own analysis of population growth as a driver of infrastructure investment and the cited example of Auckland Council's plan change near Kingsland and Mount Eden stations.

**Dependencies**
Related to claim-0127 — the recommendation to upzone is supported by Australian (Infrastructure Victoria) modelling showing dispersed-city growth costs around NZD$68,000 more per home to service than compact-city growth, which the Plan cites as evidence for why upzoning near existing infrastructure is more cost-effective than dispersed development. Related to claim-0125 — both reflect the Plan's broader theme of extracting more value from existing infrastructure (pricing reform for roads in claim-0125; density uplift near transit in claim-0126) rather than building new capacity.

**Policy implications**
- Central and local government should target upzoning specifically at areas with spare or cost-effective-to-expand capacity in water and transport networks, not apply blanket upzoning everywhere.
