- Auckland Council's ongoing plan changes near rail stations (e.g., Kingsland, Mount Eden) should be treated as a model for sequencing upzoning with existing infrastructure capacity elsewhere in NZ.
- Funding mechanisms should be designed so that councils like Tauranga, which the Plan notes can "lose money on growth," do not face a financial disincentive to enable growth near infrastructure.
- Integrated spatial planning reforms (replacing the RMA framework) should formally link transport investment decisions to upzoning commitments to ensure the value-capture logic is realised in practice.
- Performance monitoring of infrastructure investments like the City Rail Link should include housing/density uplift near stations as a key success metric, not just transport patronage alone.

**Relevant institutions**
Te Waihanga, Auckland Council, HUD, MBIE, Waka Kotahi NZ Transport Agency.

---
## claim-0127: Australian modelling finds dispersed-city growth costs ~NZ$68,000 more per home to service than compact growth

**Statement**
Modelling by Infrastructure Victoria (Australia) found that the infrastructure required to service each home in a more dispersed city cost about AU$59,000 (around NZ$68,000) more than in a more compact city, with more consolidated cities found to have stronger economies and more affordable infrastructure.

**Meaning (plain English)**
Australian research found that building homes in spread-out, low-density suburbs costs tens of thousands of dollars more per home in infrastructure (roads, pipes, etc.) than building the same homes in a more compact, denser layout — and compact cities also tend to have stronger economies.

**Why it matters**
This is the key piece of international evidence Te Waihanga cites to support its recommendation for upzoning around NZ transport corridors; it provides a concrete dollar figure that NZ policymakers can use to argue that compact growth is fiscally cheaper for infrastructure providers and ratepayers than continued urban sprawl.

**Mechanism**
Dispersed development requires longer roads, pipes, and other linear infrastructure to service the same number of homes compared to compact development, where infrastructure networks can be shared across more households per kilometre of network — so cost per home rises as density falls.

**Evidence**
Cited in Te Waihanga, National Infrastructure Plan 2026, Section 6.1, attributing the modelling to Infrastructure Victoria (Australia). This is Australian evidence used as supportive context for NZ policy, not original New Zealand-specific modelling; evidence strength is model-based, and the NZD figure is the Plan's own approximate currency conversion of the original AUD estimate, not an independently modelled New Zealand cost.

**Dependencies**
Supports: claim-0126 — this Australian cost evidence is cited directly by the Plan as the empirical justification for its recommendation to commit to upzoning around key NZ transport corridors, on the logic that compact growth near existing infrastructure is cheaper to service than dispersed growth.

**Policy implications**
- NZ councils and central government should treat this figure as indicative, not precise, when building the fiscal case for compact growth — it is an Australian estimate with a rough currency conversion, not a New Zealand-specific costing.
- Te Waihanga or MBIE should consider commissioning equivalent New Zealand-specific modelling of dispersed versus compact servicing costs to validate or refine this imported estimate.
- Infrastructure funding policy (e.g., development contributions, growth infrastructure charges) could be informed by this cost differential to ensure dispersed development bears its true servicing cost.
- This evidence strengthens the policy case for sequencing greenfield growth approvals behind brownfield/compact intensification where existing infrastructure has spare capacity.
- Because "compact" and "dispersed" are not precisely defined in this passage, any NZ application should pair this figure with locally defined density thresholds before using it in formal cost-benefit analysis.

**Relevant institutions**
Te Waihanga, MBIE, HUD, local government, Auckland Council.
---
## claim-0128: NZ Government supported or supported in-principle all 16 National Infrastructure Plan recommendations

**Statement**
The New Zealand Government "Supported" or "Supported In-Principle" all 16 recommendations put forward in the National Infrastructure Plan 2026, as stated in the Ministerial Foreword to the Government Response.

**Meaning (plain English)**
Every one of the 16 recommendations from the Infrastructure Commission's 2026 Plan got at least a qualified yes from the Government — none were rejected outright. But "supported" and "supported in-principle" are two different things: the first means the Government is acting on the recommendation roughly as proposed, while the second means it agrees with the goal but is doing it a different way.

**Why it matters**
This sets the framing for how to read the entire Government Response document — universal nominal acceptance signals political alignment with Te Waihanga's diagnosis of NZ's infrastructure problems, but it does not guarantee the Plan's specific implementation mechanisms will be adopted. For NZ infrastructure policy tracking, the real substance lies in which recommendations were "supported" outright versus "supported in-principle" with an alternative approach.

**Mechanism**
Not causal — this is a governance/process claim describing the Government's formal acceptance posture. It implies that subsequent recommendation-by-recommendation responses (such as claim-0131, claim-0132, claim-0133, claim-0134) should be read against a backdrop of declared full acceptance, even where implementation diverges from the Plan's specific proposals.

**Evidence**
NZ Government (2026), Government Response to the National Infrastructure Plan 2026, Ministerial Foreword (Hon Chris Bishop, Minister for Infrastructure), p.2. Evidence strength is descriptive — a direct statement of government position, not an empirical study.

**Dependencies**
None listed in the relationships block. (The claim file lists claim-0131 and claim-0132 under "Related" as examples of recommendation-specific responses that instantiate this overall acceptance, but no formal supports/qualifies/depends_on relationship is declared.)

**Policy implications**
- Analysts should not treat "supported" as equivalent across all 16 recommendations — the two-tier taxonomy (supported vs. supported in-principle) materially changes what commitment, if any, has been made.
- Full nominal acceptance of an independent infrastructure plan's recommendations is itself notable given NZ's history of plans being shelved (see claim-0129) — it raises the bar for follow-through.
- Cross-party comparison matters: the Green Party Response separately claims to support all 16 recommendations "in full," while flagging implementation concerns, suggesting cross-party consensus on goals but disagreement on mechanisms.
- Future monitoring of the Plan's recommendations should track delivery against the specific commitments in each recommendation's response, not just the headline "supported" status.
- Treasury and the Infrastructure Commission should establish a tracking mechanism to verify that "supported" and "supported in-principle" commitments are followed through, given the repeated historical pattern of unimplemented plans.

**Relevant institutions**
Treasury, Te Waihanga (NZ Infrastructure Commission), Minister for Infrastructure, Office of the Minister for Infrastructure.

---
## claim-0129: NZ has produced infrastructure plans in 2010, 2011, 2015, and 2026, with some recommendations repeated unchanged for over a decade

**Statement**
The 2026 National Infrastructure Plan is not New Zealand's first infrastructure plan — earlier plans were produced in 2010, 2011, and 2015 — and some recommendations in those earlier plans are identical to those put forward in the 2026 Plan, over a decade later.

**Meaning (plain English)**
New Zealand has tried to fix the same infrastructure problems at least four times since 2010, and some of the exact same recommendations keep reappearing without having been acted on. This is presented by the Minister as evidence that the problem isn't a lack of good ideas — it's a lack of follow-through.

**Why it matters**
This history is the central justification the Minister gives for why the 2026 Plan might be different: it was produced independently by Te Waihanga rather than by the government of the day, which the Minister suggests may improve durability of implementation. For NZ policy observers, it is a warning that headline government acceptance of recommendations (claim-0128) has not historically translated into delivery.

**Mechanism**
The Minister attributes the repeated-but-unimplemented pattern partly to earlier plans being produced by, or closely tied to, the government of the day — creating weaker durability across changes of government — contrasted with the 2026 Plan's arm's-length production by the Infrastructure Commission, separate from government. This is offered as an explanation for past failure and a rationale for expecting a different outcome this time, rather than as an empirically tested mechanism.

**Evidence**
NZ Government (2026), Government Response to the National Infrastructure Plan 2026, Ministerial Foreword, p.1. Evidence strength is descriptive — a ministerial statement of historical fact and interpretation, not a study.

**Dependencies**
None listed in the relationships block. (The claim file cross-references claim-0128 under "Related" as context — claim-0129's history of repeated unimplemented plans is the backdrop against which claim-0128's full acceptance of the 2026 Plan should be judged — but no formal relationship is declared.)

**Policy implications**
- Independent production of infrastructure plans (by Te Waihanga rather than government departments) may be a structural feature worth preserving across future plan cycles, on the Minister's own stated logic.
- Repeated identical recommendations over 15+ years is itself a measurable indicator of policy failure that could be tracked systematically rather than left as anecdote.
- Future infrastructure plans should include an explicit "implementation tracking" mechanism that follows recommendations across plan cycles and electoral terms.
- The pattern suggests institutional and fiscal-cycle barriers to long-horizon infrastructure delivery are more important than diagnostic gaps — policy attention should shift from identifying problems to building durable implementation mechanisms.
- Treasury and Te Waihanga should jointly maintain a public register mapping each plan's recommendations against subsequent delivery, to make repeat-recommendation patterns visible in real time rather than retrospectively.

**Relevant institutions**
Te Waihanga (NZ Infrastructure Commission), Treasury, Minister for Infrastructure.

---
## claim-0130: NZ ranks top 10% of OECD for infrastructure spend but bottom 10% for quality and efficiency

**Statement**
Over the last 20 years New Zealand spent on average around 5.8% of GDP per year on infrastructure, placing it in the top 10% of OECD countries for infrastructure spend, while ranking in the bottom 10% of the OECD for the quality and efficiency of those investments.

**Meaning (plain English)**
New Zealand is not under-spending on infrastructure compared to other rich countries — quite the opposite, it spends more than almost all of them. But it gets unusually poor value for that spending: lots of money goes in, but the quality and efficiency of the resulting infrastructure lags nearly the whole OECD.

**Why it matters**
This reframes the standard NZ political narrative that infrastructure problems stem from insufficient funding — the Government's own response states the core issue is poor value-for-money in how money already being spent is allocated and delivered, not a lack of capital. This shifts the policy focus from "spend more" to "spend better."

**Mechanism**
Not a causal claim in itself, but the source attributes the spend-quality gap to weaknesses spread across the investment lifecycle — from a lack of asset management and long-term plans, to inefficient funding and financing, to poor project selection, to the absence of benefits realisation reviews — rather than to any single bottleneck. This implies that fixing the gap requires lifecycle-wide reform rather than a single intervention such as increased funding.

**Evidence**
NZ Government (2026), Government Response to the National Infrastructure Plan 2026, Overview of the Government Response, p.6, citing Te Waihanga (NZ Infrastructure Commission) research. Evidence strength is descriptive — the figures are sourced to Te Waihanga analysis rather than independently derived or peer-reviewed by the Government itself.

**Dependencies**
None listed in the relationships block.

**Policy implications**
- Policy debate should shift away from "more infrastructure funding" framing toward "better infrastructure delivery systems" — asset management, project selection, and financing efficiency are the binding constraints, not capital availability.
- The Government should prioritise lifecycle-wide reforms (e.g. mandatory asset management plans, stronger project selection criteria, post-implementation benefits realisation reviews) over simply increasing capital allowances.
- Because NZ already ranks in the bottom 10% of the OECD on quality and efficiency, value-for-money metrics should be tracked and published over time to verify whether reforms (e.g. those responding to Recommendations 1, 5, 12) are actually closing the gap.
- Treasury's capital allowance advice (see claim-0131) should weight efficiency-of-delivery indicators alongside raw spending levels when assessing agency investment intentions.
- International benchmarking against OECD peers should be maintained as a standing accountability mechanism rather than a one-off finding.

**Relevant institutions**
Te Waihanga (NZ Infrastructure Commission), Treasury, OECD (as benchmark), MBIE.

---
## claim-0131: Treasury commits to using Te Waihanga's Forward Guidance as regular input to capital allowance advice (Recommendation 1)

**Statement**
In response to Recommendation 1 (needs-based capital allowances), the New Zealand Government committed that the Treasury will use the Infrastructure Commission's Forward Guidance as a regular input to medium-term fiscal projections and will use agencies' investment intentions to inform advice on future capital allowances.

**Meaning (plain English)**
The Government agreed to formally fold Te Waihanga's independent long-term infrastructure needs analysis ("Forward Guidance") into the Treasury's regular budget planning process, rather than treating it as a one-off reference document. Agencies' own stated investment plans will also feed into how much capital the Government allocates in future Budgets.

**Why it matters**
This is one of the more concrete and fully "Supported" (not merely "in-principle") commitments in the Government Response, giving Te Waihanga's independent forward-looking infrastructure needs assessment a recurring formal role in fiscal decision-making — a structural change to how capital allowances are set, rather than a one-off gesture.

**Mechanism**
By embedding Forward Guidance into the Treasury's standard advice process to the Minister of Finance, the mechanism aims to align capital allowances more closely with assessed long-term infrastructure need rather than ad hoc annual budget bargaining, narrowing the spend to quality/efficiency gap described in claim-0130 by improving how investment levels are set, not just how much is spent.

**Evidence**
NZ Government (2026), Government Response to the National Infrastructure Plan 2026, Recommendation 1: Needs-based capital allowances, p.12. Evidence strength is descriptive — a direct quotation of a government policy commitment, not an empirical study.

**Dependencies**
Claim-0128 (Related, not formally typed): this claim is one concrete instance of the Government's broader stated acceptance of all 16 Plan recommendations — it shows what "Supported" (the strongest response category) looks like in practice for one specific recommendation.

**Policy implications**
- Treasury should formalise Forward Guidance as a standing input in its medium-term fiscal projection methodology, with transparent documentation of how it is weighted against other factors (debt targets, operating balance targets, economic conditions).
- Because capital allowance decisions remain subject to multiple competing inputs, this commitment should not be read as guaranteeing Forward Guidance will be decisive — monitoring is needed to see whether it meaningfully shifts allocations.
- The Treasury's required Budget 2027 Policy paper reporting to Cabinet on how Forward Guidance and agency plans were considered creates a near-term accountability checkpoint worth tracking.
- Agencies should be incentivised to produce robust, comparable investment intentions data, since this now directly feeds capital allowance advice.
- This mechanism, if implemented as described, could partially address NZ's poor OECD ranking on investment quality and efficiency (claim-0130) by improving the needs-assessment stage of the investment lifecycle.

**Relevant institutions**
Treasury, Te Waihanga (NZ Infrastructure Commission), Minister of Finance.

---
## claim-0132: Government commits to regional spatial planning to align infrastructure investment under the LTMA and LGA (Recommendation 12)

**Statement**
The New Zealand Government's response to Recommendation 12 (integrated spatial planning) commits to regional spatial planning, led and informed by central government, that is intended to align infrastructure investment decisions under the Land Transport Management Act 2003 and the Local Government Act 2002 within regional spatial plans.

**Meaning (plain English)**
The Government plans to introduce a new system of regional spatial plans, with central government playing a leading role, that would coordinate transport funding decisions and local council funding decisions so they pull in the same direction within each region, rather than being made separately under different laws.

**Why it matters**
Fragmented planning and investment decision-making across multiple statutes (transport law, local government law, resource management law) has been a long-standing critique of NZ's planning system; this commitment, if delivered, would be a significant structural reform aimed at integrating infrastructure, housing, business development and environmental planning under one regional framework.

**Mechanism**
Regional spatial plans are intended to both inform and be informed by national infrastructure priorities, guided by an understanding of delivery costs and demand pressures, and to align funding and investment decisions made under separate pieces of legislation (LTMA 2003, LGA 2002) so that transport and local government funding decisions are coordinated within a single regional planning document rather than determined independently.

**Evidence**
NZ Government (2026), Government Response to the National Infrastructure Plan 2026, Recommendation 12: Integrated spatial planning, p.24-25. Evidence strength is descriptive — a statement of policy intent and design, not an empirical evaluation.

**Dependencies**
Claim-0128 (Related, not formally typed): this is a specific instance of the Government's overall "Supported" or "Supported in-principle" acceptance of all 16 recommendations — here classified as fully "Supported," the document's strongest response category, though the underlying mechanism is not yet operative (see Policy implications).

**Policy implications**
- This commitment is contingent on passage of the Natural Environment Bill and Planning Bill, which were before the Environment Select Committee at the time of writing (due to report back by 26 June 2026) — implementation timing should be treated as uncertain, not settled.
- Under the Bills as introduced, the first core suite of national instruments would not be issued until roughly 9 months after Royal Assent (estimated Q2/Q3 2027), meaning the spatial planning mechanism described is prospective rather than currently operative.
- The Government's new National Policy Statement for Infrastructure, and amendments to the National Policy Statements for Renewable Electricity Generation and Electricity Networks, are designed to integrate with this future spatial planning system — these interim instruments warrant monitoring as bridge measures.
- Local and regional government should prepare for a transition where LTMA and LGA-derived funding decisions become subject to alignment requirements within regional spatial plans, which may require new coordination capacity.
- Given NZ's history of infrastructure plans not being durably implemented (claim-0129), the multi-year, legislation-dependent nature of this reform makes it a high-priority candidate for independent implementation tracking by Te Waihanga.

**Relevant institutions**
HUD, MBIE, Te Waihanga, Auckland Council and regional councils, Ministry for the Environment, Waka Kotahi NZTA.

---
## claim-0133: Government response to multi-year budgeting recommendation is "Supported in-principle," citing existing multi-year appropriations (Recommendation 5)

**Statement**
The New Zealand Government's response to Recommendation 5 (multi-year budgeting) was "Supported in-principle" rather than "Supported," on the grounds that the existing annual appropriations system already incorporates multi-year features, including multi-year appropriations available for infrastructure spending over periods of up to five years.

**Meaning (plain English)**
Te Waihanga's plan recommended moving to multi-year infrastructure budgeting to give more funding certainty; the Government's response is, in effect, "we already do this in some ways," rather than adopting the specific reform proposed. It points to existing multi-year appropriations and several multi-year sector plans (health, schools, defence) as evidence the underlying goal is already substantially met.

**Why it matters**
This is a clear example of the "supported in-principle" category doing real work — the Government agrees with the goal (durable, multi-year funding certainty for infrastructure) but explicitly declines to adopt the Plan's proposed mechanism, instead defending the status quo system as largely adequate. It illustrates why claim-0128's headline "all 16 supported" framing can understate how much implementation actually changes.

**Mechanism**
The response argues the existing system already provides multi-year funding certainty through several channels — appropriations typically agreed for multiple years even though formally voted annually, multi-year appropriations available for periods up to five years, and delegated multi-year funding via agency balance sheets and depreciation — and cites three sector-specific long-horizon plans (Health Infrastructure Plan, 10-year pipeline of $20bn+; Long-Term Investment Plan for School Property, 10-year pipeline; Defence Capability Plan, 15-year horizon) as complementary evidence that planning horizons are already being extended in practice.

**Evidence**
NZ Government (2026), Government Response to the National Infrastructure Plan 2026, Recommendation 5: Multi-year budgeting, p.19. Evidence strength is descriptive — a government policy position statement citing existing mechanisms, not an independent empirical assessment of whether those mechanisms achieve equivalent outcomes to the Plan's recommendation.

**Dependencies**
Claim-0128 (Related, not formally typed): this is a worked example of "Supported in-principle," the weaker of the two response categories referenced in claim-0128's overall framing — it shows what that category means in practice (agreement with intent, divergence on mechanism). Claim-0135 (Related, not formally typed): the Green Party Response separately criticises the Government Policy Statement on Land Transport 2024-2027 — the funding mechanism this claim's "in-principle" response leaves intact — for misallocating capital toward new state highways over renewals/maintenance, which the Green Party argues sits at odds with this and other recommendations.

**Policy implications**
- Because "supported in-principle" here means the status quo system is being defended rather than reformed, observers should not assume infrastructure funding certainty has materially improved as a direct result of the Plan.
- The three multi-year sector pipelines cited (health, schools, defence) provide a useful precedent and template that could be extended to other infrastructure sectors (e.g. transport, water) if multi-year certainty is genuinely the goal.
- Treasury and sector agencies should assess whether existing multi-year appropriation mechanisms (up to five years) are actually being used at the scale needed, since their existence does not by itself demonstrate sufficient uptake.
- The Green Party's critique (claim-0135) of the GPS on Land Transport suggests that even where multi-year-style planning exists, the allocation within it can still diverge from the Plan's recommendations — multi-year funding certainty and well-targeted allocation are separate problems.
- Future infrastructure plan reviews should evaluate whether "supported in-principle" responses like this one deliver outcomes equivalent to the original recommendation, rather than treating the response category itself as evidence of adequate action.

**Relevant institutions**
Treasury, Ministry of Health, Ministry of Education, Ministry of Defence, Te Waihanga.

---
## claim-0134: Gas Transition Loan Guarantee Scheme to make up to $1.2 billion in bank loans available to reduce gas reliance (Recommendation 14)

**Statement**
In response to Recommendation 14 (accelerated electricity investment), the New Zealand Government's Gas Transition Loan Guarantee Scheme, announced as part of Budget 2026, is expected to make up to $1.2 billion of bank loans available to businesses looking to reduce their reliance on gas.

**Meaning (plain English)**
The Government is setting up a scheme where it guarantees bank loans (up to $1.2 billion in total) to help businesses pay for the upfront cost of switching away from gas, as part of its response to the Infrastructure Commission's recommendation on speeding up electricity investment.

**Why it matters**
This is a concrete, costed financial instrument within the Government's broader "Supported" response to electricity investment — it signals a finance-side intervention (loan guarantees, which reduce risk for lenders and can lower borrowing costs) rather than direct capital expenditure or regulatory mandate, reflecting an investment-incentive policy lever specific to the energy transition.

**Mechanism**
A loan guarantee scheme reduces the credit risk borne by commercial banks when lending to businesses for gas-substitution investments (e.g. electrification of process heat), which is expected to lower the cost of capital and broaden access to finance for businesses that might otherwise be unable to secure affordable loans for the transition, thereby accelerating private electricity-related investment without direct government expenditure on the underlying assets.

**Evidence**
NZ Government (2026), Government Response to the National Infrastructure Plan 2026, Recommendation 14: Accelerated electricity investment, p.29. Evidence strength is descriptive — a statement of an announced policy commitment and its expected scale, not an evaluation of its effects.

**Dependencies**
Claim-0128 (Related, not formally typed): this is one of five specific measures listed under the Government's overall "Supported" response to Recommendation 14, itself one instance of the broader "supported or supported in-principle" acceptance of all 16 Plan recommendations described in claim-0128.

**Policy implications**
- The scheme is not yet in effect at time of publication — administered by the Treasury and scheduled to launch in July or August 2026, available for three years — so its actual uptake and impact should be monitored once operative rather than assumed from the announcement.
- As a loan guarantee rather than a grant or direct subsidy, the fiscal exposure to the Crown is contingent (a guarantee, triggered only on default) rather than an upfront cash cost, which has different budgetary implications than direct capital grants.
- This measure sits alongside four other measures under Recommendation 14 (Fast-track Approvals Act 2024 changes, a proposed winter energy reliability obligation, stronger Electricity Authority enforcement powers, and enhanced gas supply/demand disclosure under the Gas Act 1992) — its effectiveness should be assessed as part of that bundle, not in isolation.
- The Green Party Response dissents from a related element of the Government's energy work programme — its commitment to an LNG terminal — calling instead for a National Electrification Strategy; this signals unresolved cross-party disagreement on the broader energy transition pathway even where this specific loan scheme is uncontested.
- Treasury and the Electricity Authority should track whether the scheme's $1.2 billion ceiling is reached, under-subscribed, or insufficient relative to actual business demand for gas-substitution finance, to inform any extension or redesign after the three-year window.

**Relevant institutions**
Treasury, Electricity Authority, MBIE, Energy Efficiency and Conservation Authority (EECA).

---
## claim-0135: Green Party argues the GPS on Land Transport prioritises new state highway capital spending over renewals and maintenance, at odds with Plan recommendations

**Statement**
The Green Party Response argues that the Government Policy Statement (GPS) on Land Transport 2024-2027 — which the Government's own response relies on as a guiding mechanism for new capital investment — allocates significantly more funding to new state highway capital expenditure than to any other activity type, including renewals and maintenance, which the Green Party states is at odds with Recommendations 1, 2, 7, and 13 of the National Infrastructure Plan.

**Meaning (plain English)**
The Greens, in their dissenting section of the Government Response document, point out that the transport funding rulebook (the GPS) puts far more money into building new state highways than into maintaining or renewing existing roads and other transport assets — and argue this contradicts several of the Infrastructure Commission's own recommendations about how infrastructure money should be prioritised.

**Why it matters**
This is the clearest documented internal inconsistency in the Government Response: the same document treats the GPS as a legitimate mechanism for implementing Plan recommendations (e.g. claim-0133's multi-year budgeting response) while a coalition-government-included dissenting voice argues the GPS's actual funding allocation undermines multiple recommendations. It illustrates a gap between formal acceptance of a recommendation's intent and the practical effect of the specific instruments relied on to deliver it.

**Mechanism**
Not a causal claim — it is a critique of a funding allocation pattern. The implied logic is that asset management and value-for-money recommendations (consistent with claim-0130's finding that NZ ranks poorly on investment quality/efficiency despite high spending) require balanced investment across new capital, renewals, and maintenance; skewing funding heavily toward new state highway capital expenditure, the Greens argue, perpetuates the underlying poor-asset-management pattern the Plan was meant to fix.

**Evidence**
NZ Government (2026), Government Response to the National Infrastructure Plan 2026, Green Party Response (Hon Julie Anne Genter), p.4. Evidence strength is descriptive — this is a political party's stated position included within the Government Response document, not the Government's own assessment, and not an independent empirical analysis of the GPS allocations.

**Dependencies**
Claim-0133 (Related, formally typed in the claim file): the Government's "supported in-principle" response to Recommendation 5 on multi-year budgeting relies in part on the existing GPS-based funding framework as evidence of adequate multi-year planning; this claim directly challenges the adequacy of that same framework, arguing its allocation pattern undercuts other recommendations even where multi-year mechanics exist on paper.

**Policy implications**
- This critique should be read as a dissenting/opposition view embedded within the Government Response, not a government commitment or finding — it should not be cited as if it were the Government's own assessment.
- Because the Green Party states it otherwise supports all 16 recommendations "in full," this is narrowly a critique of one implementation instrument (the GPS), not a rejection of the Plan's substance — useful for distinguishing recommendation-level consensus from mechanism-level disagreement.
- The Green Party's partial counterpoint — that the Government response notes a larger share of rail spending is going to asset maintenance and renewals — suggests the inconsistency may be mode-specific (road versus rail) rather than systemic across all transport spending.
- Waka Kotahi NZTA and the Ministry of Transport should be asked to reconcile the GPS's road capital/renewals/maintenance split against Recommendations 1, 2, 7, and 13 to clarify whether the Green Party's characterisation is accurate and whether revision is warranted.
- Future GPS funding statements should be assessed against Plan recommendations at the time of drafting, rather than leaving the consistency check to be raised retrospectively by a dissenting party within the response document.

**Relevant institutions**
Waka Kotahi NZTA, Ministry of Transport, Treasury, Te Waihanga.
---
## claim-0136: Definition of absorptive capacity

**Statement**
The New Zealand Productivity Commission defines "absorptive capacity" as a broad concept covering physical infrastructure (transport, communications), land supply and housing infrastructure, core public health and education services, and broader community infrastructure; this capacity is not a fixed constraint in the long run, but it limits how quickly a country can successfully accommodate population growth in the short run.

**Meaning (plain English)**
"Absorptive capacity" is the Commission's term for everything a country needs in place — roads, pipes, schools, hospitals, housing — to comfortably absorb new people, whether they arrive through immigration or natural population growth. It can be expanded over time with investment, but in the short term it acts as a real limit.

**Why it matters**
This concept reframes debates about immigration levels in New Zealand: the question is not simply "how many migrants" but "does the country have the infrastructure and services to accommodate them well." It shifts policy focus toward infrastructure investment and planning rather than migrant numbers alone.

**Mechanism**
Population growth (from any source) places demand on a relatively fixed short-run stock of physical and social infrastructure; until that stock is expanded through investment, rapid growth generates congestion, housing shortages, and strain on services, even though the same growth could be absorbed smoothly given sufficient lead time and investment.

**Evidence**
NZ Productivity Commission (2022) inquiry report, drawing on its own deliberation and submissions; evidence strength is rated descriptive (a conceptual/definitional finding rather than a quantitative estimate).

**Dependencies**
None listed in the relationships block beyond related (non-hierarchical) links: claim-0137 (the $104bn/$106bn NZ infrastructure gap, which quantifies the absorptive-capacity constraint), claim-0094 (a secondary citation of this report's productivity conclusion in a Koi Tū population strategy report), and claim-0161 (SVAR evidence that immigration shocks raise house prices and household credit, consistent with housing being a binding absorptive-capacity constraint).

**Policy implications**
- Immigration policy should be explicitly sequenced with infrastructure investment planning rather than set independently of it.
- Restricting immigration can relieve short-run pressure but does not fix the underlying infrastructure shortfall — investment is the durable solution.
- Because NZ's capacity pressures predate the pre-Covid migration surge, addressing them is a general infrastructure-policy problem, not solely an immigration-policy one.
- Treasury, HUD, and Te Waihanga should use a shared absorptive-capacity framework when advising on population-linked policy (immigration, housing, regional growth).
- Long-run capacity expansion plans should explicitly model the time lag between committing to investment and capacity coming online, since short-run rigidity is the core constraint.

**Relevant institutions**
NZ Productivity Commission, MBIE, Immigration NZ, Treasury, HUD, Te Waihanga, Auckland Council.

---
## claim-0137: NZ's $104 billion infrastructure gap and $106 billion future infrastructure gap

**Statement**
New Zealand's public-sector "infrastructure gap" — the value of infrastructure the country should have built but has not — was estimated at $104 billion, with a further "future infrastructure gap" of $106 billion over the next 30 years representing the difference between planned and required government infrastructure spending.

**Meaning (plain English)**
New Zealand is already roughly $104 billion behind on public infrastructure it should have built by now, and on current spending plans it will fall a further $106 billion short of what it needs over the next three decades. These are large, persistent shortfalls in roads, water systems, hospitals, schools, and similar public assets.

**Why it matters**
This gap is the quantitative backbone of the absorptive-capacity argument: it shows New Zealand's infrastructure shortfall is large and structural, not a temporary blip caused by a recent migration surge, and that closing it requires sustained, large-scale capital investment regardless of immigration settings.

**Mechanism**
The gap reflects cumulative underinvestment relative to population and economic growth — a descriptive stock-and-flow estimate rather than a causal model; it implies that, absent a step-change in investment, existing pressures on housing, transport, water, health and education infrastructure will persist and likely worsen as the population grows.

**Evidence**
Figures originate from the NZ Infrastructure Commission (Te Waihanga), cited via Treasury's 2022 Investment Statement and reported in NZPC (2022); evidence strength is rated observational.

**Dependencies**
Related to claim-0136 (the definition of absorptive capacity that this figure quantifies) and claim-0130 (a 2026 NZ Government figure showing NZ spends a high share of GDP on infrastructure — around 5.8%, top decile of the OECD — but ranks in the bottom decile for quality and efficiency of that spending, suggesting the gap is partly a value-for-money problem rather than purely an investment-volume problem).

**Policy implications**
- Closing the infrastructure gap requires multi-decade, cross-government capital planning, not one-off funding boosts tied to immigration cycles.
- Given NZ's poor OECD ranking on infrastructure investment efficiency (claim-0130), more money alone may not close the gap — project selection, asset management, and delivery capability need parallel reform.
- The gap should be treated as a baseline planning input for any Government Policy Statement on immigration (see claim-0148), since absorptive capacity is explicitly tied to infrastructure investment.
- Because privately funded infrastructure has generally kept pace with demand, public capital programmes (especially water, transport, and health/education) should be prioritised for catch-up investment.
- Housing-enabling infrastructure (water connections, build-ready land) is flagged as a gap even within otherwise well-functioning private infrastructure markets, warranting targeted attention from HUD and local government.

**Relevant institutions**
Te Waihanga (NZ Infrastructure Commission), Treasury, MBIE, HUD, local government, NZ Productivity Commission.

---
## claim-0138: Skill-selective immigration produced a net "brain exchange" for New Zealand

**Statement**
Because New Zealand's immigration system selects mostly on skill, immigration has more than offset the outward "brain drain" of skilled New Zealanders, producing a net "brain exchange": in 2015-16, 40% of New Zealand's tertiary-educated resident population were immigrants, while only 21% of New Zealand citizens living offshore had a tertiary education.

**Meaning (plain English)**
Even though many skilled New Zealanders leave the country, the skill-selective immigration system brings in even more skilled people than it loses — so on balance New Zealand has gained, not lost, tertiary-educated people through migration, at least as of the mid-2010s.

**Why it matters**
This complicates a common public narrative that New Zealand suffers a one-way "brain drain." It suggests skill-selective immigration settings have historically been an effective offsetting mechanism, which is policy-relevant if New Zealand considers loosening skill selectivity in favour of other goals (e.g., addressing low-wage labour shortages).

**Mechanism**
New Zealand's points-based and skill-targeted visa categories filter for higher educational attainment among arriving migrants, while emigrating New Zealanders are a less skill-selected group (driven by lifestyle, family, and overseas opportunity factors as much as skill-related pull factors); the compositional difference between these two flows produces a net skill gain in the resident population.

**Evidence**
NZPC (2022), citing Carey (2019) analysis of 2015-16 stock data; evidence strength is rated observational.

**Dependencies**
Related to claim-0139 (documents that the skilled temporary visa categories have become relatively less skilled since 2012, which could erode the brain-exchange effect going forward) and claim-0094 (a Koi Tū report's more cautious framing of the same dynamic, alongside NZ's declining Global Talent Competitiveness Index ranking and 2024 net emigration spike of roughly 44,000 — developments after the period this claim's data covers).

**Policy implications**
- Skill-selectivity in residence-category immigration settings should be preserved or strengthened, given its demonstrated role in offsetting brain drain historically.
- The brain-exchange finding is a stock measure from 2015-16 and should not be assumed to hold automatically in subsequent years, especially given the 2024 net emigration spike and falling GTCI ranking documented elsewhere in this corpus.
- MBIE and Immigration NZ should monitor the skill composition of both inflows and outflows on an ongoing basis rather than relying on point-in-time studies.
- Policy debates about loosening skill thresholds for temporary visas should weigh the risk of eroding the brain-exchange advantage against labour-shortage relief.
- Better data on the skill levels of departing NZ citizens (a documented gap) would improve the precision of future brain-exchange assessments.

**Relevant institutions**
MBIE, Immigration NZ, Stats NZ, NZ Productivity Commission, Education NZ.

---
## claim-0139: Skilled temporary visa categories have become relatively less skilled since 2012

**Statement**
Since 2012, an increasing share of people approved within the Essential Skills and Work to Residence temporary visa categories have been at lower ANZSCO skill levels (levels 4 and 5), while approvals at higher skill levels (1 and 2) have remained stable in number, making New Zealand's skilled temporary visa categories relatively less skilled over this period.

**Meaning (plain English)**
Over the decade or so before this report, New Zealand's "skilled" temporary work visa system let in more and more people classified as lower-skilled, while the number of genuinely high-skilled approvals stayed flat — so the average skill level of these visa categories drifted downward, not because high-skill immigration fell but because low-skill immigration grew faster.

**Why it matters**
This is a structural shift that, left unaddressed, can dilute the skill-selectivity advantage documented in claim-0138 and feed into the low-skill, low-wage labour market dynamics described in claim-0142, with direct relevance to New Zealand's persistent productivity underperformance.

**Mechanism**
Employer demand for low-cost labour, combined with relatively permissive eligibility settings in the Essential Skills and Work to Residence categories, drew increasing numbers of lower-ANZSCO-level approvals; because this growth occurred in the lower tiers while higher-skill approvals stayed flat, the average composition shifted downward — a composition effect rather than a decline in absolute high-skill numbers.

**Evidence**
NZPC (2022), citing MBIE (2022b) approvals data, Figure 2.7, covering 2012-2021; evidence strength is rated observational.

**Dependencies**
Related to claim-0138 (the brain-exchange finding that this compositional shift could undermine over time) and claim-0142 (the low-skill, low-wage labour market trap mechanism, to which this compositional shift is a contributing input).

**Policy implications**
- Visa eligibility settings for temporary work categories should include skill-level monitoring to detect and correct compositional drift toward lower-skill approvals.
- The shift to the Accredited Employer Work Visa's wage-threshold proxy for skill (replacing ANZSCO assessment) should be evaluated against this historical compositional trend to check whether it corrects or perpetuates the drift.
- MBIE should track skill-level composition of temporary visa approvals as a standard part of immigration system reporting, not as an occasional research exercise.
- Policymakers should distinguish between absolute and compositional changes in skilled migration statistics when communicating about immigration policy outcomes.
- Any future Skill Shortage List reform (see claim-0146/claim-0147) should account for this trend when assessing whether shortage-driven approvals are reinforcing low-skill reliance.

**Relevant institutions**
MBIE, Immigration NZ, NZ Productivity Commission.

---
## claim-0140: NZ produces US$68 of output per hour worked versus an OECD average of US$85

**Statement**
In 2019/20, New Zealand produced US$68 of output per hour worked compared with an OECD average of US$85, and New Zealand's labour productivity has remained well below the OECD average over 2000-2020, with the gap widening over that period.

**Meaning (plain English)**
For every hour worked, the average New Zealand worker produces noticeably less economic output than the average OECD worker — and that productivity gap has been getting wider, not narrower, over the past two decades.

**Why it matters**
Labour productivity, not just GDP growth, is what ultimately drives sustainable wage growth and living standards; this persistent and widening gap is central evidence behind the Commission's conclusion that New Zealand has a productivity problem that immigration alone cannot fix.

**Mechanism**
This is a descriptive statistic rather than a causal claim: it implies New Zealand firms and workers are combining capital, technology, skills, and organisational practices less effectively per hour worked than the OECD average, pointing to underlying drivers such as low capital intensity, firm scale, and weak diffusion of best-practice technology and management, rather than to any single cause.

**Evidence**
NZPC (2022), citing NZPC (2021f) and OECD data, Figure 2.13, reporting GDP per hour worked in 2015 US$; evidence strength is rated observational.

**Dependencies**
Related to claim-0141 (shows this productivity gap exists despite NZ's GDP growth keeping pace with OECD peers — the shortfall is in output per hour, not aggregate output) and claim-0094 (a Koi Tū secondary citation of this broader productivity narrative).

**Policy implications**
- Productivity-focused policy (capital investment, skills, technology diffusion, competition policy) should be prioritised over labour-supply expansion as the primary lever for raising living standards.
- Immigration settings should not be evaluated primarily on their ability to close this productivity gap, since the Commission finds immigration is neither its cause nor its likely solution.
- Treasury and MBIE should track output-per-hour trends as a headline economic indicator distinct from GDP growth or employment growth.
- Sector-level productivity diagnostics may be more useful than aggregate figures for identifying where New Zealand's productivity shortfall is concentrated.
- International benchmarking (OECD comparators) should remain a standard feature of NZ productivity reporting to track whether the gap narrows or widens going forward.

**Relevant institutions**
NZ Productivity Commission, Treasury, MBIE, Stats NZ.

---
## claim-0141: NZ's GDP growth has relied on more workers and longer hours, not higher productivity

**Statement**
New Zealand's GDP growth over 2000-2020 kept pace with or exceeded other OECD comparator countries, but this was achieved by adding more workers (both local and migrant) to the labour force and by working longer hours, rather than by raising output per hour worked.

**Meaning (plain English)**
New Zealand's overall economy grew about as fast as other rich countries' economies over the last two decades, but it did so mainly by having more people working and working longer hours — not by each hour of work becoming more productive, which is the more sustainable source of rising living standards.

**Why it matters**
This distinction matters because GDP growth driven by labour input expansion is not the same as growth driven by productivity gains; it underpins the Commission's broader argument that New Zealand's growth model is reaching limits as population growth slows, and that immigration-led labour force expansion cannot substitute indefinitely for productivity improvement.

**Mechanism**
Aggregate GDP can rise either through more labour input (more workers, more hours) or through higher output per unit of labour input (productivity); New Zealand's growth accounting over this period attributes most of its GDP growth to the former channel, implying the latter — the channel most associated with rising wages and living standards independent of population growth — has been comparatively weak.

**Evidence**
NZPC (2022) Overview, summarising growth-accounting evidence; evidence strength is rated observational (classified in the YAML as causal_association, reflecting an accounting decomposition rather than an experimental or quasi-experimental causal estimate).

**Dependencies**
Related to claim-0140 (the output-per-hour gap that is the productivity-side counterpart to this growth-accounting finding) and claim-0094 (Koi Tū secondary citation of the same underlying productivity narrative).

**Policy implications**
- As population growth slows (whether from lower fertility or tighter immigration settings), GDP growth will increasingly depend on productivity gains that have so far been weak — a structural risk for future growth.
- Policy should not treat continued labour force expansion via immigration as a substitute for addressing the underlying productivity shortfall.
- Working-hours intensity as a growth driver has limits and equity costs (e.g., wellbeing, work-life balance) that should be weighed against its contribution to headline GDP figures.
- Treasury's long-term fiscal and economic projections should explicitly separate labour-input contributions from productivity contributions to growth.
- Immigration policy debates should be reframed around absorptive capacity and skill composition rather than treated as a primary productivity lever.

**Relevant institutions**
NZ Productivity Commission, Treasury, MBIE, Stats NZ.

---
## claim-0142: A low-skill, low-wage labour market trap from strong migration inflows

**Statement**
Strong migration inflows may restrict wage growth in some sectors, making jobs less attractive to local workers, which in turn encourages or reinforces some firms and industries to rely on low-cost, low-skill migrant labour rather than raising wages or investing in productivity-enhancing technology — a potential low-skill, low-wage labour market trap.

**Meaning (plain English)**
If employers can easily hire migrants for low wages, they have less incentive to pay more, train workers, or invest in better equipment — which can lock some industries into a cycle of low pay and low productivity that is hard to escape, and that also makes those jobs unappealing to local workers.

**Why it matters**
This mechanism is central to debates about whether NZ's reliance on migrant labour in certain sectors (hospitality, horticulture, aged care) is helping or hindering long-run productivity and wage growth, and whether immigration settings should be adjusted to break rather than reinforce the trap.

**Mechanism**
Easy access to low-cost migrant labour reduces the relative cost of the low-skill, low-wage production strategy compared with the alternative of raising wages and investing in capital or training; because both workers' and firms' incentives reinforce the equilibrium (workers don't invest in skills because few skilled vacancies exist, and firms don't create skilled vacancies because few skilled workers are available), the low-wage outcome can become self-perpetuating in affected industries.

**Evidence**
NZPC (2022), Box 3.5, drawing on comparator international evidence (UK: Wilson & Hogarth, 2003; Birmingham case study: Anderson et al., 2006) rather than NZ-specific causal estimation; evidence strength is rated analytical (a theoretical mechanism, contested rather than definitively proven for New Zealand).

**Dependencies**
Related to claim-0143 (NZ's low and declining returns to education, a contributing structural factor) and claim-0139 (the compositional shift toward lower-skilled temporary visa approvals since 2012, which is a plausible channel feeding this trap).

**Policy implications**
- Policy responses should focus on incentivising employer-led training and capital investment rather than primarily restricting employee access to low-wage work, per the cited evidence balance.
- Wage-floor or wage-threshold settings in temporary visa categories (e.g., AEWV median wage thresholds) can help counteract the trap by removing the low-cost migrant labour option as a substitute for investment.
- Sector-specific diagnostics (hospitality, horticulture, aged care, retail) would help identify where this trap is most active in the New Zealand economy.
- Skills and training policy should be coordinated with immigration policy, since the trap is a joint failure of both labour supply and employer investment incentives.
- The mechanism remains contested for New Zealand specifically; policy should be informed by monitoring local evidence rather than assuming the international case-study findings transfer directly.

**Relevant institutions**
MBIE, Immigration NZ, Tertiary Education Commission, NZ Productivity Commission, Treasury.

---
## claim-0143: NZ's low and declining returns to education

**Statement**
Returns to education in New Zealand are among the lowest in the OECD and have declined in recent years, suggesting a disconnect between the education system and the skill requirements of firms that can suppress investment and productivity improvements.

**Meaning (plain English)**
Getting more education or training in New Zealand pays off less, in wage terms, than it does in most other rich countries — and this payoff has been shrinking. That suggests the skills the education system produces and the skills employers actually need are increasingly mismatched.

**Why it matters**
Low returns to education weaken individuals' incentive to invest in skills and employers' incentive to create skilled roles, which compounds the low-skill, low-wage trap (claim-0142) and limits the effectiveness of skills-based immigration and domestic workforce policy alike.

**Mechanism**
This is a descriptive trend rather than an explicit causal claim in the source; it implies a possible mismatch between education-system outputs and labour-market skill demand, where low and falling wage premiums for education reduce both worker incentives to upskill and firm incentives to create higher-skill, higher-productivity roles, potentially forming a self-reinforcing low-expectations cycle.

**Evidence**
NZPC (2022), Box 3.5, citing Conway (2018); evidence strength is rated observational. No specific percentage figures are given in the available extracted text.

**Dependencies**
Related to claim-0142 (the low-skill, low-wage labour market trap, of which this finding is presented as a contributing structural factor rather than a consequence of immigration policy specifically).

**Policy implications**
- Education and training policy should be re-examined for alignment with actual employer skill demand, potentially through closer industry-education partnerships.
- Low returns to education should be treated as a domestic labour-market and education-system issue, not one to be solved primarily through immigration settings.
- The Tertiary Education Commission and MBIE should jointly monitor returns-to-education trends as an indicator of education-system effectiveness.
- Addressing this disconnect could improve the effectiveness of "use immigration to fill skill gaps" policies, since a better-aligned domestic skills pipeline reduces reliance on migrant labour for the same roles.
- Given the risk of a self-reinforcing low-expectations cycle, early and visible policy signals (e.g., employer incentives to create skilled roles) may be needed to break the equilibrium rather than relying on gradual market correction.

**Relevant institutions**
Tertiary Education Commission, Ministry of Education, MBIE, NZ Productivity Commission.

---
## claim-0144: Employer tying enables monopsony power and blocks productivity-enhancing job mobility

**Statement**
Requiring migrants to work for a single sponsoring employer ("tying") increases employer monopsony power, which can drive down wages and conditions for both migrants and local workers and prevents job-to-job mobility — a recognised route to higher productivity — because workers cannot move to firms where they would be most productive.

**Meaning (plain English)**
When a migrant's visa only lets them work for one specific employer, that employer gains outsized bargaining power over them — the migrant cannot easily quit for a better job, so the employer can pay less and treat them worse without losing them. This also stops workers moving to jobs where they would be more productive, which is bad for the wider economy too.

**Why it matters**
This is a key mechanism behind both migrant worker exploitation risk and a structural drag on productivity in New Zealand's labour market, directly motivating the Commission's recommendation (claim-0145) to allow migrants to move between accredited employers.

**Mechanism**
Tying restricts the supply side of the labour market available to a tied worker to a single buyer (the sponsoring employer), giving that employer monopsony power to set wages and conditions below the competitive level; because workers cannot exit to better-matched or higher-paying firms, the broader economy also loses the productivity gains that come from workers reallocating toward firms where their marginal productivity is highest.

**Evidence**
NZPC (2022), Part 4, drawing on established international literature (OECD, 2021b on job-to-job mobility and productivity; Norlander, 2021 on guest-worker monopsony); evidence strength is rated analytical.

**Dependencies**
Related to claim-0145 (the Commission's direct policy recommendation flowing from this mechanism, to enable migrant mobility between accredited employers).

**Policy implications**
- Visa design should minimise employer-tying wherever possible, balancing local-worker displacement concerns against the monopsony and exploitation risks tying creates.
- Allowing job-to-job mobility for migrants should be paired with adequate employer accreditation checks to manage moral-hazard risks (e.g., collusive job changes without genuine employment intent).
- Labour Inspectorate resourcing (see claim-0149) is a necessary complement to any move toward greater migrant job mobility, to monitor for gaming and poor employer behaviour.
- Immigration NZ and MBIE should track migrant job-mobility outcomes under any reformed visa system as a core indicator of policy success.
- The original rationale for tying (linking to Labour Market Tests or Skill Shortage Lists to protect local workers) should be re-evaluated against the now-documented monopsony cost.

**Relevant institutions**
Immigration NZ, MBIE, Labour Inspectorate, NZ Productivity Commission.

---
## claim-0145: Recommendation to enable migrant job mobility under the Accredited Employer Work Visa

**Statement**
The Productivity Commission recommended that, as part of the Accredited Employer Work Visa design, the Government should enable migrants to move between any accredited employer with minimal government-imposed transaction costs, while collecting information on migrants' movements to monitor for system gaming and poor employer behaviour.

**Meaning (plain English)**
Rather than locking migrants into one employer, the Commission recommended letting them switch freely between any employer that has been accredited by the Government — as long as the system tracks these moves to catch abuse.

**Why it matters**
This recommendation directly targets the monopsony and exploitation problems identified in claim-0144 and was made at a time when the AEWV system (which requires employer accreditation checks) was about to be introduced, making it a timely and implementable reform.

**Mechanism**
This claim is a normative policy recommendation rather than a causal finding: it implies that if implemented, removing employer-tying within the accredited pool would restore competitive labour-market dynamics for migrant workers, reducing employer monopsony power and improving worker-firm matching, provided monitoring systems can detect and deter misuse.

**Evidence**
NZPC (2022), Part 4, Recommendation 6; evidence strength is rated descriptive (a policy recommendation, not an empirical estimate).

**Dependencies**
Depends on claim-0144 (the monopsony mechanism this recommendation is designed to address) and is related to claim-0149 (Labour Inspectorate under-resourcing, since adequate enforcement capacity is a necessary complement to safely enabling greater job mobility).

**Policy implications**
- Immigration NZ should design AEWV mobility provisions with built-in monitoring of migrant job-change patterns to detect gaming or collusion.
- Employer accreditation standards need to be robust enough to prevent the mobility reform from being undermined by weak or fraudulent accreditation.
- Implementation should be paired with increased Labour Inspectorate resourcing, since greater worker mobility without enforcement capacity could increase monitoring blind spots.
- The reform's success should be evaluated against measurable outcomes: migrant wage growth, exploitation complaint rates, and job-to-job transition rates.
- This recommendation illustrates a broader principle — that visa system design choices, not just visa volume settings, materially affect labour-market outcomes for both migrants and locals.

**Relevant institutions**
Immigration NZ, MBIE, Labour Inspectorate, NZ Productivity Commission.

---
## claim-0146: Skill Shortage Lists are backward-looking and prone to lobbying

**Statement**
Skill Shortage Lists used to grant employers easier access to migrant workers can become cumbersome, open to lobbying from employers and worker representatives, and are inherently backward-looking because they aim to solve labour shortages that have already emerged rather than anticipate future ones.

**Meaning (plain English)**
Lists of jobs deemed to be in "shortage" — which let employers more easily bring in migrant workers — tend to reflect yesterday's labour market problems, not tomorrow's, and tend to attract pressure from interest groups trying to get their occupation added or kept on the list.

**Why it matters**
If Skill Shortage Lists are poorly targeted and politically contested, they can misallocate migrant labour access, entrench reliance on migrant workers in particular sectors, and undermine the credibility and efficiency of the immigration system as a labour-market management tool.

**Mechanism**
Shortage lists are constructed retrospectively from observed labour-market data and stakeholder input, which means by the time a shortage is identified and a response implemented, market conditions may have already shifted; the same process is exposed to lobbying because being listed brings direct commercial benefit to employers (easier, lower-cost access to migrant labour), creating an incentive to influence list composition independent of genuine labour-market need.

**Evidence**
NZPC (2022), Part 4, Finding 15; evidence strength is rated analytical.

**Dependencies**
Related to claim-0147 (the Commission's recommendation to reduce reliance on shortage lists in favour of wage-based scarcity signals) and claim-0142 (the low-skill, low-wage labour market trap, to which easy shortage-list access can also contribute by reducing employer incentives to raise wages or invest).

**Policy implications**
- Skill Shortage Lists should be redesigned as data-driven, regularly reviewed tools rather than static lobbying targets.
- Lists should inform forward-looking workforce planning (education, training, active labour market programmes) rather than function primarily as a visa-access gatekeeping mechanism.
- Greater transparency in how occupations are added to or removed from shortage lists could reduce the scope for lobbying-driven distortion.
- MBIE should pair shortage-list design with wage-growth monitoring to detect when "shortages" reflect wage suppression rather than genuine scarcity.
- Any list-based exemption mechanism should have a sunset or mandatory review clause to prevent backward-looking entrenchment.

**Relevant institutions**
MBIE, Immigration NZ, Tertiary Education Commission, NZ Productivity Commission.

---
## claim-0147: Recommendation to let wages reflect scarcity and reduce reliance on shortage lists

**Statement**
The Productivity Commission recommended that the Government encourage wages to reflect labour-market scarcity rather than exempting specific industries, firms, and occupations from general labour-market rules, and reduce reliance on shortage lists for temporary work visas in favour of data-driven indicators (vacancy data, wage movements, demand for migrant labour) reviewed at least every two years.

**Meaning (plain English)**
Instead of giving particular industries special exemptions to bring in migrant workers, the Commission recommends letting wages rise when there's a genuine labour shortage (which is the normal market signal), and using shortage lists — if kept at all — as a more rigorously data-driven and frequently reviewed tool rather than a default visa-access shortcut.

**Why it matters**
This recommendation, if implemented, would shift New Zealand's immigration settings toward market-based scarcity signalling and away from administratively determined exemptions, with implications for wage growth, sectoral reliance on migrant labour, and the credibility of the immigration system.

**Mechanism**
This is a normative recommendation rather than an empirical finding: it implies that allowing wages to rise in response to genuine scarcity would both ration labour demand efficiently and create stronger incentives for employers to invest in training, capital, and productivity-enhancing practices, addressing the same root cause identified in the low-skill, low-wage trap (claim-0142).

**Evidence**
NZPC (2022), Part 4, Recommendations 4-5; evidence strength is rated descriptive (a policy recommendation).

**Dependencies**
Depends on claim-0146 (the documented limitations of existing Skill Shortage Lists that motivate this recommendation).

**Policy implications**
- MBIE should develop standardised, transparent indicators (vacancy data, wage growth, migrant labour demand) to underpin any remaining shortage-list mechanism.
- A mandatory two-year review cycle for shortage lists would reduce the risk of lists becoming outdated or captured by lobbying.
- Repurposing shortage lists to inform education, training, and active labour market programmes (rather than gatekeeping visa access) would align immigration policy more closely with workforce development policy.
- Implementation depends on complementary data infrastructure investments (e.g., vacancy indicators, labour-market modelling), which should be funded alongside the policy change.
- This recommendation should be read alongside the Government Policy Statement proposal (claim-0148), since both aim to make immigration-labour market linkages more transparent and rules-based.

**Relevant institutions**
MBIE, Immigration NZ, Tertiary Education Commission, NZ Productivity Commission.

---
## claim-0148: Recommendation for a Government Policy Statement on immigration

**Statement**
The Productivity Commission's central institutional recommendation is to amend the Immigration Act 2009 to require a regularly published Government Policy Statement (GPS) on immigration that specifies a planning range for resident intake and criteria for managing temporary work visas with explicit reference to pressures on New Zealand's absorptive capacity.

**Meaning (plain English)**
The Commission's flagship proposal is a regular, public document — similar to how transport or fiscal policy already works — in which the Government must set out its immigration objectives, expected migrant numbers, and how those numbers account for the country's capacity (infrastructure, housing, services) to absorb them.

**Why it matters**
This is the report's central governance recommendation, designed to make the link between immigration levels and absorptive capacity explicit, transparent, and subject to public scrutiny and accountability, rather than left as an implicit or ad hoc policy setting.

**Mechanism**
A statutory requirement to regularly publish a GPS would force government to make explicit, documented choices about migrant intake ranges, link those choices to infrastructure and service capacity, and create a public accountability mechanism (via consultation and parliamentary scrutiny) that does not currently exist for immigration policy.

**Evidence**
NZPC (2022), Part 5, Recommendation 13; evidence strength is rated descriptive (a governance/institutional design recommendation).

**Dependencies**
Related to claim-0136 (the absorptive capacity concept the GPS planning range is meant to operationalise) and claim-0150 (the Te Tiriti o Waitangi engagement recommendation, which the Commission proposes should also begin with the GPS process).

**Policy implications**
- Amending the Immigration Act 2009 to require a GPS would create a durable, cross-government mechanism linking immigration settings to infrastructure and service planning.
- The GPS should specify a numerical planning range for resident intake, informed by absorptive-capacity assessments, while avoiding rigid fixed rules applied without regard to underlying causes of population change.
- The Public Finance Act 1989 fiscal responsibility model is identified as a more suitable template than the Land Transport GPS, since immigration policy is administered directly by ministers rather than through an arm's-length Crown agency.
- The GPS process should formally include Te Tiriti o Waitangi engagement with Māori as a foundational element, not an afterthought.
- Treasury, HUD, and Te Waihanga should be statutorily linked into the GPS development process given its direct dependence on infrastructure and housing capacity data.

**Relevant institutions**
Immigration NZ, MBIE, Treasury, HUD, Te Waihanga, Parliament, NZ Productivity Commission.

---
## claim-0149: Labour Inspectorate under-resourced relative to the ILO benchmark

**Statement**
Over 2015-19, New Zealand had one Labour Inspectorate inspector for every 40,000 workers — about a quarter of the International Labour Organisation's recommended benchmark of one inspector per 10,000 workers for industrial market economies — limiting enforcement against minimum employment standard breaches and migrant exploitation.

**Meaning (plain English)**
New Zealand has far fewer labour inspectors per worker than international standards recommend for a developed economy — its actual staffing ratio matches the much lower benchmark the ILO sets for less-developed countries, not the one appropriate for a country like New Zealand.

**Why it matters**
Weak enforcement capacity undermines the integrity of the entire immigration and employment system, particularly the planned Accredited Employer Work Visa scheme, which relies on credible enforcement to deter employer non-compliance and migrant exploitation.

**Mechanism**
With substantially fewer inspectors than needed, the Labour Inspectorate can investigate only a small fraction of potential breaches, reducing the probability of detection and creating weak deterrence against employer non-compliance, including wage theft, rights violations, and exploitation of migrant workers who are also disproportionately vulnerable due to visa-related dependency on employers (see claim-0144).

**Evidence**
NZPC (2022), Part 5, Finding 25, citing ILO benchmarks and 2015-19 staffing data; evidence strength is rated observational.

**Dependencies**
Related to claim-0144 (employer tying and monopsony power, which increases migrant vulnerability to exploitation that under-resourced enforcement cannot adequately counter) and claim-0145 (the recommendation to enable migrant job mobility, which the Commission ties to a need for stronger enforcement capacity to manage associated risks).

**Policy implications**
- The Government should fund the Labour Inspectorate to reach the ILO's one-inspector-per-10,000-workers benchmark within a defined multi-year timeframe.
- Enforcement capacity should scale ahead of, or in step with, any reforms (such as enabling migrant job mobility) that increase monitoring complexity.
- Investigation throughput (only around 530 cases/year against an estimated ~20,000 affected migrant workers, per claim-0153) indicates current resourcing is grossly inadequate relative to the scale of the problem.
- Resourcing decisions should be explicitly tied to the integrity requirements of the Accredited Employer Work Visa scheme.
- Regular public reporting on inspector-to-worker ratios would improve accountability and track progress toward the ILO benchmark.

**Relevant institutions**
Labour Inspectorate, MBIE, Immigration NZ, NZ Productivity Commission.

---
## claim-0150: Te Tiriti o Waitangi and immigration policy

**Statement**
Although immigration was a significant motivation behind the signing of Te Tiriti o Waitangi and continues to have disproportionate impacts on Māori, New Zealand's immigration policies and institutions have largely ignored Te Tiriti, and the Commission recommends good-faith Crown-Māori engagement on immigration policy beginning with the proposed Government Policy Statement process.

**Meaning (plain English)**
Te Tiriti o Waitangi was signed partly because of concerns about immigration, and immigration still affects Māori distinctively today, yet New Zealand's immigration system has largely operated as if Te Tiriti doesn't apply to it. The Commission says this needs to change, starting with genuine, good-faith engagement with Māori, though it admits it doesn't have the expertise or mandate to say exactly how.

**Why it matters**
This finding identifies a significant gap in New Zealand's immigration governance — its disconnect from Te Tiriti obligations — with implications for the legitimacy and design of immigration institutions, and it links directly to the broader GPS governance reform proposed elsewhere in the report.

**Mechanism**
This is a governance and institutional-design finding rather than an empirical causal claim: it implies that immigration policy decisions (who comes, in what numbers, under what conditions) affect Māori interests and the Crown-Māori relationship in ways that current institutional processes do not formally recognise or account for, and that establishing engagement mechanisms could remedy this gap.

**Evidence**
NZPC (2022), Part 6, Findings 27-28 and Recommendation 21; evidence strength is rated descriptive.

**Dependencies**
Related to claim-0148 (the GPS recommendation, which the Commission proposes should include Te Tiriti engagement as a foundational element) and claim-0151 (the settlement support recommendation, situated in the same chapter on Te Tiriti and social inclusion).

**Policy implications**
- Crown-Māori engagement on immigration policy should be built into the proposed GPS development process from the outset, not added afterward.
- The Government should explore options for a dedicated engagement body for immigration policy, analogous to existing Te Tiriti-consistent mechanisms in other policy areas (e.g., trade policy), without assuming this is the only viable model.
- Engagement processes should allow Māori to define their own interests and pace of engagement, recognising historical reasons for Māori caution about Crown-led processes.
- Immigration institutions (Immigration NZ, MBIE) should review their own processes for points of disconnect from Te Tiriti obligations, not rely solely on the GPS mechanism.
- Open questions about coordinating local/regional Māori interests with the national and international scope of immigration policy should be a explicit subject of the proposed engagement process.

**Relevant institutions**
Immigration NZ, MBIE, Te Arawhiti (Office for Māori Crown Relations), NZ Productivity Commission.

---
## claim-0151: Settlement support and migrant retention

**Statement**
New Zealand's settlement policy settings ranked in the top 10 countries on the international Migrant Integration Policy Index (MIPEX) in 2019, well above the OECD average, but the Commission recommended increased settlement-support resourcing — including business mentorship, civics courses, personalised settlement plans, and realistic living-cost information — to improve migrant retention and social inclusion.

**Meaning (plain English)**
New Zealand already does comparatively well internationally at helping migrants settle in, but the Commission says it should do more — funding things like mentoring, courses on New Zealand life, personal settlement plans, and honest information about living costs — to keep more migrants here for longer and help them integrate.

**Why it matters**
