Good settlement support directly affects whether skilled migrants stay in New Zealand long enough to deliver the economic benefits (such as the brain-exchange effect in claim-0138) that motivate skill-selective immigration in the first place; poor retention wastes the investment made in selecting and admitting skilled migrants.

**Mechanism**
Settlement difficulties — language barriers, spousal employment problems, lack of professional networks, unrealistic expectations about living costs — increase the likelihood migrants leave New Zealand before their human capital contributes fully to the economy; international evidence cited shows spousal employment in particular is a strong predictor of migrant retention (e.g., Netherlands and Norway studies), implying whole-family settlement support raises retention more than support targeted at the principal applicant alone.

**Evidence**
NZPC (2022), Part 6, Recommendation 22, citing the 2019 MIPEX ranking and international retention studies (Netherlands, Norway); evidence strength is rated observational.

**Dependencies**
Related to claim-0150 (situated in the same Te Tiriti and social inclusion chapter, reflecting a shared concern with how immigration policy affects social cohesion).

**Policy implications**
- Settlement support funding should explicitly target whole-family outcomes (spousal employment support, family settlement plans), not just the principal visa holder.
- Realistic, transparent living-cost information should be provided to prospective migrants before arrival to reduce post-arrival disillusionment and early departure.
- Despite NZ's strong MIPEX ranking, the Commission cautions against complacency given intensifying global competition for migrants post-pandemic — settlement policy should be treated as a competitive differentiator, not a solved problem.
- Business mentorship and civics programmes should be evaluated for their actual impact on retention, not just delivered as generic settlement services.
- MBIE and Immigration NZ should track retention rates by visa category and family structure to identify where settlement support investment would have the highest marginal payoff.

**Relevant institutions**
Immigration NZ, MBIE, Ministry of Social Development, Education NZ, local government.

---
## claim-0152: Skilled migrants contribute to firm productivity similarly to skilled local workers

**Statement**
Skilled migrants, whether on residence or temporary visas, contribute to New Zealand firm productivity in a similar way to high-skilled New Zealand-born workers, while migrants on "other" visas (student, Recognised Seasonal Employer, working holiday) contribute similarly to low-skilled local workers.

**Meaning (plain English)**
Migrants who come to New Zealand through skilled visa pathways add to firm productivity about as much as similarly skilled New Zealand-born workers do. Migrants on visa types not selected for skill (students, seasonal workers, working holidaymakers) contribute to firm productivity in a way that resembles low-skilled local workers — in other words, migrants' productivity contribution tracks their actual skill level and visa pathway, not their migrant status per se.

**Why it matters**
This finding supports the Commission's broader conclusion that New Zealand's skill-selection system is "working" in the narrow sense that it successfully channels skilled migrants into productive roles, while also showing that productivity outcomes depend heavily on visa category design rather than immigration per se.

**Mechanism**
This is an empirical association rather than an established causal pathway: it implies that the skill-selection mechanisms embedded in different visa categories (residence/temporary skilled visas vs. student/seasonal/working-holiday visas) successfully sort migrants into roles where their productivity contribution matches their skill level, similar to how skill sorts local workers into roles.

**Evidence**
NZPC (2022), Part 3, Finding 9, drawing on Commission research (Fabling et al., 2022) using IDI and Longitudinal Business Database data on migrant labour share and labour productivity across industries, 2005-2019; evidence strength is rated observational.

**Dependencies**
Related to claim-0144 (employer tying and monopsony power, a separate finding from the same broader Commission research programme on immigration and firm-level labour-market outcomes).

**Policy implications**
- Visa category design, not immigration volume alone, should be the primary policy lever for shaping the productivity impact of immigration.
- Because skilled migrants' productivity premium is sometimes lower than their wage premium (the reverse of the pattern for high-skilled New Zealanders), employers may be paying skilled migrants relatively more than their measured productivity contribution — a finding relevant to debates about migrant wage settings.
- Policy should avoid treating "migrant labour share" as a simple proxy for low productivity, since the relationship between migrant labour use and industry productivity is not uniform or monotonic across industries.
- Continued investment in IDI/LBD-linked research would allow ongoing monitoring of how different visa categories affect firm productivity as visa settings change (e.g., the shift to AEWV).
- Workforce planning for sectors reliant on "other" visa categories (seasonal, student, working holiday) should recognise these contribute similarly to low-skilled local labour, informing realistic expectations about their productivity impact.

**Relevant institutions**
NZ Productivity Commission, Stats NZ (IDI custodian), MBIE, Immigration NZ.

---
## claim-0153: An estimated 8% of temporary migrant workers experienced employment rights breaches

**Statement**
MBIE's best estimate, based on the 2018 Migrant Survey, found that 8% of temporary migrant workers in New Zealand — about 20,000 workers — had not received one or more of their minimum employment rights, or had been asked to pay money to their employer to get or keep their job.

**Meaning (plain English)**
Based on a 2018 survey, MBIE estimated that roughly one in twelve temporary migrant workers in New Zealand — about 20,000 people — were being denied basic employment rights or were being illegally charged money by their employer just to get or keep their job.

**Why it matters**
This figure quantifies the scale of migrant worker exploitation risk that motivates several of the report's recommendations (ending employer tying, boosting Labour Inspectorate resourcing), and it provides a benchmark against which enforcement capacity (claim-0149) can be assessed as grossly insufficient.

**Mechanism**
This is a descriptive prevalence estimate rather than a causal claim: it implies a substantial gap between formal minimum employment standards and workers' lived experience for a meaningful share of the temporary migrant workforce, consistent with the monopsony dynamics created by employer-tying (claim-0144) and the weak deterrence created by under-resourced enforcement (claim-0149).

**Evidence**
NZPC (2022), Part 5, Box 5.2, citing an MBIE (2020) extrapolation from the 2018 Migrant Survey, itself drawing on the Office of the Minister for Workplace Relations and Safety & Office of the Minister of Immigration (2020); evidence strength is rated observational, and the figure is explicitly described as an extrapolated estimate rather than a direct count.

**Dependencies**
Related to claim-0149 (the Labour Inspectorate's resourcing shortfall, against which this estimated scale of exploitation is strikingly large — around 20,000 affected workers versus only ~530 investigations per year across the whole workforce).

**Policy implications**
- The scale of this estimate (20,000 workers) should be treated as a minimum baseline for sizing Labour Inspectorate resourcing and enforcement strategy, not a precise count.
- Given the gap between estimated prevalence and actual investigation numbers, proactive detection mechanisms (not just complaint-driven investigation) are likely needed.
- The AEWV system and associated monitoring reforms should be evaluated against whether they measurably reduce this estimated 8% prevalence rate over time.
- MBIE should conduct regular, methodologically consistent migrant worker surveys to track whether exploitation prevalence is improving or worsening, rather than relying on one-off extrapolations.
- Government statements (such as the Minister's expectation that improved monitoring would double reported exploitation cases) suggest the true scale may exceed the 8% estimate, warranting cautious interpretation of any future apparent "increase" in reported cases as partly a detection effect.

**Relevant institutions**
MBIE, Labour Inspectorate, Immigration NZ.

---
## claim-0154: Immigration is neither the cause nor the likely solution to NZ's productivity problem

**Statement**
Immigration is not the cause of, nor is it likely to be the solution to, New Zealand's persistent productivity underperformance: the country's GDP growth has kept pace with OECD peers by adding more workers and longer hours rather than by raising output per hour worked.

**Meaning (plain English)**
New Zealand's weak productivity is not migrants' fault, and bringing in more or fewer migrants won't fix it either. The country has kept its overall economy growing mainly by having more people work more hours, not by becoming more efficient — and that's the real productivity problem, independent of immigration settings.

**Why it matters**
This is the Productivity Commission's headline conclusion from its entire immigration inquiry, and it reframes the national policy conversation: debates about immigration levels should not be conducted as if they were really debates about fixing New Zealand's productivity performance, because the evidence does not support that framing.

**Mechanism**
The conclusion rests on a growth-accounting decomposition (GDP growth driven by labour input expansion rather than output-per-hour gains, claim-0141) combined with international evidence that immigration typically has only small positive effects on average labour productivity; migrants may raise productive capacity in the long run, but short-run absorptive capacity constraints (claim-0136) can offset or delay these gains, especially when infrastructure and services lag population growth.

**Evidence**
NZPC (2022) Overview; the Commission states this conclusion is consistent with — though not derived independently of — the broader international literature on immigration and productivity. Evidence strength is rated observational, reflecting that this is the Commission's synthesised judgement rather than a single quasi-experimental estimate.

**Dependencies**
Depends on claim-0140 (the NZ-OECD output-per-hour gap) and claim-0141 (the growth-accounting finding that GDP growth has been driven by labour input rather than productivity); related to claim-0136 (the absorptive-capacity framing that explains why short-run costs can coexist with long-run benefits) and claim-0094 (where this conclusion was first paraphrased secondhand in a Koi Tū report, before this card became the primary citation). Related to claim-0161 and claim-0162 (SVAR evidence on immigration and productivity), covered elsewhere in this compilation — claim-0162 in particular reports an independent quantitative finding (a muted, statistically insignificant labour productivity response to an immigration shock) that the source paper explicitly cites as corroborating this NZPC conclusion.

**Policy implications**
- Immigration policy should be evaluated against its own objectives (skills, settlement, absorptive capacity, social cohesion) rather than treated as a primary lever for solving New Zealand's productivity problem.
- Productivity policy attention should focus on capital investment, technology diffusion, education-system alignment, and competition policy rather than on immigration volume.
- The short-run/long-run distinction means policymakers should not expect immediate productivity gains from skilled immigration, and should plan for absorptive-capacity costs in the interim.
- This conclusion should inform how Treasury, MBIE, and the Reserve Bank model the macroeconomic effects of changes to immigration settings.
- Future independent empirical work (such as structural VAR-based corroboration) should continue to be tracked, since this conclusion — while currently well-supported — was reached through synthesis rather than a single definitive study.

**Relevant institutions**
NZ Productivity Commission, Treasury, MBIE, Reserve Bank of New Zealand, Immigration NZ.
---
## claim-0155: NZ immigrant population share rose from 15% (1990) to 27% (2019), second-highest in OECD

**Statement**
New Zealand's immigrant share of the total population rose from 15% in 1990 to 27% in 2019, a higher share than most OECD comparator countries (including Canada at 21%, the United States at 14%, Sweden at 16%, Germany at 14%, Norway at 14%, the United Kingdom at 13%, and France at 12%), with only Australia (29%) recording a higher share among comparable economies.

**Meaning (plain English)**
Over roughly thirty years, the proportion of New Zealand's population born overseas nearly doubled, from about one in seven people to more than one in four. By international standards this is an unusually high share — New Zealand is second only to Australia among its main comparator economies.

**Why it matters**
This sets the scale of the policy problem: New Zealand has built an economy and society that is unusually dependent on, and shaped by, immigration relative to peer countries. Any debate about immigration settings, housing supply, infrastructure planning, or labour market policy in New Zealand is operating against this high and rising baseline, not a marginal phenomenon.

**Mechanism**
This is a descriptive statistic rather than a causal claim. It implies that decades of immigration policy settings (skilled migrant categories, working holiday schemes, international education pathways to residency) have compounded over time into a structurally high foreign-born population share, which in turn raises the stakes of getting immigration-linked infrastructure and labour market policy right.

**Evidence**
Javed, Power & Richardson (2026), RBNZ Discussion Paper DP2026-04, Section 1 (Introduction), citing population statistics rather than the paper's own SVAR estimates. Evidence strength is rated observational.

**Dependencies**
None listed in the relationships block beyond a general cross-reference to claim-0091 (NZ's total fertility rate decline to 1.55 by 2025), which sits in the same demographic-context cluster but is not a direct supports/qualifies/depends_on relationship — falling fertility and rising immigrant share are parallel demographic trends discussed elsewhere in this corpus. Also related to claim-0156 (effect of an expansionary immigration shock on unemployment), which uses this high-immigration context as its starting motivation.

**Policy implications**
- Immigration settings should be treated as a first-order lever of New Zealand's demographic and economic trajectory, not a peripheral policy area.
- International comparisons of housing, infrastructure, and labour market outcomes should account for New Zealand's unusually high immigrant population share relative to OECD peers.
- Long-run population projections used in infrastructure and housing capacity planning should not assume immigration intensity will revert to 1990s levels.
- Given comparability with Australia, policy learning and benchmarking exercises should prioritise the Australian experience over lower-immigration OECD countries such as the UK, Germany, or France.
- Public discourse on immigration's economic effects should be grounded in the scale of the immigrant share (27% of population), not treated as a marginal or recent development.

**Relevant institutions**
Stats NZ, Immigration New Zealand, MBIE, Treasury, RBNZ.

---
## claim-0156: Expansionary immigration shock significantly lowers NZ unemployment for ~18 months

**Statement**
In a sign-restricted SVAR model of the New Zealand economy estimated on quarterly data from 1992Q1 to 2019Q4, an expansionary immigration shock is followed by a statistically significant fall in the unemployment rate that persists for around 18 months; specifically, a 0.25 percentage-point expansionary immigration shock is associated with a fall in the unemployment rate of over 0.20 percentage points at the 2–3 year horizon.

**Meaning (plain English)**
When net migration into New Zealand rises unexpectedly, unemployment tends to fall, not rise, over the following one-and-a-half to three years. This goes against a naive expectation that more migrants competing for jobs would push unemployment up — instead, the demand migrants create (for housing, goods, and services) appears to generate more jobs than the migrants themselves fill.

**Why it matters**
This is direct, NZ-specific causal evidence that immigration inflows, identified separately from other labour market and business-cycle shocks, do not raise unemployment and instead correlate with improving labour market conditions. It directly informs RBNZ's understanding of how migration cycles interact with the OCR and the broader business cycle, since unemployment is a key input to monetary policy decisions.

**Mechanism**
The model treats an expansionary immigration shock as an exogenous rise in net migration relative to the working-age population. By the model's identification scheme, this shock generates positive co-movement between GDP and the participation rate, consistent with immigration adding both labour supply and demand (for housing, consumption, services) to the economy simultaneously, with the demand-side boost dominating in the short-to-medium run.

**Evidence**
Javed, Power & Richardson (2026), RBNZ Discussion Paper DP2026-04, Section 4.1, based on a five-shock sign-restricted SVAR (immigration, domestic labour supply, business cycle, technology, residual) following Furlanetto and Robstad (2019)'s identification approach. Significance assessed at the 68% credible interval (a looser threshold than 95%). Evidence strength is rated observational.

**Dependencies**
None listed as supports/qualifies/depends_on. Related (not hierarchical) to claim-0157 (GDP rises ~0.4% after an expansionary immigration shock), claim-0163 (the same shock explains 10–30% of unemployment and GDP variance at the three-year horizon), and claim-0165 (the unemployment fall is more pronounced for younger immigrant cohorts specifically).

**Policy implications**
- RBNZ should treat immigration cycles as a meaningful, identifiable driver of short- to medium-run unemployment dynamics, distinct from domestic labour supply or business-cycle shocks.
- Net migration surges should not automatically be assumed to raise unemployment in OCR-setting deliberations; the empirical relationship in NZ data runs the other way over an 18-month horizon.
- Labour market monitoring (MBIE, MSD) should track immigration-rate shocks as a leading indicator of subsequent unemployment movements.
- The 68% credible interval convention means this finding, while robust within the paper's own framework, is somewhat less conclusive than a conventional 95% significance threshold — policy use should reflect this moderate confidence level.
- Immigration policy settings (e.g., skilled migrant category volumes) could be considered as a complementary, if blunt and slow-acting, labour market stabilisation tool, though this is not the paper's own recommendation.

**Relevant institutions**
RBNZ, MBIE, Immigration New Zealand, Treasury, MSD.

---
## claim-0157: Expansionary immigration shock lifts NZ real GDP by ~0.4% within six months

**Statement**
In the same sign-restricted SVAR model, an expansionary immigration shock raises real GDP by around 0.4% six months after the shock, with the effect remaining statistically significant for around 18 months.

**Meaning (plain English)**
A surprise increase in net migration measurably boosts the size of the New Zealand economy within half a year, and that boost remains detectable for about a year and a half. This is a one-off lift in the level of GDP, not a permanent increase in the economy's growth rate.

**Why it matters**
This quantifies, for the first time using NZ-specific causally-identified data, the short-run GDP dividend from immigration surges — information directly relevant to Treasury and RBNZ forecasting, and to public debate that often focuses only on immigration's costs (housing, congestion) without weighing the measured output gain.

**Mechanism**
An expansionary immigration shock simultaneously expands labour supply and consumption/housing demand; in the short run, the demand-side effects (more people needing goods, services, and housing) combined with new workers filling jobs raise aggregate output before any capital or infrastructure constraints bind tightly enough to choke off the gain.

**Evidence**
Javed, Power & Richardson (2026), RBNZ Discussion Paper DP2026-04, Section 4.1, sign-restricted SVAR on RBNZ quarterly data, 1992Q1–2019Q4, significance assessed at the 68% credible interval. Evidence strength is rated observational.

**Dependencies**
None listed as supports/qualifies/depends_on. Related to claim-0156 (the accompanying unemployment fall over the same horizon) and claim-0163 (the immigration shock explains 10–30% of GDP variance at the three-year horizon), forming a coherent picture of immigration as a meaningful short-run demand-side driver of the NZ business cycle.

**Policy implications**
- Treasury's economic and fiscal forecasts should incorporate the demonstrated short-run GDP impact of migration cycles as a quantifiable component of forecast revisions, not just a qualitative risk.
- The 0.4% GDP effect is a level shift, not a growth-rate increase; policymakers should not extrapolate it into a permanent uplift to potential GDP.
- Public cost-benefit framing of migration policy should account for this measured aggregate output gain alongside housing and infrastructure costs (see absorptive capacity, claim-0136).
- Since the effect is significant for around 18 months, short-term fiscal and monetary forecasting horizons should treat migration shocks as having a comparable persistence to other recognised business-cycle shocks.
- This finding does not speak to per-capita GDP or productivity effects (see claim-0162), so should not be used to argue migration raises living standards per person.

**Relevant institutions**
Treasury, RBNZ, MBIE, Stats NZ.

---
## claim-0158: Expansionary immigration shock raises NZ labour force participation by ~0.10% for over a year

**Statement**
An expansionary immigration shock raises the labour force participation rate by around 0.10% on impact, with the response remaining statistically significant for more than a year after the shock.

**Meaning (plain English)**
When immigration unexpectedly rises, a larger share of the working-age population becomes employed or actively looking for work, and this effect lasts for over a year. This is partly mechanical — migrants themselves tend to be of working age and likely to participate in the labour force — but the model also finds a persistent, broader effect.

**Why it matters**
Participation rate movements affect headline labour market statistics that the RBNZ and MBIE rely on when judging labour market tightness; understanding how much of any participation increase is attributable to migration cycles, rather than domestic behavioural change, sharpens this judgement.

**Mechanism**
The model's sign-restriction identification scheme defines an expansionary immigration shock partly by requiring it to produce positive co-movement between real GDP and the participation rate — this is a key part of how the shock is distinguished from other labour-market disturbances in the SVAR, since incoming migrants of working age mechanically add to both the labour force and economic activity.

**Evidence**
Javed, Power & Richardson (2026), RBNZ Discussion Paper DP2026-04, Section 4.1, sign-restricted SVAR, significance at the 68% credible interval. Evidence strength is rated observational.

**Dependencies**
None listed as supports/qualifies/depends_on. Related to claim-0159 (the accompanying short-run real wage fall) and claim-0163 (the immigration shock's contribution to participation-rate variance rises sharply to 25% after three years).

**Policy implications**
- Statistical agencies and RBNZ labour market commentary should distinguish migration-driven participation rate changes from domestic behavioural shifts (e.g., retirement age changes, female workforce participation trends) when interpreting headline labour force statistics.
- Because the positive participation/GDP co-movement is partly built into the shock's identification by construction, this result should be read as confirming the model's identification scheme works as intended, rather than as wholly new evidence of a migration effect.
- Workforce planning agencies (MBIE, regional economic development bodies) should factor in a roughly year-long elevated participation effect following migration surges when planning short-term labour supply.
- This finding is consistent with, and reinforces, the GDP and unemployment results (claims 0156–0157) as part of a coherent labour-supply and demand expansion following immigration shocks.

**Relevant institutions**
MBIE, RBNZ, Stats NZ, Immigration New Zealand.

---
## claim-0159: Expansionary immigration shock reduces NZ real wages by ~0.18% short-run, likely positive long-run

**Statement**
An expansionary immigration shock reduces real wage levels by around 0.18% on impact, remaining significantly negative for at least four months, although the long-term effect on real wages is likely to be positive, based on the probability mass of the credible interval shifting into positive territory at longer horizons.

**Meaning (plain English)**
A sudden rise in immigration pushes real wages down slightly at first — for a few months, wages are measurably lower than they otherwise would have been. But over a longer horizon, the balance of evidence suggests wages are more likely to end up higher than lower as a result of the same immigration shock.

**Why it matters**
This is one of the most policy-sensitive findings in the paper, speaking directly to the long-running public debate over whether immigration suppresses wages for existing New Zealand workers. The result is nuanced: a genuine but short-lived negative effect, followed by a probable (not statistically confirmed) longer-run positive effect.

**Mechanism**
The model's sign-restriction scheme defines an expansionary immigration shock partly by requiring negative co-movement between GDP and real wages, reflecting the standard labour-supply logic that an increase in available workers initially puts downward pressure on the price of labour. Over time, as the economy adjusts — capital deepens, demand from new arrivals feeds through to firms, and skill complementarities emerge — the wage-suppressing effect is expected to fade and potentially reverse.

**Evidence**
Javed, Power & Richardson (2026), RBNZ Discussion Paper DP2026-04, Section 4.1, sign-restricted SVAR, significance at the 68% credible interval for the short-run negative effect; the long-run positive effect is described qualitatively via posterior probability mass rather than confirmed as separately statistically significant. Evidence strength is rated observational.

**Dependencies**
None listed as supports/qualifies/depends_on, but the claim file notes a direct substantive link to claim-0142 (NZ Productivity Commission's "low-skill, low-wage labour market trap" mechanism, whereby strong migration inflows may restrict wage growth in some sectors and encourage employer reliance on low-cost migrant labour rather than productivity investment) — this SVAR finding of a genuine but short-lived wage suppression effect is broadly consistent with, though more precisely time-bounded than, that qualitative mechanism.

**Policy implications**
- Wage-suppression concerns about immigration have some genuine empirical support in NZ data, but the effect identified here is short-lived (a few months) rather than a permanent wage-depressing force.
- Policymakers should avoid both overstating ("immigration permanently lowers wages") and dismissing ("immigration has no wage effect") positions; the accurate picture is a short-run dip followed by a probable longer-run recovery.
- Because the long-run positive effect is not separately confirmed as statistically significant, wage-policy responses to immigration surges (e.g., minimum wage adjustments, sector-specific labour market interventions) should not rely on an assumed automatic wage rebound.
- This finding should be read alongside the low-skill, low-wage trap mechanism (claim-0142) when designing immigration settings for lower-skilled occupational categories, where the risk of more persistent wage suppression may be greater than this aggregate, economy-wide estimate suggests.
- MBIE and the Productivity Commission should monitor sector-level wage responses to migration inflows, since this aggregate SVAR result may mask divergent effects across skill levels and industries.

**Relevant institutions**
MBIE, RBNZ, Treasury, NZ Productivity Commission, Immigration New Zealand.

---
## claim-0160: Expansionary immigration shock leaves headline CPI unchanged but raises non-tradable prices

**Statement**
An expansionary immigration shock has no statistically significant effect on headline consumer prices, but its sub-components diverge: non-tradable prices rise significantly (at the 68% credible interval) approximately one year after the shock, while tradable prices are likely to fall below baseline, based on the probability mass of the credible interval.

**Meaning (plain English)**
Immigration surges do not show up as a measurable change in overall inflation. But underneath that flat headline figure, two opposite things are happening: prices for things that are hard to import or substitute (like local services and rents) tend to rise, while prices for internationally traded goods tend to fall slightly — and these two effects roughly cancel out in the aggregate measure.

**Why it matters**
For RBNZ's inflation-targeting mandate, this means migration surges are not, on this evidence, a meaningful direct driver of headline CPI that would call for an OCR response on inflation grounds alone — but the non-tradable price increase is directly relevant to monitoring domestically-generated inflation pressure, which RBNZ tracks more closely than headline CPI in some contexts.

**Mechanism**
Non-tradable goods and services (housing-related costs, local services) are exposed to local demand pressure from population growth in a way that internationally-traded goods are not, since tradable prices are anchored by international competition and exchange rates rather than local demand. An immigration shock that raises local demand without proportionately raising tradable goods demand naturally pushes non-tradable prices up relative to tradable prices, even while the combined headline index stays flat.

**Evidence**
Javed, Power & Richardson (2026), RBNZ Discussion Paper DP2026-04, Section 4.1, sign-restricted SVAR; the non-tradable price result is significant at the 68% credible interval, while the tradable price result is described qualitatively via probability mass rather than confirmed as separately significant. Evidence strength is rated observational.

**Dependencies**
None listed as supports/qualifies/depends_on. Related to claim-0161 (house price and household credit responses to the same shock), which together with this claim form a picture of immigration-driven demand falling disproportionately on non-tradable, locally-supplied goods and assets.

**Policy implications**
- RBNZ's monetary policy framework should distinguish headline CPI stability from non-tradable (domestically-generated) inflation pressure when assessing the inflationary impact of migration cycles, since the two diverge under an immigration shock.
- Because the tradable/non-tradable divergence largely cancels out in headline CPI, using headline inflation alone to judge whether immigration is "inflationary" would miss a real and policy-relevant compositional effect.
- Sectors most exposed to non-tradable price pressure (housing, local services) should be a focus of supply-side policy (housing supply, services capacity) when managing the consequences of migration-driven demand.
- The roughly one-year lag before the non-tradable price effect becomes significant suggests this is a medium-term, not immediate, pressure that monetary policy can reasonably anticipate rather than react to in real time.
- Stats NZ's tradable/non-tradable CPI sub-indices should continue to be reported and monitored separately, as they carry distinct policy signals in the context of migration shocks.

**Relevant institutions**
RBNZ, Stats NZ, Treasury, MBIE.

---
## claim-0161: Expansionary immigration shock significantly raises house prices and household credit

**Statement**
An expansionary immigration shock is followed by statistically significant increases in the levels of house prices and household credit, corresponding with the hump-shaped responses of immigration and economic activity; forecast error variance decomposition shows the immigration shock explains around 20% of house price variation at the three-year horizon.

**Meaning (plain English)**
When immigration rises unexpectedly, house prices and household borrowing both increase noticeably, tracking the same rise-then-fall pattern as the immigration shock itself and the broader economic expansion it triggers. About a fifth of all the unexplained ups and downs in house prices over a three-year period can be traced back to immigration shocks specifically.

**Why it matters**
The authors describe this as among the "most important" results in their baseline model — it is the clearest, least ambiguous finding in the paper and speaks directly to one of New Zealand's most politically salient economic debates: the link between immigration and house prices. It provides causally-identified, NZ-specific quantification of a relationship often asserted but rarely measured this precisely.

**Mechanism**
Immigration adds to housing demand directly (migrants need somewhere to live) while the accompanying rise in economic activity, employment, and incomes (claims 0156–0158) supports higher household borrowing capacity; the same dynamic that lifts GDP and lowers unemployment also bids up house prices and expands the credit needed to finance them, consistent with the framework of "absorptive capacity" — the idea that housing and infrastructure capacity is a binding short-run constraint on accommodating population growth.

**Evidence**
Javed, Power & Richardson (2026), RBNZ Discussion Paper DP2026-04, Section 4.1 (impulse responses) and Section 4.2 (forecast error variance decomposition), sign-restricted SVAR on RBNZ data. The authors explicitly flag this as their most important baseline result. Evidence strength is rated observational.

**Dependencies**
Connects to claim-0136 (NZ Productivity Commission's definition of "absorptive capacity," covering housing infrastructure and land supply as a short-run constraint on accommodating population growth), of which this SVAR finding is an independent quantitative illustration. Also related to claim-0160 (non-tradable consumer price increases under the same shock) and claim-0164 (the contrastingly small variance contribution to labour productivity), together forming a coherent demand-side bundle: immigration shocks raise activity, employment, prices, and asset values together, but not productivity.

**Policy implications**
- Housing supply and infrastructure policy (zoning reform, land supply, construction sector capacity) should be treated as the primary lever for managing the house-price consequences of immigration cycles, since the underlying demand-side link is now causally identified in NZ data.
- RBNZ's financial stability monitoring should treat immigration shocks as an identifiable contributor to household credit growth and house price cycles, alongside more commonly tracked drivers like interest rates and credit availability.
- Given the ~20% variance contribution at three years, immigration policy settings (volumes, timing) could be considered a meaningful, if partial, lever for managing house price cycles — though housing supply responsiveness is the more direct and durable solution.
- This finding strengthens the case for linking immigration policy settings to housing and infrastructure capacity planning (absorptive capacity) rather than treating the two as separate policy domains.
- Because the result is explicitly flagged by the authors as their clearest finding, it should be weighted heavily in any policy synthesis of this paper relative to the more uncertain price-level and productivity results.

**Relevant institutions**
RBNZ, HUD, MBIE, Te Waihanga (NZ Infrastructure Commission), Auckland Council and other territorial authorities, Treasury.

---
## claim-0162: Labour productivity shows muted response to immigration shock despite NZ's high-skilled migrant mix

**Statement**
Despite New Zealand's high-skilled immigration composition, labour productivity shows a muted, statistically insignificant response to an expansionary immigration shock in the sign-restricted SVAR model, a result the authors note is consistent with the New Zealand Productivity Commission's (2022) conclusion that immigration is neither the cause of, nor the solution to, New Zealand's productivity challenges.

**Meaning (plain English)**
Even though many migrants to New Zealand are highly skilled, an immigration surge does not produce a measurable bump in how productive the economy is per worker or per hour. This goes against the natural assumption that more skilled workers should mechanically raise average productivity, and instead echoes an earlier official finding that immigration is essentially a non-factor in New Zealand's long-running productivity problem.

**Why it matters**
This is significant because it is an independent, quantitative, causally-identified confirmation — using a completely different method and dataset — of a conclusion previously reached qualitatively by an official government inquiry. Convergent findings from two independent approaches substantially strengthen confidence that immigration policy is not a viable lever for fixing New Zealand's persistent productivity underperformance.

**Mechanism**
The authors note this null result runs counter to the a priori expectation generated by NZ's high-skilled immigration composition — one would expect skilled migrants to lift average labour productivity, yet the SVAR finds no significant such effect. This is a descriptive/null finding rather than a confirmed causal mechanism; the implication is that whatever forces hold back NZ productivity growth (low capital intensity, weak competition, distance from markets, firm-level managerial capability, etc.) are not meaningfully altered by immigration-driven labour supply changes in either direction.

**Evidence**
Javed, Power & Richardson (2026), RBNZ Discussion Paper DP2026-04, Section 4.1, sign-restricted SVAR; corroborates NZ Productivity Commission (2022) (source-0027). Evidence strength is rated observational.

**Dependencies**
Corroborates claim-0154 (NZ Productivity Commission 2022 finding that immigration is neither the cause of, nor the solution to, New Zealand's productivity challenges — the Commission concluded NZ's GDP growth has kept pace with OECD peers by adding workers and hours rather than raising output per hour), covered elsewhere in this compilation. The paper itself explicitly draws this connection, making claim-0162 a case of independent quantitative corroboration of a qualitative conclusion already in this corpus, not a citation being mistaken for a primary finding. Also related to claim-0164 (the correspondingly small ~10% variance contribution of immigration shocks to productivity) and claim-0166 (the contrasting robustness finding that older-cohort immigration shocks do raise productivity significantly).

**Policy implications**
- Immigration policy should not be promoted, nor opposed, as a solution to New Zealand's productivity underperformance — two independent lines of evidence (NZPC's qualitative review and this paper's causal SVAR estimate) now converge on this conclusion.
- Productivity policy effort (competition policy, capital investment incentives, management capability programmes, R&D settings) should be directed at levers other than immigration volume or skill-mix settings.
- The "high-skilled immigration should mechanically raise productivity" assumption embedded in some skilled-migrant policy design should be revisited, since it is not borne out in aggregate NZ macro data.
- Given the convergence of qualitative and quantitative evidence, government productivity strategy documents (Treasury, MBIE) should treat this as a settled empirical point rather than an open question warranting further immigration-productivity studies.
- This finding should be read alongside claim-0166, which suggests the muted aggregate result may mask a real positive effect specific to older immigrant cohorts — a nuance relevant to skilled migrant category design.

**Relevant institutions**
NZ Productivity Commission, Treasury, MBIE, RBNZ, Immigration New Zealand.

---
## claim-0163: Immigration shock explains up to ~30% of NZ GDP and unemployment variance, 25% of participation rate variance, at three-year horizon

**Statement**
In forecast error variance decomposition, the immigration shock explains between 10% and 30% of the variation in real GDP and the unemployment rate over a three-year horizon, reaching around 30% for both by that horizon — described in the paper's abstract as a "major contributor" — while its contribution to participation rate variation rises sharply to around 25% after three years; the immigration shock also explains over 40% of variation in the immigration rate itself, which the authors treat as a validation check on model identification.

**Meaning (plain English)**
Roughly a third of all the unexplained ups and downs in New Zealand's GDP and unemployment rate over a three-year period can be traced back to immigration shocks. This makes immigration one of the most important single drivers of New Zealand's business cycle — not a minor or peripheral factor.

**Why it matters**
This is a striking and policy-relevant magnitude: it positions immigration shocks alongside (or above) more conventionally emphasised drivers of the New Zealand business cycle, such as commodity price or monetary policy shocks, in terms of their contribution to macroeconomic variation. It justifies treating migration forecasting and modelling as core macroeconomic infrastructure for Treasury and RBNZ, not a secondary input.

**Mechanism**
This is a variance-decomposition statistic describing how much of the unpredictable variation in each variable is attributable to the immigration shock specifically, as distinguished from the model's other four structural shocks (domestic labour supply, business cycle, technology, residual). It implies that migration cycles are not just statistically significant in direction (as the impulse response functions show) but also quantitatively large relative to other macroeconomic disturbances affecting GDP, unemployment, and participation.

**Evidence**
Javed, Power & Richardson (2026), RBNZ Discussion Paper DP2026-04, Section 4.2 (forecast error variance decomposition) and abstract, sign-restricted SVAR on RBNZ quarterly data, 1992Q1–2019Q4. Evidence strength is rated observational.

**Dependencies**
None listed as supports/qualifies/depends_on. Related to claim-0156 (unemployment impulse response) and claim-0157 (GDP impulse response), for which this claim supplies the variance-decomposition counterpart, and to claim-0164 (the much smaller ~10% variance contribution to labour productivity), which highlights that immigration's macroeconomic importance is concentrated in activity and labour market variables rather than productivity.

**Policy implications**
- Treasury and RBNZ macroeconomic forecasting models should treat net migration forecasts as a first-order input to GDP and unemployment projections, comparable in importance to other major recognised shocks.
- Immigration policy volume decisions (e.g., changes to skilled migrant or working holiday visa caps) should be understood as having macroeconomically significant, not merely demographic, consequences.
- The over-40% variance contribution to the immigration rate itself should be read as a model-validation result confirming the shock is well identified, not as a substantive economic finding requiring separate policy response.
- Given the scale of immigration's contribution to GDP and unemployment variance, scenario planning exercises (Treasury's economic and fiscal updates, RBNZ's Monetary Policy Statements) should include explicit migration-shock scenarios.
- Forecasting agencies should be cautious about treating migration as exogenous noise in macro models; this evidence indicates it is a major, quantifiable systematic driver of NZ's business cycle.

**Relevant institutions**
RBNZ, Treasury, MBIE, Stats NZ, Immigration New Zealand.

---
## claim-0164: Immigration shock explains under 10% of NZ labour productivity variance, smallest of any model variable

**Statement**
The immigration shock explains less than 10% of the variation in labour productivity over the full forecast horizon in the sign-restricted SVAR's variance decomposition — the smallest contribution of any variable in the model, smaller even than household credit (16%), the OCR (15%), and the real exchange rate (13%), which the authors themselves describe as "negligible" — consistent with the muted productivity impulse response found elsewhere in the paper.

**Meaning (plain English)**
Of all the things immigration shocks affect in the New Zealand economy, labour productivity is affected the least — even less than variables the authors already consider unimportant, like the exchange rate or the official cash rate. This reinforces that immigration moves jobs, activity, and house prices a lot, but barely moves how productive the economy is.

**Why it matters**
This variance-decomposition result is the quantitative complement to claim-0162's directional finding: it shows not only that the productivity response is statistically insignificant in any given period, but that across the entire forecast horizon, immigration accounts for almost none of the variation in productivity relative to other macroeconomic forces. This closes off any residual argument that immigration might be an important, if hard-to-detect, productivity driver.

**Mechanism**
As a variance-decomposition statistic, this is descriptive rather than causal: it implies that the structural forces driving NZ's labour productivity over time (capital investment, technology adoption, firm management, competition, scale and distance effects) operate largely independently of immigration cycles, reinforcing the conclusion that immigration policy is not a meaningful lever for productivity outcomes in either direction.

**Evidence**
Javed, Power & Richardson (2026), RBNZ Discussion Paper DP2026-04, Section 4.2, sign-restricted SVAR forecast error variance decomposition. Evidence strength is rated observational.

**Dependencies**
Supports claim-0162 (the muted, statistically insignificant labour productivity impulse response to an expansionary immigration shock) by supplying the variance-decomposition evidence that the same null result holds across the whole forecast horizon, not just in impulse-response terms. Also related to claim-0163 (the much larger 10–30% variance contributions to GDP, unemployment, and participation), highlighting the contrast between immigration's macroeconomic importance for activity/labour variables versus its near-irrelevance for productivity.

**Policy implications**
- This strengthens the case (alongside claim-0162) that immigration settings should not be designed around productivity objectives, since immigration shocks contribute negligibly to productivity variation by either measure used in the paper.
- Agencies responsible for productivity strategy (Treasury, MBIE, NZ Productivity Commission successor functions) should look to capital deepening, competition, and firm capability channels rather than migration volume or composition.
- The comparison to "negligible" variables like the OCR and exchange rate gives a useful benchmark for communicating to non-technical audiences just how small immigration's productivity footprint is in NZ data.
- This finding should temper expectations in any future immigration policy reform pitched partly on productivity grounds (e.g., expanding skilled migrant categories to "boost productivity").
- Researchers and forecasters should treat productivity as effectively decoupled from short-to-medium-run immigration cycles in NZ macro models, reserving migration-productivity questions for longer-run, compositional analysis (e.g., by migrant age, as in claim-0166) rather than aggregate shock-based models.

**Relevant institutions**
NZ Productivity Commission, Treasury, MBIE, RBNZ.

---
## claim-0165: Younger-cohort immigration shock significantly lowers unemployment and tradable prices, likely raises house prices

**Statement**
A robustness check splitting immigrants by age in the sign-restricted SVAR finds that a "younger" (15–29 years) immigrant shock produces a statistically significant fall (at the 68% credible interval) in the unemployment rate and in the level of tradable prices, while house prices are likely to increase, based on the probability mass of the credible interval lying predominantly in positive territory.

**Meaning (plain English)**
When the immigration surge is specifically composed of younger migrants (aged 15–29), the unemployment-reducing effect found in the overall model becomes even clearer and is joined by a fall in the price of internationally traded goods. House prices also tend to rise, though this particular result is less statistically certain than the unemployment and tradable price findings.

**Why it matters**
This age-disaggregated robustness check shows that the baseline results are not uniform across all migrants — younger migrants drive a disproportionate share of the labour-market effects. This is directly relevant to immigration policy design, since visa categories (working holiday, student, early-career skilled migrant) can be tuned to target or avoid this age band depending on policy objectives.

**Mechanism**
Younger migrants are more likely to be entering the labour force for the first time, taking up entry-level and lower-tenure positions, and are more economically active relative to dependents than older cohorts; this concentrates the labour-supply and consumption effects of immigration shocks within this specific age band, amplifying the unemployment and tradable-price effects relative to the all-ages baseline.

**Evidence**
Javed, Power & Richardson (2026), RBNZ Discussion Paper DP2026-04, Section 5 (robustness check, younger 15–29 vs older 30–44 immigrant age groups), sign-restricted SVAR; this robustness check splits the baseline immigration shock by age band rather than re-estimating the full five-shock identification scheme separately for each group. Evidence strength is rated observational.

**Dependencies**
None listed as supports/qualifies/depends_on, but the claim file notes this result refines claim-0156 (the baseline, all-ages unemployment response to an expansionary immigration shock) by showing the effect is concentrated in, or driven by, younger migrants specifically. Also related to claim-0166 (the contrasting older-cohort robustness check, where productivity rather than unemployment is the significant outcome).

**Policy implications**
- Immigration visa categories aimed at labour market participation and filling entry-level roles (working holiday schemes, student-to-work pathways) should be understood as the primary drivers of the unemployment-reducing effects found in the aggregate model.
- Policymakers seeking to manage tradable-sector price pressure or competitiveness could consider that younger migrant inflows are associated with measurable downward pressure on tradable prices, a channel distinct from the non-tradable price effects found in the aggregate model (claim-0160).
- Because the house price effect for younger migrants is less statistically certain than for unemployment, housing-policy responses to younger-cohort migration should rely on the more general house-price evidence (claim-0161) rather than this specific age-disaggregated result alone.
- This age-based disaggregation supports designing immigration settings with explicit attention to age composition, not just skill level or visa category, when forecasting labour market effects.
- MBIE and Immigration New Zealand should consider age composition of net migration as a distinct forecasting variable for short-run unemployment and tradable price effects, alongside overall migration volume.

**Relevant institutions**
Immigration New Zealand, MBIE, RBNZ, Treasury.

---
## claim-0166: Older-cohort immigration shock significantly raises NZ labour productivity at ~1-year horizon

**Statement**
A robustness check splitting immigrants by age finds that an "older" (30–44 years) immigrant shock produces largely insignificant responses across all model variables except labour productivity, which shows a statistically significant increase (at the 68% credible interval) around the one-year horizon and remains positive throughout the forecast horizon — a result the paper's conclusion summarises as "labour productivity is likely to increase with an increase in the inflow of relatively older immigrants."

**Meaning (plain English)**
When the immigration surge is made up specifically of migrants aged 30–44, the usual effects on jobs, wages, and house prices mostly disappear — but, unlike the all-ages baseline, this older-cohort shock does produce a real, measurable lift in labour productivity after about a year. This is the opposite pattern to younger migrants, who drive unemployment effects but not productivity ones.

**Why it matters**
This is one of the paper's two headline robustness findings and offers a more nuanced, age-specific qualification to the otherwise null productivity result in the baseline model (claim-0162): the muted aggregate productivity response may be masking a genuine positive effect specific to migrants in their thirties and early forties, who are more likely to bring established skills, experience, and career-stage human capital than younger arrivals.

**Mechanism**
Migrants aged 30–44 are more likely to have established work experience, advanced qualifications, and specialised skills accumulated prior to migration, compared with younger migrants who are more likely entering the labour force for the first time; this human-capital and experience profile plausibly explains why an older-cohort immigration shock raises measured labour productivity while a younger-cohort shock instead drives labour market quantity effects (unemployment, prices) without a productivity gain.

**Evidence**
Javed, Power & Richardson (2026), RBNZ Discussion Paper DP2026-04, Section 5 (robustness check) and Section 6 (conclusion), sign-restricted SVAR. Evidence strength is rated observational.

**Dependencies**
Qualifies claim-0162 (the muted, statistically insignificant baseline labour productivity response to an expansionary immigration shock, pooling all migrant ages) by showing that this null aggregate result coexists with a genuine, significant positive productivity effect specific to the older (30–44) age cohort — the aggregate null result is therefore a composition effect, not evidence that immigration cannot raise productivity under any age profile. Also related to claim-0165 (the contrasting younger-cohort robustness check, where unemployment and tradable prices, not productivity, are the significant outcomes).

**Policy implications**
- Skilled migrant policy design should consider that the productivity benefits of immigration found in this paper are concentrated in the 30–44 age cohort, not immigration in general — age-blind productivity arguments for immigration policy are not well supported by this evidence.
- This finding offers a partial, evidence-based qualification to claim-0162's conclusion that immigration is not a solution to NZ's productivity challenges: a more precisely targeted, older-cohort-weighted immigration policy might plausibly contribute more to productivity than the aggregate finding suggests, though the paper does not make this policy recommendation directly.
- MBIE and Immigration New Zealand could consider age profile, not just skill or qualification level, as a relevant dimension in skilled migrant category design if productivity is an explicit policy objective.
- Given that other variables show largely insignificant responses to the older-cohort shock, policymakers should not expect older-cohort immigration to deliver the same unemployment or GDP effects found in the younger-cohort or aggregate results — its primary identified benefit in this evidence is productivity, not labour market quantity effects.
- Future research and monitoring (RBNZ, MBIE) should further disaggregate migrant cohorts by age and skill jointly, since this paper's binary young/old split is itself a simplification that may obscure further heterogeneity.

**Relevant institutions**
Immigration New Zealand, MBIE, RBNZ, NZ Productivity Commission, Treasury.

---
## claim-0167: Manufacturing's share of NZ employment fell from 25% to under 10%, 1976–2013

**Statement**
Manufacturing's share of national employment in New Zealand more than halved between 1976 and 2013, falling from 25% of jobs to less than 10%.

**Meaning (plain English)**
One in four New Zealand jobs was in manufacturing in 1976; by 2013 it was fewer than one in ten. This is one of the largest structural shifts in the modern NZ labour market.

**Why it matters**
This decline reshaped which cities and towns prospered, since manufacturing employment was geographically dispersed across many towns while the growing service sectors that replaced it concentrated disproportionately in large cities (see claim-0168).

**Mechanism**
Global trade liberalisation, automation, and the relative growth of services as incomes rose progressively reduced the share of the workforce needed in goods production, a pattern common across advanced economies but with outsized spatial consequences in New Zealand given its town-based manufacturing base.

**Evidence**
Coleman, Maré & Zheng (2019), NZ Productivity Commission Working Paper 2019/1, Census employment data for 30 of the largest NZ urban areas, 1976–2013. Evidence strength is rated observational.

**Dependencies**
Supports claim-0168 (the disproportionate concentration of subsequent service-sector growth in large cities, especially Auckland).

**Policy implications**
- Regional and industrial policy should treat manufacturing decline as a structural, largely irreversible trend rather than a cyclical downturn to be reversed through sector-specific support.
- Towns historically dependent on manufacturing employment require diversification strategies rather than manufacturing-revival strategies.

**Relevant institutions**
MBIE, NZ Productivity Commission, Te Waihanga.

---
## claim-0168: Two-thirds of NZ's finance employment growth, 1976–2013, occurred in Auckland

**Statement**
The structural shift from manufacturing to services in the New Zealand economy between 1976 and 2013 disproportionately favoured large cities: two thirds of the national increase in finance-sector employment over this period occurred in Auckland, even though Auckland held just over a third of the financial sector workforce at the start of the period.

**Meaning (plain English)**
As the economy shifted from making things to providing financial services, Auckland captured a share of the new finance jobs almost double its starting share of the sector — far more than population growth alone would predict.

**Why it matters**
This shows the sectoral shift away from manufacturing was not spatially neutral: it actively widened the gap between Auckland and the rest of the country, independent of any deliberate regional policy.

**Mechanism**
Finance is a sector with unusually strong agglomeration economies — thick labour markets for specialised skills, proximity to corporate head offices and regulators, and dense professional networks — so growth in this sector concentrates disproportionately wherever those conditions already exist most strongly, which in New Zealand is Auckland.

**Evidence**
Coleman, Maré & Zheng (2019), NZ Productivity Commission Working Paper 2019/1, location-quotient analysis of Census employment data, 1976–2013. Evidence strength is rated observational.

**Dependencies**
Supported by claim-0167 (the manufacturing decline that drove the underlying sectoral reallocation) and reinforced by claim-0169 (a similar, smaller pattern in the wholesale sector).

**Policy implications**
- National economic strategy should recognise that sector-driven urban concentration is a structural feature of services-led growth, not a policy failure to be corrected through regional redistribution alone.
- Secondary cities seeking finance-sector growth face a strong agglomeration headwind that generic business-attraction incentives are unlikely to overcome.

**Relevant institutions**
MBIE, Auckland Council, NZ Productivity Commission.

---
## claim-0169: Auckland's wholesale employment grew 42% faster than its initial share predicted

**Statement**
Based on its 1976 share of national wholesale-trade employment, Auckland would have been expected to capture 56% of the national increase in wholesale-sector jobs between 1976 and 2013; it actually captured 79% — wholesale employment grew 42% faster in Auckland than its initial industry share would predict.

**Meaning (plain English)**
Even after accounting for the fact that Auckland already had a large slice of the wholesale sector, it still grew its share faster than expected — confirming that something about being Auckland, not just starting big, drove the additional growth.

**Why it matters**
This "excess growth beyond expected share" calculation is a more rigorous test of agglomeration tailwinds than raw growth figures, since it nets out the city's starting size advantage.

**Mechanism**
Location-quotient-based expected-share calculations isolate the portion of a city's sectoral growth attributable to agglomeration and competitiveness effects, beyond what its initial industry composition alone would predict.

**Evidence**
Coleman, Maré & Zheng (2019), NZ Productivity Commission Working Paper 2019/1, location-quotient shift-share analysis. Evidence strength is rated observational.

**Dependencies**
Illustrates the same agglomeration tailwind documented economy-wide in claim-0168.

**Policy implications**
- This methodology (expected vs. actual sectoral share) is a useful diagnostic for distinguishing genuine local competitiveness gains from simple compositional effects when evaluating regional economic performance.

**Relevant institutions**
MBIE, NZ Productivity Commission.

---
## claim-0170: Slow-growing and small NZ cities have higher labour-market "excess churn"

**Statement**
Slowly growing and small New Zealand cities exhibit substantially higher "excess churn" (job creation and destruction beyond what net employment change requires) than fast-growing and large cities; a regression across 65 industries and 30 urban areas finds coefficients of −0.27 on log city growth and −0.017 on log city size (R² = 0.80).

**Meaning (plain English)**
In declining or small towns, far more jobs are being created and destroyed behind the scenes than the net change in employment would suggest — workers are being shed from some firms and absorbed by others at a higher rate, even when the town's total job count barely moves.

**Why it matters**
High excess churn signals costly labour-market reallocation friction: workers in struggling towns face more job loss and re-employment search than the headline employment figures imply, which has welfare and retraining-policy implications beyond simple unemployment counts.

**Mechanism**
In slow-growing or small local economies, firms cannot smoothly scale employment to match demand; instead they shed and hire in discrete, lumpy adjustments, producing more gross job flows per unit of net employment change than in larger, faster-growing markets with deeper, more liquid labour pools.

**Evidence**
Coleman, Maré & Zheng (2019), NZ Productivity Commission Working Paper 2019/1, regression of 65-industry excess churn on city growth and size, N=30 NZ urban areas. Evidence strength is rated observational.

**Dependencies**
None listed.

**Policy implications**
- Active labour market policy (retraining, job-search support) should be weighted more heavily toward slow-growing and small towns, since headline employment stability there may mask high underlying worker turnover and displacement.
- Excess churn could be monitored as an early-warning indicator of local labour market distress, distinct from the unemployment rate.

**Relevant institutions**
MSD, MBIE, Te Waihanga.

---
## claim-0171: Manufacturing shock effects diverge sharply by regional specialisation

**Statement**
A negative employment shock to a regionally specialised manufacturing industry causes net job losses elsewhere in the local economy (a 100-job shock yields a 36-job net employment loss), whereas an identical shock to a widely distributed manufacturing industry is followed by net employment gains elsewhere (a 100-job shock yields a 60-job net employment increase).

**Meaning (plain English)**
Losing jobs in a manufacturing industry that is concentrated in just a few towns (e.g. a specific export-processing plant) drags the rest of the local economy down with it. Losing the same number of jobs in a manufacturing industry found in most towns (e.g. general engineering) is actually followed by job gains elsewhere in town — workers and resources get reabsorbed.

**Why it matters**
This is a striking and counterintuitive asymmetry: the type of manufacturing matters more than the size of the shock for predicting whether a town recovers or spirals downward.

**Mechanism**
Regionally specialised manufacturing is more likely tied to immobile local resources (e.g. processing of local primary output, or a single dominant employer with specific local infrastructure), so its loss removes resources and confidence that cannot be quickly redeployed locally. Widely distributed manufacturing draws on more generic, transferable skills and infrastructure that can flow more easily into other local industries when a shock hits.

**Evidence**
Coleman, Maré & Zheng (2019), NZ Productivity Commission Working Paper 2019/1, Bartik (1991) shift-share shock methodology applied to NZ city-industry employment data, 1976–2013. Evidence strength is rated observational.

**Dependencies**
Contrasts with claim-0172, where primary-sector shocks show no compensating expansion at all — primary sector shocks are even more damaging than regionally specialised manufacturing shocks. Related to claim-0173 on the limited explanatory power of initial industry mix.

**Policy implications**
- Regional economic resilience planning should classify local manufacturing exposure by specialisation type (regionally specialised vs. widely distributed), not just by sector or total employment, when assessing vulnerability to economic shocks.
- Towns dependent on a single specialised manufacturing plant or industry face materially higher risk of compounding job losses following a shock than towns with more generic manufacturing bases.

**Relevant institutions**
MBIE, Te Waihanga, regional economic development agencies.

---
## claim-0172: Primary-sector shocks are amplified, not absorbed, by the local economy

**Statement**
A negative employment shock to a city's primary sector is amplified rather than offset by the rest of the local economy: a forecast loss of 100 primary-sector jobs is associated with an actual total loss of 108 jobs (63 in the primary sector itself, plus a further 45 in other sectors), with no compensating expansion elsewhere.

**Meaning (plain English)**
When a town's farming, forestry, or fishing-based economy takes a hit, the rest of the town's economy doesn't pick up the slack — it gets worse too. A 100-job primary-sector loss becomes a 108-job total loss with no silver lining anywhere else in the local economy.

**Why it matters**
This is the most severe of the shock-response patterns documented in this study: primary-sector-dependent towns face compounding rather than cushioned downturns, with direct relevance for rural and provincial economic policy in New Zealand.

**Mechanism**
Primary-sector activity typically anchors local supply chains (transport, processing, retail, services) in rural and provincial towns to an even greater degree than manufacturing does, so its loss removes the demand base for the rest of the local economy without any substitute sector able to absorb the displaced spending and labour.

**Evidence**
Coleman, Maré & Zheng (2019), NZ Productivity Commission Working Paper 2019/1, Bartik (1991) shift-share shock methodology, own-shock coefficient β=0.63 (95% CI 0.4–0.8). Evidence strength is rated observational.

**Dependencies**
Contrasts with claim-0171, where widely distributed manufacturing shocks are followed by compensating job gains elsewhere.

**Policy implications**
- Provincial and rural economic resilience strategy should treat primary-sector shocks as the highest-risk shock category for compounding local economic decline, warranting proactive diversification support ahead of shocks rather than reactive support after the fact.
- Given the absence of local compensating mechanisms, central government transfer and retraining support is likely to be more necessary for primary-sector-shocked towns than for towns affected by widely distributed manufacturing shocks.

**Relevant institutions**
MBIE, MPI, Te Waihanga, regional economic development agencies.

---
## claim-0173: Initial industry mix explains only ~10% of NZ cross-city growth differences

**Statement**
Differences in cities' initial industry mix explain only about 10% of the differences in cross-city employment growth rates between 1976 and 2013; most of the variation is attributable to differential local performance within industries rather than which industries a city happened to specialise in.

**Meaning (plain English)**
Having "the right" industries to start with barely matters. What actually separates fast-growing from slow-growing New Zealand cities is mostly about how well each city's businesses performed within their industries, not which industries those happened to be.

**Why it matters**
This undercuts a common policy intuition — that regional growth gaps are mainly about industry composition (e.g. "this town just doesn't have the growth industries") — and redirects attention toward local competitiveness factors instead.

**Mechanism**
The Esteban-Marquillas (1972) shift-share decomposition separates city growth into a national-growth component, an industry-mix component, and a local-competitiveness (allocative/dynamic) component; the small size of the industry-mix component implies the latter dominates.

**Evidence**
Coleman, Maré & Zheng (2019), NZ Productivity Commission Working Paper 2019/1, Esteban-Marquillas (1972) shift-share decomposition applied to NZ city-industry employment, 1976–2013. Evidence strength is rated observational.

**Dependencies**
Qualifies claim-0168 and claim-0169 — Auckland's gains came disproportionately from out-performing its expected share within sectors, not merely from having the right industries to begin with. Related to claim-0174 on declining regional specialisation.

**Policy implications**
- Place-based economic development policy should focus on local competitiveness factors (skills, infrastructure, business environment, agglomeration access) rather than attempting to engineer a "better" industry mix for lagging towns.
- Industry-attraction strategies premised on the idea that the right sector mix alone will close regional growth gaps are not well supported by this evidence.

**Relevant institutions**
MBIE, Te Waihanga, regional economic development agencies.

---
## claim-0174: NZ cities diversified rather than specialised, 1976–2013

**Statement**
New Zealand cities and towns became more industrially diversified, not more specialised, between 1976 and 2013: 23 of 30 urban areas saw their Regional Specialisation Index decline, and the fastest-growing areas diversified faster (−0.10 points on average) than slower-growing areas (−0.04 points).

**Meaning (plain English)**
Towns and cities that grew the fastest didn't double down on one or two strong industries — they broadened the range of industries they hosted. Specialisation, as a growth strategy, is not what New Zealand's growth cities actually did.

**Why it matters**
This challenges a "comparative advantage through specialisation" narrative for regional growth and instead supports a broader, diversification-friendly view of how successful New Zealand cities have actually grown.

**Mechanism**
As cities grow, they tend to attract a widening range of services and supporting industries (the same forces captured in central place theory and urban hierarchy models), so successful growth is associated with industrial broadening rather than narrowing.

**Evidence**
Coleman, Maré & Zheng (2019), NZ Productivity Commission Working Paper 2019/1, Regional Specialisation Index (Gini-coefficient-based), 1976–2013. Evidence strength is rated observational.

**Dependencies**
Related to claim-0173 on the limited explanatory power of initial industry mix for growth differences.

**Policy implications**
- Regional development strategies premised on building a single "signature industry" or narrow specialisation for a city or town run counter to the empirical pattern of successful NZ city growth, which has favoured diversification.

**Relevant institutions**
MBIE, Te Waihanga, regional economic development agencies.

---
## claim-0175: Services-led urban concentration is unlikely to be reversed by regional policy

**Statement**
The long-run decline of manufacturing and the growth of services has structurally favoured large cities through stronger agglomeration benefits, a shift the authors characterise as unlikely to be reversed or offset by regional policy interventions.

**Meaning (plain English)**
The economic forces that have made Auckland and other large cities grow faster than smaller towns are deep, structural, and global in nature — they are not the kind of thing a regional grant programme or local incentive scheme is likely to reverse.

**Why it matters**
This is a sobering conclusion for regional policy: it suggests that interventions aimed at redistributing growth away from large cities toward smaller towns are working against, rather than with, the underlying economic forces.

**Mechanism**
Services sectors (especially those with strong agglomeration economies, like finance and professional services) benefit disproportionately from city size in ways manufacturing historically did not to the same degree, so a structural economy-wide shift toward services mechanically favours large cities regardless of regional policy settings.

**Evidence**
Coleman, Maré & Zheng (2019), NZ Productivity Commission Working Paper 2019/1, synthesis of findings in claims 0167–0174. Evidence strength is rated observational.

**Dependencies**
Synthesises claim-0168 (sectoral shift favouring Auckland) and claim-0047 (urban primacy reduces growth, with a non-linear relationship), while drawing a more pessimistic conclusion specifically about the tractability of regional interventions.

**Policy implications**
- Central government should calibrate expectations for regional development programmes accordingly: such programmes are more credible as a way to manage the consequences of urban concentration (e.g. supporting declining towns) than as a way to reverse it.
- Resources aimed at "rebalancing" growth toward secondary cities should be evaluated against the realistic ceiling this evidence implies, rather than assumed to be capable of fully offsetting agglomeration-driven concentration.

**Relevant institutions**
MBIE, Te Waihanga, Treasury, regional economic development agencies.

---
## claim-0176: Non-tradeable productivity programmes offer the widest regional benefit

**Statement**
Government programmes that improve the productivity of non-tradeable (locally-serving) businesses are likely to produce more widely distributed regional benefits than sector-specific or place-specific interventions targeted at particular industries.

**Meaning (plain English)**
Rather than trying to pick winning industries for particular towns, the most broadly useful thing government can do is help ordinary local businesses — retailers, tradespeople, local services — become more productive, since every town has these businesses regardless of its specialisation.

**Why it matters**
This gives regional policy a constructive, evidence-based alternative to industry-targeted interventions that the same study finds are unlikely to reverse urban concentration (claim-0175).

**Mechanism**
Non-tradeable, locally-serving businesses are present in every city and town by definition (they serve local demand rather than competing nationally or internationally), so productivity-enhancing interventions targeted at this business segment automatically reach every location rather than being concentrated wherever a particular tradeable industry happens to be located.

**Evidence**
Coleman, Maré & Zheng (2019), NZ Productivity Commission Working Paper 2019/1, conclusion drawn from the preceding shift-share and shock-response analysis. Evidence strength is rated observational.

**Dependencies**
Follows from claim-0175 (structural urban concentration is unlikely to be reversed by regional interventions) as the constructive alternative policy direction.

**Policy implications**
- MBIE and regional economic development programmes should prioritise general business-productivity support (digital adoption, management capability, access to finance) over sector-specific subsidies when the explicit goal is broad-based regional benefit.
- This reframes "regional development" away from picking winning industries and toward raising the productivity floor of the universally-present non-tradeable business base.

**Relevant institutions**
MBIE, regional economic development agencies, NZTE.

---
## claim-0177: The Roback model splits location attractiveness into Quality of Life and Quality of Business

**Statement**
The Roback spatial-equilibrium framework decomposes a location's attractiveness into two distinct, separately estimable indices: Quality of Life (QL), reflecting how much households value living there net of wages and rents, and Quality of Business (QB), reflecting how much firms value operating there net of wages and rents.
