**Meaning (plain English)**
A place can be great to live in but bad to do business in, or vice versa — this framework gives each quality a separate, measurable score, derived from how much people are willing to accept in lower wages, or pay in higher rent, to live or work there.

**Why it matters**
This conceptual split is essential for understanding why household population growth and firm-driven economic growth do not always move together across New Zealand cities — a recurring theme in the claims that follow.

**Mechanism**
Under the Roback model's equilibrium assumption, any amenity or disamenity a location offers gets fully "priced in" via local wages and rents until households and firms are indifferent between locations; QL and QB are then backed out from the resulting wage and rent differentials.

**Evidence**
Preston, Maré, Grimes & Donovan (2018), Motu Working Paper 18-14, theoretical model following Roback (1982, 1988) and Maré and Poot (2018), estimated on NZ census data 1976–2013. Evidence strength is rated theoretical.

**Dependencies**
None listed; this is the foundational definitional claim for claims 0178–0185.

**Policy implications**
- Policy evaluation of place-based interventions should specify which of QL or QB (or both) is the intended target, since interventions that raise one do not necessarily raise the other.

**Relevant institutions**
HUD/MHuD, MBIE, Auckland Council.

---
## claim-0178: QL and QB are negatively correlated across NZ cities

**Statement**
Quality of Life and Quality of Business are substantially negatively correlated across New Zealand cities (correlation coefficient −0.57): only Christchurch, Tauranga and Queenstown rank above average on both measures in every period studied, and only 18% of all settlement-year observations achieve this combination.

**Meaning (plain English)**
Places that are great to live in tend not to be great places to do business, and vice versa. Very few New Zealand places manage to be good at both simultaneously.

**Why it matters**
This tradeoff is central to understanding the divergent growth patterns of New Zealand places — and to why a single "most attractive place" ranking can be misleading without specifying for whom (households or firms).

**Mechanism**
The negative correlation arises from the underlying drivers documented in claims 0182–0183: population growth (which raises QB) tends to reduce QL through congestion, while the natural and lifestyle amenities that raise QL (sun, water, low density) are generally found away from the largest, most business-attractive cities.

**Evidence**
Preston, Maré, Grimes & Donovan (2018), Motu Working Paper 18-14, correlation analysis across 130 NZ settlements, 1976–2013. Evidence strength is rated observational.

**Dependencies**
Synthesises claim-0182 (population growth lowers QL) and claim-0183 (population growth raises QB). Related to claim-0185 (divergent household/firm preferences).

**Policy implications**
- Spatial strategy documents that claim a place can simultaneously maximise both liveability and business attractiveness should be scrutinised against this empirical tradeoff.
- Multi-objective place-based policy needs to be explicit about which objective (QL or QB) is being prioritised in any given location, given how rarely both are achieved together.

**Relevant institutions**
HUD/MHuD, MBIE, Auckland Council, regional councils.

---
## claim-0179: Climate significantly affects Quality of Life in NZ cities

**Statement**
Climate significantly affects a New Zealand city's Quality of Life: a one standard deviation increase in rainfall is associated with a 0.27 SD decrease in QL, while a one standard deviation increase in monthly hours of sunshine is associated with a 0.21 SD increase in QL.

**Meaning (plain English)**
Sunnier, drier places score meaningfully higher on quality of life than wetter, cloudier ones — climate is not a minor footnote to liveability, it is one of its statistically strongest predictors.

**Why it matters**
Because climate is essentially fixed for a given location, this establishes a structural, policy-invariant component of place attractiveness that spatial and population strategies must work around rather than try to change.

**Mechanism**
Climate directly affects outdoor amenity, recreation opportunities, and day-to-day wellbeing, and these effects are capitalised into local rents and wages exactly as the Roback model predicts, producing measurable QL differences across NZ's diverse climate zones.

**Evidence**
Preston, Maré, Grimes & Donovan (2018), Motu Working Paper 18-14, city fixed-effects regression, 1996–2013 period, both coefficients significant at 5%. Evidence strength is rated panel_fe.

**Dependencies**
Related to claim-0180 (water proximity also raises QL) and claim-0189 (preliminary finding that climate explains ~25% of total amenity variation).

**Policy implications**
- Population and regional strategy should treat climate-driven QL differences as a fixed constraint that some places (e.g. Northland, top of the South Island) hold a permanent natural advantage in, rather than a gap that policy can close.
- High-QL, lower-QB locations may be well suited to policies supporting retirement migration, remote work, or lifestyle-sector development rather than firm-attraction strategies.

**Relevant institutions**
HUD/MHuD, regional councils.

---
## claim-0180: Water-proximate NZ cities have higher Quality of Life

**Statement**
A New Zealand city located by the water (coastal or near a significant water body) has a Quality of Life measure approximately 0.36 standard deviations higher than an inland city, a relationship that holds consistently across both the 1976–1991 and 1996–2013 periods.

**Meaning (plain English)**
Being near the coast or a lake is a durable, large amenity advantage for a town's liveability — bigger in this study than the rainfall or sunshine effects — and it has not changed in strength over four decades.

**Why it matters**
This is a stable, geography-determined QL advantage, useful for understanding why coastal and lakeside towns (e.g. Tauranga, Queenstown, Whitianga) consistently rank highly on liveability measures.

**Mechanism**
Proximity to water provides recreational, aesthetic, and lifestyle amenities that are capitalised into local rents and wages following the same Roback mechanism documented in claim-0179 for climate.

**Evidence**
Preston, Maré, Grimes & Donovan (2018), Motu Working Paper 18-14, city fixed-effects regression, significant at 5% in both periods studied. Evidence strength is rated panel_fe.

**Dependencies**
Related to claim-0179 (climate also significantly affects QL).

**Policy implications**
- Coastal and lakeside towns' persistent appeal for lifestyle and retirement migration should be treated as a structural geographic advantage in regional population planning, distinct from, and additive to, climate effects.

**Relevant institutions**
HUD/MHuD, regional councils, Department of Conservation.

---
## claim-0181: Education's link to Quality of Life strengthened sharply after the mid-1990s

**Statement**
Education's relationship with Quality of Life strengthened markedly between the two halves of New Zealand's post-1976 history: there was no relationship between a city's workforce education share and QL in 1976–1991, but by 1996–2013 a one standard deviation increase in education was associated with a 0.27 SD increase in QL (highly significant).

**Meaning (plain English)**
Before the mid-1990s, having more educated workers in a town made no measurable difference to how liveable it was. Since then, more-educated towns have become noticeably more liveable — education and a high quality of life have started moving together.

**Why it matters**
This shift suggests a changing relationship between human capital and place attractiveness in New Zealand, potentially reflecting the rise of "creative class" or knowledge-economy amenity preferences documented internationally since the 1990s.

**Mechanism**
The direction of causality is not established: it is consistent with educated workers increasingly sorting into already-attractive places, and/or with the cultural, social, and amenity infrastructure associated with an educated workforce (cafes, arts, services) itself becoming a more prominent driver of liveability over time.

**Evidence**
Preston, Maré, Grimes & Donovan (2018), Motu Working Paper 18-14, city fixed-effects regression comparing 1976–1991 to 1996–2013. Evidence strength is rated panel_fe.

**Dependencies**
Contrasts with claim-0184, where education's relationship with Quality of Business is negative over the same later period — a further household/firm divergence.

**Policy implications**
- Place-based strategies aiming to raise liveability for skilled workers should recognise this is a relatively recent (post-1990s) and still-developing relationship, not a long-established structural feature of NZ cities.

**Relevant institutions**
HUD/MHuD, Ministry of Education, regional councils.

---
## claim-0182: Population growth reduced QL in earlier decades, with a weaker effect since

**Statement**
Population growth has a congestion-disamenity effect on Quality of Life that has weakened over time in New Zealand cities: a 10% population increase was associated with a 0.09 SD decrease in QL during 1976–1991, a relationship that became weaker and less precisely estimated in 1996–2013.

**Meaning (plain English)**
Growing fast used to make a town measurably less pleasant to live in, mainly through congestion-type effects. That penalty has become smaller and less certain in more recent decades — possibly because growing towns have gotten somewhat better at managing the downsides of growth.

**Why it matters**
This is the household side of the QL/QB tradeoff documented in claim-0178: it shows growth's congestion costs to residents are real but have moderated, even as growth continues to strongly benefit firms (claim-0183).

**Mechanism**
Rapid population growth strains existing infrastructure, increases traffic and crowding, and raises competition for local amenities faster than these can be expanded, producing a temporary or partial QL penalty; the weakening of this effect over time may reflect improved infrastructure responsiveness or changing residential preferences.

**Evidence**
Preston, Maré, Grimes & Donovan (2018), Motu Working Paper 18-14, city fixed-effects regression comparing 1976–1991 to 1996–2013. Evidence strength is rated panel_fe.

**Dependencies**
Contrasts directly with claim-0183 (population growth robustly raises Quality of Business in both periods) — together these substantiate the QL/QB tradeoff in claim-0178.

**Policy implications**
- Growth management policy in fast-growing NZ cities should continue to prioritise infrastructure investment that keeps pace with population growth, since the congestion-disamenity channel, while weakened, has not disappeared.

**Relevant institutions**
HUD/MHuD, NZTA, Auckland Council, regional councils.

---
## claim-0183: Population growth robustly raises Quality of Business in NZ cities

**Statement**
Population growth robustly raises a New Zealand city's Quality of Business: a 10% increase in population is associated with a roughly 0.1 SD increase in QB, significant at the 1% level in both the 1976–1991 and 1996–2013 periods.

**Meaning (plain English)**
Bigger, faster-growing towns are consistently better places to do business — this has been true for at least four decades and shows no sign of weakening, unlike the matching effect on liveability (claim-0182), which has faded.

**Why it matters**
This is direct New Zealand-specific evidence for the agglomeration logic underpinning much of this knowledge base: firms benefit from city scale, persistently and robustly, across a 40-year sample.

**Mechanism**
Larger, growing population centres provide firms with thicker labour markets, larger customer bases, and denser supplier networks — the standard agglomeration channels of matching, sharing, and learning (see claim-0187) — all of which raise the returns to operating in that location.

**Evidence**
Preston, Maré, Grimes & Donovan (2018), Motu Working Paper 18-14, city fixed-effects regression, coefficients 1.374*** and 1.067*** across the two periods. Evidence strength is rated panel_fe.

**Dependencies**
Contrasts with claim-0182 (population growth lowers Quality of Life). The raw (unconditional) correlation between QB and log population remains strong (approximately 0.76–0.83, weighted) across all census waves from 1976 to 2013, weakening only once large cities are excluded from the sample — corroborating this fixed-effects result.

**Policy implications**
- Cities seeking to improve their business environment have a strong, persistent lever available simply through enabling population growth (e.g. via housing supply reform), independent of other business-specific interventions.

**Relevant institutions**
MBIE, NZTE, Auckland Council, regional councils.

---
## claim-0184: Education and health workforce shares are negatively linked to Quality of Business

**Statement**
A New Zealand city's workforce shares in education and health are negatively associated with its Quality of Business: in 1996–2013, a one standard deviation increase in education workforce share is associated with a 0.43 SD decrease in QB, and health with a 0.37 SD decrease, both highly significant.

**Meaning (plain English)**
Towns with a larger share of their workforce in education and health tend to score worse on business attractiveness — but this is likely a sign that government deliberately places schools and hospitals in places that need economic support, not that teachers and nurses make a town bad for business.

**Why it matters**
This is a striking contrast with claim-0181, where education has a positive and strengthening relationship with Quality of Life over the same period — a further illustration of how household and firm preferences for the same underlying factor can diverge.

**Mechanism**
The authors suggest this likely reflects reverse causation: government-funded education and health facilities are often deliberately sited in lower-business-amenity locations specifically to support disadvantaged communities, rather than education or health activity itself depressing the local business environment.

**Evidence**
Preston, Maré, Grimes & Donovan (2018), Motu Working Paper 18-14, city fixed-effects regression, 1996–2013. Evidence strength is rated panel_fe.

**Dependencies**
Contrasts with claim-0181 (education positively and increasingly associated with Quality of Life) and reinforces claim-0183 (population growth, not workforce composition, is the dominant robust driver of QB).

**Policy implications**
- This negative association should not be read as evidence that education and health investment harms local business environments; siting decisions for these facilities should continue to be guided by equity and need rather than business-attractiveness considerations.

**Relevant institutions**
Ministry of Education, Ministry of Health, HUD/MHuD.

---
## claim-0185: Households and firms prefer systematically different NZ locations

**Statement**
Households and firms systematically prefer different New Zealand locations: households are drawn to sunny, dry locations near water (high Quality of Life), while firms are drawn to larger cities (high Quality of Business), producing the negative QL-QB correlation documented in claim-0178.

**Meaning (plain English)**
What makes a place good to live in (climate, water, low congestion) and what makes it good to do business in (size, density, agglomeration) are largely different things — and New Zealand's geography means very few places offer both.

**Why it matters**
This is the synthesising conclusion of the QL/QB analysis: it explains why population growth and business growth strategies for New Zealand places will often need to be designed and evaluated separately, rather than assumed to move in lockstep.

**Mechanism**
Households value direct consumption amenities (climate, scenery, low congestion) that are largely independent of, or even reduced by, city scale, while firms value production amenities (market size, labour pool depth, supplier density) that increase directly with city scale — these are simply different amenity bundles valued by different economic actors.

**Evidence**
Preston, Maré, Grimes & Donovan (2018), Motu Working Paper 18-14, Section 6 discussion synthesising the preceding regression results. Evidence strength is rated observational.

**Dependencies**
Synthesises claim-0178 (negative QL-QB correlation), claim-0182 (population lowers QL), and claim-0183 (population raises QB). The authors note QB may be measured less reliably than QL, since the model uses residential rents as a proxy for commercial rents.

**Policy implications**
- Spatial and population strategy should explicitly decide whether a given place-based policy is targeting household wellbeing (QL) or firm productivity (QB), since policies optimised for one will often work against the other given this systematic divergence.

**Relevant institutions**
HUD/MHuD, MBIE, Auckland Council, regional councils.

---
## claim-0186: Migration to cities continues while agglomeration benefits exceed congestion costs

**Statement**
In the Henderson (1974)/Duranton (2008) urban migration equilibrium model, people migrate to cities as long as the private agglomeration benefits of city size exceed its congestion costs; city size settles where the marginal agglomeration benefit of additional population equals the marginal congestion cost.

**Meaning (plain English)**
People keep moving to a city only as long as what they gain from being there (better jobs, higher wages, more opportunities) outweighs what they lose (commute time, crowding, higher costs). A city's size naturally settles at the point where these two forces balance out.

**Why it matters**
This is the foundational conceptual model behind the empirical QL/QB work in claims 0177–0185: it explains why cities don't grow indefinitely, and frames congestion management as the policy lever that determines how large a city's beneficial size can become.

**Mechanism**
As a city grows, agglomeration benefits (matching, sharing, learning — see claim-0187) initially rise faster than congestion costs, but eventually congestion costs catch up and rise faster, creating a natural equilibrium city size determined by the relative strength of these two forces.

**Evidence**
Donovan (2019), conference/seminar presentation drawing on Henderson (1974) and Duranton (2008). Evidence strength is rated theoretical.

**Dependencies**
Provides the theoretical scaffolding for the empirical QL/QB results, including claim-0178 (negative QL-QB correlation across NZ cities).

**Policy implications**
- Policies that reduce congestion costs (transport investment, congestion pricing) effectively raise the population threshold at which a city's net benefits turn negative, allowing cities to grow larger while sustaining net positive welfare — the core logic behind "enable agglomeration, manage congestion" as a policy framing (see claim-0194).

**Relevant institutions**
NZTA, MBIE, Auckland Council.

---
## claim-0187: Agglomeration economies operate through matching, sharing, and learning

**Statement**
Agglomeration economies arise from three distinct sources — Matching (a thicker local labour market improves worker-firm match quality), Sharing (co-located firms split infrastructure and input costs), and Learning (proximity facilitates knowledge spillovers) — and these economies vary heterogeneously by sector and by worker skill level.

**Meaning (plain English)**
Cities make workers and firms more productive in three distinct ways: it's easier to find the right job or the right employee in a big labour market (matching); firms can share costly infrastructure and suppliers (sharing); and people learn faster from being near other smart, skilled people (learning). How much any of this matters varies a lot by industry and skill level.

**Why it matters**
This taxonomy is the standard decomposition used throughout urban economics to explain why density raises productivity, and it underlies the skill-differentiated preliminary findings in claim-0193.

**Mechanism**
Each channel operates somewhat independently: matching improves allocative efficiency in the labour market; sharing reduces fixed costs per firm through scale in shared inputs; learning raises the rate of knowledge accumulation and innovation through proximity and face-to-face interaction — together these are the standard explanation for why productivity rises with density.

**Evidence**
Donovan (2019), conference/seminar presentation, standard urban economics taxonomy. Evidence strength is rated theoretical.

**Dependencies**
Related to claim-0186 (the agglomeration-vs-congestion migration equilibrium) and claim-0193 (preliminary finding that production agglomeration benefits high-skilled workers most, while consumption agglomeration benefits all skill levels).

**Policy implications**
- Policies aiming to strengthen agglomeration benefits should be designed with awareness of which channel (matching, sharing, or learning) they are targeting, since the appropriate intervention differs — e.g. transport and labour-market policy for matching, shared infrastructure provision for sharing, and innovation/cluster policy for learning.

**Relevant institutions**
MBIE, NZTE, Callaghan Innovation.

---
## claim-0188: Congestion is a negative externality concentrated in publicly-provided goods

**Statement**
Congestion functions as a negative externality that disproportionately afflicts publicly-provided goods — such as transport, education, and recreation — where price signals are inefficient or non-existent, unlike privately-provided goods where prices can ration access.

**Meaning (plain English)**
Roads, schools, and parks get congested precisely because there's usually no price charged for using them at peak times, so there's nothing to stop everyone trying to use them at once — unlike a private good, where the price naturally limits how many people use it.

**Why it matters**
This framing explains why congestion problems cluster so heavily around public infrastructure and provides the economic rationale for solutions like congestion pricing, which this knowledge base documents as more effective than road investment alone for managing transport congestion.

**Mechanism**
Without a price mechanism to ration access, publicly-provided goods are consumed up to the point where the marginal private benefit of use is zero, well beyond the socially optimal level once congestion costs to other users are accounted for — a classic externality problem.

**Evidence**
Donovan (2019), conference/seminar presentation, standard public economics framing. Evidence strength is rated theoretical.

**Dependencies**
Related to claim-0186 (the agglomeration-vs-congestion migration equilibrium), where congestion costs are the counterweight to agglomeration benefits.

**Policy implications**
- Congestion pricing and other demand-management mechanisms that introduce price signals into otherwise unpriced publicly-provided goods are the structurally correct response to this externality, consistent with this knowledge base's existing findings on transport congestion pricing.

**Relevant institutions**
NZTA, Auckland Council, Ministry of Education.

---
## claim-0189: Climate explains ~25% of NZ amenity variation in preliminary dynamic modelling

**Statement**
Preliminary findings from a dynamic extension of the Rosen-Roback model suggest climate explains approximately 25% of the variation in New Zealand location amenities, with the top of the South Island having the most highly valued climate and, among major cities, Tauranga and Auckland having the best climates.

**Meaning (plain English)**
A quarter of what makes one New Zealand place more or less amenity-rich than another comes down to climate alone. Of the country's major cities, Tauranga and Auckland have the best climates; the very best climate overall is at the top of the South Island.

**Why it matters**
This quantifies, more specifically than the published QL regressions (claim-0179), just how dominant a role climate plays in shaping place attractiveness in New Zealand — a fixed, policy-invariant factor that spatial strategy must work around.

**Mechanism**
This figure derives from an unpublished dynamic extension of the Rosen-Roback model (with Grimes and Maré) that decomposes total estimated amenity value into component sources, of which climate is found to be the single largest.

**Evidence**
Donovan (2019), conference/seminar presentation, preliminary/unpublished findings from ongoing research. Evidence strength is rated model_based.

**Dependencies**
Consistent with, but more specific than, claim-0179 (climate's significant effect on Quality of Life in the published QL regressions).

**Policy implications**
- This is a preliminary, unpublished finding and should be treated as indicative rather than definitive pending peer review and publication; it should not be used as a precise input to formal policy modelling without independent verification.

**Relevant institutions**
HUD/MHuD, regional councils.

## claim-0190: Density controls squeeze workers via both higher rents and lower wages

**Statement**
Preliminary modelling finds that artificially raising rents lowers wages, so policies that restrict urban development — such as density controls — squeeze workers on two fronts simultaneously: higher housing costs and lower wages.

**Meaning (plain English)**
Restricting how much housing can be built doesn't just make rent more expensive — preliminary modelling suggests it also depresses wages, hitting workers twice over rather than just once.

**Why it matters**
If confirmed, this implies the true cost of land-use restrictions to workers is larger than a housing-cost-only analysis would suggest, strengthening the already substantial case for housing supply reform documented elsewhere in this knowledge base (e.g. claim-0075).

**Mechanism**
The slide deck does not detail the specific mechanism beyond the summary finding; a plausible channel is that higher rents reduce effective real wages and labour mobility into high-productivity locations, which in turn weakens the agglomeration and matching benefits that would otherwise support higher nominal wages.

**Evidence**
Donovan (2019), conference/seminar presentation, preliminary/unpublished findings from a dynamic Rosen-Roback model extension. Evidence strength is rated model_based.

**Dependencies**
Related to claim-0075 (removing all NZ housing supply constraints would raise GDP and labour force participation) and claim-0191 (housing supply is affected by adjustment frictions).

**Policy implications**
- This finding, if confirmed through peer review, would strengthen the economic case for housing supply reform (zoning liberalisation, density enablement) by showing the wage channel as an additional, distinct cost of restriction beyond the housing-cost channel already well documented.
- As a preliminary, unpublished finding, it should be treated as suggestive rather than as a robust quantitative input pending formal publication.

**Relevant institutions**
HUD/MHuD, Auckland Council, MBIE.

## claim-0191: NZ housing supply exhibits "sticky quantities"

**Statement**
Preliminary modelling finds that New Zealand housing supply is affected by adjustment frictions — "sticky quantities" — meaning the housing stock adjusts slowly to changes in demand rather than instantaneously.

**Meaning (plain English)**
When demand for housing changes, the actual number of houses built doesn't catch up quickly — there's a lag, a stickiness, baked into how the housing market responds.

**Why it matters**
This provides a plausible underlying mechanism for the well-documented decline in New Zealand's housing supply elasticity over recent decades (claim-0106), framing it not just as a regulatory choice but also as an inherent friction that policy must actively work to overcome.

**Mechanism**
The slide deck does not specify the source of the friction (e.g. construction sector capacity, consenting timeframes, finance availability), only that it exists and that housing quantities adjust with a lag relative to underlying demand shifts.

**Evidence**
Donovan (2019), conference/seminar presentation, preliminary/unpublished findings from a dynamic Rosen-Roback model extension. Evidence strength is rated model_based.

**Dependencies**
Consistent with, and offers a possible underlying mechanism for, claim-0106 (NZ's declining housing supply elasticity since mid-century) and related to claim-0190 (the rent-wage squeeze from density controls).

**Policy implications**
- Housing supply reform should anticipate adjustment lags between policy change (e.g. upzoning) and actual housing delivery, and should pair zoning reform with measures that reduce frictions in construction capacity, consenting, and finance to shorten this lag.
- As a preliminary, unpublished finding, the specific source and magnitude of the friction should be treated as indicative pending further research.

**Relevant institutions**
HUD/MHuD, MBIE, Te Waihanga, Auckland Council.

## claim-0192: Urban development generates positive regional spillovers

**Statement**
Preliminary modelling finds that urban development generates positive regional spillovers, meaning development in one location can raise welfare or productivity in surrounding locations rather than simply displacing activity from them.

**Meaning (plain English)**
Building more housing or development in one place doesn't just help that place — preliminary modelling suggests it also benefits the surrounding region, rather than simply pulling activity away from neighbouring towns.

**Why it matters**
This challenges a zero-sum framing of regional development (where one place's gain is assumed to be another's loss) and instead supports a positive-sum view in which enabling development anywhere can benefit a wider region.

**Mechanism**
The slide deck does not detail the spillover channel, but plausible mechanisms include expanded labour market catchments, increased demand for regional suppliers, and improved regional connectivity benefiting nearby towns when a hub location develops.

**Evidence**
Donovan (2019), conference/seminar presentation, preliminary/unpublished findings from a dynamic Rosen-Roback model extension. Evidence strength is rated model_based.

**Dependencies**
Related to claim-0190 (the cost of restricting urban development extends to wages as well as rents).

**Policy implications**
- This strengthens the case for enabling urban development at a regional rather than purely local scale, since the benefits of development in a given city are not necessarily confined to that city's own boundaries.
- As a preliminary, unpublished finding, the spatial extent and magnitude of these spillovers should be treated as indicative pending further research and publication.

**Relevant institutions**
HUD/MHuD, Te Waihanga, regional councils.

## claim-0193: Production agglomeration benefits high-skilled workers most; consumption benefits all

**Statement**
Preliminary modelling finds strong positive agglomeration economies that differ by channel and skill level: in production, agglomeration benefits are strongest for high-skilled workers, while in consumption, agglomeration benefits accrue to both high- and low-skilled households.

**Meaning (plain English)**
Living in a big, dense city boosts highly skilled workers' wages the most through production-side effects. But everyone — regardless of skill level — benefits from the wider variety and quality of goods, services, and amenities a big city offers as a consumer.

**Why it matters**
This has direct distributional implications: it suggests low-skilled workers and households gain less from urban density through the wage channel but still gain through the consumption/amenity channel, complicating simple narratives about who benefits from agglomeration policy.

**Mechanism**
This preliminary finding extends the standard agglomeration framework (claim-0187) to allow households to differ by skill level, separately estimating agglomeration effects operating through the production side (wages, matching to jobs) and the consumption side (variety, quality, and availability of local goods, services, and amenities).

**Evidence**
Donovan (2019), conference/seminar presentation, preliminary/unpublished findings from a dynamic Rosen-Roback model extension allowing for household skill heterogeneity. Evidence strength is rated model_based.

**Dependencies**
Extends claim-0187 (the matching/sharing/learning taxonomy of agglomeration economies) to a skill-differentiated setting.

**Policy implications**
- Distributional analysis of agglomeration-promoting policies (e.g. density enablement, transport investment) should account for this skill-differentiated pattern: low-skilled workers may need complementary skills and labour-market policy to capture the production-side wage gains that high-skilled workers receive more readily from urban density, even though they share in the consumption-side benefits.
- As a preliminary, unpublished finding, this should be treated as indicative pending further research and publication.

**Relevant institutions**
MBIE, MSD, Ministry of Education, HUD/MHuD.

## claim-0194: The "Amenable City" reframes urban policy beyond the industrial city model

**Statement**
The "Amenable City" is proposed as a successor policy framing to the industrial city: a city that delivers a bundle of amenities valued by both households and firms, explicitly acknowledges pricing failures and heterogeneous preferences, and accepts megatrends like technology and climate change while pursuing dynamically efficient policy rather than a fixed end-state.

**Meaning (plain English)**
The old idea of a city built primarily around factories and industrial jobs is over. The proposed replacement vision is a city that consciously delivers what both residents and businesses value, accepts that some things (like pricing failures and differing preferences) can't be perfectly fixed, and focuses on continuously improving rather than reaching some final, ideal state.

**Why it matters**
This is a normative policy synthesis that draws together the conceptual (agglomeration-congestion equilibrium, matching/sharing/learning) and preliminary empirical (climate, rent-wage squeeze, spillovers, skill-differentiated agglomeration) findings from this source into a single forward-looking policy frame for New Zealand cities.

**Mechanism**
The framing combines an "enable agglomeration, manage congestion" operating principle (claim-0186, claim-0188) with explicit acknowledgement of the QL/QB tradeoff (claim-0178, claim-0185) and a process-oriented ("dynamically efficient") rather than end-state-oriented approach to adapting to long-run trends like technology and climate change.

**Evidence**
Donovan (2019), conference/seminar presentation, normative policy synthesis. Evidence strength is rated theoretical.

**Dependencies**
Synthesises claim-0186 (agglomeration-congestion equilibrium), claim-0188 (congestion as a public-goods externality), and claim-0178 (the household/firm QL-QB tradeoff) into a single policy framing.

**Policy implications**
- Urban and spatial strategy documents should consider explicitly adopting an amenity-bundle framing — addressing both household (QL) and firm (QB) amenity needs together — rather than treating liveability and business-attractiveness policy as separate workstreams.
- Given the "dynamically efficient" emphasis, urban policy frameworks should build in periodic review and adaptation mechanisms rather than being designed around a single fixed target urban form.

**Relevant institutions**
HUD/MHuD, MBIE, Auckland Council, Te Waihanga.
